# Sri Krishna-Chaitanya-charitamrita

*The great poem (mahakavya) of Kavi Karnapura in twenty sargas*

*Kavi Karnapura*

*Translated from the Sanskrit into English*

---

## Translator's Note

The *Sri Krishna-Chaitanya-charitamrita* is the Sanskrit *mahakavya* (great poem) of Kavi Karnapura (Paramananda Sena), the
youngest son of Shivananda Sena, who as a child received Mahaprabhu's mercy. The poem in twenty sargas was completed in
1464 Shaka (1542 CE), in the month of Ashadha (20.49); the author's main source, he says, was the notes of Murari Gupta
(20.42–43). It opens with the lament of the devotees after the Lord's disappearance and then tells His whole life, from
His appearance in Navadvipa to the last years in Puri, with special fullness for the Ratha-yatra and the festivals of Nilachala.

**Source.** The Sanskrit text follows the edition of Haridasa Shastri (Vrindavana), checked by hand against the scan; 1,912
verses by our count. Only Karnapura's text is translated; the translations and commentaries of the editions are not
included. Interpretation follows Srila Bhaktisiddhanta Sarasvati.

---

## Sarga One — verses 1–29

**Metre:** verses 1–24 — *mandakranta* (17 syllables to the pada); verses 25–29 — *shikharini* (17 syllables).

**Contents of the passage:**
- 1–7 — mangalacharana, the auspicious invocation: Gauranga as Krishna who has become golden.
- 8–12 — the poet's self-abasement: why and how he undertakes to write.
- 13–29 — **the frame narrative:** Mahaprabhu has disappeared from the world, and the devotees lament their separation from Him. The poem opens not with His birth but with grief after His disappearance: the story is told from the point of loss.

**1.** He who once, on the land of beautiful Vrindavana, dense with being, consciousness and bliss, dark in His radiance, danced with golden-limbed maidens alike in their radiance — was it not from the union of their constant, ever firmer embraces that He Himself became golden? Glory to Him, to Gauranga, who has taken Navadvipa for His support!

*Note.* *Kim* is not a question of perplexity but a poetic conjecture (*utpreksha*): the poet offers an explanation of Chaitanya's golden colour. This is the principal theological image of the whole poem: Gauranga is Krishna, who has taken on the form and colour of His beloved gopis.

**2.** By the sprinklings of the nectar of His bodily beauty, so abundant in sweetness — sprinklings that are like liquid shining gold and quench all burning without remainder — and by the streams of honey from the lotus of His beautiful feet, the world seems washed before one's very eyes. To Him let there ever be obeisance.

**3.** We worship Him whose arms, like staffs, reach to the edge of His knees; whose body is adorned with a most brilliant circle of light, rows of fierce, unrestrained radiance; whose playful sidelong glance slips all the way to His ears; whose broad cheeks flush a deep red; and who sports like a hundred lions.

**4.** The charming light of the gems of His beautiful nails, soft as nectar-rays, flashed forth to wash away the dullness ingrained within the three worlds; and then, with the inundation of the flooding waves of His own ocean of love, He again produced a stupor. Oh, wonder! Therefore let the world of living beings serve Him here.

*Note.* A play on words: *jaḍimā* is the dull inertness of the material world; *jāḍya* is the stupor of ecstasy, one of the eight *sattvika-bhavas* (immobility from an excess of feeling). The one is washed away by the light; the other comes with love.

**5.** That He filled the three worlds to overflowing with the tastes of His own playful pastimes, with the joys of service to the feet, with the raptures of dance — this, I think, is that very mercy, eternally new, [shown] here once again. Let the world of living beings bow to it again and again, ever more deeply.

*Note.* *Pāda-sevā-vilāsa* can be understood both as "the play of service to the feet" (the devotees' service to Him) and as "the play of the feet" in dance.

**6.** In them this loveliness, full of beautiful sweetness, went beyond the limit of speech; in them profound compassion reached its fullness; in them this artfulness, charming to all — ah! — attained its consummation. Bow down to that pair of lotus feet of Gauranga.

*Note.* *Vaidagdhī* is not merely "cleverness" but refinement in art, play and love. It is one of Krishna's qualities in Gaudiya aesthetics.

**7.** Wonderful indeed! What wise man is able to express even a particle of the artfulness and other [virtues] of Him, the ocean of virtues, the abode of loveliness? Even if He, Srila Gaurangachandra, were to multiply His own power twofold in order to tell of it — even He Himself, the Mighty One, would not be able.

*Note.* The repetition *śaktaḥ śaktaḥ* rests on two meanings of the word: "mighty" (of Him) and "able" (to tell).

**8.** He is the Lord of the life of the beautiful, best maidens of Vraja. Having heard of that play of His — rich in the loveliness of pastimes and filled with the nectar of youth — from the mouths of those who are bees at His lotus feet, ah, this someone, his heart trembling, speaks of it out of rashness.

*Note.* "This someone" (*ko 'pi eṣaḥ*) is the poet himself. In the edition the first word is printed with an old form of अ that resembles ग्र.

**9.** Where is He, whose feet are honoured, as with the lamps of *arati*, by the jewels set in the crowns of Brahma and the others, the crest-jewels of the circle of all the cities of the gods? And where am I, utterly foolish, my heart inclined to frivolity alone? His compassion is great — therefore let the virtuous never reject this one.

**10.** Whatever I saw, and what I also heard; what was told again and again to me, so exceedingly foolish of mind, by those whose very life is the play of His pastimes; whatever [I] retained here and there — of that this insignificant one tells a little, being under the sway of His mercy.

*Note.* An important testimony to the sources of the poem: part of it the author saw himself (he was a child while Mahaprabhu was present in this world), part he heard from His close companions. *Līlā-vilāsais tat-tat-prāṇaiḥ* — the analysis is tentative: "by those whose life is these pastimes."

**11.** Whether this effort is completed or not — what is that to me? In glorifying the pastimes of the Lord, however much [is told], so much is great good fortune. Or else: one who retells them in proportion to his power is no cause for laughter at all, since this life-story in its entirety cannot be measured even by Brahma.

**12.** If in this [work], alas, there should be some flaw — though not from carelessness — the wise will surely never dwell on it, for they are intoxicated with songs of the pair of [His] beautiful lotus feet. Therefore I have no concern about them even for a moment.

*Note.* *Na pramādāt* in the first line — so also in the Gosvami edition (GGM); the sense "though not from carelessness" is rendered literally.

**13.** The Lord of the life of the beautiful, best maidens of Vrindavana flooded the whole universe to the brim with waves of the nectar of love; again and again He gave [all] a special taste of the nectar of those pastimes; and then, unable to bear separation from those maidens, He went back to be near them once more.

*Note.* Here the poet explains **Mahaprabhu's disappearance**: Krishna returned to the gopis, without whom He could not bear to be. The link with verse 14 ("He disappeared") supports this reading.

**14.** Thus, having once more tasted the flood of the nectar of those pastimes, He whose compassion had been made manifest under the pretext of teaching — alas, alas! — disappeared. Those whose life He was remain here somehow, as if abandoned by life itself: some, most pitiable, are on the earth; others have departed [after Him].

*Note.* *Ihā* — the vowel is lengthened for the metre (the *mandakranta* requires a long syllable at this point). *Śikṣā-vyāja*, "the pretext of teaching": Chaitanya's preaching is only the outward reason for His coming; the inner reason is the tasting of His own pastimes (verse 13).

**15.** "O Gauranga, dearest! Alas, alas, O Lord, friend of the wretched! Oh, oh, woe — You who were the very embodiment of our wealth, our kin, our life and our lineage!" Thus, again and again, the most piteous stream of words of those who wept at once broke the heart, and even walls, into a hundred pieces — ah!

**16.** Some, utterly weakened by the heat of the fire of separation from Him, saw the world as empty, utterly empty, as at the time of the dissolution of the universe; and to drive away with those laments the pain caused by a hundred wounds of inward tears, they wept incessantly with loud cries of "Ah! Ah!"

**17.** "Alas, alas, O You who are dearer than life! In separation from You the earth is constantly sunk in a flood of the fire of agitation and grief — she who formerly, through the tender, charming, tender wanderings of Your feet, was most blessed, most fortunate, exceedingly holy."

**18.** "Ah, what long, long pious deeds did the earth perform everywhere before, that through the juices of Your feet she was Rasa in the highest sense, 'full of taste'? Alas, now, separated [from You] — ah! — by force of circumstance she has made her other name, Sarvamsaha, 'All-enduring', true to its meaning."

*Note.* A play on two Sanskrit names of the Earth: *rasā* ("earth", but also "full of *rasa*, of taste") and *sarvaṁsahā* ("earth", literally "bearing everything").

**19.** "Alas, O Lord, O dearest, O lord of our hearts, O ocean of compassion, O extinguisher of boundless offences, O beloved, O dearest of all — alas, we are lost! In this most impassable separation from Your feet the whole world, sunk [in grief] and wretched, sighs under heaps of the greatest misdeeds."

**20.** "Those who were tender, His closest friends — they alone have departed. Blessed are they, whose sole goal was love alone for the feet of the Lord. Oh, shame and woe! We met the Lord Himself and had that very association with Him, and yet our life-breaths, tormented by separation from Him, remain within. Oh, shame — how hard they are!"

*Note.* *Prāṇāntas* is parsed as *prāṇāḥ antaḥ* ("the breath of life within"). Compare verse 26.

**21.** "Those who, having known even for a moment that fragrance or sweetness of His beautiful lotus feet, do not at once abandon everything — alas, what are they then? Beasts? Or trees rooted [in the ground]? Or stones? Heaven forbid — are they not devoid of consciousness?"

*Note.* *Śiva śiva* is an exclamation of horror and aversion, like "Heaven forbid!" It occurs many times in this passage.

**22.** "Satisfied by the mere tasting of the juice of the pair of His lotus feet, having utterly abandoned wealth, kin and home, their sole goal love alone, these saints, supremely accomplished — ah! — wander about like this everywhere, poor: in the depths of the wilderness, on the mountains, in the forests."

*Note.* A play on the two meanings of *santaḥ*: "being" (poor) and "saints".

**23.** "Having drunk with the eyes the juice of the pair of His beautiful lotus feet, having inhaled their fragrance, having drunk the wine of love, sweet with affection, having tasted the sweetness of His words — ah, who among the living will bear separation from Him? Heaven forbid! Alas, alas — how, ah, will one bear it?"

*Note.* The verse lists all the senses: sight, smell, taste and hearing. The very fullness of that association makes the separation unbearable.

**24.** "Even today, by the fragrance of the pair of His lotus feet, all [people] everywhere give up their attachments; all their bonds fall away without end, and they go on wandering quite freely, in the raptures of dancing, with kirtanas. Who could bear the fire of separation from Him — from Him Himself?"

*Note.* "Even today" means the time when the poem was written, after Mahaprabhu's disappearance: the kirtana goes on.

**25.** "How then were those supremely compassionate feet ever seen, ah? And how, alas, is this person as hard as a heap of thunderbolts? And how, oh, does he still remain an abode of love for Him? And how — Heaven forbid! — [has come] this slaughter wrought by fate, in separation from Him?"

*Note.* *Dambholi* is the vajra, Indra's thunderbolt weapon; in Sanskrit it is the standard of indestructible hardness (the same word is used of diamond). *Prakara* means "heap, multitude": the man is harder not than one vajra but than a whole heap of them. With this verse the metre changes to *shikharini*. The syntax of the verse is broken — a deliberate device: speech falters from grief.

**26.** "The world, I think, is empty; the earth too has melted in the mass of the fire of grief, and all people, exhausted, are melting there. And yet these bereaved life-breaths — Heaven forbid! — do not depart. Oh, what a wonder! Heaven forbid! How perverse are the ways of fate!"

*Note.* The last line is metrically incomplete: 15 syllables instead of 17. So also in the Gosvami edition (GGM); the lacuna apparently goes back to a common manuscript tradition (?).

**27.** "Oh, even today how many here, hearing for a single moment even a particle of the multitude of His dear qualities, have given up the body! And we, who always heard [Him], saw [Him] and constantly experienced happiness, live without Him. Heaven forbid! This is a great sin."

*Note.* The sense: others give up the body out of love as soon as they hear of Him, while we, who saw Him, remain alive — and in this lies our guilt. The contrast in the second half of the verse and the link with verses 20 and 26 support this reading. A misprint in the edition: संश्रृण्वन्तः → संशृण्वन्तः.

**28.** "Oh, truly fortunate beyond measure [was] this earth, full of the juices of His beautiful feet, of His Name, and of the great glory of the multitude of His qualities! Yet now, torn apart by the fire of separation from Him, she does not split asunder. We do not know, even for a moment, the limit of the play of fate."

*Note.* A paradox: the earth is "torn apart" (*vidalitā*) yet "does not split" (*dalati no*). It is the same thought as that of the life which does not depart (verses 20 and 26).

**29.** Thus, here, bearing these hot, long sighs and loud lamentations, a body wasting away more and more, and a most abundant water of the eyes, they remembered the multitude of His dear qualities, wept most piteously without rest, and — alas — swooned again and again. That was a wonder.

*Note.* The edition has *rudanto viśrāntam*; we read it with the omitted avagraha sign: *rudanto 'viśrāntam* ("without rest"). In the first word the anusvara is almost worn away in the print; *-ṣṇaṁ* is restored from the metre.

*Iti śrī-kṛṣṇa-caitanya-caritāmṛte mahākāvye prathamaḥ sargaḥ* — Thus ends the first sarga of the mahakavya "Sri Krishna-Chaitanya-charitamrita".

---

## Sarga Two — verses 1–122

**Metre:** *vaṁśastha* (12 syllables per pada); verses 120–122 — *viyoginī*.
**Contents of the passage:**
- 1–8 — the glory of Navadvipa and the Ganga. Verses 5–8 form a *kalāpaka*: four verses, one sentence.
- 9–11 — the inhabitants of Navadvipa; Advaita.
- 12–18 — Srivasa, Jagannatha Mishra and Shachi; their eight daughters pass away.
- 19–21 — Vishvarupa.
- 22–24 — Shachi's pregnancy of thirteen months.
- 25–31 — spring comes before its time and adorns herself for the Lord's coming (27–31 are a *kulaka*).
- 32–37 — the full-moon night of Phalguna; the signs before the birth.
- 38–44 — the appearance of the Lord; the eclipse and the cries of "Hari!"; the horoscope.
- 45–55 — the beauty of the world serves each feature of His form.
- 56–59 — Nilambara Chakravarti; the birth rites.
- 60–62 — infancy; the name Vishvambhara.
- 63–67 — childhood play, dancing, first steps.
- 68–79 — Shachi binds Him; Nimai, seated on a heap of pots, teaches His mother non-duality; He throws a clod of earth.
- 80–85 — Shachi falls senseless; the wonder of the two coconuts.
- 86–89 — night: unseen guests, the jingling of anklets from bare feet.
- 90–100 — Vishvarupa takes sannyasa; the parents' grief; Nimai consoles them.
- 101–107 — play with His companions; Jagannatha Mishra's dream.
- 108–112 — Nimai teaches His mother to keep Ekadashi; "I am going" — He falls senseless.
- 113–116 — He comes to Himself; study in the teacher's house.
- 117–122 — Jagannatha Mishra's illness and passing on the bank of the Ganga; Shachi's lament.

**1.** This earth is fortunate: by the purity of her qualities she is greater than heaven, greater even than all that is heavenly. Since she is wont to bear great jewels, she therefore bore Navadvipa, a jewel exceedingly rare.

**2.** Was it not because this eager earth, her hair standing on end in the form of trees, gathered from everywhere into one place the whole store of good fortune she had amassed in many ways, that she won the renowned name "Navadvipa"?

*Note.* *Mahīruha*, "that which grows from the earth", is a tree. Trees as the *pulaka* (the hairs that stand on end in ecstasy) on the body of the Earth are a classical image of Sanskrit poetry.

**3.** "When indeed will the Lord descend?" — so she pondered, blossoming in her heart; and, carried away by the force of this longing, again and again she bore the tread of the lotus feet of the saintly.

*Note.* The earth, awaiting the Lord, "practises" on the saints in the meantime: she receives their steps as a foretaste of His.

**4.** In the guise of Navadvipa this earth bore once again, as it were, a second Mathura. Who indeed could tell the pious deeds of her whose soul is overwhelmed with the delight of the touch of the Lord's feet?

*Note.* Mathura is the birthplace of Krishna; Navadvipa, the birthplace of Gauranga, is a "second Mathura".

**5.** She bathed the slope of Shiva's beautiful matted locks, where the garland of skulls gleams, and there caught a little *shaphari* fish such as she had never caught before — the reflection of the crescent moon.

*Note.* *Dhūrjaṭi*, "he who wears heavy matted locks", is a name of Shiva. The Ganga, falling from heaven, passes through Shiva's hair; the crescent moon on his head is reflected in her water and looks like a silvery *śapharī* fish. The subject, "she" (the Ganga), is named outright only in verse 6: verses 5–8 are one sentence.

**6.** She is the purifying, charming stream of the great honey of the Lord's two lotus feet; and here [in Navadvipa], full of eagerness and carrying pure waters, she made her abode, [embracing it] on all sides.

*Note.* According to the Puranas, the Ganga flows from the foot of Vishnu; therefore her water is "the honey of His lotus feet".

**7.** Though liquid by nature, she dries up the ocean of worldly existence; though white, she has assumed a dark body; though resting on the earth, she is famed as the heavenly river; though she dispels delusion, she brings the whirling play of eddies.

*Note.* The figure of *virodhābhāsa*, "apparent contradiction": each is resolved by a double meaning. *Kṛṣṇa-vigraha* is "a dark body", but also "the body of Krishna": the Ganga issues from the foot of Vishnu. *Bhrama* is "delusion", *bhrami* "whirling, an eddy".

**8.** And she, dwelling at the edge of Navadvipa as if to impart the greatest beauty to that land, became all astir with waves of longing to obtain the fragrance of the Lord's two lotus feet.

*Note.* The edition prints «शोभामिवाधायत दन्तवासिनी», which makes no sense. We divide: *iva ādhāya tad-anta-vāsinī*, "as if imparting…, dwelling at its edge". A play on words: *utkalikā* is both "wave" and "longing". *Caturbhiḥ kalāpakam* — "a *kalāpaka* of four [verses]".

**9.** There live the best of the brahmanas, the "gods on earth". Ever devoted to righteous conduct, given over to the Supreme, unceasingly [engaged] in the rites enjoined by the Vedas, they are the injunctions of shruti and smriti in bodily form.

*Note.* *Kṣiti-deva* ("god of the earth") is a traditional designation of a brahmana.

**10.** There live the greatest of able physicians, the best of the vaishyas, faithful to their own dharma, and other people too — all ever endowed with bright, unsurpassed renown.

*Note.* *Niḥsaha*, "irresistible, unsurpassed": so in both editions (Goswami's, GGM, included), so no conjecture is needed. *Bhiṣaj* — physicians, the *vaidya* caste, to which Murari Gupta and Kavi Karnapura himself belonged.

**11.** This [town] the great-hearted Advaita Himself adorned, He who is honoured by the saintly for His supreme greatness; and it was through His love that people beheld the Lord on earth.

*Note.* According to tradition, Mahaprabhu descended in answer to the prayers of Advaita Acharya (Chaitanya-charitamrita, Adi 3). The syntax *yam etam … etad* is rough (so too in GGM): the masculine *yam etam* refers to Navadvipa.

**12.** There constantly lived one named Srivasa — exceedingly generous of mind, expert, having studied all the agamas and Vedas, the best of the saintly, supremely great-souled, the moon of the race of the brahmanas.

**13.** There shone the illustrious one renowned in these parts as Jagannatha. Born of a great family, wise, a scholar who had crossed to the far shore of the oceans of many sciences, self-controlled, he was like the one crest-ornament of the race of the brahmanas.

**14.** "He alone — with all virtues, pure of mind, learned in the Vedas — is truly worthy to be chosen": so judged the wise Nilambara Chakravarti, and gave him his daughter in marriage.

**15.** Because of her great purity she was given the name Shachi. By her virtue, by the charm of her good nature (?) and by her renown [she reached] the purest eminence — one that even Shachi, the beloved of Purandara, did not attain.

*Note.* (?) The first half of the verse is obscure; Goswami's edition (GGM) gives the same text. *Acīkḷpat* is the causative aorist of *kḷp*, "arranged, named"; cf. 2.62: *nāma acīkḷpat*, "gave a name". *Atiśuceḥ* is best taken as ablative, "because of her great purity", with a play on *śuci* ("pure") and *Śacī*. *Te 'nayā* remains unclear, and the text may be corrupt; the translation follows the general sense. The second half is clear. To be checked against another edition.

**16.** Having come [as wife] to him who was called Mishra Purandara — worthy by nature and walking the [righteous] path — Shachi shone day by day like the crescent of the moon, as Shachi shines on obtaining Purandara.

*Note.* A double play on names: *Purandara* is the second name of Jagannatha Mishra and a name of Indra; *Shachi* is the name of Gauranga's mother and of Indra's consort.

**17.** As the righteous couple lived together, the duty of householders was always fulfilled in their home, well and generously. First eight daughters were born to them one after another — and in the same way, one after another, they went to the fivefold state.

*Note.* *Pañcatvam upāyayuḥ*, "they went to the fivefold state", that is, the body returned to the five elements: a euphemism for passing away. The eight daughters who passed away are mentioned also in the Chaitanya-charitamrita (Adi 13).

**18.** And then that most noble couple became the most unhappy of people, their grief unceasing. With all their effort, for the sake of a son, they went for refuge to the merciful lotus feet of the Lord.

**19.** Then, by great good fortune, a son was born to them — Vishvarupa, adorned with a beautiful form. The fair-faced [mother] rejoiced, and the father became like a poor man who has come into great wealth.

**20.** That Vishvarupa, bearing a body proud of its beautiful form, shone like the moon. Having studied for even a very short time, he reached the far shore of the ocean of all learning.

**21.** Though still a child in years, he was wise and had studied the whole body of the agamas and Vedas. Sarasvati, [goddess of speech], as if wholly at his command, became a dancer ever dancing on the tip of his tongue.

**22.** Then, at an auspicious time, the lovely Shachi of beautiful eyes shone with a special radiance: she made [herself] like the quarter of Purandara [— the east —] which announces beforehand the coming rise of the moon.

*Note.* The east is the quarter of Indra-Purandara. Shachi is the wife of "Purandara" (Mishra), and her radiance before the birth of her son is compared to the eastern sky before moonrise: Gauranga is the "moon" (Gaurachandra).

**23.** The virtuous Shachi, great as the very earth of good fortune, joyfully bore in the nectar ocean of her lovely womb the charming beauty of the signs of pregnancy, like a new crescent of the moon.

*Note.* *Kṣapākara*, "maker of the night", is the moon. According to the Puranas the moon was born from the churning of the Milk Ocean. Here Shachi's womb is the "ocean of nectar", and the child within it the young crescent.

**24.** Gradually those months — ten and three more — drew very near to their completion. The last of them was the most auspicious month of Tapasya, the sole source of happiness for the world.

*Note.* *Tapasya* is an old name of the month Phalguna (February–March): Gauranga appeared on the full moon of Phalguna. The thirteen months of pregnancy are a tradition, recorded also in the Chaitanya-charitamrita (Adi 13). The edition has *āsannatarā* without visarga before *s-*; we read *āsannatarāḥ*.

**25.** Then that lord of the seasons, [spring], delighted at the thought "The Lord will soon be manifest!", overstepped his own time and set his foot down ahead of it. In such a matter such impatience is indeed becoming.

*Note.* *Ārti* usually means "pain, distress"; here it is the aching impatience of expectation.

**26.** Wishing to approach her lover, the mango tree, the Beauty of Spring leaned on a creeper with fresh shoots and set foot in the forest; a humming, whirling swarm of bees was her anklet.

*Note.* *Sahakāra* is the fragrant mango. In Sanskrit poetry the mango is the "husband", and the flowering creeper that twines about it his "wife".

**27.** "The Lord will soon be manifest!" — so thinking, the Beauty of Spring, ever eager, kept arranging her attire, as it were, smiling brightly like a faithful wife at the coming of her beloved.

*Note.* Verses 27–31 form a *kulaka*: five verses, one sentence. Spring adorns herself for the Lord's coming: voice, smile, hair, ear-ornaments, unguents.

**28.** She bore a beautiful voice — the artful play of the soft song of cuckoos, intoxicated by their very nature — and a slight, tender smile, fresh and laden with the sweetness of opening honey-flowers.

*Note.* *Kalakaṇṭha*, "sweet-throated", is the kokila cuckoo. *Kākalī* is a low, soft strain.

**29.** She bound up the captivating mass of her braided hair, full of the sweetness of the foliage of rows of tamala trees, and happily made dance the creepers of her brows — lines of bees, languid with intoxication.

*Note.* The tamala is a tree with dark, almost black foliage; in Gaudiya poetry it is a constant image of Krishna's complexion. *Madhuvrata*, "one vowed to honey", is a bee.

**30.** Having made ear-ornaments of new, charming shoots, she opened her lovely eyes; captivating with a necklace of strings of clove flowers, she put on a garment of navamalika blossoms.

*Note.* "She opened her eyes" — the flowers opening. The edition reads *नवनालिका*, a misprint: Goswami's edition (GGM) reads *nava-mālikā* (navamalika, a kind of jasmine).

**31.** Overjoyed, she applied a fine body-unguent — the liquid kunkuma of the petals of ashoka garlands — and on her charming breast, the buds of keshara, sandal paste of sweet flower pollen.

*Note.* The ashoka is a tree with scarlet flowers; the keshara, a tree with fragrant buds (bakula or nagakeshara). *Pañcabhiḥ kulakam* — "a *kulaka* of five [verses]" (27–31). The edition misprints *कुट्नल* for *कुड्मल*.

**32.** The ten quarters grew clear, the sky shone spotless, most auspicious winds blew, the minds of all people grew serene, and the water of the river became clear.

**33.** Then the moon, his disc full, delighted by the embrace of the Full-moon Night, shone exceedingly, the delight of the world, kissing again and again the face of the fair lady of the East.

*Note.* In Sanskrit the moon (*śaśāṅka*, "hare-marked") is masculine and the full-moon night (*paurṇamāsī*) feminine; hence the image of lovers. To keep it, the moon is "he" in the translation.

**34.** The lord of the seasons, [spring], led the way; likewise the fortnight was the bright one; likewise [the day was] the best of lunar days, the full moon. Truly, the rise of the auspicious brings every excellence in its train.

**35.** At that time the cuckoos, intoxicated by spring, from then on raised a universal cry of "Victory!"; from then on the bees took up their dance; and then for the first time the southern wind blew.

*Note.* *Vanapriya*, "darling of the forest", is the cuckoo. *Madhu* means both "spring" and "honey". The southern wind from the Malaya hills is a traditional sign of spring.

**36.** That wind, the son of the sandal mountains, blew slowly, weighed down by the burden of heavy flower clusters, with fresh embraces for each creeper in turn, carrying the honeyed nectar of lotuses as drops of sweat from the heat.

*Note.* "The son of the sandal mountains" is the wind from the Malaya hills in southern India, where sandalwood grows. *Nidāgha-vāri*, "the water of heat", is sweat: the wind is pictured as a lover, heated by the embraces of the creepers.

**37.** Then, at the rising of the blessing of the world's blessings, the world was filled with serenity; and darkness, [overcome] by His lofty splendour, was left forever without shelter.

**38.** Then the Lord, having come to the earth, shone with great splendour, illuminating all the quarters — as if a multitude of moons had flashed forth at once and fallen to the earth together with masses of lightning.

**39.** Then an eclipse took place, and again and again rose the loud cry of the people: "Say 'Hari'!" For otherwise, by His mere appearance, the sankirtana of His own name would not have been made manifest.

*Note.* According to tradition, Mahaprabhu appeared during a lunar eclipse, when people, as was the custom, were bathing in the Ganga and chanting the name of Hari (Chaitanya-charitamrita, Adi 13). *Hariṁ vada* is the Sanskrit form of the Bengali "Hari bol!" The poet's thought: the eclipse was needed so that the Lord's appearance would coincide with the kirtana of His name.

**40.** At that time Rahu, the tormentor of the moon, cruelly and joyfully afflicted the treasury of nectar, thinking: "Enough of you! Now another, of cool rays, has risen on the earth."

*Note.* *Vidhuntuda*, "tormentor of the moon", is Rahu, the demon who, according to legend, swallows the moon at an eclipse. *Aruntuda* — "wounding to the quick". The "treasury of nectar" is the moon.

**41.** The Lord, longing for unceasing pastimes of love, wished to appear in the very chanting of His own name. Behold: at that very moment He filled the world with a flood of the nectar of His name!

**42.** Then, beholding this sprout of the tree that is the sole resting place where Beauty displays her play, the father and the mother plunged again and again into an ocean of happiness and could do nothing else.

*Note.* *Śrī* means both "beauty" and the goddess Lakshmi.

**43.** Then Mishra, by virtue of the store of pious deeds he had performed, became, merely by seeing his son, the very abode of happiness; and at that very time he gave away to throngs of brahmanas the wealth he had earned, beyond all measure.

**44.** By His mere appearance the planets at once became most favourable to Him and stood in high exaltation. The sign was the one called Simha (Leo), and the chief nakshatra was Purva-phalguni.

*Note.* *Tuṅga* in jyotisha is "exaltation", the position in which a planet has its greatest strength. The horoscope of Mahaprabhu is discussed in detail in the Chaitanya-bhagavata (Adi 3, "The Lord's horoscope") and the Chaitanya-charitamrita (Adi 13).

**45.** Whatever charming thing shines in the world became of itself a festive gift to Him; for apart from Him there is here, in the whole circle of the earth, no vessel worthy of charm.

*Note.* The edition misprints *यतुकत्व*; we read *यौतुकत्व* (*yautuka*, a gift, a present made on a festive occasion). Verses 45–55 are "the service of beauty": each beautiful thing in the world becomes the servant of one feature of His form.

**46.** The lord of the full-moon night, together with spring, gained a doubled, charming beauty and became the servant of the moon of His face; for desire runs after what is hard to attain.

*Note.* *Mādhava* is a spring month (Vaishakha), here spring itself. "The lord of the night" is the moon.

**47.** The beauties that dwell in wakeful red lotuses constantly served His eyes; a swarm of whirling bee-wives served His brows; the lustre of fresh shoots served His two ears.

*Note.* *Vinidra*, "unsleeping", means opened (the lotus closes for the night).

**48.** A fresh sesame flower at once took up the service of His nose, [graced] with lofty beauty; the charms of a round mirror served the charming round of His two cheeks.

*Note.* The sesame flower is a classical comparison for a beautiful nose. The syntax of the first half (*sevayā babhūva nāsā-puṭam*) is rough, so too in GGM; the translation follows the sense. In the third pada GGM reads *vibhramaṁ* (accusative) instead of *vibhramāḥ*.

**49.** Fresh bandhuka flowers, fresh shoots, corals and bimba fruits, all at once and constantly, served with their beauty His lips — exceedingly tender, charming to the world.

*Note.* The bandhuka is a red flower; the bimba a red fruit. Both are standard comparisons for lips.

**50.** The dense, shining moonlight of a beautiful autumn night served His smile, which charms the world; while the lustre of pearls, eager for the riches [it will gain] when His row of teeth appears, for now only stands waiting.

*Note.* (?) The first pada is one syllable longer than the *vaṁśastha* metre allows — so too in Goswami's edition (GGM: *śobhāsura*); probably the correct reading is *śaran-niśā-bhāsura-sāndra-candrikā* ("the shining, dense moonlight of an autumn night"). The newborn has no teeth yet: hence the pearls, which will serve His teeth, "for now only stand waiting".

**51.** The charm of a wondrous golden conch clung to His neck, alluring with its three folds; likewise the lustre of fresh, glossy molten gold served His long, lovely arms.

*Note.* A neck with three folds, "like a conch", is one of the marks of a great personality (*mahāpuruṣa-lakṣaṇa*).

**52.** His soft and beautiful hands were honoured by the charms of rows of very tender shoots and by multitudes of the beauties of opening red lotuses; champaka buds clung to His fingers.

*Note.* The champaka is a tree with golden, elongated, fragrant flowers; the champaka bud is a traditional comparison for fingers.

**53.** A heap of great gems came with all its own supreme splendour to the row of His nails. The indescribable beauty of the foothills of the golden mountain served this broad expanse of His chest.

*Note.* "The golden mountain" is Meru. *Kāpīm* = *kā api imam*.

**54.** The slenderness of a lion's waist, shining with grace, came to His waist. The rising charm of a young shoot, endowed with graceful beauty, clung to His navel.

**55.** The lustre of golden plantain trees clung to the pair of His thighs, gently rounded and tender; the blossoming charm of the red lotus — to the pair of His feet, most tender and beautiful.

**56.** Then Nilambara Chakravarti came here, his inner self full of happiness, and by the many [auspicious] signs that he reckoned both Shachi and Mishra Purandara were brought to joy.

*Note.* Nilambara Chakravarti is Gauranga's maternal grandfather, learned in jyotisha; it was he who cast the infant's horoscope (Chaitanya-bhagavata, Adi 3; Chaitanya-charitamrita, Adi 13).

**57.** "He will deliver again and again both families, His father's and His mother's, bringing them abundant happiness" — so everyone here said in many ways, and, cleansed of sin, attained the highest joy.

**58.** The wise Mishra Purandara, the sole abode of happiness, performed the birth rites in due order, and again and again honoured assemblies of brahmanas with flowers, betel and fragrant sandal paste.

*Note.* *Jātakarma* is a samskara, the rite performed at the birth of a child.

**59.** Then in due course [Mishra], joyful and honoured above all by the world, performed the auspicious rite of the "rising". Day by day, together with His age, the happiness of the parents grew until it passed all bounds.

*Note.* *Utthāna*, "rising", is evidently the rite in which the infant is first brought out of the lying-in chamber (*sūtikā-gṛha*).

**60.** Then in time He played on the ground again and again on His two lovely round knees, and by the touch of His whole body He took away the burning distress of the earth, whose soul had so long been tormented by separation.

**61.** By a few tender first stirrings of His sweet, lisping play of speech — like a spray of drops from the sparkling waves of an ocean of nectar — the swan of His father's heart shone with eagerness.

*Note.* A play on words: *mānasa* is "heart, mind" and also Manasa, the Himalayan lake towards which, in poetic tradition, swans are drawn.

**62.** "This world will be [filled] with His kirtana and His other [pastimes], full of the nectar of playful loveliness" — as if thinking thus, [the father,] generous of mind and lovely in his intentions, gave Him the name Vishvambhara.

*Note.* *Viśvam-bhara* — "He who bears, sustains and fills the universe". The edition, like GGM, has *भविष्यति* ("will be"), and the phrase is elliptical. The reading *भरिष्यति* ("will fill") would explain the name directly, but the edition does not support it.

**63.** His body flashing with the radiance of a mountain of molten gold, with the charm of a smiling moon-face, His round forehead framed by tossing dark curls, this little one shone, clad in the gleaming wind.

*Note.* "Clad in the wind" means naked. *Asakau* is a diminutive of *asau* ("he"): an affectionate "this little one".

**64.** Having entered upon fresh childhood, the Lord grew like the young moon, ever new — like the greatest of great jewels, drawn out of the treasury of all sweetness and giving exceeding joy.

**65.** To the charming "jhan-jhan" of His bracelets, with the beauty of His necklaces of coral, pearls and gems, and to the tinkling of the little bells that alone hung upon the round of His hips, the playful child danced continually.

**66.** Then in time He, the ocean of mercy, placing little by little on the earth His lotus feet, full of honeyed sweetness, took away her burning distress, born of long separation.

*Note.* His first steps. Verse 60 described His crawling; now the earth receives not His knees but His feet.

**67.** He of lovely form, wishing to sport in playful games with boys of His own age, struck them with bunches of the freshest shoots, and was struck in turn by each of them in their delight.

**68.** Once His mother saw Him playing ceaselessly with those children; and she, most noble-minded, with a show of feigned anger, for a moment made ready to seize Him.

**69.** Seeing her thus, He was filled with anger and broke many pots one after another. Seeing Him like this, Shachi, frightened, bound Him herself, quite openly.

*Note.* A direct parallel to the *Damodara-lila*: Yashoda binds Krishna for a broken pot. Murari Gupta names this parallel outright: "in ancient times Yashoda likewise bound this child with ropes for cows".

**70.** Before His mother's eyes Mahaprabhu went up onto a heap of pots piled one upon another, at the most despised place where leftover food is thrown away, displaying a wisdom wholly given to knowledge.

*Note.* A famous episode: Nimai sits on discarded pots in which food had been cooked (by the rules of purity they are unclean) and instructs His mother. It is found in the Chaitanya-bhagavata (Adi 7, the same chapter in which Vishvarupa takes sannyasa) and in Murari Gupta ("in the mood of Dattatreya").

**71.** Seeing her son gone to that exceedingly unclean place, Shachi spoke in words faltering with fear: "Child, do not stay in an unclean place! Make Yourself pure and come to my lap."

**72.** Hearing His mother's words, Mahaprabhu expounded the nature of being — of the conscious and the non-conscious: "Mother, understand this word of Mine: give up the delusion in your mind, troubled as it is by confusion."

**73.** "This whole universe, with all that moves and does not move — whatever is seen — is delusion and nothing more. How then can there be purity, or impurity, Mother? That is strange indeed."

**74.** "For since there is no multiplicity here in this Self, there is no room at all even for 'this', 'I' and 'mine'. That one Self alone always remains; all this, which is other than it, is delusion."

*Note.* This is the teaching of Advaita Vedanta (absolute non-dualism). The Gaudiya Vaishnava tradition does not share it, and later Chaitanya Himself refutes it in His debates with the Mayavadis (Chaitanya-charitamrita, Madhya 6, 25). Here the child is playing the sage: the Chaitanya-bhagavata (Adi 7) and Murari state plainly that He taught "in the mood of Dattatreya". The comic side of the scene: a little boy sitting on scraps of leftover food lectures His mother that purity does not exist.

**75.** "Or else, consider this: in all bodies — of gods, of mortals and of Rakshasas — dwell those five: earth, water, space, light and wind. Therefore everything, being made of them, is indeed undivided."

*Note.* *Mahas*, "light, radiance", here stands for the element of fire (*tejas*).

**76.** "Therefore in truth everything is pure, and nothing here on earth is in the least impure." As He was speaking thus, noble-minded Shachi quickly seized Him with both hands.

**77.** Then she brought water of the river of the gods, bathed her son and was supremely glad. But after a time Shachi saw Him just so, in that very place, and scolded Him with words.

*Note.* *Surāpagā*, "the river of the gods", is the Ganga.

**78.** "Foolish boy, why do You go again and again to the unclean place? What forbidden thing are You after?" At these words the Lord grew angry in the midst of His mother's speech, His eyes red and rolling with wrath.

**79.** "I have told you again and again before: nothing here is impure — everything is full of consciousness. And still you always reproach Me!" With these words He struck His mother with a clod of earth.

**80.** Then, stricken by the pain of that blow, she, tender by nature, fell to the ground. And He, with melting heart, crying "Ah, Mother!", hastily climbed into her lap.

*Note.* In the edition the end of the verse is printed illegibly; we read *dravan-manāḥ* ("with melting heart"), as in verse 82.

**81.** Then the women came running together and in haste sprinkled her face with very cool water. And she too, her mind still clouded, rejoiced, sprinkled by the flood of nectar of contact with His body.

*Note.* *Pratiruddhayā* ("checked, obstructed"; so also GGM) might be emended to *pratibuddhayā* ("awakened"). Here the text is understood without emendation: *pratiruddhayā dhiyā*, "with a clouded mind" — she rejoices while still half-conscious.

**82.** A certain woman, her heart melting and inclined to jest, said to Mahaprabhu, the one beloved of the world: "If You give Your mother a coconut, she will be well again at once."

**83.** Hearing these words of hers, He went out from beside her in haste — and at once gave [her] a pair of fruits, their stalks still moist, as though struck down [from the palm] that very moment.

*Note.* The boy runs out and returns at once with two coconuts — a small miracle that the grown-ups cannot explain (vv. 84–85).

**84.** The brahmanas' wives saw that the child had obtained those fruits, which by their very nature are hard to come by even for others. Greatly astonished, they said to Him: "Where did You get this pair of fruits?"

**85.** Suddenly, in great anger, He checked them with a menacing "Hum!" and said nothing at all. "What is this wonderful deed of His? Not even the lord of creatures knows it, not even Bhava!"

*Note.* *Prajeśa*, "lord of creatures", is Brahma; *Bhava* is Shiva. The last line is the women's words.

**86.** Once, in the middle of the night, Shachi was lying in her own house together with her son. Again and again she perceived the house as if filled with many people, and she said to her son:

*Note.* The unseen guests are, according to tradition, demigods who come to see the Lord.

**87.** "Go quickly, dear child, to Your father's room." "Very well," [He said,] and went; and as He went farther away, the charming sound of ankle bells from His beautiful lotus feet grew louder.

*Note.* *Tulākoṭi* is an anklet with little bells. The paradox — the sound grows as He moves away — is a sign of the miraculous; it continues in verse 88.

**88.** Both father and mother then heard the beautiful, charming jingling of anklets from His bare lotus feet — like the hum of a bee rejoicing in a lotus that has blossomed out of season.

*Note.* *Kevala*, "alone, mere": the feet are bare, there are no anklets on them, yet the jingling is heard. A lotus opens by day; "out of season" means at night — this too is a sign of the miraculous. The singular verb with two subjects is a permissible licence; in the edition the end of the line is smudged (*tadāśṛṇo…*).

**89.** Fearfully the two said to each other: "Where does this loud sound of ankle bells come from?" And just then Mishra, delighted by the sound of the anklets, embraced his son, who had come near.

**90.** Then the elder brother, Vishvarupa, reached sixteen years and entered the fair age of youth. He had reached the farther shore of the shoreless ocean of virtues and regarded this universe as equal to himself.

*Note.* *Dvy-aṣṭa*, "twice eight", is sixteen. To see the world as equal to oneself is the mark of a sage (*sama-darśin*, Bhagavad-gita 6.29).

**91.** He was endowed with omniscience; his consciousness clung to the lotus feet of the Lord, and his mind was unattached to the world. Great of intellect, he had attained complete tranquillity and self-control.

**92.** Then the father, contemplating the auspicious rite of marriage, searched earnestly in his thoughts for a bride suited to [his son's] virtues and beauty; and [the son] in a moment knew that intention of his.

**93.** Perceiving that his father was eager with such desires, the great-hearted Vishvarupa, wishing to abandon everything, left home, crossed the heavenly river [the Ganga] and went away.

**94.** That ocean of virtues, full of no small splendour, completed the rites and accepted sannyasa. For the minds of the desireless, the great of intellect, do not wander restlessly toward this fruitless world.

*Note.* Vishvarupa's sannyasa is narrated in the Chaitanya-bhagavata, Adi 7, and the Chaitanya-charitamrita, Adi 15. The word *vibhrama* is used twice: in the first line "splendour, grace", in the last "restless wandering" of the mind.

**95.** Then, hearing of this, the father and the mother lamented aloud and swooned. Then, recovering and desiring his welfare, they bestowed their good blessings on their son there.

*Note.* The verbs *akarot* and *mumoha* are singular with two subjects.

**96.** "He has only just reached youth — and, alas, he has already embraced the life of a renunciant! Therefore, O Creator, show mercy, so that he may always remain devoted to this dharma."

**97.** Thus the grieving pair lamented bitterly again and again. Then they took his younger brother, their charming son, onto their lap and rejoiced, wholly comforted by the delight of contact with His body.

**98.** And the ocean of compassion, His heart ever melting, embraced the creeper of His mother's body and spoke to His mother, and likewise to His father, with words full of a flood of nectar — words that would give life even to the dead:

**99.** "Since My elder brother, peaceful as he is, has forbearingly left you and Father and gone away, from this moment I alone will render all service to Father and to you, constantly."

**100.** Then, as the parents listened to that nectar of His speech — sweet in tone and very deep in meaning — their bodies were at once bathed in many joys, and their hair stood on end.

**101.** Their hearts were surely flooded by that stream of nectar of contact with His body: for, not contained within, it overflowed, as it were, and came out by way of their two eyes.

*Note.* That is, as tears of joy. The edition's *अक्षिण* is a misprint: the Gosvami edition (GGM) reads *yad-īkṣaṇa-dvandva-pathena* ("by the path of the pair of eyes"; at the end GGM has *nirgataḥ*).

**102.** Most compassionate, He studied and was always devoted to serving His parents, bringing them joy. And with the boys of His own age, as their friend, He played without end and got up games for them.

**103.** Once, seeing that his son was self-willed, the father scolded Him again and again. Then at night, as he slept, that man of utterly pure mind, possessed of no small good fortune, saw a dream.

**104.** [A brahmana said in the dream:] "Thinking, as it were, 'Will my son always be so self-willed, His mind restless and wholly absorbed in play?' — you do not hold in due esteem Him who has thus revealed a golden form."

*Note.* Who is speaking becomes clear from verse 106. This dream of Jagannatha Mishra is also told in the Chaitanya-bhagavata, Adi.

**105.** "As an animal, though it enjoys the pleasant touch of a great gem and even looks it over, cannot discern the valuable from the worthless — for whence can discernment come to one who is foolish by nature?"

**106.** Thus, scolding him with these words, the brahmana spoke, his eyes red with great anger. Then at once [Mishra] awoke, good-hearted [as he was], and, greatly astonished, told it all.

**107.** Hearing of that dream, the people were greatly astonished and filled with enthusiasm. In mind and in words they rightly deemed the Lord, the bull among men: "He is a great soul."

*Note.* *Puruṣarṣabha*, "bull among men", that is, the best of men — a common epic epithet.

**108.** Then once, while He was staying in His own house, the Lord — blazing with the splendour of the rising sun, the masses of darkness dispelled by His own radiance — said to His mother, who stood before Him:

**109.** "Mother, listen to what I am going to say." "Very well," — Shachi accepted His words. "Whatever is said, dear child, all of it will be done. Speak, dear child, what You would say."

*Note.* The edition has *yam ucyate*; we read *yad ucyate*.

**110.** "Mother, never take food on the day of Hari," He then said. And Shachi, adorned with the fullness of good fortune, accepted what her son had said.

*Note.* *Hari-vāsara*, "the day of Hari", is Ekadashi, the eleventh lunar day, on which Vaishnavas fast. The boy teaches His mother to keep Ekadashi.

**111.** Then, casting aside the pure betel, fruits and other things that had been offered [to Him], He said to His mother: "Mother, take care of the body of your son. I am going away at once."

*Note.* The text has *calitaḥ*: "I am going" (literally, "I have set out").

**112.** Having said this, Mahaprabhu rose up — and fell to the ground like millions upon millions of myriads of lightning flashes. Seeing Him thus unconscious, Shachi in alarm sprinkled Him with Ganga water for a long time.

**113.** Then, His mind steadied by awakening, His eyes shining like newly opened lotuses, He rose up, enveloped in His natural splendour, and shone like the autumn moon.

*Note.* *Prabodha* is used twice: the "awakening" of the mind and the "opening" of the lotus.

**114.** Then, hearing of such a thing, the father too came in person, full of astonishment, and spoke words that expressed his true meaning, after this manner: "What is this? This — what is it?"

*Note.* The last pada, with its repetitions (so also in GGM), conveys bewildered speech. *Rītitaḥ*, "after the manner [of]", is taken here as describing how the father spoke.

**115.** What people here can know His intentions, His conduct, His hints, His play? For [they are known] neither to the Self-born [Brahma], nor to the Vedas themselves, nor even to Bhava and the other mighty lords.

*Note.* The last pada is corrupt in the edition ("भवोऽपित वत् प्रभोवो भविष्णवः"); the Gosvami edition (GGM) gives *bhavo 'pi tāvat prabhavo bhaviṣṇavaḥ*, and the same can be made out in the corrupt setting — so we read it. In the first pada GGM has *tad-āśrayaṁ* ("His shelter") instead of *tad-āśayaṁ*.

**116.** Living in His teacher's house, He of great intellect brought all branches of learning to the fulfilment of their purpose; and of themselves they entered Him in a moment, like rivers eagerly rushing into the ocean.

**117.** Then His father, his youth gone by, reached old age; stricken with fever, he grew extremely weak. Seeing him in such a state, the Lord in distress brought him to the bank of the Ganga.

*Note.* By custom, one whose end is near is brought to the bank of the Ganga.

**118.** With a grieving heart the compassionate [Lord] placed His father's foot on His chest and lamented bitterly: "Father, where are you going? Alas, how can you go, abandoning Me — an utterly wretched, lonely child?"

**119.** Hearing the joy-giving nectar of His words, this best of brahmanas, at the hour of his end, said to his son: "I have entrusted You to the feet of Raghunatha; [now] may I be at peace."

*Note.* Raghunatha is Rama. *Syām* is first person: "may I be happy".

**120.** Then she [Shachi] embraced her husband's two lotus feet and, in a faltering voice, with this lament, again and again bathed them abundantly with the water of her eyes:

*Note.* From verse 120 the metre changes to *viyoginī*: by the custom of the mahakavya, the closing verses of a sarga are written in a different metre.

**121.** "O my lord, will you really leave me — grieving, utterly forlorn, like a *kurari* bird? Where are you going now? Take with you your own maidservant, worn down by many sorrows."

*Note.* *Kurarī* is a bird of prey (the osprey); in Sanskrit poetry it is the symbol of a plaintive, lonely cry.

**122.** While in the sky the hosts of gods, out of joy, rained down flowers abundantly and unceasingly, and on earth the people were absorbed in kirtana, he, standing in the midst of the heavenly river, passed away.

*Note.* *Nir-vā*, "to go out, to be extinguished", that is, to pass away. To leave the body in the waters of the Ganga amid kirtana is held to be a blessed end; hence the gods' rain of flowers.

*Iti śrī-caitanya-caritāmṛte mahākāvye dvitīyaḥ sargaḥ* — Thus ends the second sarga of the mahakavya "Sri Chaitanya-charitamrita".

---

## Sarga Three — verses 1–144

**Metre:** *upajāti* (11 syllables per pada) and *vaṁśastha* (12 syllables) in alternation; verses 143–144 — *praharṣiṇī*.

**Contents of the passage:**
- 1–4 — Gauranga's youth; His teachers.
- 5–11 — the meeting with Lakshmipriya on the way to the Ganga.
- 12–24 — the match: Shachi keeps silent, Nimai reproaches His mother, Vanamali obtains her consent.
- 25–39 — Vanamali at the house of Vallabhacharya; the praise of the bridegroom; the consent of the bride's father.
- 40–49 — the preparations: the fixing of the date, the *adhivasa*, the *nandimukha-shraddha*, music and the cries of "ulu-ulu".
- 50–58 — Shachi, being a widow, cannot perform the rites; Nimai weeps for His father and consoles His mother.
- 59–62 — the bride is adorned; the procession with the palanquin to Vallabha's house.
- 63–70 — the wedding rite: the *varana*, the exchange of flowers and the first glance, the giving away of the bride, *arghya* and *madhuparka*.
- 71–74 — the return home; Shachi welcomes the newly-weds.
- 75–81 — married life; a description of Lakshmipriya's beauty (*nakha-śikha*, "from the toenails to the crown of the head").
- 82–95 — the journey to East Bengal: the people are enchanted by Him; the river Padmavati; He teaches there for several months.
- 96–101 — at home Lakshmipriya serves Shachi and pines for her husband; the snakebite.
- 102–105 — the physicians are powerless; on the bank of the Ganga she leaves her body.
- 106–118 — Shachi's lament over her daughter-in-law; the funeral rites.
- 119–125 — Nimai returns and consoles His mother: "She was not merely a human being."
- 126–144 — the second marriage: the matchmaking of Kashinatha, the consent of Sanatana Mishra, Vishnupriya; the *adhivasa*, the procession, the rite.

**1.** With His youthful and beautiful body, filled with streams of the nectar-water of fresh loveliness, the god of the flower bow anointed Him once more as crown prince — sovereign of youth over all young men.

*Note.* "The god of the flower bow" is Kama, the god of love. A play on words: *yauvarājya* is "the rank of heir to the throne", but also "the kingdom of youth" (*yuvan*, a young man). The body full of streams of loveliness is itself, as it were, the vessel of the anointing.

**2.** He studied under the worthy pandita named Vishnu and also under Sudarshana, who was filled with great joy. Was it not out of joy that He did these two a great kindness, granting them the position of His teachers?

*Note.* There are two teachers here, Vishnu and Sudarshana.

**3.** Then He mastered learning under the grammarian Gangadasa. Because that teacher used to give knowledge to the brahmanas, it was by that very merit [of his] that the Lord studied with him.

*Note.* Gangadasa Pandita was Nimai's principal teacher of grammar; all the biographies mention him.

**4.** Laughing with throngs of jesting fellow students, and with especial eloquence, He — the jewel of the line of the brahmanas who dwell on the earth — spread talk full of playful wit.

*Note.* *Urvī-sura*, "god of the earth", is a brahmana (cf. *kṣiti-deva*, 2.9).

**5.** Once the Lord, whose golden body was full of beauty, went by chance, joyfully, to the house of the worthy and excellent acharya named Vanamali, for the relish of conversation.

**6.** Having conversed with him, He was returning happily, and there on the road He made a certain golden creeper, pouring forth nectar, as it were, the guest of His eyes.

**7.** She was the daughter of Vallabhacharya — Lakshmi herself, who had descended together with Him. Going with her friends to bathe in the river of the gods, she there came into the path of the Lord's eyes.

*Note.* *Sura-dīrghikā*, "the pond of the gods", is the Ganga. This scene of the meeting by the river is found also in the Chaitanya-bhagavata, Adi 10.

**8.** Seeing her, His beloved of old, He plunged into the ocean of happiness. Whom indeed in this world does natural love not overpower with delight when the occasion offers?

*Note.* *Prāktana*, "former, of old": Lakshmipriya is the eternal consort of the Lord, Lakshmi.

**9.** Seeing such a lovely woman, the ocean of mercy rejoiced in His heart. Without a gem, a golden necklace strand does not attain a rare charm.

**10.** The girl had come with a single step from childhood a little way over the border of youth: her childish restlessness was breaking off and shyness was being born. Seeing Him, she rejoiced continually.

**11.** Having gazed at His beloved, He returned, sporting just as before with those friends of His. Studying, ever intent on going over His lessons, the Supreme Lord shone, His charming play hidden and steady.

**12.** Then on the next day the acharya named Vanamali — a great soul, pure and versed in the proper rules of conduct — came to the Lord's house, bowed low and paid homage to Shachi, and entered into conversation [with her].

*Note.* Vanamali Acharya acts as the matchmaker (*ghaṭaka*), as in the Chaitanya-bhagavata, Adi 10.

**13.** "O lady, for your son a certain maiden has been chosen, most blessed in qualities, beauty and character: that fair daughter of Vallabhacharya, like Lakshmi embodied, descended to the earth."

*Note.* *Vṛtā* ("chosen") can be understood in two ways: "chosen [as a bride] for your son", or "[the maiden] who has chosen your son". (?) *Kṣititaḥ* is an ablative ("from the earth"); the sense requires *kṣitau* ("on, to the earth"). The text is the same in Goswami's edition (GGM).

**14.** "If you desire, O glorious lady, that Lakshmi of lovely limbs, a jewel of a bride of blameless character, then let but a slight effort be made in the matter of the marriage of your jewel of a son."

**15.** Having heard the nectar of his words, she fell silent and said nothing at all. She did not [fully] trust his word, and wished to note the signs of the same [desire] in her son as well.

**16.** Hearing no word at all from Shachi straight away, that excellent acharya went away very sad. Then, his face clouded by the musings of his mind, he saw Mahaprabhu.

**17.** He — the young moon of Navadvipa, moon-faced, lovely as a thousand moons — seeing the acharya, joyfully bowed to him, embraced him firmly and calmly said:

**18.** "Where had you gone, O great soul? And why do you go [along] so downcast?" He said: "I had gone to see the feet of your mother; now I go [away] sad."

**19.** Hearing this, the ocean of mercy said nothing to him and went to His own house. But that dejection of his made the ocean of compassion Himself very sad.

**20.** Then, having come home, the Lord asked His mother in a voice deep as thunder: "Mother, what did you say to the excellent acharya, that he goes away so downcast?"

**21.** "Why was your mind not inclined to agree with him? Why was what he said not approved? To act just so that a good and saintly man is pleased — in this a good man is the standard."

*Note.* Mahaprabhu indirectly reveals His own wish.

**22.** Understanding that her son consented, she joyfully sent her own people at once to bring the excellent acharya quickly. For what is not accomplished by His wish?

**23.** He came quickly, bowed to Shachi and said: "What does my mistress command me? Any charge is carried out [by me] with bowed head. So let it be enjoined: I am your servant."

**24.** Then she said: "Dear son, what has been proposed here — you yourself are the authority to carry out that very thing. You are most affectionate, a friend, our kin, and dear to us yourself."

*Note.* (?) *Yo 'sti* is grammatically unclear (masculine beside the neuter *vijñāpitam*); the sense is clear from the context. *Tāta* is an affectionate address of an elder to a younger person. The text is the same in Goswami's edition (GGM).

**25.** Then, having heard her words, that steady and pure man, who had thoroughly studied all the scriptures, bowed to Shachi and set out at once, wishing to arrange the business of His marriage.

**26.** In great joy, his heart full of eager excitement, he went to the house of Vallabhacharya. Seeing him, the other at once rose: going out to meet [a guest] is indeed the foremost honour paid by a saintly man.

**27.** Vallabha showed that unique jewel among the gods of the earth to an excellent seat, and afterwards, humble in his bearing, he inquired. A steady man always shines with humility.

**28.** "Your coming here is clearly a favour you have shown me. What business, then, remains [for me to do]? It is for you, O wise one, to tell it."

**29.** Hearing this, the noble one, blameless in soul, eager to bring about the arrangement of the Lord's marriage, at once spoke words of auspicious tone, the hairs of his body standing on end with joy:

**30.** "The son of Mishra Purandara is excellent in qualities. Has not the Bodiless One himself taken on a body? For He has assumed this body made of beauty, which enchants the people of the three worlds."

*Note.* *Atanu*, "the Bodiless One", is Kama: according to the legend, Shiva burnt him with his glance, and since then the god of love has had no body. From verse 30 the metre is *vaṁśastha*.

**31.** "He is one whom the Creator, skilled in creating, first gathering together the whole store of beauty, fashioned with such mastery in the work of creation — some god of love made of nectar."

*Note.* The Creator (*vidhi*) is Brahma.

**32.** "Having fashioned the moon of His face, the Creator recognized it as, it seems, the greatest masterpiece of his own of all time; and every moment, even with all four mouths, he exclaims: 'Excellent! Excellent!'"

*Note.* The Creator, Brahma, has four faces.

**33.** "Therefore His grace is fitting for your daughter, as a wish-fulfilling tree is for a wish-fulfilling creeper. Let there be a union, as of the finest pearl and the finest gem — a mine of delight for all the people of the world."

**34.** Having heard this, the gentle brahmana Vallabha, a store of widely renowned fame, reflected once more with an auspicious mind and then joyfully, in friendship, said to that unique jewel of the twice-born:

**35.** "O noble one, if this now comes to be, it is through the conjunction of my fortune of fortunes. May the joining of such a one as He with such [a bride] be charming, as is fitting."

*Note.* (?) The syntax of the verse is uneven; the sense is given from the general course of the speech. The text is the same in Goswami's edition (GGM).

**36.** "If the Lord be very gracious to me, or if a great dawning of fortune be mine, if my daughter has some very great merit — then such a one will surely be her husband; that is certain."

**37.** "As the strand of a golden necklace attains completeness through its threads, with a fine gem set in the place of the central stone, so by your virtues may the union of these two come about."

*Note.* A play on words: *guṇa* is both "thread" and "virtue"; *nāyaka* is both "the central, principal stone of a necklace" and "the hero, the bridegroom". From verse 37 the metre is again *upajāti* (verse 38 is *vaṁśastha*).

**38.** Greatly pleased by that modesty of speech in the one who had spoken thus, Vanamali said to him: "Through such true humility of yours everything will quickly come to pass with complete auspiciousness."

*Note.* The nominative *ūcivān* ("having spoken") agrees grammatically with *saḥ*, but in sense it refers to Vallabha, who has just spoken; the translation follows the sense.

**39.** Having thus conversed with one another, that foremost of the twice-born came again to Shachi's abode. Of no small fortune, his heart trembling with the excitement of the wedding, he related everything.

*Note.* The edition has *jagāda* ("said"); with the locative *nilayeṣu* one would expect *jagāma* ("went"). The text is the same in Goswami's edition (GGM). The translation follows the sense: "came".

**40.** Having thus learned all that, Shachi felt her own body full of joy. Considering Him the embodied heap of her own good fortune, she spoke privately to her jewel of a son:

**41.** "Let the time for it be fixed — one rich in the special auspiciousness of a wedding and bringing joy." Then, having heard this, the Lord, the treasure-house of all arts, made the reckoning in His mind.

*Note.* *Kalā-nidhi* — "treasure-house of arts", but also "treasure-house of digits [of the moon]", an epithet of the moon.

**42.** At His mother's bidding He, full of captivating charm, then quietly gathered abundant provisions, and He fixed a time adorned with an auspicious ascendant. Was it not He Himself who was then adorned?

*Note.* In the last pada we read, with the Gosvami edition (GGM), *svayam eva* ("He Himself"), which gives the full metre (our text has *svam eva*, a syllable short). The sense: the auspicious time adorned Him, or rather He Himself was its ornament. *Lagna* — the ascendant sign of the zodiac at the moment of the rite.

**43.** A tumult of music arose, accompanied by the lovely cadences of the mridanga; and likewise there arose there a great, unbounded surge of dance among the joyful dancers.

**44.** With the sound of the Vedas [chanted] by the brahmanas on every side, with the sound of mridangas mingled with cries of "Jaya!", with incense of sandal, aguru and ushira — that time shone.

*Note.* *Aguru* — the fragrant aloe-wood; *uśīra* — vetiver, a fragrant root.

**45.** Having obtained the blazing splendour of the adhivasa, conferred by the jewels among the brahmanas dwelling on earth, the Lord then shone like the nectar-rayed moon beside Rohini.

*Note.* *Adhivāsa* — the rite on the eve of the wedding: consecration, anointing, the offering of gifts. Rohini — the nakshatra, the Moon's favourite wife.

**46.** Then they gave the brahmanas betel, garlands and sandal in abundance, and all those venerable men shone with smiling faces, immersed in an ocean of joy.

**47.** Then Vallabha, the knower of rites, came with the jewels among the worthy gods of the earth and with the beautiful wives of the brahmanas, and performed the auspicious adhivasa of Mahaprabhu with fragrances and sweet-scented garlands.

*Note.* *Gīrvāṇa* ("god"), like *urvīsura*, here means a brahmana.

**48.** Then, in the morning adorned by the spotless sun, the Lord Himself performed His bath according to the rules, and then, to honour the forefathers as is fitting, He performed the nandimukha shraddha.

*Note.* *Nāndīmukha-śrāddha* — a rite in honour of the forefathers performed before a joyful occasion, a wedding among others.

**49.** Then there was a tumultuous festival: with the sound of the brahmanas reciting the Vedas, with the sound of mridangas and the roar of panava drums, and with those cries of "ulu-ulu" issuing from the mouths of lovely women.

*Note.* *Ulūla* — the jubilant cry of women (*ulu-dhvani*), without which no Bengali wedding takes place to this day.

**50.** Then Shachi said to the wives of the brahmanas, each of whom was eager to perform those various rites of worship: "It is not for me, bereft of my husband's lotus feet. This must be done by you yourselves."

*Note.* By custom a widow takes no part in the auspicious wedding rites. A bitter detail amid the festivity: at her son's wedding Shachi stands aside. From verse 50 the metre is *vaṁśastha*.

**51.** Hearing these piteous words of His mother, the Lord, overwhelmed with grief at the memory of His father, wept: tears as large as pearls ran down His broad and splendid chest.

**52.** Seeing her son like this at that time, [Shachi,] very sad herself, said, together with the crowd of women: "Dear son, how can You, while performing an auspicious rite, shed tears from Your eyes? It is inauspicious!"

*Note.* *Pitaḥ*, literally "O father": the affectionate address of a Bengali mother to her son (Beng. *bābā*, "daddy").

**53.** At these words of His mother the Lord heaved a long, deep sigh; His broad chest drooped, and the moon of His face grew pale like the moon at dawn. Then He spoke piteously:

**54.** "O Mother, have I no riches or people, that you speak such words? Tell Me, why today, scorched by grief, do you in dejection insist on leaning on others?

**55.** "You yourself have seen how wealth was scattered eagerly and abundantly to the brahmanas and good people — betel and fragrant garlands, ornaments and garments in profusion.

*Note.* The metre here is again *upajāti*: in this sarga Kavi Karnapura freely alternates *vaṁśastha* and *upajāti*.

**56.** "Though I am bereft of My father, My power is unimpaired. Why, O Mother, do you speak thus before Me? We are always capable even of the deeds of immortals — and yet We do only what is customary in the world."

*Note.* The Lord half-reveals His divinity: "We are capable of the deeds of immortals." The plural is the majestic "We".

**57.** Having heard what He said, His mother comforted Him with sweet words and anointed her son's chest with unguents of sandal and aguru.

*Note.* The edition prints *sāntvayitā*; we read *sāntvayitvā*.

**58.** Adorned with garlands of flowers, with ornaments and unguents, His face smiling, His noble lustre full of the sweetness of the three worlds, He shone as if chosen [as husband] by the goddess of beauty herself.

*Note.* *Vṛta* — "chosen" as husband, as at a *svayaṁvara*, the rite at which a bride chooses her bridegroom.

**59.** At that very moment the brahmana Vallabha, having completed the rites for the forefathers and the gods, adorned his daughter, whose limbs were [already] adorned [by beauty], with a multitude of very precious ornaments.

**60.** Then the brahmanas, sent by him, went to bring the bridegroom, the best of all who possess beauty, and at once they spoke loudly, in auspicious tones:

*Note.* A play on words: *vara* is both "best" and "bridegroom".

**61.** "O child, let the journey be made now. May these roads be auspicious!" Then He, joyful, mounted the palanquin and set out with relatives, brahmanas, good people and others.

**62.** He entered [Vallabha's] beautiful, charming house, distinguished by rows of blazing lamps. Then the brahmana came forward to meet Him and led Him into the midst of the house — Him, the whole treasure of his own good fortune.

**63.** With water for the feet and the other offerings, the brahmana chose Him as bridegroom — Him, the great lord of herbs [the moon] of Navadvipa. And Mahaprabhu, chosen by him, then shone, having assumed a body lovely with the beauty of the three worlds.

*Note.* *Oṣadhīśvara*, "lord of healing herbs", is the moon: in Indian belief the moon nourishes plants. *Varaṇa* — the rite in which the bride's father "chooses" the bridegroom.

**64.** Gaurachandra Himself, His broad chest shining with golden armlets and other ornaments, wearing a necklace, stole the splendour of the golden mountain made lovely by the beauty of wish-fulfilling trees.

*Note.* A play on words: *hārī* is "wearing a necklace" and also "captivating, stealing". The golden mountain is Meru, where the wish-fulfilling trees grow.

**65.** The brahmana brought his beautifully adorned daughter — like the light of the autumn moon, which bathes the host of maidens of the quarters and by its radiance dispels the masses of darkness — and gave her to the Lord.

**66.** Beholding the lotus feet of the Lord — her long-cherished desire, now come to fruit — the girl took shelter of them and bore a surpassing radiance, like the goddess of beauty herself choosing her own husband.

**67.** With the rapture of love the two cast handfuls of flowers at each other — and at that very moment their glances appeared together, as [the meeting] of Chitra and the moon.

*Note.* The exchange of flowers and the first glance of bride and groom (Beng. *śubha-dṛṣṭi*, "the auspicious glance") are rites of the Bengali wedding. (?) The edition has *abhikṣā*; we read *abhīkṣā* ("glance"). The dual *śaśinoḥ* is unclear; the text is the same in the Gosvami edition (GGM). Possibly: "that [glance] of the two moons", i.e. bride and groom are two moons, and the wondrous (*citrā*) glance between them is like the bright star Chitra.

**68.** Then the brahmana, the knower of the rite, sat down to give his daughter to the Lord according to the rule, and with his two eyes full of love he offered the bridegroom water for His feet — heartfelt, welling up from within his heart.

*Note.* That is, the tears of love became the water for the feet.

**69.** Then the pure-minded [brahmana] offered in due order to Him, the best of bridegrooms, whose form is worthy of worship, that worthy arghya adorned with madhuparka, and a beautiful seat with a grass mat.

*Note.* *Arghya* — the respectful offering of water; *madhuparka* — a mixture of honey, curd and ghee offered to an honoured guest and to the bridegroom; *viṣṭara* — a mat of kusha grass.

**70.** Having given his daughter to Him, the honoured one, the brahmana, deeply moved, felt a joy befitting [the occasion]. And these joys, abundant in his mind, then burst outward — in the hairs of his body [standing on end].

**71.** Then, when the glorious great festival had concluded, the great ocean of mercy took His beloved with Him and shone, the charm of His lotus face radiant, like the moon united with its moonlight.

**72.** Vishvambhara Mahaprabhu, having scattered delight to all the people and relieved the sorrows of the world, took Lakshmi — the embodied beauty of the very essence of loveliness — and went home.

*Note.* (?) *Viśvārti-bharaiḥ* ("with loads of the world's sorrows") does not fit the sense; probably *viśvārti-haraiḥ* ("removing the world's sorrows"), and the translation follows this reading. The text is the same in the Gosvami edition (GGM).

**73.** Then Shachi, with throngs of brahmana wives, her mind overwhelmed by the many joys of her son's wedding, led the bride and her son into the house.

*Note.* *Asphūrtimattā*, literally "a state without manifestation": the mind is so full that it cannot act.

**74.** Delighted, Shachi gave the brahmanas wealth, clothes and sandal in many ways, and then found the highest peace: for the undertakings of great people reach their completion.

**75.** Living thus in His own home, the Lord rejoiced with His beloved Lakshmi. Always doing what His mother planned, He carried out the duties of a householder nobly and generously.

**76.** With that stream of nectar — the touch of her beloved's body — she watered the tree abiding in her heart, laden with clusters of her heart's desires and with a multitude of flowers of happiness; and she shone exceedingly.

*Note.* *Hṛdayeśaya* is read as *hṛdaye-śaya*, "abiding in the heart" (cf. *hṛc-chaya*, love): the tree of love in her heart. It may also be taken as "[the tree] of the lord of the heart": then the husband is the wish-fulfilling tree and the wife the stream that waters it.

**77.** [Her young breasts —] are they two lotus buds in the pond of her bosom? Or two charming fruits on the creeper of her necklace? Or two chakravaka chicks in the ocean of loveliness? Or the two frontal globes of the elephant of the god of love?

*Note.* Verses 77–80 are the description of the heroine's beauty (*nakha-śikha*) traditional in a mahakavya; all the comparisons are conventional images of Sanskrit poetry. *Koka* — the chakravaka, the ruddy shelduck; a pair of these birds is the emblem of conjugal love.

**78.** Or are they two little golden pitchers, lovely in workmanship, [fashioned] by the Creator wishing to display his own skill? Bearing her breasts — two newly begun caskets [holding] the fullness of the Lord's joy — she shone.

*Note.* *Yugmakam* — "a pair": verses 77–78 form a single sentence.

**79.** With her face — its eyes lowered in modesty, touched by a smile, with the lustre of her lovely lips — and with her smiling cheek, pale as a madhuka flower, she stole the heart of the very sovereign of hearts.

*Note.* *Madhūka* — a tree with pale cream-coloured flowers.

**80.** With the bud of her breast of lovely contour, with her slender waist showing its folds, and with her lovely rounded hips, the beauty always captivated the heart of the lord of her heart.

*Note.* *Balad-valīka* — probably the *trivalī*, the three folds on the abdomen, a mark of feminine beauty.

**81.** Then the loveliness of Gauranga Mahaprabhu, refreshed by bathing in the current of the nectar-ocean of contact with her body, became exceedingly, utterly charming.

**82.** Having thus spent some days here, the Lord, the one ocean of compassion, went for the sake of [showing] mercy in the direction of Maghavan [— to the east], together with throngs of good people enriched by His own true mercy.

*Note.* *Maghavan* is Indra, the lord of the east. This is Nimai's journey to East Bengal (Chaitanya-bhagavata, Adi 14, "The Lord visits East Bengal"; Chaitanya-charitamrita, Adi 16).

**83.** Wherever the Lord appeared — unprecedented, like a hundred moons — there the people, seeing the Lord, were enchanted by the very nectar of His beauty.
