**84.** Seeing the moon of His face, never seen before, in that treasure-ocean of the nectar of loveliness, the people drank it continually with their eyes, yet never reached the end of the agitation of their thirst.

**85.** They said to one another: "Who is He? Whose [son] is this noble one? And by what merit did some supremely blessed woman bear this very tender youth in her womb?"

**86.** "This is the Bodiless One himself, who has taken on a body, making his own beauty twofold! Never anywhere has such a one come within the range of our eyes or of our ears."

*Note.* The Bodiless One (*Anaṅga*) is Kama, the god of love (cf. *Atanu*, 3.30).

**87.** Likewise the women, drinking the taste of the nectar of His face with the lotuses of their eyes, said: "Who is this, who by the lustre of His body alone constantly puts to shame all the pride of Kandarpa?"

*Note.* *Kandarpa* is Kama, the god of love.

**88.** "Which woman is the creeper [clinging] to this heap of good fortune? Incomparable indeed is the play of this playful one! What woman, having conceived love (*rati*) for Him, would not put Rati to shame? Wondrous is this creation!"

*Note.* A play on words: *rati* is "attraction, love", and Rati, the consort of the god of love. The first pada admits more than one reading; the text is the same in Goswami's edition (GGM).

**89.** Hearing the words uttered by the innocent young women, the Lord, the youthful moon of Navadvipa, looked at them with an affectionate sidelong glance, steeped in the splendour of loveliness.

**90.** He by merely hearing whose name embodied beings cross the dreadful ocean of worldly existence — He Himself came into the path of their eyes. Who, then, can describe the mercy of Mahaprabhu?

**91.** By bathing in it, the merciful Lord made the river Padmavati fortunate. Honoured by the saintly, Mahaprabhu dwelt in a good house on its bank.

*Note.* The Padmavati is the Padma, the main channel of the Ganga in East Bengal (now Bangladesh). *Dvīpavatī*, "having islands", means a river. This episode is also in the Chaitanya-bhagavata, Adi 14.

**92.** With its great, high and beautiful sandbanks, sparkling with its swift current, and filled with the flood of nectar of contact with His body, that river then became equal to the river of heaven.

*Note.* The river of heaven is the Ganga.

**93.** With its waves for hands, its *śapharī* fish for eyes and its broad sandbanks for hips, the Padmavati, equal in qualities to doe-eyed maidens, gave Him unceasing delight.

*Note.* *Śapharī* — a small, quick, glittering fish, a classical comparison for the darting eyes of a woman.

**94.** Giving joy to the great lotuses of the eyes of the great souls possessed of a multitude of merits, He rejoiced, moving with the gait of an intoxicated king of elephants, absorbed in strolls that captivated the heart of the world.

*Note.* "An elephant's gait" is a classical comparison for a majestic, unhurried step.

**95.** Right there the Lord — tender of heart, the moon of whose face gave delight to the people of the world, whose radiance equalled that of millions of moons — spent some months teaching.

**96.** Meanwhile here, in her own house, Lakshmi, whose only activity was remembrance of the lord of her life, was devoted to the service of her mother-in-law — massaging her lotus feet, cleaning and so on.

**97.** Incessantly, long and with an eager heart thinking of reunion with the lord of her life, she performed service in the house of God, sweeping, drawing *svastika* signs, smearing [the floor] and so on.

*Note.* The *svastika* is an ancient Indian auspicious sign (from *su-asti*, "may it be well"), drawn on the floor and at the threshold. Smearing the floor (with clay and cow dung) is a traditional purification. The "house of God" is the household shrine.

**98.** Because of her very gentle and pure ways and her very soft speech filled with nectar, Shachi considered her Shri [Lakshmi] embodied — as if another body of her son.

**99.** Thus, in the house there, with her daughter-in-law as her only companion, thinking of her son's return, she passed the time for long, fixing on her daughter-in-law her greatly increased affection for her son.

**100.** Understanding in time, by the movements of her own heart, the intention of the Lord, who was roaming she knew not where, she — alas! — took on that very pain of separation, and then, with great restraint, fixed her mind on Him here.

*Note.* (?) The verse is obscure. *Tam* (masculine) is taken here as "on Him" (the Lord), with *manaḥ akarot*, "set her mind"; one may also read *tadā tirodhāt*, "for the sake of disappearance [from the world]" — cf. the snakebite in the next verse. This prepares her departure (v. 101). The text is the same in Goswami's edition (GGM).

**101.** And then, by fate, a serpent — most cruel, utterly vile and hard of heart — came into the house and seized with its hard fangs the foot of the daughter-in-law, fragrant as an autumn lotus.

*Note.* *Cakṣuḥ-śravas*, "hearing with the eyes", is a serpent: according to Indian belief, snakes have no ears and hear with their eyes. Lakshmipriya's departure through a snakebite is also described in the Chaitanya-charitamrita, Adi 16.20–21, where the snake is called "the serpent of separation" (*viraha-sarpa*).

**102.** Seeing her in such a state, the grieving Shachi, who loved her daughter-in-law, then set about removing the poison; and with long effort she also summoned a great number of the best experts in this matter.

*Note.* *Prasaṅgottama* is unclear; it is understood here as "the best [experts] in this matter", i.e. healers of snakebite. The text is the same in Goswami's edition (GGM).

**103.** They applied means of many kinds for removing the poison, but they could do nothing at all. Then, understanding it to be the doing of fate, they all fell into bewilderment, helpless.

**104.** Considering it to be ordained by the Lord, [Shachi,] worn by affection for her daughter-in-law and burnt by extreme grief, carried the blessed daughter-in-law, wasted in body, to the bank of the Ganga — as if [carrying] another Ganga there.

*Note.* As with Jagannatha Mishra (2.117), one who is about to leave the body is by custom carried to the bank of the Ganga.

**105.** Then, when a celestial chariot shone in the sky and the dwellers of heaven had arrived with showers of flowers, she embraced tightly in her heart the lotus feet of her husband, and then left her body.

**106.** Then the most unhappy Shachi, bewildered, placed her daughter-in-law on her lap and, weeping and lamenting, spoke with difficulty in heavy words, her voice faltering from agitation and sorrow:

**107.** "My son, your beloved, went away having entrusted you to me, the sorrowful one. How, then, deprived of you, shall I see His face — I, now the dwelling-place of sorrow alone?"

**108.** "You constantly showed love and great respect, with special devotion, to this person. How, then, is she now, overcome with lamentation, not looked at [by you] even for a moment?"

*Note.* *Ayaṁ janaḥ* ("this person") — Shachi speaks of herself, with humility.

**109.** "As soon as I merely called you, you would give an answer full of joy, timidity, smiles, devotion and modesty. Alas, how is it, then, that now you do not speak to me as I weep in grief?"

**110.** "Or is it that there is not even a drop of love for me in you, that by fate such a lot has befallen you? But can it be, O sweet-spoken one, that you are going without seeing Him — the lord of your life, my son?"

**111.** "He, the lord of your life, always gave you joy, O you who ever revealed new love [to Him]. Is it that there is not even a drop of love in you for Him, O dear child, since you act thus?"

*Note.* *Mātaḥ* ("O mother") is an affectionate Bengali form of address from an elder to a younger; cf. *pitaḥ* to a son (3.52).

**112.** "O fair-browed one, you who were always thinking of your husband's return and were in tears — on seeing me, you would timidly and bashfully wipe away your tears."

**113.** "You, who gave over the grief of separation from the lord of your life to shyness and to timidity before me — modest outwardly, tormented in your heart — lived thus every day."

*Note.* (?) The syntax with the datives *trapāyai*, *sādhvasāya* is unusual. The sense: she hid her grief from her mother-in-law. The text is the same in Goswami's edition (GGM).

**114.** "O hard-hearted one, how can you, such as you were, go away so suddenly, and whither, without seeing face to face that nectar-rayed moon of His face — the only beloved of the night-lily of your own heart?"

*Note.* The night-lily (*kumuda*) opens only under the moon — a constant image of the loving heart. In the edition lines 2 and 3 are in reverse order; the order has been restored according to the metre.

**115.** "O serpent, most cruel of mind! How could you pass over me and bite this daughter-in-law, gentle by nature? Did pity not touch you even for a moment? Surely this was not fitting!"

*Note.* Shachi would rather the serpent had bitten her instead.

**116.** "This body, tender as a *śirīṣa* flower, which was hurt even by flowers and by the warmth of breath — how, then, did it endure the utterly unbearable heat of the fire of your poison? I am struck down!"

*Note.* The *śirīṣa* (*Albizia lebbeck*) is a tree with the finest, downy flowers — the standard of tenderness in Sanskrit poetry.

**117.** Thus, utterly wretched, lamenting every moment, she continually bathed with affection the moon-disc of her daughter-in-law's face in the abundant water of her two eyes.

**118.** Having completed with difficulty the rites befitting the pyre, she went home, doing nothing but weep. "How will my jewel of a son look upon the house thus empty of His wife?" — so she grieved.

**119.** Then, just a few days later, the golden moon with nectar rays returned home. Having tirelessly delivered the people of that land, He now desired to remove His own mother's sorrows.

**120.** Though she saw her son, so long absent, she did not feel such joy [as before]; for the most unbearable separation from her daughter-in-law then grew more intense through Him.

**121.** Again and again He made obeisance on the ground like a stick and took the dust of her two feet; and seeing her so grieved, the Lord questioned her, although in His mind He knew.

*Note.* *Daṇḍavat*, "like a stick": the full obeisance, in which one lies stretched full length on the ground. To take the dust from the feet of elders is a sign of respect.

**122.** Then, bathing His mother's whole body in the nectar of His own speech, He said: "Mother, you seem as if dimmed. Why are you so? Pray, tell Me."

**123.** Thus Mahaprabhu, smiling again and again, understood everything at once, though it was left unspoken: it was as if the waters welling up abundantly from her two eyes had told it plainly.

*Note.* Mahaprabhu's smile here is not indifference but knowledge: for Him Lakshmipriya's departure is no loss (vv. 124–125). (?) In the edition the last pada is a syllable short of the metre; the Gosvami edition (GGM) reads *ākhyānitam eva*, which also does not give the full metre.

**124.** "Mother, this daughter-in-law of yours has gone to the other world, and there is indeed a great cause for it. She could never have been a mere human being: she came to the earth for some purpose."

*Note.* The edition has *mānuṣo* (masculine); the sense requires *mānuṣī*.

**125.** "I indeed know all of it — the cause for which she came to the earth and [for which] she departed thus. Therefore, Mother, give up your bewilderment in this matter."

**126.** Having thus heard her son's words, Shachi quickly attained supreme peace. Together with her son, and likewise with her other kinsfolk and her own riches, she rejoiced like Shachi, the consort of Indra.

*Note.* A play on the name: Shachi, the Lord's mother, and Shachi, the wife of Indra, the queen of heaven.

**127.** Then, having long pondered in her mind the matter of the marriage of her jewel of a son, she, undaunted in spirit, at once summoned Kashinatha, the best of brahmanas.

*Note.* Kashinatha (Kashinatha Pandita) is the brahmana go-between at Mahaprabhu's second marriage, to Vishnupriya (Chaitanya-bhagavata, Adi 15, "The marriage to Vishnupriya").

**128.** Having brought the brahmana, she, wishing to find a way to arrange her son's marriage, quickly said to him: "Let Sanatana, the best of brahmanas, be asked to give his daughter to my son."

*Note.* Sanatana Mishra is the raja-pandita of Navadvipa, the father of Vishnupriya (Chaitanya-charitamrita, Adi 16). (?) *Vivitsuḥ* (desiderative of *vid*, "to find": "wishing to find") is masculine, though the subject is Shachi — an irregularity of the edition. The Gosvami edition (GGM) has the same text.

**129.** As soon as he had heard these words, the brahmana, who knew the rules, wishing to arrange [the match], quickly and joyfully conveyed to Sanatana all that auspicious matter of the wedding.

**130.** Then, having heard those words and deliberated with his kinsmen, [Sanatana] reflected thus: "This must certainly be done." And then he joyfully said: "Let the time be fixed; this is to be done."

**131.** Having heard all that was said, the brahmana joyfully and swiftly went to Shachi and reported it to her. Hearing it, she too, whose good fortune was by no means small, was delighted and filled with bliss.

**132.** Then a messenger sent by Sanatana came to her there, bowed and said: "He begs [leave] to give to your son [his daughter], lovely of form and excellent in virtue and beauty."

**133.** "Having obtained the dear Vishnupriya, let your son make her, as it were, one whose name is true to its meaning. When the wedding has taken place, may the two be perfectly happy with each other, like Uma and Mahesha."

*Note.* *Viṣṇu-priyā* means "beloved of Vishnu": by becoming the wife of Gauranga (Vishnu), she will make her name come true. Uma and Mahesha are Parvati and Shiva, the model of conjugal happiness.

**134.** Having gone back, he reported to that foremost of brahmanas all that had been said. And the worthy pandita Sanatana too (?) rejoiced with those everlasting — "sanatana" — floods of joy.

*Note.* A play on words: *Sanātana* (the name) and *sanātana*, "eternal, everlasting". (?) *Udāse* is the reading of the edition; the sense "rejoiced greatly" is conjectural (the verb *ud-ās* usually means "to sit up" or "to remain indifferent", which does not fit; the text is perhaps corrupt). The Gosvami edition (GGM) has the same text.

**135.** He of spotless fame, his heart trembling with eagerness, gathered auspicious provisions; and having fixed the time, wishing speedily to perform his daughter's adhivasa, he rejoiced.

**136.** Then, at the [appointed] time, adorned with an auspicious ascendant and shining on every side, the high-minded [Sanatana] performed the auspicious adhivasa: the desires of the great-minded attain their fruit.

**137.** Then he gave betel, garlands and sandal to the foremost brahmanas; and, joyful, he had them, sent [by him], perform the adhivasa before the son-in-law.

*Note.* *Jāmātur agre* can mean both "in the presence of the son-in-law" (the brahmanas sent by the bride's father perform the adhivasa at the bridegroom's house) and "first of all, the son-in-law's adhivasa".

**138.** Then at daybreak the Lord bathed in the waters of the heavenly river and performed His daily rites; and after a little while the brahmanas joyfully adorned Him amply with finery.

*Note.* The heavenly river is the Ganga. A play of sounds: *alaṁcakrur alam*.

**139.** And they said: "O virtuous One, may You be victorious! It is well: the auspicious time for the wedding has come." Hearing this, the merciful Lord mounted a charming palanquin and set out in procession.

**140.** His body golden as molten gold, seated in a palanquin white as an autumn cloud, He at once surpassed the peak of Sumeru rising above the Milk Ocean.

*Note.* The image: Gauranga's golden body in the white palanquin is like the golden mountain Sumeru above the white Milk Ocean.

**141.** Seeing the son-in-law arrive near, he joyfully rose to meet Him — and the hairs of his body rose together with him; with water for the feet, a seat and the rest he performed the choosing of the bridegroom, and in a moment the giver of the bride gave away his daughter.

*Note.* *Prodgamya* is a play on words: Sanatana "rose" to meet the bridegroom, and his hairs "rose" from joy. The choosing of the bridegroom is the *varaṇa*. *Kūkuda* is a rare word: "one who gives an adorned daughter in marriage".

**142.** The wives of the brahmanas reverently performed for Him the rite of protection with svastika signs, incense and lamps; then the brahmana brought his daughter and gave her to His lotus feet.

*Note.* *Nirmañchana* is the rite in which lamps and incense are waved around the bridegroom to ward off the evil eye.

**143.** Amid the roar, breaking forth, of clear-toned pataha drums and mighty dhakkas, the distinct beating of the mardalas, splendid cries of "Jaya!" and a rain of flowers, the two shone, smiling at each other with lovely faces.

*Note.* The metre changes to *praharṣiṇī* (13 syllables) for the closing verses of the sarga. Pataha, dhakka and mardala are kinds of Bengali drums.

**144.** Thus, with cries of "Jaya!" accompanied by the loud sounds of instruments, He brought the bride home; and Shachi, joyful, her heart delighted, at once led that jewel among women into her house.

*Iti śrī-kṛṣṇa-caitanya-caritāmṛte mahākāvye tṛtīyaḥ sargaḥ samāptaḥ* — Thus ends the third sarga of the mahakavya "Sri Krishna-Chaitanya-charitamrita".

---

## Sarga Four — verses 1–77

**Metre:** *drutavilambita* (12 syllables per pada); verses 73–74 — *toṭaka*; verse 76 — *śikhariṇī*; verse 77 — *vaṁśastha*.
**Parallels:** Chaitanya-bhagavata, Adi 12–13 (Nimai the teacher; the victory over Keshava Kashmiri); Chaitanya-charitamrita, Adi 16.

**Contents of the passage:**
- 1–4 — married life with Vishnupriya; the beauty of Gauranga.
- 5–20 — Gauranga as a teacher among His students (the verses are built on *yamaka*, the repetition of syllables; many passages are obscure).
- 21–24 — the journey to Gaya with Chandrashekhara Acharya.
- 25–49 — a poetic interlude: a lotus pond, sunset, night, morning, mountains and deer along the way (the story stands still; continuous *yamaka*).
- 50–55 — the river Chora: the fever, the cure with the water from the brahmanas' feet; Rajagiri (Rajagriha); the entry into Gaya.
- 56–61 — **the meeting with Ishvara Puri; the initiation**.
- 62–65 — the shraddha at Gaya (the Phalgu, Pretashila, Manasa); at the footprint of Vishnu — the first ecstasy.
- 66–68 — the impulse to go to Madhuvana (Vraja); a voice from the sky bids Him return.
- 69–75 — the return home, music; Shachi distributes gifts.
- 76–77 — the beginning of kirtana (the month of Magha).

**1.** Then, there in His own house, together with His beloved, He — the moon risen from the ocean of the nectar of mercy, the friend of the white lilies of His own people's hearts — accepted the splendid life of a householder.

*Note.* The moon rose from the churned Milk Ocean; the white lily (*kairava*) opens under the moon. A play of sounds: *gṛhe jagṛhe gṛhamedhitā*.

**2.** He, full of sweet, tender and cooling charm, by the radiance of His pure body, which had the lustre of the beautiful colour of molten gold, stole the sweet and exceedingly lovely beauty of the Bodiless One.

*Note.* A play on words: *avapuṣo vapuṣo*, "from the Bodiless One … of the body". The Bodiless One is Kama, the god of love (cf. 3.30).

**3.** The god of love, seeing Him with his heart, at once firmly concluded: "Beside Him, how will my fame as 'the churner of minds' not be laughed at by people whenever it is spoken of?"

*Note.* *Hṛdayeśaya*, "he who lies in the heart", and *manmatha*, "churner of the mind", are both names of Kamadeva. The edition has *amūm* (feminine); the sense requires *amum* (masculine, "Him"). The Gosvami edition (GGM) has the same text.

**4.** Filled with the taste of mercy, He filled the world with the very cooling juices of His lotus feet. Ah, those here who cease from speaking of Him — do they [truly] live on the earth? They are mere bearers of bodies.

*Note.* A play on words: *tanu-dharā*, "body-bearers" — *dharāsu*, "on the earth".

**5.** Then, having become a teacher, He taught the throng of brahmanas with the play of His restless eyes, like blossoming lotuses — so that Guru himself, [the teacher of the gods,] was confounded by His genius, in great fear.

*Note.* A pun: *pratibhayāti-bhaya*, "by genius — great fear". *Guru* is both "teacher" and Brihaspati, the preceptor of the gods and the standard of learning.

**6.** As He — His body golden as gold, His effulgence sweet — shone even in the assembly of His various students and joyfully taught them, that most lovely lustre of His cast forth the juice of nectar.

*Note.* A play of sounds: *su-rucirā rucir āsa*. *Āsa* is taken as the perfect of *as*, "to throw, to cast forth", not as "was".

**7.** With the moon of His face, sweet with the nectar of His smile, constantly bathing His clear lips in the rays of His teeth, Mahaprabhu shone with dense lustre, His radiance fresh and lovely.

*Note.* The edition has *घनरुधा*; the Gosvami edition (GGM) reads *ghana-rucā*, "with dense lustre", and we read so.

**8.** As Mahaprabhu looked upon the faces of all His students, the quarters were made ever red by the reddish beauty of His eyes, full of playfulness and surging with compassion.

*Note.* A play of sounds: *karuṇayā aruṇayā*.

**9.** With the palm of His hand moving in circles with the great graces of His speech, He shone, flashing all around, like a golden mountain [displaying] the play of a rhythmic tandava dance of risen, emerging veins of red ochre.

*Note.* The red palm against the golden body is like veins of red ochre (*gairika*) on a golden mountain. *Tāṇḍava* is a vigorous, rhythmic dance (the dance of Shiva). (?) The end of the verse is syntactically obscure; it is rendered according to the sense. The Gosvami edition (GGM) has the same text.

**10.** "Is it this? Or this? What is [the meaning] of this? Or which [word] of this? Is this the sense — or perhaps this?" — the clamour that three or four clever [students] raised all around Him was His delight.

*Note.* A lively scene: the students vie with one another over an interpretation. A play of sounds: *tri-caturaiś caturaiḥ*, "by three or four clever ones".

**11.** Again and again the bees, now alighting all at once on the Lord's face, now scattering, exultant in the fluttering of their outspread wings, lent the maidens of the quarters the charm of rows of [ornaments black as] buffalo horn.

*Note.* The bees take the Lord's face for a lotus (cf. v. 12). In the edition the last pada reads *dig-abalā abalāvali-vibhramāḥ*; we read with the Gosvami edition (GGM) *dig-abalā-gavalāvali-vibhramā*: *gavala* is the horn of the wild buffalo, a byword for blackness.

**12.** "Is it a swaying, red young shoot? Or is it a lotus in its water?" — so wondering, the bee, as if it had lost its wits, then fell upon the petal of the Lord's hand (?) with a touch of its teeth.

*Note.* A play of sounds: *kisalayaṁ salayaṁ*, *sa-kamalaṁ kamalam* (*kam* — "water"); *vāruṇam* is *vā aruṇam*, "or red". (?) *Rada-laṅghanayā* is obscure (literally "by the overstepping of the teeth"). The Gosvami edition (GGM) has the same text.

**13.** Because of the most lovely petal of His hand, which long sprinkled the pure nectar of the moons of His nails, and because of its lustre, the bee, which had long dwelt on the white creepers, turned away from them.

*Note.* The verse is built on *yamaka*, the repetition of identical syllables with different meanings: *rucireṇa cireṇa*, *kara-dalena dalena*, *dhavalatā valatā*. The translation follows the sense; many words in such verses cannot be analysed unambiguously. The Gosvami edition (GGM) has the same text.

**14.** (?) Sweetness, perceiving on every side His pre-eminence at the head of the beauties of human beings — did it not also come to know His skill in the scriptures, as He arranged on earth the prescribed good for the virtuous?

*Note.* (?) The verse is very obscure (*yamaka*: *dhurīṇatāṁ … dhuri*, *agamad āgama*). The translation is conjectural. The Gosvami edition (GGM) has the same text.

**15.** Having long drunk through the path of their ears the nectar of words flowing from the moon of the Lord's face — a nectar that gives life even to the dead — they, all their sins subdued, attained joy.

*Note.* A play of sounds: *mudam itā damitā*.

**16.** His garment had the lustre of the bright fortnight of the moon. Did He not take the beauty of the parrot's beak in a single sip and hold it in His sweet lips? — for it spreads over those sweet [lips].

*Note.* The edition gives *nudadhe* and *madhu-rayo* ("a stream of honey"); we read with the Gosvami edition (GGM) *nu dadhe* and *madhurayor*: the Lord's lips are red like a parrot's beak.

**17.** Beholding the moon of His face — resplendent as a freshly blossomed lotus, enchanting the world with the honeyed liquid of its smile, full of rasa, bringing [red lotuses] to paleness — they laughed for joy.

*Note.* *Samayantam aśoṇatām*, literally "bringing to un-redness": beside His face the red lotus loses its colour.

**18.** Let this moon, [touched] by the ray of His face turned towards it, again and again lose its loveliness; and let spring, charming with flowers filled with the juice of honey, become insipid.

*Note.* Again a *yamaka*: *vidhur asauṣṭhavatāṁ … vidhur asau*, *madhu-rasānvita … madhur asādhu-rasau*. (?) The form *rasau* is unclear. The Gosvami edition (GGM) has the same text.

**19.** When the Lord was teaching thus with His mighty ocean of the rasa of inspired insight, the people, beholding His sweetness, cried aloud: "Has not a new nectar, as it were, come down to the earth?"

*Note.* A play of sounds: *nava-sudhā vasudhām*.

**20.** Thus Mahaprabhu, the treasure-house of mercy, spent a number of days teaching; and day by day, by the mighty splendour of His own body, He also quelled the heaps of the torments of worldly existence.

*Note.* The verb *samanayat* works twice: "spent [the days]" and, echoing *śamanayat*, "quelled [the torments]".

**21.** After that, wishing to go to Gaya together with the husband of His mother's sister, He brought joy to the hearts of good people by the graces of His own charming movements.

*Note.* The edition reads *nija-yato raya-* (obscure); we read *nija-manorama-* with the Gosvami edition (GGM). The husband of Shachi's sister is Chandrashekhara Acharya (Acharyaratna). The pilgrimage to Gaya is a turning point: there Nimai meets Ishvara Puri and receives initiation (Chaitanya-bhagavata, Adi 17; Chaitanya-charitamrita, Adi 17).

**22.** First, exultant at the undertaking of setting out, He completed the rites; and, honoured again and again by the crowd of brahmanas resounding with cries of "Jaya!", He whose eyes were like fresh lotuses grew ever fuller of joy.

*Note.* Nimai went to Gaya to perform the shraddha rites for His father. A play of sounds: *jaya-svanavatā nava-tāmarasa*.

**23.** With the throngs of brahmanas as the moon with the hosts of stars, pouring forth the nectar of His radiance upon the lands like the moon, and delighting on the way in good conversation with them, the Lord considered that time filled with sweetness.

**24.** Somewhere near a river He saw a most charming spot, and when He in His grace sat down there, the bees were far from unwilling to drink the honey of His lotus feet, [taking them for] land lotuses.

*Note.* *Na anutsukāḥ*, "not unwilling", that is, greedily eager: a double negative. *Sthala-padma*, the "land lotus", is the hibiscus.

**25.** [The row of lotuses in the pond] — with quiet dark bees for eyes, gleaming with lovely filaments for teeth, with the circles of opened lotuses for faces, full of sweet and excellent juice, rich in honey…

*Note.* Verses 25–26 are a *yugmaka*, one sentence. The subject is *kamalinī-tati* (v. 26), "the row of lotuses" in the pond, described as a beautiful woman.

**26.** …enlivened at every step by the gait of the female swans, slow with love, and lovely in form — this row of lotuses gave Him joy. For what connoisseur of beauty would not delight at a lake?

*Note.* Here begins the descriptive insertion traditional in a mahakavya: the lake, the sunset and the night (descriptions of forest and twilight). The story stands still; this is a poetic exercise. Verses 25–40 are saturated with *yamaka* (repetition of syllables) and are deliberately ambiguous.

**27.** Is it not just this that the bees, maddened by the intoxication of drinking honey, say again and again: "Let the connoisseur on the face of the earth become a young elephant in the groves of opened lotuses"?

*Note.* The humming of the bees is presented as their speech. A young elephant in a lotus pond is an image of carefree enjoyment. A play of sounds: *kalabhatāṁ labhatām*.

**28.** (?) "What connoisseur would remain indifferent at a lake? What connoisseur would not press her breasts [— the lotus buds —] to his chest? How then could those two, tender as lotus buds, in any way not be esteemed?"

*Note.* (?) The verse is obscure and erotically coloured (lotus buds are the stock comparison for a woman's breasts); it continues the speech of the bees, and "she" is the row of lotuses of vv. 25–26. The translation is tentative; *nayau* is unclear (perhaps to be divided *na yau*, "which… not"). The text is the same in the Gosvami edition (GGM).

**29.** (?) Here the blue lotuses, swaying in a ring with the winds and the waters, again and again [strove] to rival and to merge with the equal beauty of the eyes of fair-eyed women — how many, and what sweet (?)…

*Note.* (?) The verse is almost pure *yamaka* (*dṛśāṁ sudṛśām*, *tulayituṁ layitum*, *kuvalayaṁ valayam*, *pavanair vanair*, *kati vā kati*) and cannot be analysed with certainty. The translation is tentative. The text is the same in the Gosvami edition (GGM).

**30.** The white-winged [swan], not seeing amid the fine ripples her beloved, who had been carried off by a great wave, went in agitation over the fine ripples [towards] him, making all the water billow greatly (?).

*Note.* The whole verse is built on the repetition *tanu-taraṅga / atanu-taraṅga* ("fine ripples / great wave"). *Atanu*, "the Bodiless One", is also a name of Kama, so *atanu-taraṅga* can be heard as "a wave of love". The translation follows the sense.

**31.** Crying sweetly, fluttering her wings, cherishing an unfeigned affection for her beloved, stirring the water in circles, sweet with intoxication, she also produced joy for the all-pervading Lord.

*Note.* (?) A play of sounds: *akṛtakā kṛtakā*, *mada-kalodaka-lolita*. *Cakrikā* is unclear (whirlpools? or the *cakravāka* bird?). The text is the same in the Gosvami edition (GGM).

**32.** Looking around at the lush bank of the pond close by, rippled by the rush of small waves, He attained the highest joy: for one who loves truly delights in what is very beautiful.

*Note.* A play of sounds: *sarasaḥ sarasam* ("of the pond" — "lush"), *su-rucire ruciḥ*.

**33.** The breeze, laden with the coolness of pure spray, swaying the blue lotuses in the shape of rings, gently and very slowly (?), brought about a great removal of [His] fatigue.

*Note.* (?) *Balaya-sādhvasa* is unclear; the general sense of the verse is clear: a cool breeze with spray blew slowly and removed the great fatigue of the road. The text is the same in the Gosvami edition (GGM).

**34.** Then, seeing Him walking lightly on the way (?), displaying a great upsurge of happiness, will not the lord of the day, deeply reddened by the juices of love, fall, overwhelmed?

*Note.* The description of the sunset begins: the sun reddens and "falls" below the horizon. *Anurāga* means both "love" and "reddening". (?) *Alantam* is unclear. The text is the same in the Gosvami edition (GGM).

**35.** Then, seeing the sun gone to the Western Mountain, about to fall, like a ripe fruit in form, the lotus pond, together with the bees that flew out [of it], at once lost its sap.

*Note.* Day lotuses close at sunset, and the bees leave them. A play of sounds: *gata-rasā tarasā*.

**36.** As the swarm of bees, sweetly humming in their intoxication, flew out on all sides and alighted nowhere, that night, with its night-lilies, then became joyful, as if possessed of an inborn enmity [towards the day lotuses].

*Note.* Night and day are "enemies": the night-lilies (*kumuda*) open when the day lotuses close. A play of sounds: *kumudinī mudinī*.

**37.** The pond of night-lilies, though already intent on a new blossoming, was thoroughly awakened by the bees — as happens to a beloved [awakened] by her ardent lover when her long sulking has come to an end.

*Note.* *Māna*, the "sulking" or jealous estrangement of the beloved, is an important notion in the aesthetics of love.

**38.** Though ceaselessly intoxicated by the sweetness of the honey of the day lotuses, the bees, the lickers of flowers, again resorted to the night-lilies with unceasing desire: indeed, the best of good people [ever seek] a new taste.

*Note.* *Nava-rasa*, "new taste", can also be heard as "the nine rasas" of poetics. A play of sounds: *nava-rasā vara-sādhu*.

**39.** Then at night the merciful one, wishing to sleep, His mind at peace, rested in some settlement from His wandering, together with all His people, in sound sleep.

*Note.* A play of sounds: *ghaṭanato 'ṭanato*. *Nīvṛt* is "a district, a settlement".

**40.** Then, on the next day, as a light wind was blowing, eager delight came to the lofty heart of the ocean full of compassion as He gazed at the loveliness of the dawn.

**41.** At once, by the mass of petals — some contracting, others opening anew — [all] of equal charm; then by the swarm of bees rising and entering; and by the pollen, too, flying away and settling…

*Note.* Verses 41–42 are a *yugmaka*. The verse is built on pairs of opposites (contracting / opening, rising / entering, flying away / settling): at dawn the night-lilies close and the day lotuses open. The edition reads *samadi*; we read *sapadi* ("at once") with the Gosvami edition (GGM).

**42.** …at daybreak the night-lily and the day lotus on all sides gave the most manifold joy to Him, Mahaprabhu, as He went on His way — as if they were Variety herself, appointed over the Creator's handiwork.

**43.** (?) The flocks of birds that delight in the sky — their unfeigned, artless charm, desired by the heart — brought joy to the Lord in His heart by their sight, by remembrance and by their singing.

*Note.* (?) The verse almost defies analysis: it is unbroken *yamaka* (*hṛdaye hṛdaye*, *akṛtako 'kṛtako*, *smaraṇato raṇato*, *divi-ratā viratā*). The translation follows only the general sense. The text is the same in the Gosvami edition (GGM).

**44.** The mountain lands, entering the path of the all-pervading Lord's eyes, as if awakened after a long [sleep], at once raised in celebration a continuous cry of "Jaya!" with the calls of various birds.

*Note.* The road to Gaya passes through hilly country (in present-day Bihar). A play of sounds: *sapadi sampadi*.

**45.** [The mountain land] — bright green, [and] with masses of the lustre of orpiment; somewhere having the lovely radiance of gold; somewhere a plain (?) dark in its own colour, like a raincloud; and somewhere white with masses of clear white stones…

*Note.* Verses 45–46 are a *yugmaka*. The edition reads *rucāñcayaiḥ*; we read *rucāṁ cayaiḥ* ("with masses of lustre") with the Gosvami edition (GGM). *Haritāla* is orpiment, a yellow mineral; *harita* itself can mean both "green" and "yellowish".

**46.** (?) …when the good Lord came within her sight, that exceedingly beautiful land at the foot of the mountains, smiling here as if with masses of blossoming flowers, [her] caves (?)…, shone.

*Note.* (?) The second pada (*darī vadarī vidhurāyitā*) cannot be analysed with confidence, and the translation follows the general sense. The text is the same in the Gosvami edition (GGM).

**47.** On the way the Lord saw a quiet spot made a shelter by the gayals of the mountains (?), adorned by roaming *sṛmara* deer and thickly filled with numerous *raṅku* deer.

*Note.* The *gavaya* is the gayal, a forest ox; the *sṛmara* and the *raṅku* are kinds of deer.

**48.** (?) That herd of deer, lying [at rest], yet at every step making its feet dance most swiftly — would it not, by the charms of the glances of its slanting eyes, captivate Him, full of widespread lovely rasa?

*Note.* (?) The verse is obscure; the translation follows the sense. The text is the same in the Gosvami edition (GGM).

**49.** Thus He, the golden treasure-house of nectar, eager to see the various wonders on the way, went on, His heart immersed in happiness, protected by the virtuous of many good kinds — and He shone.

*Note.* *Vividha-sad-vidha* probably means "of various stations" (householders and renunciants).

**50.** On the way, in the river Chora, the Lord eagerly performed bathing, the offering of water to the forefathers and worship. Then His body became feverish. The conduct of the Lord is not to be fathomed.

*Note.* The episode of the fever on the way to Gaya is also in the Chaitanya-bhagavata, Adi 17: Nimai is cured by drinking water that has washed the feet of brahmanas (v. 52 ff.). The Gosvami edition (GGM) reads *cīra-nade* ("in the river Chira"); we keep *cora-nade* of our edition: in Murari (1.15.8) this is the river Chorandhayaka. A play of sounds: *na caritaṁ caritam*.

**51.** "How has this illness come about in My body on the way, causing Me hindrance?" Reflecting thus, the one merciful [friend] of the world addressed the assembly of brahmanas.

*Note.* A play of sounds: *nijagade jagad-eka*.

**52.** Then, having pondered deeply in His mind, Mahaprabhu Himself prescribed as the remedy for allaying His own fever the water [that had washed] the feet of the brahmanas, the gods on earth. For the ocean of mercy never abandons mercy.

*Note.* Cf. the Chaitanya-bhagavata, Adi 17: the Lord drinks *vipra-pādodaka*, the water from the feet of brahmanas, to show the glory of the brahmanas.

**53.** And then, by that water from their feet, Mahaprabhu's fever at once subsided. Who in the world may know His deeds, deeply hidden, concealed in the three worlds?

**54.** Then the Lord, having come with the company of brahmanas to Rajagiri, joyfully and with devotion performed there the excellent worship of the forefathers; and at the conclusion (?) also at the lake of Brahma.

*Note.* Rajagiri (Rajagriha, present-day Rajgir) and the lake of Brahma, the Brahma-kunda, are sacred places of pilgrimage near Gaya where the shraddha is performed. (?) *Uparame*, "at the conclusion", is uncertain.

**55.** Having completed there, in proper order, the rites for the forefathers at all the best holy places, He then entered Gaya together with throngs of brahmanas, eager, their souls possessed [by fervour].

*Note.* A play of sounds: *aviśad āviśad*.

**56.** Then that golden youthful moon, the Lord, at once seeing him who was renowned as "Puri" preceded by "Ishvara", attained in His heart, then and from that moment on, an unfailing joy.

*Note.* **The meeting with Ishvara Puri** is a key moment in Mahaprabhu's life: in Gaya He receives initiation from him (Chaitanya-bhagavata, Adi 17; Chaitanya-charitamrita, Adi 17). Ishvara Puri is a disciple of Madhavendra Puri, who is important also in the story of Nityananda.

**57.** Bowing to him and falling to the ground, His heart flooded with abundant joy, He then spoke with humility words deep in tone like a thundercloud, delightful in their propriety:

**58.** "O master, by an abundance of great good fortune I have beheld this pair of your lotus feet. Tell [Me] how, by the mighty power of devotion to Hari, the ocean of worldly existence may be dried up."

*Note.* *Prabhavataḥ* is taken as an ablative agreeing with *guṇāt*: "by the mighty power". A play of sounds: *prabhavato bhava-toyadhi*.

**59.** Hearing these words of Mahaprabhu, that great-souled ascetic rejoiced and, a treasure-house of compassion, gave the mantra to the Lord; and therefore the Lord considered him one who had shown Him mercy.

*Note.* *Manu* here is the mantra of initiation. According to the Chaitanya-bhagavata (Adi 17), Ishvara Puri gave Nimai the ten-syllable mantra (*daśākṣara*). A play of sounds: *kṛta-dayaṁ tad ayaṁ*.

**60.** Having received this mantra of the Lord of the lovely women of Vraja, He, the one merciful [friend] of the world — adorned with the sprouts of hairs standing on end and shedding streaming tears — humbly spoke these [words]:

*Note.* "The mantra of the Lord of the women of Vraja" is the mantra of Krishna (the Gopala mantra). These signs of ecstasy are the first in the poem: from this moment Nimai the scholar becomes Gauranga the devotee. A play of sounds: *nijagade jagad-eka*.

**61.** "O lord of ascetics, through meeting your feet the purpose [of My life] has here been very greatly fulfilled." And, [showing] thus devotion to one's own guru, He received the dust of his feet and strewed it on His hand.

*Note.* Literally, "the dust from [his] feet was strewn on the hand by Him who received [it]"; the sense is that the Lord took up the dust of His guru's feet [to place it on His head]. A play of sounds: *vicakare ca kare*.

**62.** Then, having bathed according to rule in the river Phalgu, He satisfied the forefathers with libations; and then, on the Mountain of the Dead, He offered the pinda — His eyes red with compassion, redder even than the dawn.

*Note.* The Phalgu is the river at Gaya, the chief place of the shraddha. The *piṇḍa* is a ball of rice offered to the forefathers. The edition has *śva-mahībhṛt*; the Gosvami edition (GGM) reads *śava-mahībhṛt*, "the mountain of the dead", that is, Pretashila near Gaya. A play of sounds: *karuṇato 'ruṇato 'py aruṇekṣaṇaḥ*.

**63.** Then, having come down, He — the one moon of hearts — performed the rites for the host of forefathers at Dakshina-manasa, and likewise at Uttara-manasa, together with throngs of sensitive-hearted brahmanas.

*Note.* Dakshina-manasa and Uttara-manasa are sacred ponds at Gaya, places of the shraddha. A play of sounds: *sahṛdayair hṛdayaika*.

**64.** Then, having performed the rites for the host of forefathers on the Gaya-shiras, the Lord, beholding the print of Hari's feet, at once — together with the saintly people — attained a joy that filled His heart.

*Note.* *Gayā-śiras*, "the head of Gaya" (by tradition, the head of the demon Gayasura). The Vishnupada is the temple at Gaya with the footprint of Vishnu in stone. According to all the biographies (Chaitanya-bhagavata, Adi 17), it is at Gaya that Nimai is first seized by the ecstasy of love; in Karnapura — at the sight of the print of Hari's feet. A play of sounds: *sahasā saha*.

**65.** "How is it that I, gazing on the print of Hari's feet, have not become softness itself?" As He thought thus, a torrent poured from His eyes, and an abundant bristling of His hairs arose.

*Note.* The paradox: His self-reproach for "hardness of heart" is itself the first outburst of love. *Saḥ*, "he", is taken as the subject: the Lord speaks of Himself in the third person, and *mṛdutā eva*, "softness itself", is the predicate. A play of sounds: *vipulakaḥ pulakaḥ*.

**66.** Thus, by such an impulse of His own, the Lord at once left all people and became jubilant [and eager] to go then to Madhuvana, His body shaking with trembling.

*Note.* Madhuvana is one of the twelve forests of Vraja (Mathura). Nimai's impulse to go to Vrindavana after His initiation is also found in the Chaitanya-bhagavata and the Chaitanya-charitamrita (Adi 17). A play of sounds: *madhu-vane dhuvanena*.

**67.** Then at once there arose from the sky a bodiless voice, having the excellent form of a new cloud: "Mahaprabhu will come there again. [Now] be [at] Your own home, to gladden it once more."

*Note.* (?) The syntax of the voice is uneven: the third person "will come" stands beside the imperative *bhava*, "be". The "form of a new cloud" is probably the deep rumble of a rain-cloud. Mahaprabhu will indeed go to Vrindavana later, already a sannyasi (Chaitanya-charitamrita, Madhya 17). The text is the same in the Gosvami edition (GGM).

**68.** Hearing that supreme voice from the sky, Mahaprabhu, delighted, then set out to go to His own home, surrounded by brahmanas of noble mind.

**69.** Then, after a delay of only a few days, learning that her son had come near, she [Shachi] filled her house with festivities — for the desires of the very great are unobstructed.

*Note.* The edition has *sumahatāṁ mahatā* ("in the very great — by a great [desire]"); the Gosvami edition (GGM) reads *sumahatām ahatā*, "the desires of the very great meet no obstacle", and so we read.

**70.** From the mighty, simultaneous beating of soft mridangas, yashas (?), patahas, ringing panavas, kahalas, bronze cymbals and fine mardalas there arose a sound — unprecedented, as if towering up [to the sky].

*Note.* The list of instruments: *mṛdaṅga*, *paṭaha*, *paṇava* and *mardala* are drums; *kāhala* is a large horn; *kāṁsya*, bronze cymbals. (?) *Yaśaḥ* in the list is obscure: perhaps the name of an instrument, or else an epithet of the patahas ("of renown"). The text is the same in the Gosvami edition (GGM). A play of sounds: *abhūn nirabhūta*.

**71.** Then, after a long time, the very handsome son reached home and joyfully beheld His mother, skilled in the ways of the world, her heart flooded with great happiness.

*Note.* Plays of sounds: *su-rucireṇa cireṇa*, *sva-jananī jana-nītiṣu*.

**72.** Then the Lord again and again took with His palm the dust from His mother's feet and placed it on His head; then, eager, He fell on the ground like a rod, again and again showing propriety and humility.

**73.** All at once the music rang out — of fine metal instruments and fine stringed instruments, together with the wind instruments; music arose, music arose — the source of impetuous ardour, the source of impetuous ardour!

*Note.* The verse is sound-painting: each pada is repeated twice, like the echo of an orchestra. *Ghana* (solid, i.e. metal), *tata* (stretched, i.e. stringed) and *śuṣira* (hollow, i.e. wind) are classes of instruments in Indian musical theory; the fourth class is *avanaddha*, drums covered with skin (v. 70). *Sughanam sutatam* may also be heard adverbially: "densely, unbrokenly". The end of the verse is read according to the Gosvami edition (GGM): *abhūd rabhasodyama-bhū rabhasodyama-bhūḥ* (our edition has *abhūr abhasodyama-*). In the edition the verse is wrongly numbered 74.

**74.** Then, seeing the body of her son — like some new golden creeper, tender, adorned with soft white cloth, joyful and radiant — Shachi gave away wealth for His sake.

*Note.* Again *yamaka*: *kāñcana-kāñcana*, *mṛdulāṁ mṛdula-*, *muditām uditām*. The first *kāñcana* is probably *kāṁcana*, "some, a certain", agreeing with *latām*.

**75.** Gladdened by her son's arrival, she gave to the throng of brahmanas, together with the dancers, musicians and others, and also to the throng of mendicants, the wealth she had secretly stored up from her means.

**76.** Thus the Lord of abundant compassion, who soothes the torments of all embodied beings, returned home from Gaya at the end of the month of Pausha. Then, from the beginning of the month of Magha, day after day, without limit, He scattered over the earth, with the rasas of His own kirtana, the manifestation [of His divinity] and inspired ecstasy.

*Note.* The metre changes to *śikhariṇī* — the close of the sarga. Pausha is December–January, Magha January–February. **The beginning of kirtana** in Navadvipa is the opening of the Madhya-lila (Chaitanya-bhagavata, Madhya 1).

**77.** Thus the ocean of compassion, having in a moment cast off all [other] activities, sings at every moment, again and again, with all His heart; at every step He weeps with unrestrained voice, the hairs of His body bristling.

*Note.* The metre of the last verse is *vaṁśastha*.

*Iti śrī-kṛṣṇa-caitanya-caritāmṛte mahākāvye caturthaḥ sargaḥ samāptaḥ* — Thus ends the fourth sarga of the mahakavya "Sri Krishna-Chaitanya-charitamrita".

---

## Sarga Five — verses 1–129

**Metre:** *praharṣiṇī* (13 syllables per pada); verse 126 — *śikhariṇī*; verse 127 — *śārdūla-vikrīḍita*; verses 128–129 — *drutavilambita*.
**Parallels:** Chaitanya-bhagavata, Madhya 1–2 ("The beginning of sankirtana").

**Contents of the passage:**
- 1–9 — after Gaya: Nimai weeps with love; Shachi asks her son for *prema*; the blessing of the brahmanas.
- 10–14 — He loses the sense of time; at the sound of the Name He falls to the ground; four months of kirtana and teaching.
- 15–21 — **the manifestation of Varaha in the house of Murari Gupta**.
- 22–31 — at Srivasa's: "Do you see Shiva, Skanda and Brahma?"; Advaita comes, and the Lord reveals Himself to Him just as He had resolved; straw between the teeth.
- 32–37 — summer comes; Gaura, radiant, goes to Srivasa.
- 38–50 — the **mahabhisheka** in the house of Srivasa: the bathing with the waters of the Ganga; the Lord sits on the throne of the Deities; light breaks through the chinks.
- 51–52 — all recognise in Him the Lord of the three worlds; a flute sounds.
- 53–60 — the Lord passes into the living quarters; the brothers adorn the house, Gadadhara prepares the bed; the town shines brighter than Ilavrita; a festival for all.
- 61–68 — Gadadhara weaves flower ornaments, dresses and anoints the Lord.
- 69–80 — the wives of the brahmanas are admitted to the Lord: "Always think of Me"; gifts; the Lord gives away cloth, chews betel, accepts offerings.
- 81–90 — Advaita's dance; **Shachi** before the Lord: "He is not your son!"; the touch of His foot, her ecstasy.
- 91–99 — a night of pastimes; the round of the houses of the four brothers; "I am leaving" — the devotees' threat; the Lord falls senseless.
- 100–110 — dawn, a long swoon; kirtana brings Him back; the effulgence withdrawn: "the summer sun has become the autumn moon".
- 111–118 — "I remember nothing"; Srivasa's jest about maya; eleven watches without sleep or food.
- 119–124 — the bath, worship in the house of Srivasa, the return to His mother.
- 125–127 — eight months (from Jyaishtha to Pausha) of dancing in the house of Srivasa.
- 128–129 — Gadadhara is constantly at the Lord's side.

**1.** Having then come to His home, He shone without limit with His kirtana and the like, shaken by sobs. Seeing Him constantly so, His mother was filled with wonder: "What is this? What is it?"

**2.** Once at night Shachi, rich in great fortune, seeing for the first time the weeping of her son as He lay [in bed], said to Him anxiously: "Ah, O dear child, tell me — why do You weep so?"

**3.** When, hearing that, the Lord answered nothing — softened by love, His whole body bathed in the water of His eyes — she became extremely, boundlessly anxious; and then, by the dispensation of providence, she understood this too: "It is *prema*, love."

**4.** Understanding this, pure-hearted Shachi, her eyes moist, humbly and earnestly begged her son: "You give me all the wealth [You have] — why then do You not now give [me] that [wealth] called *prema*?"

**5.** "This love which You obtained at Gaya — unknown to the gods, exceedingly hard to attain — give it here to me, a wretched woman, O dear child, if Your affection for me remains even for a moment."

**6.** Hearing her words thus, Gaurachandra, softened by affection, gave His mother a reply: "O Mother, it will surely be yours in time, when the very great mercy of the Vaishnavas comes to you."

*Note.* An important theological motif: even the mother of God receives prema not from Him directly but by the mercy of His devotees. Cf. Chaitanya-bhagavata, Madhya 22 ("The forgiveness of Shachidevi"): Shachi receives forgiveness for her offence against Advaita.

**7.** Hearing that, she attained great joy, her heart delighted: "Then it will surely be mine!" And Gauranga, perceiving His mother's heart, humbly spoke further to the best of the brahmanas:

**8.** "My mother seeks this love boundlessly, and this most weighty devotion to the feet of the Lord. Let blessings be bestowed by you, so that both may soon be hers." And thereupon they spoke accordingly.

**9.** Thus the Lord, sometimes weeping, would pour from His eyes hundreds upon hundreds of streams on His breast; sometimes, rolling on the ground, He would again and again expel copious mucus from His nose.

*Note.* Kavi Karnapura is not afraid of the "unlovely" bodily details of ecstasy — tears, mucus from the nose, rolling on the ground. This is deliberate realism: the Chaitanya-bhagavata and the Chaitanya-charitamrita write in the same way. *Uraḥsu* is plural, literally "on the breasts" — a poetic plural, or "on the breasts [of those beside Him]".

**10.** Having passed the whole day from dawn onward weeping, with loud cries and abundant tears of love, the Lord, when night had come and He awoke [to outer consciousness], in His agitation supposed that it was day.

*Note.* *Yāminyā bhavati sati* is irregular; the sense of a locative absolute is required: "when night had come".

**11.** At twilight He wept somehow with unrestrained voice; and if in the morning outer consciousness somehow returned to Him, Gaura in His agitation would ask: "So the night is passing? How much [is left]? When [will it end]?" For Him there was no distinction of time.

*Note.* In the edition the first pada is a syllable short of the metre; the Gosvami edition (GGM) reads *kim api rudan vimukta-kaṇṭhaḥ* ("somehow weeping with unrestrained voice"), and the missing *rudan* is restored.

**12.** As soon as a single Name entered the path of His hearing, He would roll on the ground with all His strength, and shone with long, trembling sighs and with a stream flowing from the corners of His eyes.

**13.** Murmuring with longing, without end, "Krishna, Krishna, Krishna!", sometimes with a broken and choked voice, He shone with the hairs of His body standing on end in masses from joy. Such He was almost every day.

**14.** Bathing at the beginning of the day, He performed worship; every day He ate with joy what had been offered [to God]; and teaching also the noble and worthy brahmanas, He thus spent four months, beginning with Magha.

*Note.* The four months are Magha, Phalguna, Chaitra and Vaishakha (January–May).

**15.** Once the ocean of mercy, softened by love, His hairs standing on end, went to the house of Murari the physician. There He at once entered the shrine of the house and settled there, convulsed with tears.

*Note.* Murari Gupta is a physician (*vaidya*), Nimai's boyhood friend and the author of the earliest biography (the Kadacha). The episode of Varaha's manifestation in Murari's house is found in the Chaitanya-bhagavata (Madhya 3) and in Murari Gupta himself.

**16.** "What a wonder! Who is this mighty One who bears this boar's body, [holding up] the earth on a pair of tusks? Like a great mountain, He pierces [Me] ever more, reaching to the very vitals!" Saying this, He crept backwards.

*Note.* **The manifestation of Varaha:** Mahaprabhu takes on the mood of Varaha, the boar avatara who raised the Earth from the waters on His tusks. The edition has *bhūye*; we read *bhūyo* ("ever more, again") with the Gosvami edition (GGM).

**17.** Having said this, He at once took on His mood: moving over the ground on His arms and knees, His very restless eyes rolling, He also let out that long roar, "Hum!"

**18.** At once He seized a brass water-vessel with the tips of His teeth and, inspiring great fear, raised His head and flung it far away. After that He said insistently to Murari Gupta: "Describe My natural form!"

*Note.* The vessel lifted with the teeth is the image of Varaha raising the Earth on His tusks.

**19.** Hearing that, Murari fell to the ground, terror-stricken: "We do not know this own form of Yours. You Yourself know Yourself thus, by Yourself alone." So he answered, with words spoken in the Gita.

*Note.* Murari quotes the Bhagavad-gita (10.15): *svayam evātmanātmānaṁ vettha tvaṁ puruṣottama* — "You Yourself know Yourself by Yourself, O Supreme Person."

**20.** Then the Lord, smiling, replied to him in words moist with honey: "What, indeed, does this Veda know? Out of bewildered confusion it eternally gropes [for Me] like a blind man."

**21.** Having spoken thus and recited once more what the Shruti declares, the Lord, laughing, said with mockery: "Truly, the Vedas have no such power to know Me." And saying this, He went to His own home.

*Note.* Which text of the Shruti is meant is not specified. A probable parallel is Taittiriya Upanishad 2.4: *yato vāco nivartante aprāpya manasā saha* ("from which words turn back, together with the mind, without reaching it").

**22.** The next day, at His own house, He — endowed with the beauty of millions of moons, His form possessing the very highest excellence, all radiant — spoke to Srivasa, who stood before Him, in words accompanied by mighty roars:

*Note.* *Kṣapeśa*, "lord of the night", is the moon.

**23.** "Hey, do you not see the five-faced, the six-faced and the four-faced assembled here?" And he replied: "No, I do not see this company assembled here — the six-faced and the rest."

*Note.* The five-faced is Shiva, the six-faced Kartikeya (Skanda), the four-faced Brahma: the gods have come to see the Lord. Compare 2.86, where the invisible guests in the house are seen by Shachi alone.

**24.** When this had been said, the brahmana saw his younger brother, Shripati by name, standing behind him. Shripati came up and softly, close to his ear, told him the news that Advaita had arrived, wishing to see the Lord.

*Note.* Shripati is one of Srivasa's brothers (Srivasa, Sri Rama, Shripati, Shrinidhi). The arrival of Advaita: Chaitanya-bhagavata, Madhya 6 ("The meeting with Sri Advaita").

**25.** "Has the Acharya come here?" Learning that it was so, He at once rose, came out into the courtyard and — a mass of glorious, great effulgence — made reply in words broken by a roar at every one:

*Note.* (?) *Tasyai* (dative feminine) is unclear; perhaps it stands for *tasmai*, "to him". The Gosvami edition (GGM) has the same text. Grammatically *samutthitaḥ*, *āgatya* and *pratyūce* share one subject, *saḥ … mahita-mahā-mahaḥ-samūhaḥ*, that is, the Lord; the reply follows in verse 26.

**26.** (?) "Ah, soon they will know there — all those who, now devoid of qualification, will go to the earth…" Having said this, the Lord, shaken by heavy roars, went swiftly to the house of Srivasa.

*Note.* (?) The sense of the first half is obscure. Perhaps it refers to those who are not yet worthy to see His divinity. The Gosvami edition (GGM) has the same text.

**27.** Swiftly entering there, He shot the bolt firmly across both leaves of the door; and, transforming (?), brilliant with His innate radiance abundantly revealed, Gaurachandra shone forth in the secluded chamber.

*Note.* (?) *Vikurvan*, literally "transforming, undergoing change", may refer to the ecstatic transformations of His body or to the manifestation of His own form; the meaning is uncertain.

**28.** Advaita, who had come from his own home, made a firm vow in His mind: "He must be seen [by me] with His innate [form] fully manifest." And at that very time He appeared outside, at the leaves of the door.

*Note.* The motif of a test: Advaita wants the Lord Himself to reveal to Him His divine form. In the Chaitanya-bhagavata (Madhya 6) Advaita sees the Lord in the form for which He had prayed.

**29.** Then Shripati, the younger brother of Srivasa, the moon of the brahmana race, timidly announced his arrival at once; and, just on learning this, the Lord Himself unbolted the door.

**30.** Beholding the Lord in His splendour exactly as He had resolved in His mind, the deeply loving Advaita took a bunch of straw between His teeth and fell on the ground like a rod.

*Note.* Straw between the teeth is an ancient Indian sign of complete submission: "I am like a cow, your beast". Rupa Gosvami does the same on meeting Mahaprabhu (Chaitanya-charitamrita, Madhya 1).

**31.** Seeing Him, the Lord too swiftly raised the glorious [Advaita] up with both His arms, as if [He were] His own self; then, in His joy, He embraced Him with special closeness, never loosening His hold, and bathed Him again and again with torrents of flowing tears of love.

*Note.* The edition reads *svayam iva munninnāya*, the Gosvami edition (GGM) *svayam iva munnināya*: in both cases the *m* is a linking sound before *unni-* ("raised up"), so the sense is clear.

**32.** By such various, exultant pastimes spring, the leader of the seasons, was wearied. And now, at its end, does not summer, delighted at seeing the Lord's dancing, laugh with the blossoming of jasmine?

*Note.* The change of seasons marks the passage of time: spring gives way to summer (*grīṣma*, the hot season, May–June). The blossoming of jasmine as "laughter" is a classical image.

**33.** These days, utterly bereaved, as it were, by their fresh separation from spring — do they not take, without end, the path of burning their bodies in the flames of the blazing fire that is the sun?

*Note.* *Surabhi*, "the fragrant one", is spring. *Jātavedas* is Agni, fire. The image is of a bereaved wife who enters the fire after her husband.

**34.** Although the unrestrained rays of the sun, blazing again and again, dry up the water that is the lotus's support, even so [the sun] gives the lotus very great happiness: for pain too, when caused by the beloved, becomes dear.

*Note.* The sun is the "beloved" of the day-lotus. A thought important for Gaudiya aesthetics: pain from the beloved is sweet (compare Shikshashtaka 8).

**35.** Constantly putting to shame the hot-rayed summer sun with His own ever-new radiance, Gaura, on the next day, while teaching the sons of brahmanas, had His own radiance burst forth and become manifest.

**36.** Thus, shining with such innate radiance, overwhelmed, the slender stem of His whole body a beautiful rosy hue, His two feet like fresh, opening red lotuses, He left all company and went to the house of Srivasa.

**37.** The people on the road watched with wonder as He went swiftly with the steps of His red lotus feet — possessing the radiance of a host of rising suns, His eyes like fresh, fully opened red lotuses.

**38.** At once entering his house, the Supreme Lord took His place upon the veranda of the shrine and, with His radiance manifest, called Srivasa, who was within the house, meditating, wholly fixed on one object.

**39.** Hearing that, the foremost of brahmanas at once openly set aside his meditation and the rest and came out of the house. Opening his eyes, he saw standing before him the Supreme Lord, of mighty effulgence, His body tall and majestic.

**40.** Agitated by the surges of His own manifest radiance, His glorious body [aglow] with fresh, reddening rays, Gaurachandra declared: "Quickly perform My ceremonial bathing with the waters of the river of the gods!"

*Note.* The **mahabhisheka** is the royal bathing of the Lord in the house of Srivasa, the beginning of the *maha-prakasha* (Chaitanya-bhagavata, Madhya 9–10, "The completion of the maha-prakasha-lila").

**41.** Hearing that, his noble brothers — Sri Rama and the others — eager and overpowered by the joy of His acts, at once brought the articles; and the articles seemed to come into the state of being brought of their own accord.

**42.** Then in an instant some brought a hundred new pitchers from all sides and likewise filled them with water: all the women who were nearby quickly went to fetch water from the heavenly stream, [the Ganga].

**43.** Wishing to receive the bath, Sri Gaura sat shining in the midst of the courtyard on a seat made of gambhari wood, and at once took on the beauty of a peak of Meru risen above the Ocean of Milk.

*Note.* (?) *Ābhikṣā* is not a known word; perhaps it stands for *abhikhyā*, "splendour, beauty".

**44.** With the waters of the daughter of Jahnu, brought very quickly and well scented with fragrances — with camphor, aguru and heavy sandalwood — Srivasa, his heart joyful, bathed Him to his heart's content.

*Note.* The "daughter of Jahnu" (Jahnavi) is the Ganga.

**45.** The very clear streams fall on His body; and, by His rays — far-reaching, boundless, continually flashing, golden like a streak of gold on a touchstone — [the stream] of the bathing waters is, as it were, made golden-limbed.

*Note.* *Gaurāṅgī kriyate*, "is made *gaurāṅga*, golden-limbed": the waters, as it were, take on the Lord's own name.

**46.** This bathing with a hundred pitchers of the pure waters of the Ganga, clothing His body in an instant like a cloth, was accomplished; and the earth, tasting that water, with sighs [of joy], attained in full measure the fulfilment of its purpose.

**47.** At the end of the bath Srivasa wiped the water from His limbs with an excellent cloth, using both hands, and afterwards, with overflowing joy, served Him with a pair of very fine, white, pure garments.

*Note.* The pair of garments is the lower (*dhoti*) and the upper (*uttariya*).

**48.** Taking the pair of fine garments, Sri Gaura took on the beauty of Meru wrapped in a veil of mist; and, overwhelmed by His mighty, supreme effulgence, He swiftly entered the shrine.

**49.** At once entering within it, Gaurachandra, filled with great effulgence, pushed aside on all sides the gathering of the Deity forms of the gods and Himself sat down at ease upon the couch of charming lustre.

*Note.* The Lord Himself sits on the throne of the Deities — a sign of the *maha-prakasha*, the manifestation of His divinity (Chaitanya-bhagavata, Madhya 9: "The Lord sat on the throne of Vishnu").

**50.** Finding no room inside the house, His rays broke their way out through the chinks; and at that time the heaped-up joy of the crowd of people came out from within their hearts in an abundance of bristling hair.

*Note.* A parallel: light from within breaks out through the chinks — joy from within breaks out through the bristling hair on the skin.

**51.** At that moment all, immersed in joy, recognised Gauranga as the Lord of the three worlds; and then all of them heard for a long time the enchanting sound of the beautiful flute issuing from the lotus of His mouth.

*Note.* The sound of the flute is the sign of Krishna: those present recognise Him in Gaura.

**52.** In that heat all the people attained incomparable happiness; their bodies shone with masses of bristling hair, and that thrill was joined with the waters of their eyes — while Sri Gaura, manifest in such a form, was triumphant then.

*Note.* The second line is printed unclearly in the edition; the reading *aśobhad aṅgam* ("the body shone") is chosen by the metre (⏑ — ⏑ — —) and the sense. *Tat-tāpe*, "in that heat" (the summer of verses 32–37, or the heat of His radiance); a misprint for *tat-kāle*, as in verse 50, is not excluded (?). The Gosvami edition (GGM) reads *athoñcad-aṅgam* in the second line, which is also unclear.

**53.** Then Gauranga, bursting forth with supreme, great effulgence, beautiful with unceasing radiance, said to Srivasa, the one jewel among brahmanas: "From this excellent chamber I shall go into the inner part of your house."

*Note.* *Bhūsura*, "a god on earth", is a brahmana. "This chamber" is the shrine (*deva-veśma*, verse 48); "your house" is Srivasa's living quarters.

**54.** Hearing that, all [Srivasa's] brothers, overpowered by joy, in an instant lavishly adorned his house, and they screened the middle door with many hangings so that it was well concealed.

*Note.* Srivasa's brothers are Sri Rama, Shripati and Shrinidhi. *Samaskṛta* (not *saṁskṛta*) is used for the metre, in the sense "adorned".

**55.** After that Srivasa said to Gadadhara: "Let the couch and everything else be carried there." Thus addressed, Gadadhara, boundlessly melted with love and forgetful of what he himself was doing, carried everything there.

**56.** Quickly spreading a fine canopy above, he hung very beautiful chamaras along its edge; laying the best of mattresses upon the couch, he then covered it with splendid, excellent cloths.

*Note.* *Candrātapa* (literally "moonlight") is an awning, a canopy over a throne or couch. A *chamara* is a whisk of yak tail, a royal insignia.

**57.** Then Gauranga, going to that house, shone, easily eclipsing with His rays the might of the sun. He shines like hundreds upon hundreds of crores upon crores of lightning flashes that have burst open and, as it were, come to rest upon the earth.

*Note.* A crore (*koṭi*) is ten million.

**58.** Setting down His pair of lotus feet on the ground with playful grace as He walked, He, filled with great effulgence, took His place on the supremely captivating couch — like a fine peak of Meru upon the summit of another mountain.

**59.** Then the whole city, suffused with His pure, golden, supreme, flashing rays, shone as if crushingly and unceasingly reducing to nothing the beauty of Ilavrita, created by the Lord of creatures.

*Note.* Ilavrita is the central region (*varsha*) of Jambudvipa, around the golden mountain Meru; the Lord of creatures (*Prajesha*, Prajapati) is Brahma. The edition (and the Gosvami edition) reads *pramatham*, "crushingly"; an earlier conjecture *prathamam* ("for the first time") is unnecessary. The edition also has *पुः*, corrected to *पूः* (nominative of *pur*, "city"). The Gosvami edition (GGM) has the same text.

**60.** By whom was sandalwood not ground then? How many did not prepare betel? By whom were hundreds of flowers not brought then? With what great festivities was the earth not filled at that time!

*Note.* *Gandha-sāra* is sandalwood.

**61.** By whom, out of joy, was a *rasa* not at once set flowing all around — own brother, as it were, to the experience of unbroken bliss — through the work of preparing milk and the like, with camphor, pepper and sugar?

*Note.* The syllable *-nu-* in the second line is poorly printed in the edition; *anubhava* is restored by metre and sense. A play on words from the theory of *rasa*: *vibhāvana* ("preparation", but also *vibhāva*, the cause of an emotion), *anubhava* (echoing *anubhāva*, its outward expression), and *rasa* itself — both "drink, juice" and "aesthetic relish". Camphor and pepper are the usual spices for sweetened milk in Bengal.

**62.** Then Gadadhara himself, at once and eagerly, with most devoted skill, wove a multitude of garlands from fragrant, exceedingly lovely flowers — as if he were weaving them from the desires of his own heart.

*Note.* The epithets are double: *sāmoda* is "fragrant" and "joyful", *atirucira* "very lovely" and "much desired"; they fit both the flowers and Gadadhara's desires.

**63.** [He made] a crown suited to the curling locks, and likewise a pair of ear ornaments suited to the pair of beautiful ears; then a necklace, charming with skilfully fashioned knots of flowers, and a lovely ornament for the forehead.

*Note.* All the ornaments in verses 63–65 are made of flowers. *Vataṁsa* is the same as *avataṁsa*, an ear ornament.

**64.** He also made a string [for the chest], exceedingly charming through great skill in weaving, a pair of armlets, a pair of bangles and a pair of bracelets, and for all His fingers a set of lovely rings filled with true beauty.

*Note.* *Keyūra* is an armlet worn on the upper arm; *valaya* and *kaṅkaṇa* are two kinds of bracelet worn on the wrist.

**65.** In due order, in a moment, he made a lovely girdle, anklets for the lotus feet and, after that, ornaments for their toes; and thereupon, full of longing, he spent a long time putting them on Gaura's body.

**66.** And he [Gadadhara], a heap of endless good fortune, eagerly and thickly anointed this body of His — from the toes to the beautiful expanse of the forehead — with fine pastes of sandalwood, aguru, camphor and kunkuma.

*Note.* *Kunkuma* is a saffron (red-yellow) paste.

**67.** His body was thickly anointed with fine, delightful pastes made of sandalwood and aguru; yet its rays powerfully overspread this [anointing] — radiances beautifully golden like a heap of gold…

*Note.* Verses 67–68 form one sentence: the genitive *vapuṣaḥ* and the instrumentals *tejobhiḥ… uddyotaiḥ* continue in verse 68 (*tejobhir nija-vapuṣo… saṁbhinnaḥ*).

**68.** …with all these flower ornaments, with these pastes of sandalwood and kunkuma, and with the naturally golden rays of His own body — commingled with all these, whom did Gaurachandra come to resemble?

*Note.* *Malayaja* ("born on Malaya") is sandalwood from the Malaya hills. The rhetorical question "whom did He resemble?" means: no one.

**69.** Seeing the hangings before the door, in the courtyard, the wives of Srivasa and of all his brothers thought in their minds: "We shall not see the Lord at all!" — and they were sorely distressed.

*Note.* The hangings on the middle door — verse 54.

**70.** Gauranga at once understood that all those virtuous wives of the brahmanas were in such a state, and then gave the command: "Why do they not come into the house? Let them all come!"

**71.** Learning this command of His, Srivasa joyfully called them at once; and all of them, in agitation, joyful and shy, entered the house to see Him.

**72.** Entering and seeing Him, beautiful with His true radiance manifest, they attained an incomparable joy such as they had never known before; and, filled with contentment, they fell to the ground, wholly surrendered to His lotus feet.

**73.** Saying again and again, "Be always with your minds fixed on Me!", Sri Gaura, an ocean of supreme virtues, deeply moistened by the sprinkling of the nectar-juice of compassion, placed His pair of lotus feet on the head of each one of them.

*Note.* *Mac-cittā bhavata* echoes the Gita: *mac-cittaḥ* (18.57–58), *man-manā bhava* (9.34, 18.65).

**74.** And Srivasa and the others, great among the very greatest saints, looking on Him, full of true radiance, at first fully took Him then to be the very joy of each one's own heart, embodied, as it were.

**75.** They all laid here at His lotus feet everything they had that was rich in gold; and with these [gifts] the couch at once became like a fine wish-fulfilling creeper that brings forth jewels in abundance.

*Note.* *Khaṭṭā* — so in the edition (compare *khaṭvā* in verse 55); it is the Bengali form of the same word. The *kalpa-latā* is the heavenly wish-fulfilling creeper.

**76.** Then, putting on splendid silk, He whose body is golden like gold cast off the pair of cotton garments; and that cloth He, the ocean of mercy, Himself lovingly bestowed on the wives of the brahmanas.

*Note.* *Kṣauma* is cloth of linen or silk.

**77.** Pleased, He again gave other pure cloth as *prasada* to these, His dearest ones; and, seated upon the couch, playful, He shone, making all those [things] beautifully alive with His play.

*Note.* *Prāsādya* is "what is given as grace", that is, as *prasada*. The end of the verse is unclear: *tāni* (neuter) may refer to the garments or to all the gifts. The Gosvami edition (GGM) has the same text.

**78.** Wiping off [the paste] applied only a moment before, He again puts on a paste of sandalwood of His favourite fragrance; casting off the garlands put on only a moment before, He again, in seclusion, puts on garlands of flowers.

**79.** As He constantly chewed betel, the spittoon would at once be filled again with the very copious chewed [betel] He discarded; and so the wives of the brahmanas carried it away again and again.

*Note.* *Vahala* = *bahala*, "thick, copious" (*b* and *v* are often interchanged in Bengali orthography; compare *bāraṁ-bāram* for *vāraṁ vāram*).

**80.** The Lord at once smelled whatever was to be smelled; the ocean of mercy partook of whatever was to be enjoyed and accepted whatever was to be taken — thus Gauranga did everything, again and again, playfully.

**81.** Then the merciful [Lord] commanded the wise Advaita, the best of masters: "Dance, at once!" Hearing that, He, His heart rejoicing, danced together with the great [Vaishnavas], who sang, overwhelmed with happiness.

**82.** At Srivasa's word there came, trembling, that lady, the mother of all the people of the world, who was known as the mother of Mahaprabhu; and at that time, standing before the Lord, she was afraid.

*Note.* This is Shachidevi.

**83.** Seeing her, Mahaprabhu at once, as if startled, turned His lotus face half away to the side. Seeing this, Srivasa, pained at heart, said fearfully to Gaurachandra:

**84.** "This is not at all worthy of You, who are supremely compassionate and merciful! And if it is worthy, then where do we, too, stand as Yours? This lordliness does not befit You, O best of lords!" And after that the brahmana quickly said to her:

*Note.* In the second line the edition has *योग्यञ्चे द्वयमपि*; we read *yogyaṁ ced vayam api*. Literally, "where shall we be Yours?" The Gosvami edition (GGM) has the same text.

**85.** "Come, fall on the ground and bow down!" And when, having heard this, she still hesitated, he said to her kindly: "He is not your son! Quickly fall to the ground and bow down [to Him]!"

**86.** Reflecting thus, the lady at once fell to the ground and bowed down to the Lord. At that moment the wise Srivasa, his heart melting with fear, said apprehensively to the Lord:

**87.** "Show mercy to her, then, O Bhagavan, O Lord, so that she may not regard You as a son, so that she may surrender at Your feet. Only by that shall I too find peace."

*Note.* In Karnapura Srivasa asks the Lord to replace Shachi's maternal feeling with reverence. The later Gaudiya Vaishnava tradition, by contrast, sees maternal love (*vatsalya*) as one of the highest expressions of love; here we have an episode of the *maha-prakasha*, when the Lord revealed His majesty.

**88.** When this had been said, that great-hearted Lord, renowned for His mercy, at once placed His beautiful lotus foot on her head and, bestowing mercy on her just so, joyfully said ["So be it"].

*Note.* The Lord's words are not in the text: *uvāca* has no direct speech; the words in brackets are our supplement. (?) Perhaps *tathaiva* ("just so") itself is His reply. The Gosvami edition (GGM) has the same text.

**89.** The moment His lotus foot touched [her] — what a wonder! — she, shedding copious tears from her eyes, became bewildered, her slender body thrilled with hairs standing on end; and, all shame utterly lost, she gave herself up to unrestrained dancing.

*Note.* The edition has *स्पृष्टै* and *देहषष्टि* — misprints; we read *spṛṣṭe* (locative absolute) and *deha-yaṣṭi* ("body-stalk", of a slender figure).

**90.** With many kinds of effort they forcibly brought her back [to her senses], and only after a very long time, as it were, did she regain her composure. Weeping, her body washed with the water of her eyes, shaken, she went home in fear.

**91.** Two short watches of the night had already passed; and Gauranga — His eyes like the petals of a lotus just opened for the first time, full of supreme playfulness, merciful — spent its [remaining] two watches thus, in pastimes.

*Note.* A *yāma* (*prahara*) is a watch, a quarter of the night, about three hours. The watches are "short" because these are summer nights (verses 32–37).
