**92.** Some He [satisfied] with embraces, others with kisses, and others again with chewed [betel]; thus the treasure-house of supreme mercy fully satisfied [them all] by His great and beautiful playful pastime.

*Note.* *Carvita* is betel chewed by the Lord — the greatest *prasada*.

**93.** Thus He came once more to the shrine and, staying there a little while, shone; after that, exceedingly compassionate, He went in turn to the four houses of the four brothers.

*Note.* The four brothers are Srivasa, Sri Rama, Shripati and Shrinidhi; each had his own house around a common courtyard.

**94.** Having thus manifested His radiance, playing in many ways, the Lord, whose golden body was exceedingly lovely, came once more to the shrine and said to them all: "Enough of delays — so I am going."

*Note.* (?) "I am going" — in the light of verse 95: leaving this manifestation (or this body). The Gosvami edition (GGM) has the same text.

**95.** Hearing these words of His, Advaita and all the others spoke just so: "If that is so, then we too shall at once tie a sword to our necks and abandon this body!"

*Note.* The edition has *बद्धैतत्*, a misprint for *बद्ध्वैतत्* (*baddhvā etat*). *Evam evam ūcuḥ* — so for the metre (the edition runs it together as *एवमेव मूचुः*).

**96.** Then Gauranga laughed and at once said: "What is this? What is this word you are saying?" Having said this, He stood still for a moment — and then, with roars, fell to the ground. What a wonder!

*Note.* A *hunkara* is a thunderous cry of "hum!", a sign of ecstasy; compare Advaita's hunkaras.

**97.** Thus, having rolled on the ground for a long time, the Lord, His body golden like gold, became motionless — like a lovely peak of the golden mountain that has just fallen to the earth and blazes ceaselessly.

*Note.* The golden mountain is Meru.

**98.** And again, having rolled in the dust in the middle of the courtyard, He of the lovely form fell still, as if swooning: no movement or the like, no answer whatever, no tremor — and no stirring of breath at all.

**99.** As He had flung His arms upon the ground, so they remained, just the same, for a long time; and His beautiful pair of feet stayed just as He had cast them down a moment before, forgetful of the movements of His body.

**100.** While things were thus, the night passed away, and at once the sun had risen. But Gaurachandra, who through His swoons had entirely lost all activity, did not regain His natural state at all.

*Note.* *Paryāsīt* is from *pari-√as* ("to be over, come to an end").

**101.** Weighed down by thousands of burdens of sorrow, they all kept saying to one another, "What is this? What is it?" — and, seeing the Lord lying motionless on the ground, their bodies bathed in sweat, they swiftly swooned on every side.

**102.** "This night has passed in a moment, the sun has risen; now half a ghatika has gone — and that too is past; then half a watch, and after it this whole watch has gone by. Alas, alas! Why, even so, has the Lord not awoken?"

*Note.* A *ghaṭikā* is 24 minutes; a *yāma* (watch) is about three hours.

**103.** Constantly repeating this, afflicted with sorrow, they were intensely tormented, thinking, "He has gone!" Having laid Him down in that dwelling and fastened the door with a very firm bolt, they sat down [around Him].

*Note.* *Calitaḥ*, "has gone": in their fear, that He had departed from this world (cf. vv. 94–95). *Pur* here means "dwelling".

**104.** Then Advaita, with a hundred roars, sprinkled His face with water flung from His hand; yet Gauranga did not regain consciousness — there was no quiver, not even the breeze of breath.

**105.** Having reflected and resolved in their minds, they performed that kirtana, sweeter than honey, deep and most solemn. Yet, though hearing it, the Lord Gaurachandra for a long time did not return to His natural state.

**106.** When, through the paths of hearing, the nectar of this very sweet kirtana had unceasingly entered His heart, at once the thrill [of joy] spread again over the hairs of His body — and together with it [joy spread] among them, their hearts gladdened.

*Note.* *Harṣa* means both "joy" and "the bristling of the hairs".

**107.** Just as the bristling of the hairs arose all over Gaura, who was returning to His natural state, together with their joys, so then the Lord there slowly turned onto His side — and together with that their heavy sorrows turned away.

*Note.* A play on *vivṛtti*: "turning [of the body onto its side]" and "turning back, cessation". The Gosvami edition (GGM) has the same text.

**108.** Having long and now clearly relished that kirtana, Gauranga, who had lain motionless, slowly rose. And when the Lord in turn gave up the manifestation of His effulgence, His attire and ornaments were then taken off by them.

*Note.* *Vyaghaṭi* is the passive aorist of *vi-ghaṭ*, "to come apart, to be undone": together with the withdrawal of the effulgence the festive attire of the great bathing (vv. 61–68) is removed. One may also understand that they dressed Him again in His ordinary clothes.

**109.** Having risen, the Lord then [went] to the wall of the shrine and withdrew the manifest fire of His effulgence. Once more He assumed a soft, sweet beauty — as if the summer sun had become the autumn moon.

*Note.* *Deva-geha-bhittim* (accusative) stands without a verb of motion; the text may be corrupt. The Gosvami edition (GGM) has the same text.

**110.** Then, recovering His breath for a moment, He parted the petals of His lips with the gleam of the beautiful flowers of His teeth and replied — like one who has slept long and, waking at the end of sleep, knows nothing at all:

**111.** "Can it be that I, sunk in deep sleep, have spent so much time sleeping, for so long? I remember only that at dawn I was teaching the twice-born. How strange this is!"

*Note.* At this time Gaura is still teaching grammar (vv. 10–14: "four months of kirtana and teaching").

**112.** Then Srivasa, pure in heart, smiling slightly, said to Gaurachandra in jest: "This maya of Yours can no longer prevail now: it has become thoroughly known!"

*Note.* In the edition the second line reads *श्रीवासो विमल मनाग्विहस्य* — 11 syllables instead of 13. We restore *vimala-manā manāg* (one of two adjacent *manā* having dropped out), which gives the correct metre (?). The Gosvami edition (GGM) reads *vimalaṁ manāg*, with the same missing syllable.

**113.** Hearing that, Gaurachandra replied in astonishment: "What? What are you saying? Why do you laugh at Me so freely? I know nothing of all this — not for a moment!"

**114.** Thus three watches passed there, in addition to the eight watches that had elapsed here before. And all that time the people had no bath, no household work, no other activity, no sleep — they did not even lie down.

*Note.* Eight watches make a day and a night; together with the three, eleven watches — about 33 hours.

**115.** Thus that time — those eleven long, very long watches — became like a single blink of the eye; and during them not even for a moment did the eyelashes of the people, great in their happiness, move.

*Note.* *Vivṛtti*, "turning, movement" — here, of blinking. Eleven watches passed "like a blink" (*nimiṣa*), and yet no one blinked.

**116.** With eyes eager as after a long fast, with ears freed from deafness, with hearts as if newly born, these people remained all the while as though motionless.

**117.** The wives of the brahmanas, enchanted by the supreme grace bestowed by Gauranga, gazed [at Him] unwearied, without blinking, and from the abundance of joy, outer and inner, did not remember for even a moment their bodies or anything else.

**118.** Beholding thus the supreme secret, they did not feel the dominion of hunger and thirst at all. And moreover, if two days were like a moment, what place was there here for hunger and the other properties of the body?

**119.** When all this had ended, [the devotees], with supreme joy and with their hearts wholly given to it, made ready the finest substances for rubbing the body; and Gauranga, the ocean of the rasa of supreme mercy, set about bathing together with them.

*Note.* *Udvartana* — rubbing the body with fragrant pastes before bathing.

**120.** After the bath they all went each to his own house; and Gauranga, going again to his [Srivasa's] house, shone there, honoured by the four brothers headed by Sri Rama and by their wives.

**121.** With garlands and fragrances, with fine garments and ornaments, and with thick pastes mixed with liquid sandal — all offered with a love that was new every day — Gauranga again attained incomparable happiness.

*Note.* *Dhīra-paṅka* — literally "firm, dense paste".

**122.** Having anointed His body, limb by limb, with sandal, having adorned His form with many garlands, dressed Him in a fine cloth, delicate and white, and joyfully fed Him with what was fitting —

*Note.* Verses 122–123 form one sentence.

**123.** — thus, offering that fresh love of theirs day after day, the best of the brahmanas and their wives unceasingly attained a multitude of delights, gathered together by the floods of the ocean of incomparable good fortune.

**124.** Thus, having displayed the innate fire of His own effulgence and having sported for a long time, He, steady in His senses, His face sweet, then went to His own home, spreading for His mother that brimming joy.

*Note.* *Sthira-karaṇa* — "with steady, collected senses".

**125.** Thus, spreading the abundant nectar of mercy, the Lord, with great delight, in the rasas of dancing, spent eight months, from Jyaishtha to the end of Pausha — the summer and the rains, and the winter together with the autumn.

*Note.* Jyaishtha is May–June, Pausha December–January. The edition has *-atī-* and *वर्षैं*; we read *ati-* (as the metre requires) and *nidāgha-varṣair hemantam* (the fourth line begins with *र्हे*).

**126.** The rapture of dancing that the Lord displayed through these seasons, month by month, every day and every moment — not even He Himself could tell it; how much less, ah, petty humans! And how then could a thousand preceptors of the gods?

*Note.* The metre is *śikhariṇī* (17 syllables). The preceptor of the gods is Brihaspati.

**127.** Sri Gaurachandra, the Lord, always dances in the house of Srivasa alone, while his brothers wholeheartedly sing the nectarean rasa of the praise of Hari. Endlessly bathed in the pond of the nectar of His company and drinking again and again the nectar of the words that came from His mouth, they too constantly sported in the same way.

*Note.* The metre is *śārdūla-vikrīḍita* (19 syllables).

**128.** And Gadadhara Pandita, best of the virtuous, always abiding close beside Him, day after day, filled with ardent longing, serves Him — his own life, his most beloved.

*Note.* The metre is *drutavilambita* (12 syllables).

**129.** At night, too, he stays steadfastly near Him and sleeps there, full of longing. Thus the uninterrupted nectar of His pastimes was well enjoyed by him unceasingly.

*Note.* The edition has *विहरणामृतस्य*, a syllable short; *viharaṇāmṛtam asya* is restored by the metre. The repetition of *nirantaram* at the end of two lines is in the original.

Thus ends the fifth sarga, named "The Description of the Pastimes in Navadvipa", in the mahakavya "Sri Krishna-Chaitanya-charitamrita".

---

## Sarga Six — verses 1–123

**Title of the sarga:** *Bhakta-sammelana*, "The Gathering of the Devotees" (from the colophon).
**Metre:** *vasanta-tilakā* (14 syllables per pada); verses 3 and 36 and the quotation after verse 101 — *anuṣṭubh*; the quotation after verse 69 — *upajāti*; verses 122–123 — *śikhariṇī*.
**Total verses:** 123 (plus two unnumbered quotations: after 69 and after 101).
**Parallels:** Chaitanya-charitamrita, Adi 17.21–25 (the explanation of *harer nāma*); Shuklambara Brahmachari ("Are there no jackals there?") — Murari 2.3.5–9; cf. Chaitanya-bhagavata, Madhya 1.78–108 (prema in the house of Shuklambara) and 16.109 ff. (the Lord eats his rice).

**Contents of the passage:**
- 1–5 — in the house of Srivasa the Lord explains the glory of the Name: *harer nāma…* — the threefold repetition, *kevalam*, *eva*.
- 6–7 — He touches Sri Rama Pandita and the others with His feet and bestows *bhakti*.
- 8–11 — Shuklambara Brahmachari: "I have gone to Dvaraka and Mathura" — "And are there no jackals there?"; the promise of *prema*; his tears.
- 12–13 — at night the Lord bids Gadadhara give away the *nirmalya*; in the morning Gadadhara distributes the mahaprasada.
- 14 — Gadadhara adorns the Lord.
- 15–22 — clouds over the kirtana; the Lord sings with *mandiras* — the clouds disperse, the moon rises; dancing, the women's ululation.
- 23–26 — Shuklambara: "You have made Navadvipa a new Mathura"; dancing on the shoulders of friends; gifts.
- 27–34 — the miracle of the mango seed; "all this is maya — but, given to service, it becomes real".
- 35–36 — to Mukunda: what is Brahman? — the verse on "Rama".
- 37–41 — anger with Mukunda for preferring the four-armed form; "anoint me as Your servant" — His feet on Mukunda's head.
- 42–46 — to Murari Gupta: "give up 'spiritual' songs, sing of the Lord's feet"; the request of Narayana the physician (*vaidya*); the blessing on Murari to write about Gaura.
- 47–48 — Srivasa Pandita.
- 49–52 — a student: "the glory of the Name is *arthavāda*"; the Lord stops His ears and bathes in the Ganga in His clothes.
- 53–62 — at Advaita's: the embrace ("which of them is Advaita?"); "whoever says there is no bhakti in Kali is a sinner"; a brahmana who is not a Vaishnava does not come at the mental summons.
- 63–69 — "difficult knowledge" about the Atma; then: "My devotee purifies the three worlds" (+ Srimad-Bhagavatam 11.14.24).
- 70–75 — the club for the wicked; the leper who hated devotees; "this town is an impassable forest"; Advaita comes.
- 76–82 — "I have come for your sake"; Advaita's dance; Srivasa — "who then is a devotee?" — "apart from Him, there is neither Uddhava nor Shuka here"; Advaita is equal to the Lord.
- 83–85 — the ban on "spiritual" speculation; Murari: "I never even knew it".
- 86–88 — the Lord's summer dances; His attire.
- 89–97 — a portrait of Gaura dancing (arms, chest, girdle, hips, feet, lips); mridangas, mandiras, ululation; 96–97 — a *yugmaka*.
- 98–100 — the etymology of the name "Srivasa"; Murari recites verses about Rama — the Lord's foot on his head.
- 101–105 — "Ramadasa" on Murari's forehead; Srimad-Bhagavatam 11.14.20; "obey Srivasa"; to Sri Rama — "serve your elder brother"; the distribution of remnants.
- 106–108 — news of the arrival of the avadhuta Nityananda; the Lord sends for Him.
- 109–116 — the search; "He is at Nandana Acharya's"; the meeting, praise of Nityananda (detachment and love); the dust of His feet on the heads of the devotees.
- 117–123 — Nityananda is fed; at Srivasa's the Lord shows Him the six-armed, four-armed and two-armed forms; Nityananda's dance and laughter.

**1.** Once, soon after that, He came to the house of Srivasa and first of all explained the glory of the Names — this mighty [glory], the highest of all the aims of human life, bringing a joy hard to attain even for the whole host of the Shrutis.

*Note.* The four aims of human life (*puruṣārtha*) are dharma, artha, kama and moksha.

**2.** Wishing to sport in the new, great ocean of the rasa of His own pastimes, He, ever given to playfulness, first joyfully scattered over the world, with cupped handfuls of words, the charming flood of the nectar of the glory of His own Name.

**3.** The Name of Hari, the Name of Hari, the Name of Hari alone — only that! In Kali there is no other way — truly none, truly none, truly none.

*Note.* The verse is from the Brihan-naradiya Purana (38.126); Mahaprabhu explains it in the Chaitanya-charitamrita, Adi 17.21–25. Verses 4–5 are Karnapura's explanation, close to the Chaitanya-charitamrita.

**4.** This Original Person in the form of the Name does not rise on the earth at all times: let all know that [He rises] in Kali. And the statement repeated three times, and the word *eva* too, are for the firmness of the whole world, sunk in great dullness.

**5.** "This alone, exclusively" — that is what the word *kevalam* declares, so that it may be firmly understood. But for one who speaks otherwise there is no way — truly none: this is made certain by the word *eva*, [repeated] again.

*Note.* Cf. Chaitanya-charitamrita, Adi 17.23–25: *kevala* excludes knowledge, yoga, austerity and ritual; *anyathā* — "for one who does otherwise there is no deliverance"; *eva* three times — for firmness. *Kaivalya* here is not "liberation" but "exclusiveness" (an abstract noun from *kevala*). The Gosvami edition (GGM) has the same text.

**6.** Having spoken thus, the ocean of mercy then openly manifested His effulgence, took the splendid seat of honour and at once touched them all together, headed by Sri Rama Pandita, with the charming pair of His lotus feet.

*Note.* Sri Rama Pandita is Srivasa's brother.

**7.** They too at once bowed down with bent necks and joyfully attained service whose very nature is love. The beautiful Bhagavan, exceedingly charming in His affection for His devotees, abundantly gave them what they desired.

**8.** Then a certain fortunate man, Shuklambara, best of the twice-born, said to the Lord, who had manifested an extraordinary effulgence: "I have constantly gone to Dvaravati (Dvaraka) and Mathura — look upon me, wretched as I am, for a moment, and have mercy, O Lord!"

*Note.* Shuklambara Brahmachari is Gaura's neighbour in Navadvipa, who lived by begging alms. The Lord's answer in verse 9 shows that the pilgrim is Shuklambara himself.

**9.** "Are there not packs of jackals there? And what comes to them of that? Are they not jackals still?" When the all-pervading Lord had spoken thus, this bull among the twice-born, his soul full of longing, fell heavily to the ground like a stick.

*Note.* The sense: pilgrimage by itself, without bhakti, does not change one's nature — jackals too live in holy places. *Daṇḍavat*, "like a stick", is a full prostration.

**10.** And again Gaura — the moon of nectarean rays, abundantly merciful, His heart compassionate to the wretched, the sole knower of hearts — said to the brahmana: "This very day, here, you will have devotion with love for the lotus feet of the Lord."

*Note.* The Lord speaks of Krishna in the third person. *Sudhā-mayūkha*, "nectar-rayed", is an epithet of the moon.

**11.** Then the great soul at once fell again at His feet. His heart melting, his body covered with masses of bristling hairs, shaken by floods of abundant tears, he wept aloud, his voice faltering.

**12.** The splendid, great-minded Gadadhara — exceedingly noble in character, gentle by nature, his form deeply peaceful — as he lay near [the Lord] at night, was told by the Lord: "This nirmalya on My chest is to be taken off [and given] to these [devotees]."

*Note.* Nirmalya — garlands and ornaments that have already been on the Deity (here, on the Lord); the highest prasada.

**13.** Thus, whatever He joyfully gave to whichever person, that very thing Gadadhara always gave in the morning to that same person, who rejoiced — the mahaprasada left by Mahaprabhu.

**14.** Gadadhara, foremost among panditas, having strung a mass of garlands and carefully prepared a paste of fine sandal, camphor and other excellent [substances], always applied them to His limbs with eager longing.

**15.** Then once, in the evening, when the ocean of His kirtana had risen with those devotees of His lotus feet, Sri Gaurachandra, seeing the vault of the sky suddenly covered with clouds, became sorrowful, fearing an obstacle.

*Note.* The edition has *उदित*; the metre requires *udite* (locative absolute). The episode of the Lord dispersing the clouds over the kirtana: Murari 2.3.18–27; Chaitanya-charitamrita, Adi 17.89 (the Chaitanya-bhagavata does not have it).

**16.** Then, taking the finest *mandiras* in His lotus hands and making all the ragas and notes fulfilled, He joyfully sang aloud the multitude of His own qualities — beautiful, like Ananga, the bodiless, having taken a body on earth.

*Note.* A *mandira* is a pair of small hand cymbals (*karatāla*). Ananga ("the bodiless") is Kamadeva, the god of love, burnt by Shiva. The edition has *रागात्*; the Gosvami edition (GGM) reads *rāgān* ("the ragas"), and so we read.

**17.** At once the clouds, scattered by the wind, went off in every direction — and with them the sorrows of [all] hearts. The sky became perfectly clear, and the moon rose with the whole host of stars to dispel the darkness.

**18.** Reddening with love, his ray-hands outstretched, the moon, lord of herbs, has tightly embraced the charming young bride, Night, whose garment of darkness has slipped away; and he sports, as it were, [pouring out], under the guise of moonlight, a surging mass of the waves of an ocean of bliss.

*Note.* The whole verse is built on double meanings: *rajyan* — "reddening" (of the rising moon) and "falling in love"; *kara* — "ray" and "hand"; *antarīya* — "lower garment", here the darkness. *Oṣadhīśa* is the moon, which nourishes the herbs. The accusative *laharī-cayam ucchalantam* has no verb to govern it; we supply [pouring out].

**19.** While there, on the clear path of the gods, the nectar-rayed moon with the beautiful stars was pouring out nectar, Sri Gaura, the cool-rayed moon, here on earth with His own people, again delighted in the nectarean rasa of sankirtana.

*Note.* A parallel: the moon with the stars in the sky — the Gaura-moon with the devotees on earth.

**20.** As soon as the dance of Sri Gaura, the cool-rayed moon, the bewilderer of Manmatha, began, His splendid lotus feet [shone] with excellent anklets and the rest, and the pair of His coral-like hands shone once more with bracelets and with rhythms.

*Note.* *Manmatha-manmatha* — "the bewilderer of [even] the bewilderer of minds", that is, of Kamadeva; an epithet of Krishna (cf. Madana-mohana). *Lāsya* is a graceful dance.

**21.** With those loud cries of "ulu-ulu", mingled with shouts of "Jaya!", that issued from the moonlike faces of the host of brahmanas' wives, and with the joyful sounds of the host of gods standing in the sky, the very great festival became exceedingly tumultuous.

*Note.* *Ulu-dhvani* is the auspicious ululation of Bengali women.

**22.** Adorned with garlands made of masses of flowers — kunda, lotus, oleander, fresh mallika, jati and others — and having anointed His whole body with pastes of sandal, kunkuma and lustrous musk, He shone as He danced.

*Note.* *Kunda*, *mallikā* and *jāti* are different kinds of jasmine. *Mṛganābhi* ("deer's navel") is musk. The edition has *रवकलृ…*; we read *avakḷpta* with the vowel *ḷ*, as the metre requires.

**23.** But the great-souled Shuklambara fell upon the ground and said in awe to Sri Gaurachandra: "O Lord, by Your various pastimes You have now made the New Island a new Mathura indeed!"

*Note.* A play on words: *navīna-dvīpa*, "the New Island", is Navadvipa; *madhurā* is the Bengali pronunciation of "Mathura" and at the same time means "sweet". The edition has *दीपं*, a misprint for *द्वीपं*. This answers verse 8: Mathura has come to Shuklambara himself.

**24.** Having spoken thus, he praised Him at length in speech greatly faltering, now in prose, now in verse; a flood of tears flowed from him over the ground all around; his heart was full of agitation and humility, and he wept incessantly at the top of his voice.

**25.** As He dances, now for a moment He lays the massive, majestic pillar of His arm on the slope of a friend's lovely shoulder; now for a moment, His whole slender frame shaking with unrestrained trembling, He helplessly stumbles on the ground at every step.

**26.** Now for a moment, charming in the mood of the Lord, He — the sole ocean of mercy — again compassionately bestows boons on them. Thus He, the moon of the ocean of the nectar of great mercy, taught the people by countless manifestations of His pastimes, of many kinds.

*Note.* "The moon of the ocean": the moon raises the tide, and Gaura makes the ocean of mercy swell.

**27.** Now for a moment Mahaprabhu — lovelier than millions of moons, exceedingly beautiful in His [ecstatic] transformations — climbs onto the shoulder of a devotee of His lotus feet and sports in the night, making the tide of the ocean of His own people's joy surge.

**28.** On the next day, shining like a thousand rising suns, He sat on the ground and, making all the quarters around ring with echoes through the claps of His two palms, said to the devotees of His lotus feet:

*Note.* *Ahimāṁśu* ("the not-cool-rayed") is the sun. Verses 28–34 tell of the miracle of the mango tree in Srivasa's courtyard; cf. Murari 2.4.6–11; Chaitanya-charitamrita, Adi 17.79–88.

**29.** "Hey, look, look: a mango seed planted in the ground! Look — now here is the mango's sprout! Look — already it is a span high! And look again — quickly a bough has grown on it!

*Note.* A *vitasti*, a span, is about 22 cm.

**30.** "Look — in the space of a blink branches have grown here! Look — a mass of leaves has appeared all around it! Look — this very [fruit] has become ripe! And look — even its gathering has come about! This is most wonderful!

*Note.* *Grahaṇam* — "taking, gathering [of the fruit]" (?). The text is the same in the Gosvami edition (GGM).

**31.** "The tree, all its branches and all its fruit — all this is made by maya; in truth it exists nowhere. This is a conjurer's trick; and since it is false, it undergoes change and comes to nothing.

**32.** "Yet that very thing becomes nectar if people do it before the Lord for His amusement: then they obtain in great abundance a wealth of excellent relish, and from what maya has made they gain a wondrous fruit.

*Note.* A play on *phala* — "fruit [of the mango]" and "result". In the Chaitanya-charitamrita the devotees eat these mangoes.

**33.** "So it is with this whole false universe: if it always serves for service to the Lord, it becomes fully meaningful. The untrue becomes true, and what is impure becomes pure.

**34.** "Therefore people should gather everything here, false though it is, for the sake of service to the Supreme Lord. This samsara will be no obstacle to such a one, for one who is devoted to service is hindered by no one at all."

*Note.* The edition has *बाध्यते एव*; the metre requires the sandhi *bādhyata eva*. The thought of verses 31–34 is the principle of *yukta-vairāgya*: the world given over to service ceases to be an obstacle.

**35.** At this time the greatly merciful [Lord] said to the most noble Mukunda Datta, who stood before Him: "What, then, do you understand here by 'Brahman'?" And having said this, He Himself then recited [a verse]:

*Note.* Mukunda Datta was a singer, an associate of Gaura.

**36.** "Yogis take delight in the infinite, truly blissful, spiritual Self; therefore the Supreme Brahman is called by the word 'Rama'."

*Note.* The metre is *anuṣṭubh*. A verse from the Padma Purana (the "Rama-sahasranama"), quoted in the Chaitanya-charitamrita, Madhya 9.29. The verb *ram*, "to delight", yields the name *Rāma*: the Supreme Brahman is the one in whom [yogis] delight.

**37.** And again, instructing him, the Lord spoke, the two petals of His lips trembling slightly with anger: "Hey! Is it your opinion that the four-armed form is exceedingly excellent, and that the other, the two-armed, is somewhat inferior to it?"

*Note.* The edition has *न्नूनं* (*nūnam*, "surely"); we read *nyūnam* ("inferior"), as the sense requires (*kiyat*, "somewhat"). The two-armed form is Krishna, the four-armed Vishnu-Narayana.

**38.** "If you understand your own good, proclaim: 'That two-armed form is better than the other!'" Hearing those words of His, in which mercy played, [Mukunda] fell on the ground and said with joy and fear:

**39.** "I have bathed to my heart's content in the waters of the river of the gods, and my body is adorned with the dust of the feet of the holy Vaishnavas. O beautiful One, give me the parasol of Your two lotus feet over my head, and perform my consecration to the office of a servant!"

*Note.* The image is that of a royal consecration (*abhiṣeka*): the bath (in the Ganga), the anointing (with the dust of the Vaishnavas), the royal parasol (*ātapatra*) — the feet of the Lord; but Mukunda's "kingdom" is service. The edition has *प्रयेच्छ*, a misprint.

**40.** Hearing this, the Supreme Lord, His heart full of the nectar of mercy and gladdened by the nectar of his words, was pleased and then placed on his most pure head His two beautiful lotus feet — the sovereign of His own people.

**41.** Having at once received the touch of His two lotus feet, [Mukunda] — his slender frame covered with masses of bristling hair, washed by streams of water pouring from his eyes — became boundlessly filled with longing: who else could be like him?

*Note.* In the edition a syllable is missing from the fourth line (*babhūva ivātiśaya-*: the metre is broken and there is a hiatus); the Gosvami edition (GGM) reads *babhūva ka ivātiśayotsukātmā*, and so we read.

**42.** Then Sri Gaurachandra, the sole treasure-house of mercy, charming in an intense mood, said this to Murari: "Hey, is there then some 'spiritual' song composed by you? Tell the truth quickly: have you composed one or not?"

*Note.* Murari Gupta is a physician from Shrihatta, a friend of Nimai's and the future author of the "Sri Krishna-Chaitanya-charitamrita" (the *Kadacha*). *Ādhyātmika* ("concerning the higher Self") probably carries a note of reproach for a leaning towards speculation. The Gosvami edition (GGM) reads *kim u kṛtaṁ na tavāsti gītaṁ* ("have you not composed a song about the higher Self?").

**43.** "But if you desire life itself — or rather the dawnings of love — then give up this frivolity and compose a description of the greatness, form and qualities of the lotus feet [of the Lord], bathed in the nectar of beautiful mercy."

*Note.* The edition has *jīvitam ena*, a misprint; the Gosvami edition (GGM) reads *jīvitam eva* (without *tu*).

**44.** Hearing the sweet words of Mahaprabhu, Narayana, the foremost of physicians, then said to Him: "O Lord, show mercy to Murari Gupta, so that he may be able to describe Your own deeds."

*Note.* The edition prints the name in quotation marks: *«नारायणो»*. Who this Narayana the physician is remains unclear; *amam* in the edition is a misprint (the Gosvami edition (GGM) has *amum*). An important episode: here Murari receives the blessing to describe the life of Gaura — which was fulfilled in his *Kadacha*, on which Karnapura relies.

**45.** Then, having heard his request, the ocean of mercy, supremely delighted, said to him: "Whatever he says, all of it will surely be faultless, and there will be mighty power [in his words]."

*Note.* The edition has *तत् प्रार्थना*; we read *tat-prārthanām* (accusative).

**46.** Hearing His words, the good-hearted Murari was delighted and rejoiced, the hairs of his body standing erect. Wholly under the sway of an intense happiness, he felt himself immersed beyond measure, as it were, in oceans of nectar.

*Note.* A variant noted in the edition: *sukhaika-rasaḥ* instead of *sukhaika-vaśaḥ*.

**47.** The great-minded Srivasa Pandita — very noble in character, by nature delighting in devotion to Hari, very steadfast, pure, devoted to his own duty, deeply peaceful and self-controlled — that great soul rejoiced day after day in service to Him.

**48.** Thus, unceasingly — with worship, with dances and sankirtanas, and likewise with feelings of various kinds — the large-hearted Srivasa Pandita, that great soul, always rejoiced, sporting freely in His company.

**49.** On another day the Lord, while teaching the sons of brahmanas, continually spread the glorification of His own Name and qualities. And as fate would have it, one brahmana's son, understanding nothing whatsoever, spoke before the Lord at the end of it:

*Note.* Cf. Chaitanya-bhagavata, Madhya 1.252–289: after Gaya, Nimai explains every word to His students as Krishna instead of teaching grammar. The episode with the student itself (verses 49–52) — Murari 2.4.33–34; Chaitanya-charitamrita, Adi 17.72–74.

**50.** "This greatness of the Name — surely it is mere *arthavāda*!" Having heard out all such words of the scoundrel, [the Lord] stopped His ears and with great revulsion went to the bank of the Ganga, with him going ahead.

*Note.* *Arthavāda* (a term of Mimamsa) is a laudatory statement that has no direct force of injunction; to call the glory of the Name *arthavāda* is one of the ten offences against the Name.

**51.** Having bathed in His clothes, He came out [of the water] and, purified, in clean clothes, joyfully went to His own home. Whoever recounts this every day, whoever hears it, becomes wholly absorbed in love and in the Name.

*Note.* Bathing in one's clothes (*sacela-snāna*) is purification after contact with defilement — here, after the words of an offender. *Cela-vṛndaiḥ* — literally "with a multitude of clothes" (?). The edition has *प्रेम्नि*, a Bengali spelling. The text is the same in the Gosvami edition (GGM).

**52.** Thus the Supreme Lord first proclaimed in full the greatness of His own Name; and then, gradually and step by step, He likewise set aside the "spiritual" position through the rapture of service to His own lotus feet.

*Note.* *Ādhyātmika* is the same word as in the reproach to Murari (verse 42): speculative "spirituality" without *bhakti*.

**53.** Then once the Lord, the ocean of happiness, beautiful like Ananga, the bodiless, having taken a body, set out with those devotees of His feet headed by Srivasa Pandita to see Advaitachandra at His house.

*Note.* Ananga ("the bodiless") is Kamadeva, the god of love. Advaita's house is in Shantipura. The second half of the verse repeats verse 16.

**54.** Going along the road with famous dances, kirtanas and the like, singing and dancing, He came to His house. And He who knows the greatness of His own devotees in His turn fell on the ground like a rod before Advaitachandra.

*Note.* *Bhūyaḥ*, "in His turn": that is, after Advaita had fallen before Him. The edition has *भुयः*.

**55.** Then the two, supremely merciful in all the worlds, Their bodies full of longing, embraced each other; and the crowd of people wondered about Them: "Is this Advaita? Or is it He, Gaurachandra?"

*Note.* A play on the name *Advaita*, "non-dual": in the embrace the two became indistinguishable.

**56.** Seated on a clean seat, Gaurachandra, the sole store of mercy, absorbed in the play of the devotion to His own feet that He had revealed, held a lucid discourse, explaining devotion to Himself in many ways.

**57.** Then the great-souled Advaita said: "Let all those dull-witted fools who keep saying, 'In Kali there is no bhakti at all,' look [at this]!" And the Lord Himself heard it.

**58.** Then Bhagavan, His two eyes reddened with intense anger, said: "He who says 'There is none' truly has no path; his very birth is fruitless; surely he is a great sinner! For bhakti reigns in Kali always."

*Note.* The edition has *तस्यै जन्म*; the metre requires *tasyaiva*. The edition gives *tad ādi* (obscure); the Gosvami edition (GGM) reads *tadāti-krodhāruṇākṣi-yugalaḥ* ("then, with eyes reddened by intense anger"), and so we read.

**59.** After that Srivasa, seeing there a fierce brahmana who was not a Vaishnava, said thus: "Surely he will be a great obstacle to the sankirtana here. How can he be got away from here?"

**60.** "Enough, enough of your worry here: this brahmana will not come," the Lord said unfailingly. And by His mental commands he did not come at all; and meanwhile that god on earth [Srivasa] rejoiced.

*Note.* In the edition the first line (*त्वच्चिन्तयालमत्र*) lacks two syllables; the Gosvami edition (GGM) reads *tvac-cintayālam alam atra*, and so we read. In the third line the edition has *नैवागम*; we read *naivāgamat* (for the metre). *Bhūmi-deva*, "god on earth", is a brahmana; here, evidently, Srivasa himself.

**61.** Placing His right arm on the shoulder of Srivasa, the ornament of the brahmanas, and the other on the shoulder of Gadadhara, and setting His lotus foot on the head of Sri Rama Pandita, He shone, lovelier than a multitude of moons.

*Note.* *Varāṅga* ("the best limb") is the head.

**62.** Given to play in the abode of this great Lord [Advaita], He — with a pair of lotus eyes, golden as pure gold, His face smiling, at once stealing the pride of Kandarpa — shone, arranging pastimes with the crowds of His own people.

*Note.* *Maheśvara*, "the great Lord": Advaita is held to be an incarnation of Mahavishnu and Sadashiva. *Darpaka* (Kandarpa) is the god of love.

**63.** Gaura Mahaprabhu gave an explanation concerning the truth of the higher Self, very difficult for others to understand: "Here the one Atma alone unceasingly remains; and in creation it is the very same [Atma] that again shines, alone."

*Note.* Cf. Srimad-Bhagavatam 2.9.32 (*aham evāsam evāgre… paścād ahaṁ yad etac ca yo 'vaśiṣyeta so 'smy aham*). Verses 63–68 are the "difficult knowledge" (*jñāna*) that the Lord sets out in Advaita's house; cf. Murari 2.5.20–33 (the Lord expounds the path of knowledge and proclaims the highest — bhakti) and Chaitanya-bhagavata, Madhya 19.20 ff., where Advaita deliberately expounds the Yoga-vasishtha. The translation of the philosophical lines of verses 63–67 is provisional.

**64.** Thus [speaking], the ocean of mercy, dancing, opened His hands and at once clenched them again into fists. Then He, the only refuge of the world, again set forth at length that exposition of the truth whose nature is being and consciousness.

*Note.* The gesture apparently depicts the unfolding and folding up of the world (creation and dissolution) (?). The edition has *जयदेकगतिः*; we read *jagad-eka-gatiḥ*. The Gosvami edition (GGM) has the same text.

**65.** "Becoming, too, is certainly meaningless; the wise should ascertain only its true nature. For without the understanding of this, liberation — oneness with Brahman — never comes about at all."

*Note.* The syntax of the second half is unclear (*hi sā syāt*, "for it would be", hangs loose); we translate by the sense. *Etat* may also be taken as referring to the oneness: "without understanding this oneness with Brahman there is never liberation". The Gosvami edition (GGM) has the same text.

**66.** "Look: here are two fingers on the hand of a single person. The one anointed with nectar he, one and the same, gazes upon [fondly]; at the other, utterly loathsome with an oozing sore, he, tormented by disgust, will not look openly for even a moment."

*Note.* The point of the parable within the chain of verses 65–67 is unclear (?): perhaps the one Atma relates differently to its *upādhis* (coverings). The Gosvami edition (GGM) has the same text.

**67.** "Thus here that one beginningless Atma — what is called 'the remainder' — always remains. This same [Atma] does become possessed of a covering; freed from the manifest covering, [it is the Atma]; otherwise it is indeed just some jiva here."

*Note.* The edition has *भवती* and *उपाधे मुक्तो*; we read *bhavati*, *upādher muktaḥ*. *Śeṣa* — "remainder" (cf. *avaśiṣyate* in verse 63). The translation is provisional. The Gosvami edition (GGM) has the same text.

**68.** Having thus expounded at length knowledge that is by nature hard of access, and having thus made His own people understand it simply, the Lord fell silent; then, His eyes flooded with a stream of falling tears, covered with masses of bristling hairs, He sweetly said:

*Note.* *Laghutayā* — "lightly, simply", or "by showing its insignificance". The Gosvami edition (GGM) has the same text.

**69.** "With a heart melting with love, the hairs of his body bristling, now weeping and now laughing, having lost, like one insensible, the properties of the body through the intoxicating drink of love, singing and dancing — it is My devotee who always protects and purifies this entire threefold world.

'He whose speech falters, whose heart melts, who weeps again and again and sometimes laughs, who without shame sings aloud and dances — he, endowed with devotion to Me, purifies the world.'"

*Note.* The metre is *upajāti*. Srimad-Bhagavatam 11.14.24 (Krishna's words to Uddhava). In the edition the verse stands in quotation marks and without a number; the next verse of the edition is 70.

**70.** Having spoken thus, the sole ocean of mercy, His face smiling, joyfully and sweetly danced with His own people. That He Himself, setting about to dance, reveals love on the surface of the earth — that is precisely this mercy [of His].

*Note.* The edition has *प्रेमप्रकाशयति* written as one word; we read *prema prakāśayati*.

**71.** On the next day Advaita, radiant as the spotless sun, arrived there to see the treasure-house of mercy. While He, having bathed and performed His worship, was about to come, the Lord in the meantime went to the house of Srivasa.

**72.** Having gone there, He smiled slightly, placed a flower on a staff and, with a roar, He whose eyes were long like lotuses said thus: "Aho! This worship of the club has been performed for the subduing of the wicked!"

*Note.* The edition has *मनाग्धसितं*; we read *manāg ghasitam* (*manāk* + *hasitam*). The club (*gadā*) is the weapon of Vishnu and of Balarama.

**73.** "There is one most wicked hater of My devotees, his body oozing with sores from many kinds of leprosy. I shall, moreover, make him an utter denizen of hell, and likewise his wretched disciples food for dogs and jackals!"

*Note.* Probably Gopala Chapala, who offended Srivasa and was struck with leprosy (Chaitanya-charitamrita, Adi 17.37–59; Murari 2.13).

**74.** "I wish to go from here into the forest! [But] this very land [is a forest]: some here are like tigers, others likewise are monkeys, some likewise are like grass, and some are trees. Therefore this very [place] is a vast, impassable forest!"

*Note.* The first hint of the future sannyasa. *Hari* can also mean "lion".

**75.** "It is heard that Advaita has come — why then does He not come for so long? So I shall surely go to Him there." Just as He was reflecting thus, Advaita fell to the ground before Him and in awe bowed down again and again.

**76.** Then He quickly raised Advaita, took Him by the hand and said: "Surely it is for your sake that I have come here." Having spoken thus, the ever-merciful [Lord] sat down together with Him on a couch and thereupon shone openly.

*Note.* The edition has *तन्तु* = *taṁ tu*. "I have come for your sake": Advaita called Gaura down to earth by His prayers (Chaitanya-charitamrita, Adi 3).

**77.** Then, at His command, the great soul Advaita danced wildly, the workings of His mind overwhelmed by an excess of happiness. Seeing all this, the Lord, the moon of the ocean of rasa, joyfully said to Him, filling His heart with rasa:

**78.** "O great soul, these [devotees] constantly pray for love; and so, for your sake, it will surely be given [to them]." And He [Advaita] in turn said: "These are devotees of the pair of Your lotus feet; truly, they are indeed a [worthy] vessel for it."

**79.** Thus, seated with them on moonlit nights, shining with a radiance like that of the rising mild-rayed [moon], He said to Advaita in particular: "It is you indeed who are a devotee; surely it is for your sake that I have come here to the earth."

*Note.* *Akhara-dyuti*, "whose rays are not harsh", is the moon.

**80.** Hearing this, Srivasa, the best of brahmanas, then addressed that boundlessly merciful Lord of the earth with sweet words and with fear: "This is Your mercy! Who is this devotee, that the Lord Himself has said this [of him]?"

*Note.* Srivasa's remark is double-edged; it can also be understood as "Who is a devotee [by himself]? It is only Your mercy." *Īśa ūce* stands for *īśaḥ ūce*: the hiatus shows the nominative, "the Lord Himself has said", not the vocative *īśa* (which would give *īśoce*). The Gosvami edition (GGM) has the same text.

**81.** His two lips trembling with anger, He said to Srivasa Pandita in harsh words: "Is there here, apart from Him, any devotee of Mine — an Uddhava, or a Shuka — that you should have come to such an opinion?"

*Note.* The sense: Advaita is to the Lord what Uddhava and Shukadeva are; there is no other such here. *Daśana-cchada*, "the covering of the teeth", is the lip.

**82.** "In this land of Bharata, is there manifest anyone apart from Advaita who is worthy of the homage of all the hosts of the immortals? Therefore He must be regarded as equal to Me; nowhere in the world is there ever anyone like Him."

**83.** Hearing those words, he [Srivasa] fell silent. Then the merciful [Lord] again said this and that: "Here those like you must never, not even for a moment, engage in this 'spiritual' [speculation], even with the tip of the tongue.

**84.** If it is ever uttered aloud even for a moment, the supremely rare bhakti-yoga will not be granted." When the Almighty had spoken thus, the great souls said: "Let me forget it — make it so, make it so!"

*Note.* *Mama vismṛtiḥ* — literally "let there be forgetfulness in me": each of them says it.

**85.** Murari said: "O Lord, I have never known any speculation on the higher Self at all! O Bhagavan, show mercy!" And Mahaprabhu said to him on the road: "You know it: I have heard it here before."

*Note.* The edition has *tat pathi*; the Gosvami edition (GGM) reads *taṁ pathi* ("to him on the road"), and so we read. Cf. verses 42–43, where the Lord rebukes Murari for his song on the higher Self.

**86.** Thus, through the nectar of His dancing and singing, that summer season constantly took on coolness as its nature throughout the whole world of men and again made the pond of the mind full — a wonder, this!

*Note.* A paradox: the summer, which dries up ponds, here fills them.

**87.** Having made on His head a peerless, charming ornament of fine, white, comfortable cloth, a captivating string of gleaming, beautiful corals on His neck, He, golden as the mountain of gold, is victorious as He sets about the dance.

*Note.* The ornament of cloth on the head is a turban. The mountain of gold is Meru.

**88.** With armlets, wristlets, gleaming bangles and the rest, which share in the special play of His two unrestrained arms, His spread fingers gleaming with golden rings, He, the bewilderer of Manmatha, is victorious as He sets about the dance.

**89.** His hands lovely as freshly blossomed lotuses, lighting up all the quarters with the outpouring of streams of His radiance, surpassing the peak of Meru with the splendour of His chest, His lips sweet, He dances without pause.

**90.** Beautiful in His garment, a swaying, charming loincloth, lovely with the girdle gleaming over it, He — the precious jewel in the crown of the host of unrestrained dancers, a connoisseur of the relish of play — shines sweetly in the dance.

*Note.* *Dhaṭī* is a strip of cloth, a loincloth.

**91.** He steals hearts with the play of His gleaming, freely swinging girdle hanging down over the round of His splendid hips; and under the guise of the broad round of His two thighs He bears, as it were, a pair of lovely, well-rounded *rāma-kadalī* banana trunks.

*Note.* *Rāma-kadalī* is a variety of banana; the smooth banana trunk is the canonical image of the thigh in Indian poetry.

**92.** Bearing the pair of His splendid lotus feet, blossoming with excellent anklets and the rest and with the rays of His shining nails — moonstones — and a lovely lap given to play, He, golden as molten gold, is victorious at the festival of the dance.

*Note.* *Indu-maṇi* (moonstone) — the Lord's toenails. In the edition *परमङ्कतलं* is understood as *param aṅka-talam*.

**93.** The soles of His feet tinged with the lustre of gleaming coral, moving over the earth with charming and sweet placing [of His steps], His radiance sweet, tender and lovely, He shone as He set about the dance, like beautiful Ananga, the bodiless, having taken a body.

**94.** With resounding mridangas, karatalas, mandiras and the rest, with lovely songs led by loudly flowing melodies, with the very loud cry of "ulu-ulu" rising from the lotus faces of the host of brahmanas' wives, there was a great festival.

*Note.* The *mridanga* is the clay drum of kirtana; *karatalas* are hand cymbals.

**95.** With voices as captivating as the call of the male cuckoo, their lotus hands adorned with pairs of fine mandiras, they [the brahmanas' wives], seeing His dance, at once joyfully sang aloud, sweet with delight, their bodies covered with bristling hairs.

*Note.* The subject is the brahmanas' wives of verse 94. The *kokila* is the Indian cuckoo, whose voice is an image of sweet singing.

**96.** His body covered with bristling hairs, washed by streams of falling tears, His limbs drenched with waves of sweat, with the eight states of ecstasy and with complete rapture, the Lord — the precious jewel in the crown of the host of unrestrained dancers —

*Note.* Verses 96–97 are a *yugmaka* (marked as a pair in the edition). The eight states are the *aṣṭa-sāttvika-bhāvas*: stupefaction, perspiration, bristling of the hairs, faltering of the voice, trembling, change of colour, tears and fainting. The text has *nartana* ("dance") where verse 90 has *nartaka* ("dancer"); we translate as in verse 90.

**97.** — lovely with the lustre of the pair of His red lips, like young shoots trembling in the wind of His vehement breathing, charming with the varied lustre of His lips washed by the rays of His teeth — shone with a dance full of play.

**98.** Having danced thus, He, rich in skilful playfulness, sat down there on a clean, excellent seat made lovely by a new blanket, and sweetly spoke to the exceedingly fortunate Srivasa Pandita:

**99.** "'Sri' is just this — devotion to Vishnu; and you are its abode (*vāsa*), its dwelling: devotion to Vishnu shines in you. [So] you are invested with the sweet name 'Srivasa'." Afterwards He said to Murari: "Recite [your] poetry!"

*Note.* The etymology of the name: *śrī* (here *viṣṇu-bhakti*) + *vāsa* ("abode").

**100.** And he recited many poems of his own composition, singing of the qualities, form and pastimes of Sri Raghavendra. Having thus heard the eight verses on Raghunandana, the lion among kings, [the Lord] then placed His foot on his head.

*Note.* Murari Gupta is a devotee of Rama (cf. Chaitanya-charitamrita, Madhya 15.137–157). He himself described his "Ramashtaka" and this blessing in the *Kadacha* (Murari 2.7.6–18); according to the Chaitanya-charitamrita, the Lord called him "Ramadasa". The fourth line has a syllable too many: the metre requires *mūrdhni* (*मूर्द्ध्नि*) for *मूर्द्धनि* (?).

**101.** "O [Murari], by My grace be you 'Ramadasa'!" — and He wrote on his forehead this very word of four syllables. Then Mahaprabhu, charming in form, the boundless ocean of mercy, sweetly recited a verse:

*Note.* *Rā-ma-dā-sa* — four syllables; "Ramadasa" means "servant of Rama". Murari is a devotee of Rama (cf. verse 100).

"Neither yoga nor Sankhya nor dharma, O Uddhava, neither study of the Vedas nor austerity nor renunciation wins Me over as does devotion to Me that has grown strong."

*Note.* The metre is *anuṣṭubh*. Srimad-Bhagavatam 11.14.20. Unnumbered in the edition.

**102.** Having thus sweetly recited [the verse], He, His lips bathed in the rays of His teeth, then said to the brahmanas who had come: "Whatever Srivasa says here — when and how he says it — that from now on you must strictly do."

*Note.* A play on words: *dvija* is "twice-born" (a brahmana) and "tooth" (which is born twice).

**103.** Then He said to Sri Rama Pandita: "Always render this service to your elder brother, for it is service to Me Myself. By this may everything turn out for your good." Speaking thus, He shone, the moon of His face beautiful.

**104.** Having partaken there of the milk, betel nuts and garlands offered by Srivasa Pandita, Bhagavan, the ocean of mercy, His face gracious with kindness, then gave the remnants to those devotees of His lotus feet.

**105.** Thus He spent the whole night — He, the moon-disc of whose sweet face shines with the radiance of millions of moons. And when the hot-rayed [sun] rose, they bowed to Mahaprabhu and went home, each to his own.

**106.** And then those great souls, for whom He alone is the great remedy of life, made lovely by bathing in the divine river and having performed worship, came each in his turn from the shrine of the Deity, again to see His lotus feet.

*Note.* The divine river is the Ganga.

**107.** Then Mahaprabhu, seeing them assembled, said very steadily, in a voice deep as a cloud's: "The wise avadhuta Nityananda has come here. Have you heard? How could we see Him?"

*Note.* An *avadhūta* is a wandering ascetic who has "shaken off" all conventions. The beginning of the episode of the meeting with Nityananda (Chaitanya-bhagavata, Madhya 3–4; Chaitanya-charitamrita, Adi 17.11).

**108.** "O Rama Pandita, Mukunda, Murari Gupta, Narayana! Quickly, set out from here in haste: that great soul, full of abundant fortune, is here. Go and bring Him, glorious in His majesty!"

*Note.* Narayana is probably the same "foremost of physicians" as in verse 44.

**109.** Thus commanded by Mahaprabhu, they went and searched the roads thoroughly, but did not see Him. Coming back together, they said: "He is not to be seen anywhere at all. Alas, what is to be done?"

**110.** Then Bhagavan said again: "Now He is not to be seen; you will see Him here in the evening. So go now each to your own home, and then come here." "Very well," [they said,] and went home.

**111.** Then in the evening, walking along the road with them, the merciful [Lord] saw the physician Murari and calmly said: "That avadhuta is in the house of Nandana Acharya. Go there quickly and bring Him here."

*Note.* In the Chaitanya-bhagavata (Madhya 3), Nityananda, on arriving in Navadvipa, stayed in the house of Nandana Acharya.

**112.** Thus He came there and saw Nityananda, and Nityananda beheld the Lord face to face. And, bowing to Him, [the Lord] — lovelier than the moon, the ocean of the nectar of mercy — spoke sweetly, courteously and humbly, His voice modulated with feeling:

*Note.* After the second line the edition gives a variant in parentheses, *(पश्चात्)* — probably for *sākṣāt*. *Kāku* is a modulation of the voice that expresses feeling.

**113.** "On earth You are an ocean of incomparable, great glory — [and yet] You dry up the ocean of samsara. Bringing joy to all the races of embodied beings, You utterly throw into turmoil the hearts of the godless.

*Note.* A paradox: an ocean that dries up an ocean.

**114.** "Though You have abandoned the multitude of people, the pair of Your lotus feet is always wholly the refuge of all people. In this world You constantly take refuge in detachment — and yet a great, unceasing love is seen [in You]."

*Note.* The figure *virodhābhāsa* ("apparent contradiction"): *vairāgya* is "detachment", literally "absence of *rāga*", and at the same time a great *rāga* (love for Krishna) is seen. *Pravirata* (literally "ceased") is unclear here; perhaps another play on words. The Gosvami edition (GGM) has the same text.

**115.** Having spoken thus, He again performed sankirtana and dancing together with the devotees of His lotus feet; and there, with the dust of the avadhuta's feet, He made the heads of His own people utterly, supremely pure.

**116.** Going thus along the road with those [devotees], the son of Shachi spoke about that supreme avadhuta: "First comes knowledge, then devotion, then detachment — so He [Himself, by His life,] declares. Therefore He indeed is supreme."

*Note.* The sense of the second half is unclear. Cf. Srimad-Bhagavatam 11.2.42: *bhaktiḥ pareśānubhavo viraktiḥ*. In the edition this verse is mistakenly numbered "117" (the next one is also numbered 117). The Gosvami edition (GGM) has the same text.

**117.** Having reflected thus, the ocean of compassion on the next day diligently busied Himself with a meal for Him [Nityananda]. When He had been well fed, the Lord then anointed each of His limbs with sandal, kunkuma and the rest, and rejoiced.

*Note.* *Bhikṣā* — alms; a meal offered to a sannyasi or an avadhuta.

**118.** On another day this divine one, in the attire of an avadhuta, came to the house of Srivasa, very hungry. The foremost of brahmanas [Srivasa] rejoiced, invited Him, gave Him a meal, and afterwards anointed Him with sandal.

*Note.* *Bhagavān* here refers to Nityananda.

**119.** Having eaten, He [Nityananda] rested there. And there too appeared the treasury of compassion: coming into the shrine, the Lord took an excellent seat, made charming by a fine blanket, and shone.

**120.** Then that moon, Gaura, said to the avadhuta: "Look, look at Me, since You have taken the trouble [to come]!" Thus addressed, He saw nothing [special] in His [the Lord's] body: the great soul did not understand it at all.

*Note.* The division of *naiva tad abuddha* is unclear. We take *na eva* with *abuddha* and the great soul (*mahānubhāvaḥ*) as Nityananda: "did not understand it at all"; then *jñātvā* in verse 121 refers to the Lord, who "understood this". One may also take *mahānubhāvaḥ* as the Lord, who "understood this" already here. The verse prepares verses 121–122.

**121.** Understanding this, the most compassionate [Lord] then said to those [present]: "Go outside quickly, all of you." And when they had gone, He [Nityananda] then saw in His [the Lord's] body a most mighty radiance, [like that] of hundreds of crores of suns.

**122.** Having at once beheld that form of His — first supremely beautiful with six arms, and then sweet with the supreme grace of four arms — He swiftly, suddenly [beheld] again that wondrous two-armed [form], and then contemplated it again and again.

*Note.* The metre is *śikhariṇī* (17 syllables). The revelation of the *ṣaḍ-bhuja* (the six-armed form) to Nityananda — Chaitanya-bhagavata, Madhya 5.92–94: in the hands are the conch, disc, mace, lotus, plough and pestle (the form of Vishnu and Balarama); Nityananda loses consciousness. The iconography of "the arms of Rama, Krishna and Gaura" is not given by the Chaitanya-bhagavata here. In Karnapura the six-armed form is followed by the four-armed and the two-armed forms.

**123.** Having thus beheld all this — this supremely enchanting, very sweet nectar of the form of the ocean of mercy — Nityananda, immensely delighted, His body flooded by an ocean of happiness, danced wildly, given to clapping His hands from excess of happiness, and laughed loudly.

*Note.* The metre is *śikhariṇī*. The edition has *संप्लावितनुः*; we read *saṁplāvita-tanuḥ*.

Thus ends the sixth sarga, named "The Gathering of the Devotees", in the mahakavya "Sri Krishna-Chaitanya-charitamrita".

---

## Sarga Seven — verses 1–107

**Title of the sarga:** none — the colophon of the edition gives no title for this sarga.
**Metre:** *pramitākṣarā* (12 syllables per pada: ⏑⏑— ⏑—⏑ ⏑⏑— ⏑⏑—); verse 98 — *śārdūla-vikrīḍita*; verses 99 and 102 — *upajāti*; verse 100 — *praharṣiṇī*; verse 101 — *vasanta-tilakā*; verses 103–106 — *śālinī*; verse 107 — *sragdharā*.
**Total verses:** 107 (plus an unnumbered quotation after verse 100).

**Contents of the passage:**
- 1–2 — at night Gaura weeps in His sleep; Shachi asks: "Why are You weeping?" — "I have seen the Sweet One."
- 3–12 — a description of Krishna as seen in the dream, from the peacock feathers to the hem of His garment; Shachi's joy.
- 13–20 — some days later, in the house of "the best of brahmanas" (cf. 6.118–123), Gaura reveals to Nityananda the six-armed form (conch, disc, club; flute, lotus, bow), then the four-armed and the two-armed; Nityananda showers Him with flowers and dances.
- 21–24 — Nityananda's dance; He is Balarama.
- 25–29 — the Lord sends the devotees with Nityananda to Kamalaksha (Advaita, "Rudra"); Advaita exults on hearing of the six-armed form.
- 30–33 — Advaita comes and roars like a lion; the Lord reveals His splendour in the shrine of Srivasa ("Narada"); worship, the distribution of gifts.
- 34–37 — liberation is insignificant for the devotees; a night of dancing, the morning bath.
- 38–40 — the beginning of the rains: the clouds cover the moon and the sun, put to shame by Gaura.
- 41–47 — pictures of the rains: the clouds weep for joy, the lotuses hide, the clouds are a parasol over the dancing Gaura, the earth grows soft and turns red from His feet.
- 48–49 — Haridasa comes; the Lord embraces him and bids that he be given a seat of honour.
- 50–52 — Haridasa sits on the ground; the Lord anoints and feeds him; Haridasa dances in the Lord's house.
- 53–56 — Advaita is sent home; Nityananda receives garments as *prasāda* and distributes them among the devotees.
- 57–62 — the evening kirtana; Nityananda whirls "like a wheel", rolls on the ground and disappears together with Srivasa; the search.
- 63–69 — Gaura appears saddened; dancing; play with the devotees' garments; Nityananda dances — the Lord bids [them] drink the water from His feet.
- 70–75 — the joy of the gods; Haridasa's dance; the Lord performs *pūjā* to Advaita and gives Him mahaprasada.
- 76–78 — a wandering mendicant with his son sees Gaura dancing and falls unconscious.
- 79–82 — **Nrisimha**: after the *śrāddha* Srivasa recites the "Thousand Names"; Gaura, club in hand, runs to him; the people flee.
- 83–85 — the Lord calms down: "Have I wronged the people?" — "This is only the greatness of Your mercy."
- 86–90 — a singer of songs about Shiva; Gaura dances on his shoulders like Shiva; Srivasa and Mukunda recite hymns to Shiva.
- 91–93 — a brahmana woman takes the dust of His feet — Gaura throws Himself into the Ganga; Nityananda pulls Him out.
- 94–97 — the lament of the devotees; the night at a brahmana's house; in the morning, the crossing of the Ganga; He is brought home.
- 98–102 — "If I leave My mother…"; Murari: "No one will blame [You]"; Murari recites the verse of Sudama (Srimad-Bhagavatam 10.81.16); the Lord reveals His majesty: "This body is *sac-cid-ānanda*."
- 103–106 — Gaura is bathed again with the waters of the Ganga; He Himself sheds tears; kirtana.
- 107 — Gaura with the devotees cleanses the world of false knowledge and sweeps the temples.

**1.** On another day, at night, as He lay asleep, He wept unrestrainedly and very loudly. Seeing her son in such a state, Shachi in fear spoke to the sole Lord of the world:

*Note.* Cf. the beginning of Sarga Five (verse 2): the same motif of weeping at night, now with the cause explained.

**2.** "Why then are You weeping, dear child?" she said. And He then answered His mother thus: "O Mother, overwhelmed by sleep, I have seen Him — the Sweet One, the Sweet One!"

**3.** "[He had] the lustre of a bank of fresh dark-blue clouds and was adorned with fresh peacock feathers; a mass of thick, lush, very curling, spreading locks overspread His forehead;

*Note.* Verses 3–10 are a portrait of Krishna "from head to foot" (*keśādi-pādānta*), all one sentence in the nominative ("He was…"). *Nīlakaṇṭha* ("blue-throated") is the peacock. The edition has *प्रसरत-*; the metre requires *prasṛta*.

**4.** He bore the charm of the bees circling about, intoxicated by the nectar of His crest-garland of clustered celestial flowers; the creepers of His sweet eyebrows played languidly, and His pair of eyes extended to His very ears;

*Note.* The edition has *सुरसुन*; the metre requires *sura-sūna* ("celestial flowers"). *Cilli* — eyebrow (late Sanskrit).

**5.** His lotus eyes were red as the dawn; joyful, He was sweet with dense, deep waves of glances; by the curves of His lovely sidelong glances He was Madana to the whole world; the circles of His cheeks, touched by a smile, gleamed like mirrors;

*Note.* *Madana* is Kamadeva, the god of love; *jagan-madana*, "intoxicating the world", i.e. Madana-mohana. The edition has *प्रमदो*; possibly *pramada* is part of the next word (?). The Gosvami edition (GGM) has the same text.

**6.** with His pair of ears, gleaming with the play of golden earrings, He stole the heart of the world; He was sweet with the nectar of the lustre of His sweet lips, which shone like a shoot of a fresh coral tree;

*Note.* *Kaḍamba* — "shoot, stalk".

**7.** His face was lovely with the blossom-cluster of the lustre of His flower-like teeth and with the redness of His shoot-like lips; His lovely chin was sweet with the sweetness of honey; on the slope of His neck, pure as a conch, He wore a necklace;

*Note.* A neck "like a conch" — with three folds, a mark of beauty.

**8.** His neck gleamed with the creeper of a necklace of many fresh pearls, the slopes of His shoulders gleamed; the expanse of His chest glittered with the shining, excellent Kaustubha fastened on a golden thread;

*Note.* The *Kaustubha* is the jewel on the chest of Vishnu/Krishna, obtained at the churning of the ocean. The edition has *परिकलृप्त*; we read *parikḷpta*.

**9.** sweet, with a broad, full, lovely chest made charming by the elegance of a fresh garland of celestial flowers; on His tender, mighty arms, which reached to His fine knees, gleamed excellent armlets and lovely bracelets;

*Note.* Arms reaching the knees (*ājānu-bāhu*) are a sign of a great man.

**10.** His waist could be spanned by a palm; the lovely, undulating curves of His belly and hips shone with grace; He was wrapped up to the navel in a golden cloth, whose shining hem graced the tips of His feet;

*Note.* An "undulating" belly — with three folds (*trivali*), a canon of beauty. The golden cloth is the *pītāmbara*, Krishna's yellow garment.

**11.** with the rays of His smile He bathed the whole circle of the directions; sweet with compassionate sidelong glances, [tender as a] lotus. Seeing Him so, I was suddenly overcome by complete bewilderment from the abundance of happiness.

*Note.* *Kamalaḥ* (masculine, "lotus") as an attribute is unusual; perhaps *komalaḥ* ("tender") should be read. As an adjective *kamala* also means "rose-coloured, lotus-hued" (cf. verse 18). The Gosvami edition (GGM) has the same text.

**12.** And now, overwhelmed, I weep again and again: I have plunged into an ocean of happiness." Hearing her son's words, Shachi at once rejoiced, the hair on her body standing on end.

**13.** And the Lord, with the torrents of tears from His eyes, seemed to bear two oceans on His chest. After some days He came to that excellent house of the best of brahmanas and shone [there].

*Note.* No subject is named in the second half; we take it, as the grammar suggests, to be the Lord (*prabhuḥ* of the first half), with *tad idam* (neuter accusative) going with the house. One may also understand Nityananda, who in verse 14 is "there" (*tatra*); Nityananda's name is not in the verse. "The best of brahmanas" is not named either; in 6.118 Srivasa is called "the foremost of brahmanas", and the revelation of 6.119–122 takes place in his house. Verses 14–22 evidently repeat and expand the episode at the end of Sarga Six (6.120–123): the revelation of the six-armed form to Nityananda. The Gosvami edition (GGM) has the same text.

**14.** And there the lord avadhuta, whose form is worthy of worship, who has washed away all the filth of the age of Kali, again [drank] the most beautiful nectar of the sweet form of the ocean of compassion —

*Note.* The sentence continues in verse 15.

**15.** drank it again and again with the cup of His eyes, and all that abundance [of beauty] did not reach the end of His thirst. And then [He beheld] Him, beautiful and six-armed: on the right He held the weapons — a conch, a disc and a gleaming club;

*Note.* *Dara* — the conch (*śaṅkha*).

**16.** and with His three other beautiful arms — a flute, a fine lotus and the Sharnga bow; [He was like] a young shoot of a tree of molten gold, reddened with compassion;

*Note.* The set of attributes differs from the later tradition (Chaitanya-bhagavata, Chaitanya-charitamrita: the bow and arrow of Rama, the flute of Krishna, the staff and waterpot of a sannyasi): in Karnapura they are the weapons of Vishnu (conch, disc, club, lotus, the Sharnga bow) and the flute of Krishna.

**17.** charming, with a string of pearls shining on His chest, which was radiant with the lustre of the excellent Kaustubha; His pair of cheeks lit up by makara-shaped earrings gleaming at the lobes of both ears;

*Note.* The *makara* is a mythical sea creature; makara earrings (*makara-kuṇḍala*) are an attribute of Vishnu/Krishna. The edition has *गण्डग्युमम्*, a misprint for *गण्डयुगम्*.

**18.** beautiful with His lovely conch-like neck shining with an excellent necklace of fresh blue gems, [like] a lotus; in a lovely garment golden as the rays of the newly risen sun, with the gleaming, heavy curves of His hips.

*Note.* Again *kamalam* as an attribute (cf. verse 11). The Gosvami edition (GGM) has the same text.

**19.** Seeing the ocean of compassion so, this lord avadhuta rejoiced immensely. After that, the enchanting form with four lovely arms, and then the two-armed one…

**20.** …having seen [these], the good-hearted [Nityananda], His mind filled with wonder, in private showered Him with a heap of flowers. And after that, His heart bursting with the weight of great joy, He began to dance.

*Note.* A play on *sumanāḥ* ("good-hearted") and *sumanas* ("flower"). Verses 19–20 are one sentence (*avalokya* governs both forms).

**21.** Tightly embracing His own people, the compassionate [Nityananda], who bestowed an abundance of joy on His own people, was utterly carried away by the art of dancing, from bathing in the river of nectar of the glorification of Hari.

**22.** He became joyful, regarding with an even mind the people of all three worlds as His own — He, by receiving a particle of the pollen of whose pair of lotus feet the gods are beside themselves [with happiness].

*Note.* The edition has *जपमात्मनः*, a misprint for *जनमात्मनः*.

**23.** He by whose mercy all people on earth always obtain varied fortune at once — how could human beings on earth take the measure of Him, the ocean of immense greatness?

*Note.* Verses 23–24 might be taken as Gaura's words; but by the construction (*atha… nijagāda* in 24cd) they are rather the author's, and Gaura's speech is verse 25.

**24.** — He who, with qualities worthy of honour, is renowned throughout the earth as Balarama. And then Gaura, the cool-rayed moon, immensely merciful and charming, said to His own people:

*Note.* Nityananda is Balarama, the elder brother of Krishna.

**25.** "This avadhuta is now going to the house of Kamalaksha Deva for a meal. Go together with Him, and before Him [Kamalaksha] proclaim the true greatness of this one [the avadhuta]."

*Note.* Kamalaksha is the worldly name of Advaita Acharya (Chaitanya-charitamrita, Adi 13.62 and elsewhere).

**26.** Coming together with Him [Nityananda] to Him who is known on earth as Rudra, and here, in a family of brahmanas, bears the name Kamalaksha, they made obeisances again and again, falling on the ground like rods.

*Note.* Advaita is an incarnation of Sadashiva (Rudra) and of Mahavishnu (Chaitanya-charitamrita, Adi 6).

**27.** Seeing Him, who had come down [to earth], they related to Him what the Lord had said. And He [Advaita], having heard of the six-armed and four-armed forms of Him who had assumed a golden body on the surface of the earth —

*Note.* Verses 27–28 form one sentence: *karuṇālayasya* ("of the abode of mercy", 28) goes with *vihita-gaura-tanoḥ*.

**28.** — [of Him], the abode of mercy, plunged into an ocean of happiness and rejoiced immensely. Then they, gladdened by the words of that report, having made [everything] known, drew near and bowed down.

*Note.* *Tan-nivedana-vacaḥ* may also be understood as His (Advaita's) words in reply to the report.

**29.** Taking the dust of the lotus feet of that abode of mercy, they followed His pair of feet. And on the next day this Kamalaksha Deva, whose virtues are not slight, came [to the Lord].

*Note.* The "abode of mercy" here is evidently Kamalaksha himself (in verse 28 the same epithet refers to Gaura). The Gosvami edition (GGM) has the same text.

**30.** And then, seeing Gaura, the moon, He [Advaita] became splendid with a roar like that of an intoxicated lion. And when He, the master Girisha, of qualities worthy of honour, had arrived here, the Lord of the worlds —

*Note.* *Girisha* ("lord of the mountain") is Shiva: Advaita as Shiva. The roar is Advaita's *huṅkāra*.

**31.** — the immensely kind moon of Gauda then at once revealed a manifest splendour in the shrine among the dwellings of him who is renowned on earth as Narada.

*Note.* Srivasa is an incarnation of Narada (Chaitanya-charitamrita, Adi 10.8; *Gaura-ganoddesha-dipika*). Gauda is Bengal.

**32.** Having then shown him the manifest splendour, the immensely merciful one bestowed happiness on him. And they, seeing Him in such a form, danced intensely, sang and rejoiced much; having worshipped Him with flowers, fruits, betel nuts and riches, with great happiness they bowed down on the ground like rods.

*Note.* The verse has six padas (so in the edition).

**33.** Having mercifully accepted their offerings from all sides, the compassionate [Lord] gave these very things — cloths and flowers — to them. Great is the mercy of the abode of mercy!

**34.** Thus they, absorbed in happiness, at once deemed very trifling even liberation, which the peoples of the three worlds eagerly seek in many ways and which is so hard to attain.

*Note.* *Apavarga* is liberation (moksha); cf. Srimad-Bhagavatam 3.29.13: pure devotees do not accept even liberation.

**35.** Those supreme [souls] knew neither day nor night, nor even happiness or sorrow. Did not liberation itself, though unwanted, serve them on earth, while they were still in their bodies, in the guise of their stupor?

*Note.* Stupor (*jaḍatā*, *stambha*) is one of the ecstatic symptoms; the poet compares it with the insensibility of one liberated in this life (*jīvan-mukta*).

**36.** Thus they danced, sang and rejoiced much until the rising of the sun; and having spent the whole night [so], at the beginning of the day they were overcome.

**37.** Then, at the beginning of the day, having bathed in the water of the heavenly river, they joyfully beheld the most tender feet of the Lord, the one supreme medicine for sustaining their life.

*Note.* The heavenly river is the Ganga.

**38.** And then, to behold this play of His dancing, did not the joyful cloud, full of a sweet, dense lustre, eagerly rise in the sky — suddenly, yet slowly?

*Note.* A play on *mudita* ("joyful") and *mudira* ("cloud"). Verses 38–40 are *utprekṣā* (poetic fancy): nature responds to Gaura. The rainy season begins.

**39.** "This moon, Gaura, sweet in radiance, shines on earth — what use is there of you now?" Was it [thinking] thus that the rising clouds at once wholly covered the moon in the sky?

**40.** Was it not from shame that the lord of the day, vanquished here by the great splendour of Gaurachandra, then sank deep into the dense rows of clouds in the sky?

**41.** The rising clouds embraced the quarters, very charmingly perfumed with the fresh fragrance and sap of blossoming kadambas, and in that very moment shed a flood of tears born of joy.

*Note.* The edition has *वासिनवतीः*; we read *vāsitavatīḥ* ("perfumed"), as the sense requires (?). The kadamba blooms in the rainy season; the quarters (*kakubh*, feminine) are the beloveds of the clouds. The Gosvami edition (GGM) has the same text.

**42.** "When the lotus of His feet, sweetest with the wine of mercy, shines on earth, what use is there here of such as we?" — [thinking] thus, this row of lotuses then sank in the lake.

*Note.* In the rainy season lotuses go under the water.

**43.** The clouds, with a gleaming lustre like the curling, thick, lovely tresses of doe-eyed women, took on with their swarms of lightning the charm of a tamala tree marked with blossoming ketakas.

*Note.* The tamala is a tree with dark bark (an image of Krishna); the ketaka is a fragrant flower with golden-white petals (the lightning on the cloud). The edition has *केतकाङ्कित तमालतरोः* as two words; we read them as a compound.

**44.** "When He dances lightly, contact with the earth greatly pains the lotus of His feet" — with this thought the clouds always made the earth soft with their waters.

**45.** When He, savouring the delight of the play of His shining lotus feet, joyfully performs His dance, at that very moment the clouds take on the beauty of a charming parasol over the one treasury of mercy.

*Note.* The parasol (*ātapatra*) is an emblem of royalty.

**46.** With the sweet radiance of His sprout-like feet, lustrous as a grove of fresh coral trees, He, the ocean of mercy, whose palms are reddish, made the earth red.

*Note.* Here *kadamba* means "multitude, grove" (cf. verse 6, where the edition has *कड़म्ब*).

**47.** By the splendour of Him dancing, His body golden as refined gold, the quarters, dark with the might of the clouds, their lustre mixed [with His], took on the blended lustre of musk and kunkuma.

*Note.* Musk is dark, kunkuma golden-red: the darkness of the clouds and the gold of Gaura. *Vārida-balavat-timirāḥ* is understood as "with darkness from the might of the clouds" (*balavat-timira* may also be simply "deep darkness"). The Gosvami edition (GGM) reads *militāḥ* ("blended", of the quarters).

**48.** Then, having come, the all-pervading Lord saw Haridasa — of a most exalted heart, with qualities most worthy of honour, a fine bee maddened by the honey of His own lotus feet — and rejoiced.

*Note.* Haridasa Thakura, the "namacharya" (Chaitanya-charitamrita, Antya 3, 11). *Āgataś ca* formally refers to the Lord; by the sense it may also be understood: "seeing that Haridasa had come".

**49.** At once tightly embracing him, the most devoted servant of His own lotus feet, the Lord of manifold mercy, by the grace of His eyes, mercifully had His own people bring an excellent seat.

*Note.* "By the grace of His eyes": by a sign of His glance.

**50.** But he, bowing, saluted that seat with his head and made his place on the bare ground his excellent seat, having taken, with a mind intent on devotion, a heap of pollen from the lotus feet of the Lord.

*Note.* Haridasa's humility: a Muslim by birth, he does not sit on the seat of honour (cf. Chaitanya-charitamrita, Antya 11).

**51.** Then the immensely merciful [Lord] anointed his body with sandal paste, placed a garland on his chest, and then fed him sweet food of four kinds — and rejoiced.

*Note.* The four kinds of food are those that are chewed, sucked, licked and drunk (*carvya*, *coṣya*, *lehya*, *peya*).

**52.** And he too, dancing after [the Lord] to the kirtana of Hari, constantly shone in the very house of the Lord; and Gaura, the moon, rejoiced, seeing him, the lover of His pair of feet.

*Note.* The edition has *निलयएव* as one word.

**53.** Then the all-pervading Lord, whose feet [are lit by] a line of lustre from the precious gems in the crowns of the host of gods, told Girisha, who had come there together with him [Haridasa], to go home at once.

*Note.* Girisha is Advaita (verse 30). The gods touch the Lord's feet with their crowns as they bow.

**54.** "So be it," [said] Girisha, He who is renowned on the face of the earth as Kamalaksha; and at the Lord's word He joyfully went home, [led] by the supreme word of the Lord of the world.
