**55.** Then, accompanying the avadhuta — the master, the ascetic who wished to depart — [the Lord] humbly said to Him: "O mighty one, accept this most great prasada, a spotless cloth."

**56.** Thus [addressed], He took one outer garment of them, and, showing compassion, the compassionate [Nityananda] gave [the rest] to those whose life's treasure is His feet — except Kamalaksha Deva.

*Note.* The edition reads *nijagāda* ("said"), which does not fit; the Gosvami edition (GGM) reads *nija-pāda-jīvana-dhanebhyaḥ*, "to those whose life's treasure is His feet", and so we read. Why Advaita is excepted is unclear (He has already gone home, verse 54).

**57.** Saluting that [gift] with their heads, they went with the master [Nityananda] to their own homes; they bathed in the waters of the heavenly river and performed worship according to the rules.

**58.** Then, towards evening, exceedingly jubilant, they came to the abode of the Supreme Lord. That master of the worlds [Nityananda] also came, with the best of His intimate friends, with a powerful splendour.

**59.** He, whose body is worthy of honour, performed with great splendour His own kirtana and dance. And then, whirling like a wheel, by the mass of splendour spreading from the play of His whirling He eclipsed the lustre of the delights of Ilavrita — He of the lovely face and the lovely nectar of words.

*Note.* The verse has six padas. On Ilavrita cf. 5.59. *Nija-kīrtana*, "His own kirtana", i.e. the kirtana of Nityananda. *Mahena* (from *maha*) may also be taken as "with a great festival".

**60.** In the intervals of dancing He tightly embraced His own people on all sides, and then, rolling [on the ground] together with them, [with] His pair of lotus hands [outspread], laughing aloud with joy, He shone forth like a lion.

*Note.* The edition has *करणाम्बुज-*, a syllable too many for the metre; we read *karāmbuja-* ("lotus hands") (?). The verse might be referred to the Lord, but verse 61 shows that it concerns Nityananda.

**61.** Having rolled about thus on the ground for a long time in the midst of the crowd of devotees, the best of lords took with Him that best of brahmanas who here on earth is Narada [Srivasa] and disappeared.

*Note.* That is, Nityananda disappeared together with Srivasa. The epithets *vibhuḥ* and *prabhu-varaḥ* could also denote the Lord; possibly it is the Lord Himself who disappears with Srivasa, as in Murari (2.10.12–13); cf. verse 63, where Gaura appears before the grieving devotees.

**62.** "He is not to be seen!" — and, greatly alarmed, they searched for Him here and there. Not finding Him anywhere, they became utterly bewildered under the weight of great sorrow.

**63.** Then the boundlessly compassionate, sweet Gaurachandra, understanding them to be piteous, their hearts in such a state, rose [before them like the moon], scattering the nectar of His most enchanting glance.

**64.** And from all sides they, as it were, insatiably drank with their eyes Him — the lotus-faced One, surrounded by a golden lustre, full of very deep joy, looking around at all with supreme mercy.

**65.** And after that the moon of the ocean of rasa danced again and again, in the rapture of the nectar of His own kirtana, together with them — overwhelmed by fervour, whose only wealth is the pair of His feet.

**66.** Then once, at the beginning of the night, the treasury of many pastimes, the ocean of happiness, pulled off all the garments of the devotees, frolicked [with them] all around, and gave them back again.

*Note.* An echo of Krishna's *vastra-haraṇa-līlā* (the stealing of the gopis' garments). The edition's *निधी* stands for *nidhiḥ* before *r* (sandhi).

**67.** After that the avadhuta, the master, came there again and, seeing Gaura in His dense radiance, from great happiness sang sweetly and danced.

**68.** And in an interval of the dancing Bhagavan, the Lord of the worlds, commanded His devotees: "Drink in turn the auspicious water that has washed the lotus feet of the avadhuta."

*Note.* *Pādodaka*, *caraṇāmṛta* — the water that has washed the feet of a saint.

**69.** Having heard this word, they bowed their heads and accepted the water of His feet. Those who live [by the mercy] of the lotus feet [of the Lord] are always devoted to His word.

**70.** Then with His speech, His playful gait, the sweetness of His glance softened by mercy, and His deep, sweet laughter, the compassionate [Lord] bestowed happiness on the people of the worlds.

**71.** Beholding the Supreme Lord always sporting thus, the hosts of the gods in the sky, together with the throngs of their beloveds, their minds intoxicated with rapture, joyfully pass their days and nights.

**72.** Haridasa, renowned as a lord, a supreme soul who holds dear the feet of Gaura, came again and danced before the Supreme Lord, the pair of his feet made charming by ankle-bells.

*Note.* *Īśvara* of Haridasa is an honorific "lord"; in the tradition he is an incarnation of Brahma and Prahlada (Gaura-ganoddesha-dipika 93–94). *Nūpura* — ankle-bracelets with little bells.

**73.** The lord Kamalaksha, who bestows happiness on the world, came again and, beholding the tender pair of the Lord's lotus feet, His mind flooded with an excess of joy, rejoiced immeasurably.

**74.** Then the best of lords worshipped Him [Advaita] with charming water for washing the feet, with dūrvā grass and handfuls of unbroken rice, with heaps of flowers and with sandalwood paste — and gave Him food.

*Note.* Thus Gaura worships Advaita according to the rite (*pūjā*): *pādya*, *dūrvā* and *akṣata* (unbroken rice), flowers, sandalwood. The food is the *mahā-prasāda* of verse 75.

**75.** Having joyfully accepted the mahaprasada with great awe and reverence, He then danced wildly together with the Lord in the ocean of happiness that is the nectar of Hari's kirtana.

**76.** Then a certain mendicant [wanderer] with an exceedingly dejected heart, accompanied by his son, beheld here something supreme in the dancing moon, Gaura, and swooned.

*Note.* The edition has *भिक्षुरेणुसङ्गतया*, against the metre; we read *bhikṣur anusaṅgatayā* ("accompanied [by his son]") (?). The Gosvami edition (GGM) has the same text.

**77.** Rising after a long time, he then said: "Oh, what have I seen? Oh, what [was it]?" And after that, joyful, he sang together with them with the charming art of song.

**78.** Thus this mendicant, immensely happy — his body seeming to double in size with the abundance of hairs standing on end, and constantly washed by the waters of his eyes — plunged wholly into the ocean of rasa.

**79.** Then once the one moon of the race of brahmanas, he who on earth is renowned as Narada, having performed the rite for the ancestors, recited the "Thousand Names" [of Vishnu], containing the great name.

*Note.* The last pada in the edition is a syllable short of the norm (11 instead of 12): the text is corrupt (?). *Paitra-kṛti* is the *śrāddha*, the memorial rite for the father. "The great name" (*bṛhat-pada*) — judging by verse 80, the name of Nrisimha (?). The episode: Chaitanya-bhagavata, Madhya 2 — hearing the name of Nrisimha in the "Vishnu-sahasra-nama", Gaura enters the mood of Nrisimha. The Gosvami edition (GGM) has the same text.

**80.** While staying in His own house, Bhagavan heard the name of Nrihari and, magnified by radiance, assumed the mood of Narasimha; and then the best of men went to his house.

*Note.* Nrihari, Narasimha — Vishnu in the form of the Man-lion. The house of Gaura and the house of Srivasa are close to each other.

**81.** Taking a huge club in His lotus hands and assuming an unbearable radiance, He ran along the road, shaking the surface of the earth with the blows of His moving lotus feet.

*Note.* The edition has *पदाम्बुजबलद्दलनैः*; we read *valad-* ("moving"; *b* and *v* are often confused) (?). The Gosvami edition (GGM) has the same text.

**82.** Then, seeing Him in such a state — running along the road with blazing swiftness — the people, feeling intense fear and extremely agitated, hastily fled in all directions.

**83.** But seeing those people given to flight, He therefore regained His composure here, put aside that huge club, and then went slowly to his house.

**84.** Having arrived there, the Lord, His heart softened from having seen the flight of the people, then said: "Am I then forever an offender toward those people?"

**85.** "O Bhagavan, there can by no means be any offence anywhere on the part of You, the Lord, the remover of all offences! This [seeming] offence is truly only the greatness of Your compassion," His own people said.

**86.** And on another day, before this ocean of compassion, a certain fine singer sang with the highest joy a song about Shiva, in the hope of the mercy of this compassionate Lord.

*Note.* The episode: Chaitanya-bhagavata, Madhya 8 — Gaura climbs onto the shoulders of a singer singing about Shiva and dances in the mood of Shiva.

**87.** Having heard the charming song, with its words of very steady tread, Bhagavan was delighted; and, climbing onto the gleaming slope of his shoulders, He danced like Dhurjati.

*Note.* *Dhūrjaṭi* ("having heavy matted locks") is Shiva. *Pada* means both "word" and "step".

**88.** His eyes rolling from intoxication, exceedingly beautiful with abundant hairs standing on end, radiant, the ocean of compassion then climbed onto his shoulders and danced like Shiva.

**89.** He who on earth is renowned as Narada recited there a hymn of praise to Girisha, and lo, the very sweet-voiced physician Mukunda sang here the hymn of praise "Mahimna".

*Note.* The "Shiva-mahimna-stotra" (of Pushpadanta) is a famous hymn to Shiva. Mukunda Datta was by caste a *vaidya* (physician). *Giriśa* ("lord of the mountain") is Shiva.

**90.** After that He left the place on his shoulders and swiftly took the best seat there, constantly delighting His own people like the moon, the friend of the night lotuses.

*Note.* *Kumuda* — white night lotuses, which open in the moonlight.

**91.** Then on another day, when the dancing had ended, a certain most excellent brahmana's wife approached, fell on the ground before [Him], and eagerly took the dust of His lotus feet.

*Note.* The episode — Murari 2.11.19–25 (the same: a brahmana woman takes the dust from the feet of the Lord as He lies [on the ground], and He throws Himself into the Ganga). In the Chaitanya-bhagavata (Madhya 17.30–47) the Lord throws Himself into the Ganga for another reason — lamenting that He is deprived of prema (because of Advaita's "trick"); Nityananda and Haridasa pull Him out, and He spends the night in the house of Nandana Acharya.

**92.** Seeing what she had done, He at once bore the weight of great sorrow; and then, extremely distraught, He swiftly ran out and threw Himself into the waters of the heavenly river.

*Note.* The edition has *बहुःदुखभरम्*, a misprint.

**93.** Seeing Him in such a state, the mighty divine avadhuta, distraught, at once pulled Gaura, the Lord, out of the water with great strength, with His pair of mighty arms.

*Note.* The second pada in the edition (*सममुद्धार परसोऽतिबलात्*) breaks the metre; *paraso* is probably a misprint for *payaso* ("from the water"); the verbal form is corrupt: the Gosvami edition (GGM) reads *samam uddadhāra*, and so we read.

**94.** Haridasa and the other companions at once ran up and, extremely distressed, surrounded the most compassionate [Lord]; in fear, with faltering voices, they wept.

**95.** The boundlessly compassionate [Lord] came together with them to the house of Murari Gupta and shone [there]; then He went on again to the house of a brahmana, and there the compassionate [Lord] spent the night.

*Note.* Which brahmana is meant is not specified; the Gosvami edition (GGM) has the same text. In the Chaitanya-bhagavata (see the note to verse 91) the Lord spends the night in the house of Nandana Acharya.

**96.** On the next day, at the hour of dawn, Bhagavan crossed the heavenly river together with them and came to the opposite bank; and there, His heart distraught, He rested only for a moment.

*Note.* *Uttara* means "opposite" or "northern".

**97.** Then, in great fear, they consoled Him again and again with entreaties, and joyfully brought the Lord home: for He is truly subject to the feeling of those who serve Him.

*Note.* The edition has *विलयैः*; we read *vinayaiḥ* ("with entreaties").

**98.** Coming again to the house of Srivasa, the Lord, Sri Gaurachandra, spoke in firm words before all who were listening: "If I leave My mother and go away somewhere, then all these people will surely say this: 'That wretch has done wrong.'"

*Note.* The metre is *śārdūla-vikrīḍita*. The first thought of departure (*sannyāsa*); cf. Sarga Six, verse 74. *Asakau* is a disparaging "this one".

**99.** Then Murari Gupta, having heard His speech, as if sprinkled with nectar, said: "O Lord, no one here will say such things about You, whose lotus feet bestow love."

*Note.* The metre is *upajāti* (11 syllables). *Bhavatsu* is the plural of respect.

**100.** Having heard such words, the ocean of mercy, delighted and filled with supreme happiness, embraced him. And he, his body thus pierced by a multitude of hairs standing on end, his heart joyful, recited in humility a verse:

*Note.* The metre is *praharṣiṇī*.

"Where am I, a poor sinner…" and so on.

*Note.* The edition gives only the beginning. This is Srimad-Bhagavatam 10.81.16 (the words of Sudama): *kvāhaṁ daridraḥ pāpīyān kva kṛṣṇaḥ śrī-niketanaḥ / brahma-bandhur iti smāhaṁ bāhubhyāṁ parirambhitaḥ* — "Where am I, a poor sinner, and where is Krishna, the abode of Sri! And yet He embraced me with His arms, [seeing in me] a brahmana, though only by birth."

**101.** Having heard what was thus said, Bhagavan, the Lord, at once assumed His own supreme majesty and, seated upon a charming seat, shone with a mighty mass of effulgence, equal to a thousand suns.

*Note.* The metre is *vasanta-tilakā*.

**102.** "Know that this body of Mine is supreme, enchanting, made of concentrated being, consciousness and bliss; apart from it there is nothing else on earth," He said.

*Note.* The metre is *upajāti*. *Sac-cid-ānanda-ghana* is the formula of the divine body (cf. Brahma-samhita 5.1: *sac-cid-ānanda-vigrahaḥ*).

**103.** Having heard these words of the Lord, Srivasa and the others were delighted; their limbs all over were covered with rising, bristling hairs, and, thoroughly bathed by streams of tears, they remained thus right where they were.

*Note.* The metre is *śālinī* (11 syllables), up to verse 106 inclusive.

**104.** And Srivasa, whose sole support is His feet, again, as before, bathed with the most pure waters of the Ganga Sri Gauranga, beautiful in all His limbs, washed by the waters of love.

*Note.* "As before" — the *mahābhiṣeka* in Sarga Five (verses 38–46).

**105.** As much of the waters of the Ganga as this brahmana poured from jars on the slender stem of Gaurachandra's body, so much water, flowing forth from love, did He pour out from the lotuses of His eyes on His own body.

*Note.* *Bhūsura* ("a god on earth") is a brahmana, here Srivasa. *Aṅga-yaṣṭi* ("the stem of the body") — a slender, graceful figure.

**106.** Seeing such a wonder again, they — maddened by love, their hearts intoxicated, they whose only support is love for Sri Gauranga — performed kirtana and dancing louder and louder.

**107.** On another day Gaurachandra, whose deeds are extremely wondrous, who is incessantly contemplated by Brahma, Shiva and the others, spreading the teaching of devotion together with His own people, cleansed everything everywhere of false knowledge and the rest — and with a good broom He constantly swept clean the abodes of the deities in every town, becoming renowned for this in the world.

*Note.* The metre is *sragdharā* (21 syllables). A twofold cleansing: of the world from false knowledge (*kujñāna*), and of the temples with a broom; cf. the later cleansing of the Gundicha temple (Chaitanya-charitamrita, Madhya 12), where the broom is an image of the cleansing of the heart. The syntax of *kujñānādyaiḥ* (instrumental instead of ablative) is not entirely clear (?). The Gosvami edition (GGM) has the same text.

*Note.* The colophon of the edition gives no title for the sarga.

Thus ends the seventh sarga in the mahakavya "Sri Krishna-Chaitanya-charitamrita".

---

## Sarga Eight — verses 1–63

**Title of the sarga:** *Śrī-vṛndāvana-vihāra-varṇana*, "The Description of the Pastimes in Vrindavana" (from the colophon).
**Metre:** *anuṣṭubh* (*śloka*); verse 61 — *śikhariṇī*; verses 62–63 — *vasanta-tilakā*.
**Total verses:** 63.

**Contents of the passage:**
- 1–9 — a leper begs to be healed; the Lord refuses: "You hated Srivasa"; the Vaishnavas are His life-airs moving outside [the body].
- 10–16 — a brahmana who was not admitted to the kirtana breaks his sacred thread and curses Gaura: "May Your samsara be cut off!"; the Lord rejoices at the curse as at a blessing.
- 17–26 — Gaura in the mood of **Balarama**: "Give [Me] honey!"; He turns white like a mountain of silver; with a plough, in a blue garment; in the house of Murari Gupta.
- 27–36 — He hurls away a strong brahmana; the days of absorption; "I have seen a man with a plough, like a mountain of silver"; Chandrashekhara Acharya sees Balarama in Gaura.
- 37–46 — the dance in the mood of Balarama; an intoxicating fragrance, swarms of bees; Sri Rama [Pandita] sees Balarama's associates descended from the sky; Vanamali sees a golden plough as large as a mountain.
- 47–55 — a rain of flowers from the gods; dawn; bathing and water-play in the Ganga.
- 56–60 — "Give Me the flute!" — "The gopis are guarding it"; the Lord in bliss: "Speak, speak!" — Srivasa tells of the pastimes in Vrindavana and on the Yamuna.
- 61–63 — Srivasa's speech on Krishna's ever-new pastimes in Vrindavana; "Speak more!"

**1.** Then once, as the Supreme Lord was walking [along the road], someone humbly bowed down to Him with affection and spoke to Him in sweet words:

**2.** "All call You the Lord of the gods of gods, the Supreme Person whose body is concentrated being and consciousness. Why then do You not deliver me?

**3.** O All, O Lord of all, save me from this utterly loathsome leprosy! My heart is tormented — show mercy, O treasure-house of mercy!"

*Note.* This is the same leper, a hater of Srivasa, as in Sarga Six, verse 73 (by tradition, Gopala Chapala; Chaitanya-charitamrita, Adi 17.37–58).

**4.** Having heard these words of his, the beautiful [Lord], adorned with the king of the twice-born (the moon) in the guise of His face, spoke, His eyes reddened with anger:

*Note.* *Dvija-rāja*, "king of the twice-born", is both the moon (which is "born twice", rising anew) and the best of brahmanas. Gaura's face is the moon.

**5.** "Ah, O sinful soul, O evil-doer who harbour hatred for My devotees! If I were to deliver you, then whom would I not deliver?

*Note.* The edition has *kiṁ tataḥ* ("what of it?"), and the line is obscure; the Gosvami edition (GGM) reads *kaṁ tataḥ*, "whom then [would I not deliver]", and so we read.

**6.** Since you have always harboured hatred towards Srivasa, you will therefore certainly be a leper in every birth.

**7.** The life-airs that are in this body are never visible; but know that the Vaishnavas are My life-airs, moving, as it were, outside [the body].

*Note.* *Bahiś-cara prāṇa*, "life moving outside the body": the devotees are the very life of the Lord (cf. Srimad-Bhagavatam 9.4.68: *sādhavo hṛdayaṁ mahyam*). The edition has *लक्ष्या* without the visarga.

**8.** Whoever, in whatever way, hates those dear ones of Mine — each of them will fall into hell in every birth.

**9.** Those who bow down before the Vaishnavas and are devoted to their commands — they alone will cross the fearsome ocean of samsara."

**10.** Having spoken thus, Mahaprabhu went to the house of Srivasa, and there Bhagavan, who is devoted to His devotees, took delight together with him.

**11.** Once, at the time of the dancing, a brahmana set out to see the beautiful Gauranga, but was stopped by the doorkeeper.

*Note.* The kirtanas in Srivasa's house were held behind closed doors (Chaitanya-bhagavata, Madhya 8 and 26; Chaitanya-charitamrita, Adi 17.41).

**12.** On another day this foul-mouthed [brahmana], [still] angry, saw the Lord of the world there, near the bank of the Ganga, and turned red with fury.

**13.** Breaking his sacred thread in two, about to pronounce a curse, he said this: "Once I went to see You at the time of the dancing,

*Note.* Breaking the sacred thread (*upavīta*) is the gesture of a brahmana's curse (cf. Chaitanya-charitamrita, Adi 17.33–35: "May Your worldly life be destroyed!").

**14.** and, stopped by Your very own doorkeeper, I was grieved by it. Therefore I give You a curse: let Your samsara be cut off!"

*Note.* A double meaning: "may Your worldly (household) life be ruined" — and "may Your round of rebirth cease" (liberation). Cf. Chaitanya-charitamrita, Adi 17.34.

**15.** Hearing that, the Lord, Bhagavan, rejoiced in His heart again and again, delighted [at the thought]: "The curse of the angry brahmana has become a blessing!"

*Note.* A foreshadowing of *sannyāsa*: the "cutting off of samsara" is the leaving of home.

**16.** Whoever hears thus of the curse [laid] on Hari is freed from a brahmana's curse. Therefore this should be heard with faith by people of pure intelligence.

*Note.* A *phala-śruti*, "the fruit of hearing".

**17.** On the next day the sun, removing with its ray-hand the seal from the lotuses, rose from the mountain of sunrise like a lover from his bed.

*Note.* At night the lotuses are closed, as if sealed; *kara* means both "ray" and "hand". *Padminī* is both "a lotus pond" and a type of beautiful woman.

**18.** Then Gaurangachandra too, teaching many virtuous brahmanas, shone like the full nectar-rayed moon.

**19.** Suddenly the Lord, overcome by agitation, His whole body staggering, His face sweet, began to say: "Give [Me] honey, give!"

*Note.* *Madhu* is honey and also an intoxicating drink (*vāruṇī*), beloved of Balarama. The episode: Murari 2.14; Chaitanya-charitamrita, Adi 17.115–120 ("Give Me honey!" — Nityananda brings Ganga water; Chandrashekhara, *ācārya-śekhara*, sees Balarama in the Lord; Vanamali sees the golden plough). In the Chaitanya-bhagavata there are similar scenes — Madhya 3.145–162 and 5.37–50 ("Bring wine!"; the Lord asks Nityananda for the plough and the club) — but not in the house of Murari.

**20.** Wonderful, wonderful is this deed of the Supreme Soul: He, golden as a mountain of gold, became like a mountain of silver!

*Note.* Balarama is white; Gaura "turns white", taking on His mood. The edition has *रोप्याचल*; we read *raupya-*.

**21.** [They saw Him] with a plough in His hand, His body adorned with a blue garment, His eyes rolling, His movements those of one inebriated with intoxication —

*Note.* The plough (*sīra*, *hala*) and the blue garment are attributes of Balarama (*Haladhara*, *Nīlāmbara*). Verses 21–22 form one sentence.

**22.** — thus at that very moment they all, filled with joy, saw the Supreme Lord assuming the mood of the son of Rohini.

*Note.* The son of Rohini is Balarama. The edition has *दधानां*; we read *dadhānam*.

**23.** Then Mahaprabhu, extremely eager, went together with all the chanting people to the house of Murari Gupta.

**24.** There too He said in sweet words, "Give [Me] honey, give!" — and, taking a vessel of water in His hand, He drank the water eagerly.

*Note.* The edition has *उक्ताम्बुपात्रं*; we read *uktvāmbu-pātram* (the sandhi of *uktvā* + *ambu*).

**25.** His restless eyes rolling from intoxication, beautiful as the lord of the night, He danced, bringing coolness to the house with white rays [like the moon].

*Note.* The edition has *घूर्न्नित* and *शैत्यं* ("coolness"); the Gosvami edition (GGM) has the same — therefore we keep *śaityam* (rather than *śauklyam*, "whiteness"): the Lord, like the moon, gives coolness.

**26.** "I am not that Krishna who is quickly satisfied by words. So bring, bring — offer [Me] plenty of honey now!"

*Note.* The sense is unclear (?). Probably Gaura, as Balarama, says: "I am not Krishna, for whom it is enough to be glorified — I want the intoxicating drink." The Gosvami edition (GGM) has the same text.

**27.** Having said this, the Lord hurled away a certain brahmana with one hand, and he fell far off, though he was a very strong wrestler.

*Note.* The edition has *इत्युनक्त्वैकेन*, a misprint.

**28.** Overpowered from the very morning by His absorption in Balarama, the lotus-eyed [Lord] would come to Himself only at nightfall — and then He would take His bath.

*Note.* *Āveśa*, literally "entering", is the state in which another form of the Lord manifests in Gaura.

**29.** On another day Bhagavan, blazing with an utterly unbearable effulgence, His mass of hair dishevelled, swooned again and again.

**30.** Him — beautiful in His absorption in Baladeva, moving with the gait of a maddened elephant, exulting like a maddened lion, His eyes rolling from intoxication,

*Note.* Verses 30–32 form one sentence.

**31.** His cheeks reddening, His radiance equal to that of millions of hot-rayed suns, His heart stirred by agitation — seeing Him thus, they then said:

**32.** "What is this, O Lord? What is this guise, what this supreme splendour? Tell [us] what the cause of it is — for You are Bhagavan, the creator of all!"

*Note.* The edition writes *कोवायं* as one word. *Bhagavān* is nominative where the vocative *bhagavan* would be expected; the metre is the same either way. We take it as "[You are] Bhagavan"; it may also be understood as an address. The Gosvami edition (GGM) has the same text.

**33.** Thus He, charming in the play of His absorption in Balarama, reeling with intoxication, spoke words that were an abode of charm, faltering again and again:

*Note.* *Lalitāspadam* (neuter) may be taken with *vacanam*, "words that were an abode of charm", or, as a noun in apposition, with the Lord: "He, the abode of charm".

**34.** "I have seen a man with a plough in His hand, wearing a blue garment, splendid like a mountain of silver — someone [wondrous], [seen] here [only] by such as Me."

*Note.* The end of the verse (*mādṛśair iha*) is unclear; the translation is tentative. The Gosvami edition (GGM) has the same text.

**35.** Then Sri Chandrashekhara Acharyaratna said to Him: "O Lord, where is that best of the strong whom You have seen?"

*Note.* Chandrashekhara Acharya (Acharyaratna) is Gaura's maternal uncle. The cases in the first line look confused: *ratnam* and *tam* are both accusative in form, and *ratnaḥ … tam* would be expected. Since *ratna* is a neuter noun, however, *-ratnam* may also be the nominative ("Chandrashekhara, the jewel among acharyas"), with *tam*, "to Him", as the object of *jagāda*. The Gosvami edition (GGM) has the same text.

**36.** Even as he spoke thus, he saw that very [one] — beautiful Gaura, bearing the appearance of the Lord, the Plough-bearer, in absorption upon absorption.

*Note.* *Āveśāveśa* is probably a dittography (a misprint). The Gosvami edition (GGM) has the same text.

**37.** Then splendid Gauranga, more beautiful than millions of moons, having assumed that mood, danced together with all of them, their hearts full of joy.

**38.** By the words of the dancing [Lord], sprinkled at every step with the juice of nectar, they easily placed the happiness of heaven beneath [their own].

**39.** Thus the Supreme Lord spent the day in dancing together with His own people, who had bathed in the ponds of the nectar of kirtana.

**40.** Then in the afternoon, when the splendid [Lord] was dancing again, the wind honoured all the quarters all around with intoxicating fragrances.

*Note.* The edition has *श्रीयते* and *समपूजयत्*; we read *śrī-yute* (as the edition itself prints in verse 50); *samapūjayat* ("honoured") is confirmed by the Gosvami edition (GGM) as well, so it is kept. *Mada-gandha* is the fragrance of musk, or of *vāruṇī*, the intoxicating drink beloved of Balarama (cf. Chaitanya-charitamrita, Adi 17.112–118: the scent of *vāruṇī* in the house of Srivasa).

**41.** Having smelt that very distinct fragrance, laden with intoxication, the bees covered the sky like sudden clouds.

**42.** The foremost of brahmanas, Sri Rama by name, saw great beings who had come down from the vault of the sky — of great beauty, of great radiance,

*Note.* Sri Rama [Pandita] is Srivasa's brother (6.6). Verses 42–44 form one sentence.

**43.** their bodies anointed with heavenly fragrances, adorned with heavenly ornaments, wearing heavenly garlands and garments — heavenly [beings], abodes of heavenly beauty and qualities,

**44.** captivating with a lotus earring worn in one ear, their heads wrapped in silken turbans, their hearts joyful.

*Note.* An earring in one ear is a mark of Balarama (*eka-kuṇḍala*); these are evidently His associates.

**45.** Others, learned in the Vedas, hearing this from his mouth, at once danced and sang ardently, with the kirtana of the names of Hari.

**46.** Right there a certain high-souled foremost of brahmanas, Vanamali, saw a plough made of gold, the size of a mountain.

*Note.* Vanamali Acharya; cf. Chaitanya-charitamrita, Adi 17.119: "Vanamali Acharya saw a golden plough in the hand of Balarama." Probably the same Vanamali who arranged the marriage with Lakshmipriya (Murari 1.9); the father of Gadadhara is Madhava Mishra, not Vanamali.

**47.** Seeing this, he was filled with wonder; overwhelmed by streams of tears from his eyes, his body covered with a flood of hairs standing on end, he then forgot his body.

**48.** Then charming, beautiful Gauranga, charming in His absorption in Halayudha, danced together with them — the auspicious [singers] of His own kirtana.

*Note.* *Halāyudha*, "He whose weapon is the plough", is Balarama.

**49.** In the sky all the hosts of the gods, with Mahendra and the Lotus-born, bowed down with bent necks, showering a multitude of flowers.

*Note.* Mahendra is Indra; the Lotus-born is Brahma.

**50.** Thus, while the splendid Lord was dancing, the night drew to its end, and the moon, as it were, softly kissed the edge of the western mountain.

*Note.* *Carama-śaila* is the mountain of sunset.

**51.** Was it not in order to see the dance of the dancing [Lord] that this young [maiden], the quarter of Purandara, became so enamoured and crimson?

*Note.* The quarter of Purandara (Indra) is the east. *Anurāga* means both "love" and "redness" (the dawn).

**52.** Then the gentle wind, constantly embraced by the moonlight, stirring the night lotuses, served the feet of Gaura.

*Note.* The edition has *सनाधुन्वन्*; we read *samādhunvan*.

**53.** Then the Lord of the worlds, the Master, having come with His own people to the heavenly river, shone like Meru among the mountains.

*Note.* The edition has *उपोथिवान्*; the Gosvami edition (GGM) reads *upeyivān* ("having come", the perfect participle of *upa-√i*), and so we read. The heavenly river is the Ganga.

**54.** Then, having plunged into the Ganga, He who is more beautiful than a golden mountain made water-play, [all] splashing one another with water from their hands.

*Note.* *Gāṅgeya*, "born of the Ganga", is gold (from the myth of Kartikeya). A play on *gaṅgā* and *gāṅgeya*.

**55.** Thus concluding pastimes of various kinds, Gaura went to His own house, as the moon [goes] to the mountain of rising.

*Note.* The text has *udayācala*, the mountain of rising (in the east), not the mountain of setting.

**56.** Laughing very sweetly, the Lord said to Srivasa: "Give Me the flute, quickly! Where is it? It is nowhere to be seen."

*Note.* From the mood of Balarama — to the mood of Krishna.

**57.** Then this best of brahmanas, laughing, said: "Your flute is guarded on all sides by the gopis."

*Note.* An echo of the *vaṁśī-caurya* — the stealing of Krishna's flute by Radha and the gopis.

**58.** Remembering again and again the pastimes in Vrindavana, the treasury of mercy, immersed in an unbroken flood of deep bliss, fell silent for a moment.

**59.** And then Mahaprabhu, exceedingly absorbed, His hairs standing on end, loudly and incessantly said to him: "Speak, speak!"

**60.** Then this Srivasa described everything at length — the play in Vrindavana and also the play on the Yamuna.

**61.** "Once, in the forest of Vrinda, when You, absorbed in love, sported incessantly with young doe-eyed [maidens], there was a splendour. If that had not been born of You Yourself, O Lord, who are never satiated, how could this rapture have become eternally new, as if ever new and new?"

*Note.* The metre is *śikhariṇī*. The thought: it is the insatiability of Krishna Himself that makes His pastimes eternally new. The syntax of *ajani* in the third line is not entirely clear (?). The Gosvami edition (GGM) has the same text.

**62.** "Once You entered incomparable Vrindavana, charming with its bowers where swarms of bees hum very sweetly, and played there as if You were the very embodiment of the mantra of the science of love, delight and rasa. That is what is dearest to You!"

*Note.* The metre is *vasanta-tilakā*.

**63.** Hearing this, the Lord, with a roar like that of a lion maddened by intoxication, sweetly said: "Speak more!" And thereupon that best of the twice-born, by His mercy, clearly recounted all His deeds.

*Note.* The metre is *vasanta-tilakā*.

Thus ends the eighth sarga, named "The Description of the Pastimes in Vrindavana", in the mahakavya "Sri Krishna-Chaitanya-charitamrita".

---

## Sarga Nine — verses 1–95

**Title of the sarga:** none — the colophon of the edition gives no title for this sarga.
**Metre:** *svāgatā* (11 syllables per pada: —⏑— ⏑⏑⏑ —⏑⏑ ——); verse 94 — *praharṣiṇī*; verse 95 — *mandākrāntā*.
**Total verses:** 95.

**Contents of the passage:** Srivasa's account of Krishna's pastimes with the gopis — the walk in the forest, the bathing in the Yamuna, the entry into the jewelled pavilion.
- 1 — Srivasa begins his account (continuing 8.60–63).
- 2–20 — the gopis enter the forest with Krishna: lightning beside a cloud; their first shyness yields to Manmatha; gestures and gaits; bees alight on the creepers of their bodies; the gopis walk embracing Krishna.
- 21–31 — the Lakshmi of the forest brings gifts; the friends tease a proud one (*kulaka* 22–26: the *mādhavī* and the jasmine tormented by bees); a gopi embraces a creeper as a friend; "the cuckoo calls 'kuhū' — it is darkness you desire".
- 32–47 — gathering flowers: Krishna is adorned with a flower, lifts the gopis to the high branches, calls Radha lotus-faced (34); a bee on Krishna; reproaches to the "bee" (*kulaka* 37–39); "there is a bower!"; balls of flowers; coaxing the proud one.
- 48–50 — Krishna turns to another; the gopis are bathed in perspiration from the forest play.
- 51–78 — the bathing in the Yamuna: splashing, the "water drum", the *śapharī* fish, lotuses and stalks hold back the garments; the washed-off colours and ornaments are gifts to the Yamuna; wet braids and garments.
- 79–93 — a *kulaka* of fifteen verses: the jewelled pavilion (walls like mirrors, moonstones and sunstones, emerald "grass", ruby "pomegranates", a crystal pond).
- 94–95 — the singing of the birds; the wish-fulfilling creepers perform *nīrājana* over the gopis with cries of "Victory!"

**1.** Then he said to Gaurachandra, thus immersed in an ocean of mighty happiness: "Listen, O best of lords: I myself shall tell [You] of Your own pastimes, performed of old."

*Note.* The second pada in the edition (*गौरचन्द्रमथ यथा सोऽभिजगाद*) has 13 syllables instead of 11; without *yathā* (*gauracandram atha so 'bhijagāda*) the *svāgatā* metre is restored exactly, so *yathā* is probably an interpolation or a misprint (?). The Gosvami edition (GGM) has the same text.

**2.** Seeing His indescribable face, the most charming of all that is charming, the playful [maidens], tender-hearted, wrought wonders with the creepers of their limbs together with their beloved.

*Note.* The edition has *श्रेयसा* ("with the good"); cf. *preyasā* in verse 3: we read *preyasā* (?). The first pada has 12 syllables instead of 11; with *tan-mukham* in place of *tad-vadanam* the *svāgatā* metre is exact — perhaps a gloss has displaced the original word (?). The Gosvami edition (GGM) has the same text.

**3.** Entering the forest together with their beloved, dark as a young tamala tree, these young women of Vraja displayed before Him the play of lightnings with a new rain cloud.

*Note.* The edition has *praviśantaḥ*, a masculine form with a feminine subject; the sense requires *praviśantyaḥ* (the metre is unchanged) (?). The golden gopis before Krishna, dark as a cloud, are like lightning on a cloud. The Gosvami edition (GGM) has the same text.

**4.** Beholding His loveliness, the beautiful women shone with love born in the heart; charming in their movements, full of languor, they became wholly filled with feeling for their master.

*Note.* *Rucirayālasa-* = *rucirayā* (agreeing with *ceṣṭayā*) + *ālasa*. The Gosvami edition (GGM) has the same text.

**5.** Then, in the hearts of the innocent young women, full of feeling and longing for Krishnachandra, Manmatha arose, dispelling the shyness born of the first [meeting].

*Note.* The edition has *साभिलास* for the usual *sābhilāṣa*. *Samudāse* is an impersonal passive perfect (*bhāve prayoga*): "it was risen by Manmatha", that is, Manmatha arose.

**6.** One, startled and abashed, tightened her loosened garment and, holding it, moved lightly and playfully to the side of her dear, smiling friends — and she shone.

**7.** When the gently blowing breeze turned aside the covering of another's bosom while Krishna looked on, she in confusion tightly embraced a dear friend.

*Note.* *Kacela* is a rare word, apparently a covering for the bosom or a bodice; possibly a corruption. The Gosvami edition (GGM) has the same text.

**8.** And another, raising both her arms, so that her full bosom was lifted, and languidly bending her whole body, stretched with a yawn before her beloved, radiant.

**9.** Abandoning the forest creeper, a swarm of bees resorted to the creeper of the body of a beautiful maiden — lovely with the buds of her full bosom, her hands putting to shame the beauty of young shoots.

*Note.* In the edition the lines are wrongly divided (*प्रोज्झ्य काननलतां — / वरनारी देहवल्लि…*): by the metre, *vara-nārī* belongs to the third pada.

**10.** Had not the beauty of the creepers joyfully passed into the creepers of the bodies of the fair-browed [maidens]? For at once, on all sides, this swarm of sweetly humming bees appeared around them.

**11.** A greatly intoxicated honey-drinking bee, longing to become a drinker of nectar, fell on the lip of one [maiden]; and she gladly did not drive it away, remembering her beloved, who is given to biting the lips.

*Note.* *Amṛta-pa-tvam* — "the state of a drinker of nectar" (*amṛta-pa* + *-tva*). *Nirāse* is an impersonal passive: "by one [maiden] there was no driving away".

**12.** Then, in order to play there, the fair-browed [maidens] displayed the loveliness of their lotus feet — a light, unhurried [gait], charming with its sweet steps, recalling the dance of a female swan overwhelmed by love.

*Note.* The syntax is unclear: the attributes *mantharam … madhura-krama-ramyam* imply "gait" (*gatim*), while the second *ramyam* is probably a noun, "loveliness". The Gosvami edition (GGM) has the same text.

**13.** As the beautiful women walked, their steps slowed by swelling love, the fullness of their rounded hips and bosoms, though it wearied them, became a source of happiness.

*Note.* In the edition the lines are wrongly divided (*…पादन्यास / भाजि…*): the boundary of the padas falls inside the word *pāda-nyāsa*.

**14.** To the sound of the golden girdle — the drum of Kama — the hips of the fair-browed [maidens], adorned with the grace of their gait, danced very slowly and languidly, like the curve of a wave.

**15.** "Tender is the lotus of your foot, it has no strength — do not go so quickly, O maiden with intoxicating eyes!" — is it not this that the anklets of the beautiful women, utterly helpless, cry out of love?

**16.** Reddened with the lac from the feet of the loving [maidens], the forest ground seemed to tell longingly of its own great love for the play of the lotus feet of each of them.

*Note.* A play on words: *anurāga* means both "love" and "redness". The earth, reddened by *yāvaka* (the red lac for the feet), seems to confess its love.

**17.** There one [maiden] shone as she followed [Him], her bosom pressed to Krishna's back, her arms resting now on one, now on the other of His shoulders, her face kissed by His face as He turned it sideways.

*Note.* The compound *sāci-tad-vadana-cumbita-vaktrā* admits two readings: either Krishna, turning His face sideways, kisses her, or she, turning her face, kisses Him.

**18.** Another, lotus-eyed, whose bosom her dearest one embraced from behind with both arms, walked on bearing them at her throat like a necklace of sapphires.

*Note.* Krishna's dark arms at the gopi's throat are compared to a necklace of *indranīla* (sapphire). The edition has *इवास्या* without the visarga — a misprint.

**19.** And of her who walked beside Him with a slow, languid gait, her arm shining on Keshava's shoulder, the hip, now touching His hip and now parting from it, shone indeed like a wave.

**20.** One, walking with her friends towards the lord of her life, her gait curved by Ananga, bending her body, laid her arm on the shoulder of the friend who followed her — and near the root of her arm her bosom rose.

*Note.* The edition has *सखिभिः*; the metre requires *sakhībhiḥ* (the tenth syllable is long).

**21.** At that very moment, in an instant, the Lakshmi of the forest herself at once came to the young women, whom Manmatha had rendered helpless in many ways, bringing a load of gifts — all that they desired.

*Note.* The "Lakshmi of the forest" is its beauty personified. Verses 22–26 that follow (a *kulaka*) are the teasing speech of one of the gopis.

**22.** "Do not torment yourself so greatly, O proud one; give up this helplessness, O moist-eyed one! O you who are fair as gold, heavy as this pique of yours has grown, it will certainly prove fleeting.

*Note.* *Māna* is the loving pique, the jealous anger of the beloved — one of the moods of the pastimes of love.

**23.** Look, O you whose eyes are like an intoxicated doe's: the *mādhavī* creeper, lovely with the youth of her blossoms, waves a shoot like your hand — and the exceedingly greedy bees torment her.

*Note.* The edition has माधुरी ("sweetness"); we read *mādhavī*, the *mādhavī* creeper (the metre is unchanged), as the image requires (cf. the jasmine creeper in verse 24). The Gosvami edition (GGM) has the same text.

**24.** And here is the jasmine creeper, lovely with her opening buds: assailed by the bees, she waves a young shoot like a hand — but could she ever quell their onslaught?

**25.** Look: this creeper, ruffled by the bumblebees, proclaims just this by the trembling of her leaves: in the heart of a fair-browed girl, intoxicated and longing for the play [of love], pique by no means, by no means remains.

**26.** Here is your obedient beloved — embrace Him! Give up, O friend, give up this empty pique!" So, in the presence of the lord of her life, a certain [maiden], expert in the moods [of love], spoke jestingly to a certain [proud girl].

*Note.* *Kulaka*: verses 22–26 form a single sentence. In the edition the first pada (*आश्रवं तमिमाश्लिष कान्तं*) has 10 syllables instead of 11 — one short syllable is missing; the transliteration *tam imam āśliṣa* restores it, and the *svāgatā* metre is then exact (the syllable *ma* was probably lost before *mā*) (?). *Āśliṣa* is an irregular imperative of *ā-śliṣ* (the regular form is *āśliṣya*); the poet uses it again in verse 28, where the metre confirms it. The Gosvami edition (GGM) has the same text.

**27.** "Why do you not sport freely in the midst of the excellent [grove] of the Beloved of Balarama, where the young mango branches, thickly covered in buds, shine within reach of the hand?"

*Note.* *Balottara* is obscure; perhaps *vanottara*, "excellent forest", should be read (?). It is not clear who is addressing whom. The Gosvami edition (GGM) has the same text.

**28.** "Welcome, O friend! At last I see you after so long — embrace me!" And with these words a certain [maiden], overpowered by emotion, impetuously embraced some creeper there, her bosom rising [as she opened her arms].

*Note.* The form रभसाभिः is unclear (one would expect *rabhasā* or *rabhasāt*); the sense is "impetuously". Intoxicated with love, the gopi takes the creeper for a friend. The Gosvami edition (GGM) has the same text.

**29.** "O fair-browed, artless girl, put right the edge of your garment, which the branch of a creeper has pulled from your bosom — lest, O friend, some foolish bee, mistaking it for the bud of a lotus, should alight here!"

*Note.* *Mugdha* is said of both: the "artless" girl and the "foolish" bee.

**30.** "Is the moonlight here not pleasing to you? It is darkness alone that you two desire — for here you listen so thirstily to the cuckoo that keeps calling 'Kuhū!'"

*Note.* A pun: *kuhū* is both the call of the cuckoo (*kokila*) and the moonless night of the new moon, favourable for meetings. The edition (like the Gosvami edition) has *tena hi*, which is syntactically obscure; we read *te na hi*, "not … to you".

**31.** "So let us go far away from here, and you stay now alone with your beloved." With such contrivances of feigned words another [friend] then made known her wish to leave.

*Note.* The edition has तिष्ट, a misprint for तिष्ठ.

**32.** "This very flower looks beautiful on You — over Your ears!" And so saying, a certain [maiden], raising her arms, adorned Him, the pitchers of her bosom touching Krishna's chest.

**33.** A certain [maiden] wished to pick flowers growing high up, and Madhava, delighted, clasped her with both arms below the hips and lifted her up, her hips pressed to His chest.

*Note.* *Udāse* is the passive perfect of *ud-as*, "to lift up"; cf. *samudāse* in verse 5.

**34.** "This face of Yours is a lotus, O Radha, and Your teeth a garland of *kunda* jasmine — what need have You of flowers?" Saying this, the most beloved lifted the tip of His darling's chin and kissed Her.

*Note.* Here Radha is named in the sarga for the first time. The edition has *°vadanā* ("with a face"); we read *°radanā*, "with teeth": a garland of white kunda flowers is the standard image for teeth. The Gosvami edition (GGM) has the same text.

**35.** Another, though bowed down by the weight of her bosom and flustered, again and again reached up, intent on gathering flowers; and as the cloth slipped from her bosom because her hand was raised, she sported upon Krishna's heart.

*Note.* The edition has उत्करा द्गलद्, a wrong division of words; we read *utkarād galad-*.

**36.** A certain [maiden] noticed a bee nestled [on Krishna] and went to remove it with her fingernails — and then saw [that she was touching] Keshava. In the whirl of her confusion her limbs grew helpless, and she sank upon her beloved's chest.

*Note.* The bee, dark like Krishna, merges with Him, and in removing it the gopi touches Keshava Himself with her nails.

**37.** "Abandoning this row of full-blown flowers, the fool will alight on the buds! You are enslaved by a bee's *rāga* — and what discernment, indeed, is there in the minds of those who are dyed [with it]?

*Note.* The beginning of a *kulaka* (37–39): the gopi addresses the bee but aims at Krishna. A pun: *rāga* means both "ardour" and "colour"; *bhṛṅga-rāga* is both "a bee's ardour" and "blackness". The syntax of *tat tvaṁ rajyatām* is unclear; we take *rajyatām* as the genitive plural of the participle, "of those who are dyed" (?). The Gosvami edition (GGM) has the same text.

**38.** You are dark, ever drunk with honey, attached to lotuses, fickle — so surely, O bee, you resemble a certain someone in every quality!

*Note.* *Padminī* is both "lotus plant" and a *padminī*, the highest of the types of women in the classical typology.

**39.** You do not know whether we can bear your onslaught or not, O most foolish one — you are given to ardour alone!" So, playfully feigning anger, a certain [maiden] spoke, hinting at Krishna.

*Note.* *No* may be taken as *na u*, "not", or as *naḥ*, "our"; in the latter case the line is a reproachful question: "Do you know what we can bear and what we cannot?"

**40.** Another, seeing a bee that had come to smell the lotus of her face, on purpose did not drive it away with her hands — only so as to hear Madhava's teasing words.

**41.** "Your gait is slow by nature; and if, besides, you have set out with your beloved, [lingering] at every step, why go any further? Look, O beautiful one: there is a bower right here!

*Note.* Verses 41–42 form a single speech.

**42.** This bee, hiding behind the petal of the dark lotus at your ear and whispering into it — do you not understand what it is saying?" So, with a smile, one [maiden] said to another.

*Note.* The dark lotus is an ornament worn over the ear. *Ava-gam* means both "to understand" and "to go"; the question may also be heard as "why do you not go?"

**43.** "What kind of garland should I weave for the slope of your neck, O you whose neck is like a conch?" Saying this, He mischievously, in an instant, took away the cloth from His beloved's bosom.

*Note.* A neck "like a conch" has three folds, a mark of beauty.

**44.** A certain [maiden] incessantly threw a multitude of flower-balls all around the Enemy of Agha, as though making an abundant offering of moons to His sweetness.

*Note.* The edition has अघदिपुं, a misprint for अघरिपुं. The white flower-balls are compared to moons. The sense of the second half is rendered approximately. The Gosvami edition (GGM) has the same text.

**45.** "The young cuckoo, intoxicated with spring and turned golden-yellow by the embraces of the blossoming mango branches, seems to be summoning Manmatha in earnest — and my heart has become utterly distraught.

*Note.* With this verse there seems to begin the speech of one of the gopis to the proud girl (45–47). The speaker is not named, however: the *iti* of verse 48, whose subject is Krishna, may also point to Krishna Himself as the speaker, "the beloved" and "the lord" (46–47) then being His words about Himself (?). The Gosvami edition (GGM) has the same text.

**46.** Do not in any way let your pique grow, O proud one; bring honey and offer it [to Him]! To be constantly and openly in the power of pique towards your lord is bad policy.

*Note.* The verse plays on the sounds of *māna*: *mānam ānaya*, *mānayo*, *mānini*, *māna-*. The beginning of the third pada can also be understood as *mā* + *anayaḥ*, "let there not be this folly". *Prathayānam* is an irregular form; one would expect *prathamānam*. The Gosvami edition (GGM) has the same text.

**47.** Give [Him] to drink the nectar of your sweet lips; revive your pitiable beloved! Surely you are the mistress of His heart here — do you really not know how distressed that heart is?"

**48.** Thus the lord of hearts — exceedingly tender, intoxicated with love, dark yet always deeply crimson — turned to another, whose heart had been won over by the qualities of her beloved.

*Note.* An apparent contradiction (*virodhābhāsa*): He is "dark" (*śyāmala*) and yet always "deeply coloured, crimson" (*guru-rāga*, that is, full of ardent love).

**49.** Adorned with flowers, their bodies stole this beauty of the forest, and by that very [deed] they shone all the more: for what is taken by the good turns to virtue.

*Note.* A pun: *sumanas* is both "a flower" and "a good-minded person"; *sat*, "good"; *guṇa*, both "virtue" and "string" (of a garland): flowers taken up go onto the string of a garland.

**50.** They whose beautiful limbs are the very height of delicacy, who melt even at the fatigue of love's play, now, from the weariness born of their long pastimes in the forest, sank in just the same way into oceans of perspiration.

**51.** Then, wearied, their limbs languid, they entered the Yamuna — rich in the nectar of the lustre of a dark rain-cloud, her lovely eyes the open lotuses — as though [entering] the very body of their beloved.

*Note.* The edition has प्रेयसन्तनुम्; we read *preyasas tanum*. The dark Yamuna with her lotus eyes is like Krishna's own body.

**52.** With her glossy, dense waves, dark as a cloud, and the clusters of flowers of her leaping spray, the daughter of the Sun suddenly attained the charm of the braided hair of the beautiful women.

*Note.* The Yamuna is the daughter of Surya. Her dark waves with their white spray are like black braids adorned with flowers.

**53.** "O fair-browed one, I cannot go even a little way; so I shall lean on you." Saying this, He, languid in form, embraced one of them and went down into the Yamuna.

**54.** Eagerly washing away almost all [the traces of] kisses and the marks of nails and teeth, the two lords of each other's hearts, each eager for victory over the other, splashed one another with handfuls of water.

*Note.* The edition (like the Gosvami edition) has *vilapayya*. We take it as a gerund of the causative of *vi-lī*, "to dissolve, wash away" (*vilāpayati*): in the water the traces of the pastimes are washed off (?). A conjectural reading *vipalāya*, "having fled [from each other]", is also possible.

**55.** A certain ever-youthful [maiden], utterly repulsed in the water by the handfuls of water [thrown by] her beloved, rose up wishing to go up onto the bank — was it because her fine silk had merged with the water?

*Note.* Alliteration: *vāri vāritatamā … vāribhiḥ*, *dukūle* — *kūlam*. The wet silk has become transparent, and the gopi hastens to the bank.

**56.** In the Yamuna a fair-browed [maiden's] waistband came loose, and the current carried off her garment; her most beloved, playfully embracing her, took her body into His arms, as though to hide it in the clear water.

*Note.* *Abhāji* we take as the passive aorist of *bhaj*: "[her] body was taken" — Krishna's embrace covers her in the transparent water. It may also be from *bhañj*, "to bend": "[His] body was bent, as though to hide in the clear water the garment carried off by the current."

**57.** As another, in fun, played before the Enemy of Madhu the coquettish, captivating game of the "water drum", her ample bosom seemed to dance to the jingling of her conch-shell bangles.

*Note.* *Jala-maṇḍuka* is a water game: striking the water with the palms so that it sounds like a drum. The edition has अपरस्य (masculine); the sense requires *aparasyāḥ* (the metre is unchanged) (?). The Gosvami edition (GGM) has the same text.

**58.** Then at the full bosoms and hips of the fair-browed [maidens] the waters, now parting, now colliding in waves, took on the stumbling of a stream at the foot of boulders.

**59.** A certain [maiden] with jingling conch-shell bangles caught hold of her beloved as He was drenching her with full handfuls of nectar-like water, and by the trembling of her hands gave Him as a reward the dance of her bosom.

**60.** A certain artless beauty longed to reach her dear one's chest; and as, in the clear water, shallow though it was, she shook her petal-like hand laden with many conch-shell bangles, she became the golden creeper of a necklace on her beloved.

*Note.* The golden-complexioned gopi on Krishna's dark chest is like a golden necklace.

**61.** At the movements of the *śapharī* fish darting about the banks of their thighs, the young women shook their hands with fear, curiosity and pride, their teeth flashing as they charmingly hissed "sh-sh!"

*Note.* The edition gives a variant: *garvam* or *harṣam* ("joy"). *Śītkāra* — drawing in the breath through the teeth — is a conventional sign in kavya of the thrill of love.

**62.** Then the water — most skilful at dispelling weariness — its waves rising on all sides from the blows of their petal-like hands, reached their bosoms, heavy with perspiration, and bathed them.

**63.** There, hearing the young women called "lotus-faced" by their dear one, the lotus riding on the waves danced, as it were: for to the worthy even to serve as a comparison is good fortune indeed.

**64.** A certain golden-hued [maiden], her body in the power of feeling, thought even Krishna, His whole body steeped in blue, to be far away in the dark water of the Yamuna.

*Note.* *Sarvam aṅgam abhisambhṛta-nīlam* qualifies Krishna ("whose whole body is steeped in blue"): the dark Krishna has merged with the dark water, and the gopi thinks He is not beside her.

**65.** The shoots of their lips without lac, their eyes with the collyrium washed away, the jars of their bosoms bereft of colour yet gleaming with the marks of nails — the beloved looked upon [all this] as if it were the close of love's play.

*Note.* The water has washed away the cosmetics, and the gopis look as they do at the close of love's play. *Vīta-rāga* — "bereft of colour (kunkuma)" and, by double meaning, "free of passion".

**66.** Their reddening eyes, their exquisite lustre as if crumpled, their whole bodies most languid, their garments clinging to their bodies for very thinness — all that was of service to their beloved.

**67.** One [maiden] hid in a thicket of lotuses, and her dear one entreated her at every step; but by the humming of the rising swarms of bees her friends clearly guessed [where she was].

*Note.* The bees fly up to the lotus of her face.

**68.** Here one [maiden], wishing to pluck lotuses, made her way deep into the throng of lotus plants — and a creeper of lotus stalk, coiling tightly round the bank of her hips, held her back, as if in anger.

**69.** The thin garment of a fair-eyed [maiden], whose knot had already loosened before for her dearest one, was loosened by the current — but at that very moment the lotus plant, like a dear friend, held it back with the creeper of its stalk.

*Note.* The edition has *श्रोतसा*, a misprint for *स्रोतसा*.

**70.** One [maiden], her body exhausted, hid herself in defiance; and as she languidly rose up [from the water], she was doubly flustered, relentlessly [splashed] with water by her beloved together with her friends.

**71.** One [maiden] tried again and again to get out, though held back by the petal of her beloved's hand; and when a *śapharī* fish clinging to her thigh turned over and frightened her, she blamed Him.

**72.** One [maiden], reaching the edge of the bank and covering her bosom with her fine wet garment, saw that it still showed through just the same — and laughed in shyness before her dear one.

**73.** Then the fair-browed [maidens], their ornaments all awry, taking particular care only of their garments, in their languor made this and that, as it were, a fine gracious gift, prompted by affection, to the daughter of the Sun.

*Note.* Many of the ornaments fell into the Yamuna. The form *alase* is unclear; it is taken here as "languid" (of the gopis). The Gosvami edition (GGM) has the same text.

**74.** With the flowers from their braids, with jewelled necklaces, with bracelets and girdles that had slipped off, with unguents washed away in the bathing — with all this the Yamuna too shone, like a true friend.

*Note.* The unguents are aguru, musk and the like.

**75.** Their braids, from which drops of water trickled abundantly as the dense waves broke, shone there like a mass of darkness made lovelier by the rising of the stars.

**76.** Did not the braids of the beautiful women, falling right down to their hips, dense as dark rain clouds, here display the oneness of the two lovers, who had attained a very mass of love?

*Note.* The dark braids of the gopis are of the same colour as Krishna, and seem to bear witness to the oneness of the lovers.

**77.** The soft, fine garment, clinging closely to the creeper of the body and streaming with water, seemed to pour back, overflowing, that very nectar of the beauty of beauties which it had drunk so abundantly.

**78.** One [maiden], shivering and hissing "śīt", covered her bosom with the creeper of her arms crossed over it — as if she had held back a pair of chakravaka birds with a creeper, so that they might not be parted day after day.

*Note.* In kavya the bosom is traditionally compared to a pair of chakravaka birds (*koka*), which, according to popular belief, are parted every night.

**79.** There, perceiving on the jewelled walls the radiance of their bodies multiplied by their own reflections, they, with eyes like a startled doe's, in their eagerness fell under the sway of wonder.

*Note.* With this verse begins the description (through *yatra*, "where") of the jewelled pavilion which the gopis enter after bathing in order to adorn themselves. The word to which all these "where"s refer is *ālayam* ("pavilion") in verse 93, which concludes the *kulaka* (79–93).

**80.** There a young maiden, when her beloved drew away her thin garment, looked at the rays of the gems, took them for a lamp and wished to cover them — and for a long time was abashed by her reflections.

*Note.* A familiar motif of kavya: the shy heroine tries to put out or cover the gems, taking them for a lamp.

**81.** There a dais of moonstones, ceaselessly oozing [moisture] under the smiling rays of the cool-rayed [moon], seemed to the young women to be built of crystal waters.

*Note.* The *candrakānta* (moonstone) is believed to exude moisture in moonlight. The edition has *संश्रवन्ति*; we read *saṁsravantī*.

**82.** There even the crystal floor, reflecting the shoot-like feet of the fair-browed [maidens] as they trod upon it, seemed so fathomless that one could not step onto it, its depths adorned, as it seemed, with red gems.

*Note.* The end of the second pada (*°m agādhāt*) is grammatically unclear (?). The red gems in the depths are the reflections of the reddened feet. The Gosvami edition (GGM) has the same text.

**83.** There, with the parrots, cuckoos, peacocks and pigeons painted on the jewelled wall as if alive, the other [birds] dwelling in the house — the very same kinds — merrily chattered.

**84.** There the most innocent [maidens], holding out a cluster of blossoms of a young mango tree to the charming beaks of painted hen-cuckoos, kept saying again and again: "Take it!"

*Note.* *Parapuṣṭa* ("nourished by others") is the cuckoo, which lays its eggs in other birds' nests.

**85.** There, by the rays of various gems flashing forth and ever mingling with one another, an ornamentation of svastikas and other [auspicious figures] shines forth — charming, beautifully wrought, and arising without any effort.

**86.** There at every step the girls of Vraja joyfully let a little fawn down from their laps and with their soft arms urged it on towards the rows of gleaming emerald stones.

*Note.* The girls take the emeralds for grass.

**87.** There some, about to adorn themselves in the galleries made of red gems, saw themselves [already reddened] and, delighted, out of that redness no longer wished to colour their bodies with kunkuma liquid.

*Note.* A play on words: *anurāgataḥ* — "from the redness" (the glow of the rubies) and "from love".

**88.** There the wish-fulfilling trees, through the intermingling of the various lustres of excellent gems, shone as if adorned with fine lamps whose bright tongues of flame blazed high.

**89.** There the young parrots, taking those red gems for ripe pomegranates, came up to them; but, feeling no [softness] under the strikes of their feet, never once applied their beaks.

*Note.* The double negative in the third and fourth padas admits different interpretations (?). The Gosvami edition (GGM) has the same text.

**90.** There the bee, in its folly, does not want a flower, taking it for a diamond, and clings to a diamond, taking it for a flower; so it was only by the will of fate that the bee ever attained the state of a "honey-drinker".

**91.** There a pond with lovely waters — on one side pinkish-white from the crystal, on the other shot through with the lustre of emeralds — was like a strip where moonlight and darkness meet.

**92.** There a chakravaka, having dived at the crystal bank and emerged at the edge of the bank of sunstone, bitterly laments for his mate, turned towards the lovely lake.

*Note.* *Dyupati-ratna* ("the gem of the lord of the sky") is the sunstone (*sūryakānta*). The edition has *prothito*; the Gosvami edition (GGM) has *protthito* ("having emerged"), and so we read.

**93.** And the [maidens] with eyes pure as blue lotuses lightly, playfully entered that captivating, spacious, wondrous pavilion to adorn themselves, as moonlight [enters] an ocean of nectar. *(A kulaka of fifteen verses.)*

*Note.* Verses 79–93 form a *kulaka*, a single sentence: all the subordinate clauses with "where" (*yatra*) refer to the word *ālayam* ("pavilion").

**94.** Through the splendid cuckoos, peacocks, parrots and swans, the fine turtle-doves, the swarms of bees and the pigeons, each raising its own voice above the others', [the pavilion] became in the highest degree an unprecedented elixir for the ears.

*Note.* The metre is *praharṣiṇī*. The subject of *sambheje* is not named; by the sense it is the "pavilion" of verse 93 (?). The Gosvami edition (GGM) has the same text.

**95.** Then, as the lovely young women went up [into the pavilion], the wish-fulfilling creepers grown within it on every side performed, as it were, *nīrājana* over them with various fragrant, sweet flowers, and with the singing of the cuckoos again and again loudly raised a joyful cry: "Victory, victory, victory!"

*Note.* The metre is *mandākrāntā*. *Nīrājana* is a rite of welcome: a lamp, flowers and the like are waved before one who is honoured.

**Colophon:** *iti śrī-kṛṣṇa-caitanya-caritāmṛte mahākāvye navamaḥ sargaḥ* — "Thus [ends] the ninth sarga in the mahakavya 'Sri Krishna-Chaitanya-charitamrita'." (The colophon gives no title for the sarga.)

---

## Sarga Ten — verses 1–80

**Title of the sarga:** none — the colophon of the edition gives no title for this sarga.
**Metre:** *svāgatā* (11 syllables per pada: —⏑— ⏑⏑⏑ —⏑⏑ ——); verses 74–80 — *mālinī*.
**Total verses:** 80.

**Contents of the passage:** the continuation of Srivasa's account — the night of Radha, Krishna and the gopis in the jewelled pavilion (classical themes of the mahakavya: sunset, moonrise, adornment, the drinking party, love's play, daybreak).
- 1–3 — the gopis prepare a couch of *mālatī* flowers and perfume the pavilion with incense; the sun sets so as not to stand in their way.
- 4–9 — sunset: the sun draws in its rays; the West holds the sun like red-hot iron (an ordeal); the darkness is the smoke of the serpents' breath; the elephants and the winged mountains of the quarters.
- 10–15 — the darkness deepens; Night lifts her veil; the swan of the moon emerges from the eastern lake; the moon is a silver casket.
- 16–27 — adornment: garments, unguents, coiffures, mirrors; some refuse ornaments ("the body is the ornament of ornaments"); Krishna gazes at His own attire.
- 28–45 — moonlight and the drinking party: the circling cup, tipsy speech and movements, kisses and bites, the "relish" (*vidaṁśa*).
- 46–73 — love's play (after the manner of the *kāma-śāstra*); 65–66 — Kama makes Radha and Krishna exchange roles.
- 74–80 (*mālinī*) — fulfilment and sleep; the lamp shakes its head: "there is nothing like this anywhere"; the sun puts out the night-lamp; the friends' morning jests ("the lake", "the bee and the lotus").

**1.** Then the maidens with intoxicating eyes, full of longing, made a spacious couch of *mālatī* flowers with a pillow, and for the sake of [their] adornment made the interior of the pavilion beautifully adorned.

**2.** [They] perfumed it with incense of black aloe-wood, whose clouds rose fragrantly and spread, leaving no gap; it was filled with motes darting to and fro in the moonbeams, and acquired a heavy, majestic fragrance.

*Note.* *trasareṇu* — a mote of dust visible in a beam of light.

**3.** Seeing the young women of Gokula thus risen up [ready] in the place of love, the lord of the day thought, "Let their love's play rise ever higher!" — and, laying aside his might, went to his setting.

**4.** "The rays that spread abundantly in all directions from me as I blaze — if they are to be abandoned here, alas, what will become of them?" Is it not with this thought that the sun draws in its rays?

*Note.* The syntax of *kīdṛśair … bhavitavyam* is rendered according to the sense (?). The Gosvami edition (GGM) has the same text.

**5.** "The moon sleeps in the house of the stars — what sort of wife are you?" To silence such talk, the woman West, the quarter of Varuna, took up the red sun like a red-hot ball of iron.

*Note.* An allusion to the ordeal (*divya*): a woman accused of infidelity proves her innocence by holding red-hot iron.

**6.** When the jewel of the sun, having made [those lands] bright, had gone to abide on another continent, the world, plunged in deep darkness, was filled [as it were] with clouds of the smoky breath of the serpents.

*Note.* *dvīpa* — a continent of Puranic cosmology. The darkness is compared to the venomous, smoky breath of the Nagas of the nether world.

**7.** "Have the elephants of the quarters come together with one another? Or have the mountains of the quarters set their wings in motion?" So [people] conjectured about its strange transformation; the darkness could no longer be contained here in the egg of the universe.

*Note.* According to the myth, the mountains once had wings, until Indra cut them off with his thunderbolt (*vajra*).

**8.** Has this world been fashioned from *tamāla* trees? Or from heaps of collyrium? Has it been dyed by the rays of Hara's [blue] throat? Or have the intervals between the quarters vanished here?

*Note.* Shiva's throat turned blue from the *hālāhala* poison that he drank.

**9.** Scorched by separation from his beloved lotuses, the lord of the day plunged into the ocean — and the world was filled with darkness rising from there like dense clouds of smoke.

*Note.* A red-hot body plunged into water gives off steam and smoke.

**10.** Have the quarters fallen? Or has the sky been toppled? Has the earth been heaved up? Or else has the universe indeed been blotted out by glossy, dense, beautiful floods of darkness?

**11.** As though out of merriment [pelting] one another with heaps of musk powder, the women of the quarters colour [with darkness] the depths of the pleasure grove before the artless young women agitated by Manmatha.

*Note.* The darkness is compared to the dark musk powder that people throw at one another at a festival. *pura-* may be understood as *puraḥ* ("in front"); possibly "the pleasure grove of the town" (?). The Gosvami edition (GGM) has the same text.

**12.** "Has he come or not?" So thinking, Night, her heart eager to see her husband, hastily lifted the veil of darkness from the face of the eastern horizon, lovely with a smile.

*Note.* Night's husband is the moon; before moonrise the east grows pale. *śyāmā* — "the dark one", an epithet of the night.

**13.** The cool-rayed moon, embracing doe-eyed Night tightly with his long, graceful hand, drew off her tightly fastened blue garment of darkness.

*Note.* *kara* means both "hand" and "ray".

**14.** The swan of the moon, its back adorned with the waterweed of its mark, bearing [in its beak] the slender lotus-stalk of its rays, slowly emerged from the middle of the lake of the eastern horizon.

*Note.* Swans are believed to feed on lotus stalks.

**15.** Like a silver casket [of cosmetics] for adorning the host of the wives of the quarters, the full moon then rose, scattering a flood of nectar, to adorn the artless young women.

**16.** The maidens with intoxicating eyes put on fine garments, pleasant to the body and richly perfumed by rubbing with scented powder — or was it the pure fame of Manmatha [that they put on]?

*Note.* In kavya fame (*yaśas*) is imagined as white; the white garments are the embodied fame of the god of love.

**17.** The firm thighs of the fair-browed [maidens], gleaming beneath the edges of their garments and charming with a golden loveliness, far surpassed the pretensions of the flag-decked pillars of Manmatha's city.

*Note.* The thighs are golden pillars; the edges of the garments are the flags upon them.

**18.** The braided hair of the fair-eyed [maidens], within which strips of white cloth perfumed with fragrances had been set for drying it, shone like a flood of darkness drinking the moonlight.

*Note.* The edition has *कचपासः* — a misprint for *कचपाशः*.

**19.** One, her hair dried and let loose, curling the tip of a lock with the lovely nails of her left hand, the beauty of her eyes fixed upon a mirror, shone like the city of Kama.

*Note.* The comparison with "the city of Kama" is not wholly transparent (?). The Gosvami edition (GGM) has the same text.

**20.** Another lovely woman, her hair dried and let loose, with the help of a skilled tiring-maid lightly and playfully anointed her body with excellent thick pastes of sandalwood streaked with *kuṅkuma*.

**21.** The artless young women applied to their bodies sandal paste mixed with thick camphor and musk and blended with the juice of *kuṅkuma* — like a paste made of the moon.

*Note.* *candra* here means "camphor" (*karpūra*), with a play on its meaning "moon".

**22.** "[My] body is itself the ornament of ornaments — so what need have I of these?" Thinking thus, one [maiden], beautiful of limb, put on nothing at all, but accepted only fine unguent and a garment.

**23.** "Unguent is only a barrier to touch — what else is it? So do not burden your body, O lovely one!" When her dear friend had said these words, one did not want even unguent.

**24.** "By the very lustre of my two eyes, which reaches close to them, my ears already gain exceeding beauty — what use is this?" Thinking so, one did not put on an ear-ornament of blue lotus.

*Note.* Eyes that "reach to the ears" are a mark of beauty. The edition has *कुवलस्य*, but the metre requires *kuvalayasya* (a syllable has dropped out).

**25.** Although this hair was beautiful even when let quite loose, [her] charming friends, as if to display their own skill, nevertheless deftly arranged it in a lovely coiffure.

**26.** "This [body] is indeed the mirror of the mirror — how then is it to be looked into?" Thinking so, one most beautiful of limb playfully gazed at one clear limb [reflected] in another, fair as the purest gold.

**27.** And the lord of their hearts, thrilling with joy, tenderly, with the play of His rolling, reddish eyes, gazed again and again at His whole body, gracefully attired by His beloveds.

**28.** When they had thus put on their garments and adorned themselves, the moon, the intoxication of wine and the Flower-arrowed [god] set about adorning them still more. Who does not become a helper of the great?

**29.** A dense flood of moonbeams, blotting out the boundaries between trees, settlements and all else, swiftly appeared — some charming silver creation of Manmatha.

**30.** The young women at once beheld the bright moonbeams that bring delight to the heart, and right after that, through wine, they received the splendour of Manmatha into their hearts.

*Note.* Here begins the scene of the drinking party (*pāna-goṣṭhī*), customary in a mahakavya.

**31.** "This very sweetness is the finest quality of young women!" — [so reasoning], the sweet round of wine increased it all the more with new changes and graceful turns born of Manmatha.

*Note.* The syntax of *iti* and the play on *sādhu-rīti* / *mādhurī* are rendered according to the sense (?). *madhu-vāra* — a round of drinking, the cup passed in turn at a feast. The Gosvami edition (GGM) has the same text.

**32.** The heart of their beloved was long held captive — by love (?) — by the beauty of the collyrium in the eyes of the fair-browed [maidens], as though by a swarm of bees playing over the petal of a red lotus.

*Note.* The eyes, reddened by wine, are the red lotus; the dark pupils and collyrium are the bees. The form *smarakeṇa* is unclear (?); it may perhaps refer to the maidens, "stricken by Smara". The Gosvami edition (GGM) has the same text.

**33.** This wine, [mirroring] the faces of the lovely, languid beauties, was as if perfumed with lotuses. The beloved, who had conceived a relish for drinking it, drank it even with His eye, ruddy in its lustre.

**34.** Had the wines, through mutual contact, become the honey of the fair-browed one's lips? For the lord of hearts, drinking deeply from her lips the sweet, cherished [draught], became all the more intoxicated.

**35.** What had never reached the path of hearing even in speech, that the young maiden at once did, under the sway of the intoxication of wine: to her heart's content she made her beloved's lip, again and again, a morsel to bite.

*Note.* The edition has *श्रवणवर्त्मनि* ("on the path of hearing"); the Gosvami edition (GGM) reads *śravaṇa-vartma na yātam* ("which has not reached the ear", that is, unheard of). We accept the reading with the negative (the metre is unchanged). *avadaṁśa* means "a bite" and also "a relish taken with drink" (cf. *vidaṁśa*, verse 38).

**36.** She who is more delicate even than a *śirīṣa* flower, and who had always known only fragrance and happiness, for long did not even know what the painful exhaustion that comes under the sway of intoxication is like.

*Note.* Perhaps *sauratam* ("[the happiness] of love's play") should be read in place of *saurabham* (?). The *śirīṣa* flower is the standard of delicacy. The Gosvami edition (GGM) has the same text.

**37.** Manmatha, assuming the liquid form of wine, seemed himself to enter into the young women: for in every limb all the knots grew slack.

*Note.* *granthi* means both "joint" and "knot" (of a girdle or garment).

**38.** The beloved, as though wishing to take back the wine she had given from her own mouth, pressed the petal of her lord's lip with the tips of her teeth; intent on biting it, she seemed to be preparing a relish [to go with the wine].

*Note.* A pun: *vidaṁśa* is both "a relish taken with wine" and "a bite".

**39.** One [maiden], her limbs powerless from the intoxication of wine, did not even notice her garment slipping; her hand held it back by itself, from long habit — that indeed was the wonder!

**40.** One thing is in the mind, another is said, and at every word there is a stumbling; one thing is undertaken, another is desired — does not this intoxication seem like madness?

**41.** Their speech came as if in halves and halves; His movements came as if in halves and halves. Was it to steal the hearts of the fair-browed [maidens] that Manmatha created the round of wine?

**42.** Did love rise from the heart of the fair-browed one into her eyes, beautiful with the redness of wine's intoxication? For under the burden of its weight they roam about here giddily, like wagtails.

*Note.* *Anurāga* means both "love" and "redness". *Khañja* usually means "lame"; *khañjam* here is evidently a shortened *khañjana*, the wagtail, a usual comparison for restless eyes. The Gosvami edition (GGM) has the same text.

**43.** When the beloved [maiden] bit her dear one's lip, the tips of her teeth took on colour. Was it, it seems, that the lac, as it departed [from the lip], perceived their purity and therefore desired to dwell in them?
