**44.** When the dearest one kissed the doe-eyed [maiden's] eye, rosy from drinking and bereft of collyrium, she, deeply reeling, as it were coloured the row of her lashes with the lustre of His lips.

*Note.* The agreement of *ghana-ghūrṇā* (feminine) is unclear; we refer it to the gopi. The Gosvami edition (GGM) has the same text.

**45.** When the dear one, from the intoxication of wine, bit their lower lips hard with the tips of His teeth, the sweet-voiced [maidens] cooed softly and plaintively indeed, with low sounds of the throat.

*Note.* Cf. the description of the sounds of love (*maṇita*) in the *kāma-śāstra*.

**46.** When the Crusher of Madhu bit hard the cup of His beloved's shoot-like lips, the shoot of her hand trembled too: for friendship indeed shares one joy and one sorrow.

*Note.* Lips and hands are both "shoots" (*pallava*), and hence "friends".

**47.** One [maiden], shaking the petals of her hands, speaking with a smile, made of her hissing breaths an unceasing festival; and in the sound of her swaying conch-shell bangles it was as if a conch were being sounded very softly.

*Note.* The sound of the conch (*śaṅkha*) accompanies festivals and rites.

**48.** The cheeks, the forehead, or the eyes, or the lip, or whatever else of the lovely women — kissing them, the dear one found each full of a different taste.

**49.** The dear one's hand, descending from twining their braided hair, met the elephant's frontal globes — their bosoms — and, reeling with a giddiness born of intoxication, plunged into the lakes of their bosoms.

*Note.* An image of the elephant: the bosom is likened to the frontal globes on an elephant's forehead, and *mada* means both "intoxication" and an elephant's must. An elephant in must plunges into a lake.

**50.** Having drawn away the covering garment, did He at once make His own feeling [their] garment? For He who lies in the heart made the red jars of their bosoms glow with love.

*Note.* *Hṛdayeśaya* ("lying in the heart") is an epithet of Kama, here applied to Krishna. *Anurakta* means both "loving" and "reddened".

**51.** An artless [maiden], saying "No-no-no-no…" in speech half uttered and half swallowed, trembled with her hands — ah, as if paying her teacher the fee for lessons in love.

*Note.* *Dakṣiṇā* is the fee given to the teacher at the end of study. The edition prints *गुरोरतशिक्षा*; we read *guro rata-śikṣā-*.

**52.** At the touch of Madhava's shoot-like hand her bosom thrilled, its hair standing on end: was the Flower-arrowed [god] tossing up a pair of full kadamba blossoms as balls?

*Note.* The round kadamba flower, covered with bristling filaments, is a usual comparison for a body on which the hair stands on end (*pulaka*).

**53.** "This is not, not at all, a pair of lotuses — so why, O bewildered One, do You set Your nail here?" — thus, lit up by the pearls of a broken necklace, her bosom, as it were, laughed at Him.

*Note.* In kavya laughter is conceived as white; hence the pearls are its laughter.

**54.** Is this not, as it were, the worship that first of all bears fruit in the festive rite of love's play of the Flower-arrowed [god]? For thus the Lord had the auspicious golden jar brought [to Him].

*Note.* A full golden jar (*maṅgala-kalaśa*) is an indispensable article of a rite; here it is the gopi's bosom. The sense of the verse is rendered approximately (?). The Gosvami edition (GGM) has the same text.

**55.** "Well done, well done! This is it — We are defeated, O moon-faced one; I surely admit it!" Was it thus [saying] that He wrote with His own hand a charter of victory on His beloved's bosom?

*Note.* The marks of the nails are the letters of the charter. *Jitāḥ smaḥ* means "we are defeated".

**56.** In the embrace, only the fair-eyed one's bosom entered her beloved's chest: for her heart, melted, came welling out through the windows of her eyes together with perspiration.

**57.** When the dearest one embraced the [maiden] with intoxicating eyes, did Shame, who had made its dwelling about the base of her thighs, then depart of its own accord, together with the garment whose knot had loosened?

**58.** As the garment slipped away, her hips — the meeting-place of Manmatha's lordly elephant — shone there with the girdle alone, as if with a chain for capturing it.

**59.** Slipping from the jars of her bosom, tossed this way and that by the play of the waves of her folds, sunk deep into the well of her navel — how could the dear one's hand be at rest?

*Note.* *Vali* — the three folds of the waist (*trivali*), a mark of beauty.

**60.** When the covering garment fell away, the languid [maiden] at once closed her eyes. It seems that Shame, driven out and departed, blocked her eyes as if in great anger.

**61.** Because the dear one's hand had held her waist in its fist, it grew bold there for a long time; caressing [it] here and there, together with her hips, it did not leave again.

*Note.* A waist that can be encircled by the fist is a mark of beauty.

**62.** When the Lord of Gokula, raising the chin of the sweet-lipped [maiden], drank deeply [of her lips], she then bound Him with the nooses of her arms — from pain? or from anger? or from joy?

**63.** This firm pressing of an arm more tender even than a flower is also for joy; and yet — ah, alas! — her eyes are full of tears: Madana is wayward indeed, even in love's play!

**64.** When the Lord of Vraja, deeply intoxicated with love, suddenly slipped from her hips, the girdle there itself became a "tongue" — was it from the sameness of the support and the supported?

*Note.* A play on words: *rasanā* is both "girdle" and "tongue", and *rasajñā* is "tongue" (literally "knower of taste"). The girdle jingled, as if it had begun to speak. The sense of "the sameness of the support and the supported" (the hips and Krishna, or the hips and the girdle) is unclear (?). The Gosvami edition (GGM) has the same text.

**65.** When the love's play of the young women was hindered [by weariness], Kama himself truly proved a granter of desires: that he then exchanged the roles of Radha and Krishna — this was most wondrous!

*Note.* This refers to *viparīta-rata*, love's play with the roles reversed, known from the *kāma-śāstra*.

**66.** Whether from weariness, or from intoxication, or from curiosity, or to test his own prowess — ah, Kama brought about an exchange of the roles of Radha and Krishna!

*Note.* The edition prints *स्वविक्रय°* ("selling oneself") — a misprint for *°vikrama°*.

**67.** A certain artless beauty, clinging to His body in love's play with Madhava with the roles reversed, once kissed [by Him] — how many times did she not kiss [Him] in return, ceaselessly, with a sweet smile, furtively glancing [at Him] again and again!

**68.** Whatever the lovely woman, having come upon Krishna's chest, did out of bold ardour, her dear one at once found it never experienced, unprecedented, ever new and new.

**69.** Since this [maiden] with intoxicating eyes grew so daringly bold on Krishna's chest, she then, from the tumultuous feelings born of bliss, again and again became as if swooning.

**70.** Her eyes closed, her bosom trembling, the creepers of her arms grown slack — all this in the young woman of Vraja served Madhava all the more.

**71.** That exhausted body of the doe-eyed [maiden] on Madhava's soft chest was as if offering [Him] a flood of nectar-juice, and all that [He] desired was fulfilled.

**72.** The Slayer of Madhu, the bee of His heart sated with the excellent nectar of His beloved's doings, intoxicated with the rapture of a circling bee, became ready for a second, excellent love's play.

*Note.* The edition prints *°सम्पदमत्तः* ("not intoxicated with wealth"?); we read *°sammada-mattaḥ*, "intoxicated with rapture", as in the Gosvami edition (GGM). *Sad-dvitīya-surate* may perhaps also be understood as "in love's play with an excellent companion" (*dvitīyā* — "companion") (?).

**73.** Her [healing] herb is that wine of her sweet lips; her speech is the best of mantras; the jars of her bosom are the two gems. Why then should the young woman of Vraja not enchant Krishna here, or bring Him to life?

*Note.* The three classical means of enchantment and healing: the herb (*oṣadhi*), the mantra and the gem (*maṇi*).

**74.** The satiety that arose in the middle of the night in the two lovers, bearing the marks of the splendour of new love's play, through close embraces, kisses and the nips of teeth — it, though it had firmly set its foot in their bodies, ceased, guilty [as it was].

*Note.* The metre of verses 74–80 is *mālinī*. The sense of the second half is unclear (?): either satiety, having firmly settled in their bodies, brought the play to an end and is "guilty" of this, or satiety itself, as an offender against love, withdrew. The Gosvami edition (GGM) reads *rajani rajani-madhye*.

**75.** At the close of Smara's battle, her brows having lost their curve, her eyes very slowly, little by little, closing, she, as it were, unstrings a bow long strung and puts back into the quiver an arrow long since loosed.

*Note.* The brows are the bow, the glance is the arrow.

**76.** When Madana's battle had ceased and the two weary lovers had fallen asleep at the end of the night, the lamp, stirred by the breeze, as if from delight shakes its head, bowed under the weight of drowsiness: "No, no, no — nowhere is there anything like this!"

*Note.* The edition prints *विधुरति* (there is no such form); the metre (⏑⏑⏑⏑) suits *vidhuvati*, "shakes" (?). The Gosvami edition (GGM) has the same text. *Alasa-bhara-vibhugnam* (neuter) is taken with *śīrṣam*: the flame, the lamp's "head", is bowed as if with drowsiness.

**77.** "Here, here I am, risen! Why then do you remain now?" — so [saying], the hot-rayed sun, red with ripened anger, at the end of the night thrust, as it were, its hand through the opening of the window lattice: did it not put out the night-lamp?

*Note.* The edition prints *परिणतकोपा*; by the sense — *pariṇata-kopāl lohitaḥ*, "red from ripened anger". The Gosvami edition (GGM) has the same text. *Kara* means both "hand" and "ray".

**78.** "The beauty of the blue lotuses has faded; the most tender lotus stalk is greyed and crumpled; the pair of chakravakas is disturbed; the lovely strip of the sandbank is marked with nails. So tell [us], O lake, what fortunate one has plunged into You?"

*Note.* An allegory: a friend teases the beloved — evidently Radha (cf. 79, where She is the "lotus") — calling Her a "lake". The lotuses are Her eyes, the stalk Her arms, the chakravakas Her bosom, the sandbank Her hips.

**79.** "Your body is grey with incomparable pollen; You have not a particle of strength to beat Your wings; You seem to reel… This is understood: there is no fault of Yours at all, O bee — it is only some power of the lotus."

*Note.* The friends tease Krishna (the "bee") and Radha (the "lotus").

**80.** Thus, in seclusion, at the beginning of the day, in the presence of their companions, there was again between the two lovers a certain indescribable conduct: full of longing, with laughter and jests on all sides marked by the beauty of soft words, eager for each other in most secret play.

**Colophon:** *iti śrī-kṛṣṇa-caitanya-caritāmṛte mahākāvye daśamaḥ sargaḥ* — "Thus [ends] the tenth sarga in the mahakavya 'Sri Krishna-Chaitanya-charitamrita'." (The colophon gives no title for the sarga.)

---

## Sarga Eleven — verses 1–89

**Title of the sarga:** none — the colophon of the edition gives no title for this sarga.
**Metre:** *śikhariṇī* (17 syllables per pada: ⏑————— ⏑⏑⏑⏑⏑—— ⏑⏑⏑—); verse 66 — *upajāti* (a quotation from the Bhagavatam); verses 87–89 — *mandākrāntā*.
**Total verses:** 89 (verse 20 has six padas).

**Contents of the passage:** the night-time play in the house of the Acharya; the sannyasa at Katwa; the journey to Puri.
- 1–6 — Gaura arranges a night-time play in the house of the Acharya (cf. Chaitanya-bhagavata, Madhya 18): the roles are assigned; the wives of the brahmanas watch in secret together with Shachi; a guard stands at the door.
- 7–18 — the entrances: Srivasa as Narada, Shuklambara as his disciple, Gadadhara in a woman's role (the bathing in the month of Magha); Narada on the power of the Name (Ajamila); Srivasa's *nāndī*; Haridasa, staff in hand, rouses the three worlds and proclaims the command of Vaikuntha.
- 19–23 — Advaita in the costume of Krishna (with the Lord's flute and peacock feather); Nityananda as an old woman; Gadadhara in a woman's attire.
- 24–34 — a *kulaka* of eleven verses: the Lord Himself, in the guise of the Beloved (Lakshmi, then Girija), enters the shrine room.
- 35–37 — the Lord on the couch, Haridasa on His lap; seven days of dancing.
- 38–39 — Srivasa's question about the fruit of the Name; the answer about the four yugas.
- 40–48 — the intention to leave; the dream of the "great saying" and Murari's interpretation (the "genitive *tatpuruṣa*": *tasya tvam asi*); Keshava Bharati; the Lord departs across the Ganga.
- 49–57 — the lament of the townspeople and the women; the initiation (the Lord whispers the mantra to the guru), the tonsure (the barber weeps; "Hare Krishna"), the saffron cloth and the staff; the departure into the Radha country.
- 58–68 — three days in ecstasy; boys crying "Hari!" save Him from the river; Nityananda summons everyone to Advaita's house; the meeting (a quotation of Bhagavatam 11.23.57).
- 69–77 — at Advaita's house; the farewell (Haridasa with a straw between his teeth); the journey to Puri with Nityananda, Gadadhara and Mukunda.
- 78–86 — Remuna (the crown of Gopinatha), Sakshi-gopala, the broken staff, Yajapura (Jajpur), Bhubaneshvara, Kamalapura; Jagannatha.
- 87–89 (*mandākrāntā*) — conclusion: the well-being of Navadvipa while the Lord was present there.

**1.** Thus, having joyfully recalled all that play of the fair-eyed [gopis], this exceedingly sweet Madana of Vrindavana, whose radiant body is fair as molten gold, made preparations on every side, with those to whom His feet are dear [gathered] before Him, to sport in those same moods.

*Note.* The sarga opens with the night-time play in the house of the Acharya; cf. Chaitanya-bhagavata, Madhya 18 (verses 28–31: the play is staged in the house of Chandrashekhara Acharya); so too Murari 2.15.7 ff. The "Acharya" here is evidently Chandrashekhara Acharya himself.

**2.** Dividing this night in due order according to the watches, He assigned suitable roles, for the most part, to His dear ones. Then, having pondered [it] unceasingly, the lover of spectacle joyfully made the very spacious courtyard in the Acharya's house the place for the dancing.

**3.** When the charming, delightful place had been arranged, the wives of the best of brahmanas, full of great eagerness, learned the Lord's wish and, together with Shachi-devi, very quietly entered the house, showing a restraint befitting the occasion.

**4.** Greatly eager to behold the Lord's dancing, they hid on the veranda of the house and sat there secretly and timidly. Unblinking, their bodies motionless, they were like lovely figures painted in fresh colours on the surface of a panel.

*Note.* The edition has *सहभयैः* (*saha-bhayaiḥ*); the form is unclear (?) and is taken here as "timidly". It is this reading of the edition that gives the second pada its seventeen syllables. The Gosvami edition (GGM) has the same text.

**5.** Having stationed at the door one [man] — steadfast, graceful and full of valour — intent that not a single [outsider] should come in there even for a moment, and having thus admitted into the house only those closest [to Him], this moon Gaura is ever victorious in great bliss.

*Note.* The guard at the door is not named in the text. In the Chaitanya-bhagavata (Madhya 18.40–45) Haridasa, staff in hand, is the "constable of Vaikuntha" (*vaikuṇṭha-koṭāla*), who warns everyone.

**6.** Then, having made one room the dressing-place, the Lord, eager for the dance and charming in His love, entered it to put on His costume. The beautiful [Lord], lovely with a flood of boundless mercy, shone among His own people, who bore supreme love for His feet.

*Note.* *Nepathya* is the backstage, the place where actors put on their costumes.

**7.** At the beginning of the night Srivasa, most dear to the lotus feet of the Lord, came in there first, having assumed the splendid guise of the best of sages: with tawny matted locks, telling a rosary of crystal beads with his hand, he entered like the foremost of brahmanas.

*Note.* The best of sages is Narada (cf. *devarṣi*, verses 9 and 11). *Urvī-deva*, "a god of the earth", is a brahmana.

**8.** Then came one of his servants, holding a water-pot and an excellent seat of *darbha* grass, his body white with the clay of the heavenly river — on earth he was called Shuklambara. Possessed by the divinity, he at once bowed to the best of sages and spoke most sweetly to the Lord's beloved one named Gadadhara.

*Note.* *Dyu-vāpī*, "the heavenly pool", is the Ganga. With the clay of the Ganga Vaishnavas draw the marks of *tilaka* on the body.

**9.** "O you, bow down to the feet of the divine sage and say this: 'May I in [the age of] Kali be devoted to the service of the feet of the beautiful Lord.'" Hearing this, the sage said: "O fair-faced one, always bathe in the river of the gods in the month of Magha."

*Note.* Gadadhara is playing a woman's role; hence *niratā* (feminine) and the address "fair-faced one". Magha (January–February) is the month of auspicious bathing in the Ganga. The edition has *अवनम्या वद इदं*; by the metre we read *avanamyāvada idam* (*ā-vada*, "say"), with hiatus before *idam*. *Devarṣiś* is formally a nominative; by the sense it is "[the feet] of the divine sage".

**10.** "Then, by that merit, you will thus meet the lotus feet of the Lord. And afterwards this was done by you [ladies]. Now, by the blessing of that best of sages, you have become dear to the Lord's feet and have come [here] — is that not known [to you], O fair-faced one?"

*Note.* Who is speaking (the sage or Shuklambara) and how *tad anu bhavatībhiḥ kṛtam idam* should be understood are unclear (?). *Yātaḥ* is masculine despite the address "fair-faced one" — a play on the fact that the woman's role is played by Gadadhara. The Gosvami edition (GGM) has the same text.

**11.** Then the divine sage himself, thrilling with rapture, loudly and gracefully proclaimed the greatness of devotion to Him, whose weight is unknown: "What shall we say of the supreme glory of the Name? For even a sinner, a brahmana in semblance only, the husband of a maidservant, was freed [by it] from a heap of sins!"

*Note.* An allusion to Ajamila (Bhagavatam 6.1–2): a fallen brahmana who lived with a maidservant was saved by uttering the name of Narayana at his last hour.

**12.** When he had spoken thus, all the people, supremely delighted, loudly performed a wondrous kirtana of the names of Hari. And Srivasa, his body thrilling, joyfully and eagerly danced there in front, as if first performing the *nāndī*.

*Note.* The *nāndī* is the benedictory prelude with which a Sanskrit drama opens.

**13.** When he, having tasted the rasa of dancing and sankirtana, had gone out, the beautiful, most sweet Haridasa entered, wearing a splendid costume. Having entered, he at once took up a fine staff and shone in the courtyard like a host of moons.

*Note.* *Vetra* — the staff of a doorkeeper or watchman. Cf. Chaitanya-bhagavata, Madhya 18.40–45, 100, where Haridasa plays the role of the watchman (*koṭāla*): staff in hand, he goes round everyone and rouses them with the cry "Wake up!"

**14.** "Let them say it loudly, loudly! Say 'Hari!' Overwhelmed by love, sing His song!" — so he cried ardently, charming as the moon, pointing with the tip of his staff; overcome by a joy that surged at every step, he strove, as it were, to awaken the three worlds fast asleep.

**15.** "From unbounded Vaikuntha, from beside the lotus feet of the Lord, I have now descended to earth first, by His command. Hear those supreme words of His command, sweet as nectar, which manifestly revive those swallowed by the serpent of Kali!

*Note.* A play on *akuṇṭha* ("unblunted, unbounded") and *vaikuṇṭha*.

**16.** "[He has no need of] people unless they always bathe in the stream of the river of the nectarean rasa of the songs of the names — this is what He now desires here. He wishes to come here and scatter this throughout the whole world; therefore let the multitudes of the qualities of the beautiful Bhagavan be glorified by you!

*Note.* The syntax of the first half (*vinā … lokān iti*) is rendered according to the sense (?). The Gosvami edition (GGM) has the same text.

**17.** Now, having drunk in His command with your ears, dance at every step, relishing the rasa of the sankirtana you perform!" Having heard this charming play of speech arisen from the moon of his face, all of them, their limbs thrilling abundantly…

*Note.* Verses 17–18 form one sentence.

**18.** …sang a charming song to the sound of ringing hand-claps and in time with a row of mighty mridangas, and the excellent [Haridasa] himself danced too. Having tasted again and again the rasa of dancing and sankirtana, he went out and afterwards rested, joyful.

**19.** Then Advaita entered there, imitating the dress and deeds of Bhagavan, joyfully holding a flute in both hands. The Lord — His very own dress, His own flute, the crest of peacock plume, His own yellow garment, and even His beauty…

*Note.* Verses 19–20 form one sentence.

**20.** …having given [all this] to Him, the Lord — His body radiant as pure gold, bearing the splendour of the supreme and noble hero — let Him act at will in this dance. Then the lotus-eyed one, indescribably enchanting with all these [adornments] in His supremely sweet form, entered the assembly and, His body anointed with sandal-paste, performed a most sweet dance to the sound of swiftly rising mridangas, keeping tempo, rhythm and measure.

*Note.* A verse of six padas. The nominatives of the first half (*kalita-kaladhautojjvala-tanuḥ*, *dhṛta-parama-san-nāyaka-ruciḥ*) agree grammatically with *prabhuḥ* (verse 19), the subject of *cakre*; by the sense they may also be referred to Advaita as the hero of the dance (?). By the grammar (*pradāya amuṁ cakre*; "with all these", *taiḥ taiḥ sarvaiḥ*) the dancer of the second half is evidently Advaita in the role of Krishna. The Gosvami edition (GGM) has the same text.

**21.** While He was dancing thus, there entered after Him the Avadhuta, relishing the costume of an old woman, His body shaking, His grey hair charmingly dishevelled. Having entered, possessed, overpowered by the very greatest madness, He then danced, tearing and breaking the appearance of the old woman whose role He played.

*Note.* The Avadhuta is Nityananda.

**22.** When He had finished, the one named Gadadhara entered with the singers, having assumed the appearance of a woman's role with all its adornments, his hands shining with bangles of conch-shell. As the mridanga resounded very swiftly and loudly, he shone there with rhythm, measure and the art of dancing.

**23.** As he danced then, shining with the splendour of his sweet costume, to the art of sweet music with a hundred modulations from a row of mridangas, was not nectar attained by the people all around — immersed again and again in an ocean of bliss, unblinking, their limbs stiffened with numbness?

**24.** [Then the Lord Himself entered.] His splendour flashed forth from His absorption in the guise of the Beloved; the radiance of His dawning smile dispelled the darkness; He was overpowered by the secret ardour of relishing. With the flowers shining in the mass of His thick, glossy, curving, luxuriant braids, He displayed the beauty of a host of stars emerging from a cloud.

*Note.* From this verse onwards Gaura is described in a woman's guise. The attributes stand in the feminine, since it is the role that is described. The edition gives a variant: *vilasat* or *vigalat* ("falling"). The subject and the verb of the long description come in verse 34, at the end of the *kulaka* (verses 24–34): "this form … manifested the mood of Lakshmi, and then of Girija — and entered the shrine room".

**25.** [His was] the playful arching of the brows, whose dancing surpassed the sportiveness of a bee; the smiling corners of the lively eyes, like the petals of shining blue lotuses; and He bore the lustre of the teeth gliding along the path of the lips, gleaming like pure copper — like moonlight mingled with the rays of the rising sun.

**26.** He bore great beauty by a spotless necklace: it loved to play about the gleaming, conch-like neck and swayed strongly over the full bosom, like the current of the river of the sky, freed from the fear of a fall from a high mountain.

*Note.* According to tradition the Ganga descends from heaven upon the head of Shiva, on Mount Kailasa. The necklace resting on the "hills" of the bosom is like the Ganga, no longer threatened by a fall.

**27.** With thighs smooth as the stems of a golden plantain, with feet akin to a red lotus in the first fair stage [of its blossoming], gracefully clad in a fine linen garment — thus the beautiful form of the Lord then entered there with a light step.

*Note.* The edition marks a *kulaka* here (verses 24–27): the subject of the whole description is *prabhoḥ śrīman-mūrtiḥ* ("the beautiful form of the Lord", feminine), and therefore all the attributes stand in the feminine.

**28.** Then, while the beautiful form was sporting in seclusion, the nectar-rayed moon shone blissfully, as if it had reached its eleventh digit. And the sandal-scented breeze, the friend of all those fragrances, blew gently, playing gracefully with the hem of that linen garment.

*Note.* The moon is divided into sixteen digits (*kalā*). "The eleventh digit" may be a hint at the night of Ekadashi (?). The Gosvami edition (GGM) has the same text.

**29.** Then, with those singers, following the swift sound of the mridanga, [the form] danced gracefully, with charming gestures, interweaving rhythm and the rest with the tempo. The waist bent, and the steps, shining with the ripeness of sweetness, were sweet with the tinkling of jewelled anklets.

**30.** And He bore the lotus of His face, open, with drops of perspiration lightly rising on it, like a golden moon studded with pearls; and on the shining forehead, framed by charming curls, He bore vermilion, like a lovely ray of the sun reddened by twilight and touched by darkness.

*Note.* Vermilion (*sindūra*) on the forehead and in the parting of the hair is the mark of a married woman. The edition has *रुज्यदलके*; we read *rucyad-alake* (?). The Gosvami edition (GGM) has the same text.

**31.** With movements of the hands, resonant with the jingling of bangles, He made a line of bees circle high above; and, raising again and again the creeper of the brows, curved like the bow of Manasija, He somehow darkened the vault of the sky.

*Note.* Manasija, "the mind-born", is Kama.

**32.** He made the languid waist dance — so slender that a hand could span it — gleaming under the strokes of the hem of the linen garment as it slipped from the bosom, and weighty in grace with the curves of its folds; and the full hips, moist with perspiration, in the gleam of pure radiance glinting from beneath the loosening knot of the sash, seemed as if of quite another kind.

*Note.* *Kara-mitam*, "measured by the hand", is taken here of the slender waist that a hand can span (?). The Gosvami edition (GGM) has the same text.

**33.** Again and again, whirling like a wheel, He scattered the flowers falling from the hair, charmingly set off by the circling bees. Did He not Himself, in the rapture of the dance, hold above the moon of His face a wondrous white parasol adorned with fine emerald stripes?

*Note.* The white flowers circling above the head are the white parasol (an emblem of royalty); the dark bees are the emerald stripes upon it.

**34.** And, His body bowed by the exceedingly great sweetness of the rapture and madness of the dance, veiled in a lovely radiance illumined by the music, that form manifested in turn first the mood of Lakshmi and then that of Girija as well — and openly entered the shrine room. *(A kulaka of eleven verses.)*

*Note.* Verses 24–34 form one *kulaka*. Cf. Chaitanya-bhagavata, Madhya 18: the Lord in the mood of Rukmini (verses 70–98), then He enters in the form of the Original Shakti (Adya-shakti, verses 120–154) and sits as Mahalakshmi (verse 163). Girija, "the daughter of the mountain", is Parvati.

**35.** Then they bowed to Her, who was seated upon a large couch, Her form full of rasa, and with artful words of praise they sang: "O Goddess, bestow love everywhere!" Then She at once took Haridasa on Her lap like a child.

*Note.* The edition has *खट्टायाः*; the usual form is *khaṭvāyāḥ* ("couch"). Cf. the Chaitanya-bhagavata (Madhya 18.203–208): the Lord in the form of the Mother of the universe feeds the devotees at Her breast like Her children.

**36.** Having thus spent the night in various playful acts and pastimes, She — He whose deeds are wondrous — went home in the morning. And then for seven days the radiant [Lord], His body anointed with sandal-paste, again performed many most sweet dances there.

*Note.* The change of gender (*sā*, "She" — *citra-caritaḥ*, "He") reflects the Lord's stepping out of the role.

**37.** All around, loudly, in every direction [rang] the sounds of mridangas and other [instruments]. All were intoxicated with rapture — how much, rich in rasa, did they not utter! For seven days, while the beautiful Sri Bhagavan was sporting, the world was full of flowers, garlands, perfumes and sandal-paste.

**38.** Then, at the end of the seven days, Srivasa, with awe, wonder and joy, said to Him who shone with the effulgence of a hundred suns: "The chanting of the names that has been ordained here in [the age of] Kali — pray, does it surely bear fruit when [performed] with guile, or not? Tell me that."

*Note.* *Śāṭhye*, "with guile", is taken as "when [the names are chanted] insincerely" (?). The Gosvami edition (GGM) has the same text.

**39.** "O brahmana, in the Krita, in the Treta, and after that in the Dvapara-yuga, everything is fully accomplished by meditation and the rest. Seeing [people's] incapacity for that in Kali, the Lord Himself manifestly became here that which is called the Name — how, then, could its fruit be small here?"

*Note.* Cf. Bhagavatam 12.3.52: "What was attained in the Krita by meditation on Vishnu, in the Treta by sacrifices, in the Dvapara by temple worship, is attained in Kali by the chanting of [the names of] Hari."

**40.** Having spoken thus, Gaura, His eyes filled with tears, said something else: "I am not able to stay. I shall certainly go home." Hearing that, Murari said in alarm: "O Lord, although it is fitting for You to do [so], looking at the people we do not think so."

*Note.* Evidently a hint at the intention to accept sannyasa. "Home" may also mean the Lord's true abode. *Gṛham abhi* may also be construed with *sthātum*: "I am not able to stay at home; I shall certainly go [away]" (?).

**41.** Then, on the next day, the beautiful [Lord], bathed in tears, said: "A certain brahmana spoke the great saying into My ear in a dream. For this reason I weep, [thinking]: 'Having left the feet of the Lord, what else is fitting for Me?' There is no peace for Me here even for a moment."

*Note.* *Mahā-vākya* — the Upanishadic "great saying" (*tat tvam asi*, "you are That"), imparted at initiation into sannyasa. Cf. the account of this dream in Murari Gupta (2.18.1–6).

**42.** Hearing this, Murari Gupta at once said: "O Lord, take that word there as a genitive *tatpuruṣa*!" Hearing this, the Master was then gladdened at heart; but the others, hearing [it all], were deeply pained at heart.

*Note.* Murari proposes a Vaishnava reading of *tat tvam asi*: not "you are That", but, as a genitive *tatpuruṣa*, *tasya tvam asi*, "you are His", that is, you are the servant of the Lord. The devotees grieve, understanding that the Lord is thinking of sannyasa.

**43.** Then by the will of providence there came before the Lord a certain sannyasi, known on earth as Keshava, with the title Bharati. Seeing the Master weeping thus endlessly, he said again and again in astonishment: "You are Shuka or Prahlada!"

*Note.* Keshava Bharati is the sannyasi from whom the Lord accepted sannyasa at Katwa. Shuka and Prahlada are the models of great devotees.

**44.** Hearing His own praise, He, doubly distraught, again wept bitterly. And this [sannyasi], greatly astonished, said again: "You are the Lord Vishnu — this I have truly understood!" Hearing this, the beautiful [Lord] desired to accept renunciation here.

*Note.* *Nyasana* (renunciation) is sannyasa.

**45.** Then Mukunda said: "Look at Him constantly, without blinking, while the Lord dwells at home and has not yet departed!" Then the Lord said to Srivasa: "Let Me tell you this: from here I shall wander ceaselessly in every direction for the sake of love."

**46.** Again Srivasa said fearfully: "How is it possible [for us], separated from You, to live? O mighty one, the heart will burn without end!" Then the Lord said: "I shall stay in your house." "So be it," [said] he, and for a moment he gained steadiness of mind.

*Note.* Cf. the Lord's promise to abide in Srivasa's house — Murari 2.18.17–23.

**47.** Then in the evening He went to Murari's house and, instructing him, related Advaita's life and told him to take shelter with Him. Then on the next day the beautiful, immensely merciful Lord, full of forbearance, at once set out for a certain district beyond the Ganga.

*Note.* The district beyond the Ganga is Katwa, where Keshava Bharati lived.

**48.** Then all of them, crushed by ever-mounting grief, anxious, lamented piteously: "O Lord, where have You gone?" As they reflected, [they realised] — alas! — that seven days before He of splendid form had been seen with a heart agitated by the desire for renunciation.

**49.** Then the people there, seeing Him thus on every side, lamented indescribably again and again with wailing and anguish: "Ah, O Creator, what is this play of yours? He, handsome as Kama, goes about wishing to take sannyasa! Oh, how cruel you are!"

**50.** The women said: "Alas, alas! Ah, Heaven forbid! Utterly hard-hearted [is he]: [such is] the caprice of the Creator! How [can] this thunderbolt strike again and again? Ah, the beauty, the character, the sweetness and exquisite loveliness — alas! — where are they, and where is sannyasa? Again and again this mind [of ours] is bewildered!"

*Note.* The turn *kva … kva* ("where the one — and where the other") expresses incompatibility. *Kaṭhinaḥ* (masculine) does not agree with *vaicitryam* (neuter): it evidently refers to the Creator.

**51.** Weeping thus, yet manifesting a spreading bliss, the Lord addressed the weeping people as "O Mother! O Father!" and said: "Indeed, bestow on Me again and again the blessing that there may be love [in Me] for the pair of the Lord's lotus feet."

**52.** Humbly coming with them to the guru's house, He, who had assumed the manner befitting a disciple, bowed down many times. Then, having performed what was prescribed and greeted His own guru, He — Heaven forbid! — earnestly spoke into his ear the great saying received in a dream.

*Note.* The edition has *स्वपुरम्* (*sva-puram*); by the sense we read *sva-gurum* (?). According to tradition, Chaitanya Himself whispered into Keshava Bharati's ear the mantra He had received in a dream: the disciple initiated the guru (cf. v. 56). The Gosvami edition (GGM) has the same text.

**53.** Then the beautiful [Lord] summoned a certain barber, supremely blessed and supremely fortunate, and — alas! — ordered him to remove His hair. But he, overwhelmed by love, weeping without end, his body trembling, out of fear — Heaven forbid! — could not do anything at all.

**54.** Then Sri Gauranga, splendid of form, said exceedingly joyfully: "Say 'Hare Krishna' loudly, again and again!" Then he, uttering it, lovely with his hair bristling on end, weeping, crushed by many sorrows, began that work.

*Note.* In the Chaitanya-bhagavata (Madhya 28.140–152) the barber is not named: he weeps and delays, and the Lord bids everyone sing the Name. The name "Madhu" (Madhava the barber) is a later tradition; it is not found in our sources.

**55.** Then those who stood there even for a moment — Heaven forbid! — crying unrestrainedly and most piteously "O Mother! O Father!", placing their hands on their heads, given again and again to reproach, cursing their own lives — how many laments did they not compose!

**56.** First He Himself, under a pretext, as it were became the guru; then He received the mantra in the manner of a disciple, scattering nothing but mercy over the world. Then, seeing the Lord filled with thrills of ecstasy and wishing to depart, he [Keshava Bharati] at once said "Take [these]!" and gave Him the saffron cloth, the staff and the rest.

*Note.* Cf. v. 52: the Lord first spoke the "great saying" into His guru's ear, and only then received the mantra from him.

**57.** Having taken the staff and the rest, Gauranga — supremely wise, though not master of Himself — spent the day [there] for the sake of keeping His guru's word. Then, having taken leave of him, He whose deeds are hidden, incomparable, supreme and beyond this world went to the Radha country, filled with hundreds of pious deeds.

*Note.* Radha (*rāḍha*; not to be confused with Sri Radha) is a region of western Bengal. The edition has *गूढोपम*: possibly *gūḍhopama-*, "like something hidden", or *gūḍha-anupama-*; we take it as the latter. The Gosvami edition (GGM) has the same text.

**58.** On the way, meditating again and again on His own deeds, overwhelmed by bliss, melted with love for His own name, powerless, He stumbled at every step. Now He sang in distress, now He cried out with a cry of anguish; now He walked slowly, now with the swift gait of the king of beasts.

**59.** Not hearing His own name in that country even for a moment, the Lord in despair [resolved]: "I shall give up My body," and went to the river. As He was sinking into the water, He was seen by children noisily crying "Say 'Hari'!" — and the Lord, melted with love, became numb with the tears that fell at every step.

*Note.* "His own name" is the name of Hari. Cf. Chaitanya-charitamrita, Madhya 3.13–18: in the Radha country the cowherd boys, seeing the Lord, cry "Hari! Hari!", and He rejoices (the same in Chaitanya-bhagavata, Antya 1).

**60.** Then, hearing them loudly say "Hari!", the Lord, maddened with love, fell to the ground and wept bitterly. Going a little farther, He then performed the proper begging of alms, and thus went on — laughing, dancing, singing, and at times weeping.

**61.** Now in the moods of a gopi, now in the moods of a servant, and now likewise in the moods of majesty, the beautiful [Lord], intent on delight through the art of dancing, melted with boundless love, walked ceaselessly westward, and for three days did not remember Himself even for a moment.

**62.** Then, when by providence His course thus turned to the south, the beautiful [Lord] came to Himself: "Where indeed am I going?" Having reflected thus in His mind, He resolved to go to Advaita's house, and spoke very sweetly to Nityananda Prabhu:

*Note.* Verses 62–63 form a single speech. Cf. Chaitanya-charitamrita, Madhya 3.16–24 (and Chaitanya-bhagavata, Antya 1), where Nityananda, having instructed the cowherd boys, cunningly leads the Lord to the Ganga and to Advaita's house in Shantipura; Murari 3.4.2–10.

**63.** "Go quickly to Navadvipa, lovely on the bank of the river of the gods, and sweetly tell those there in My words: 'Go quickly to Advaita's house; I shall come there.'" "So be it," [said] He, and set off at once.

**64.** Then, going there, He [Nityananda], joyful at heart, told each one the words of the Lord and at once brought [them all] there. And Shachi too, greatly agitated and supremely glad, set out there. What more is there to say? Navadvipa itself, as it were, had departed.

**65.** Then on the next day they suddenly saw on a certain road the beautiful moon, Gaura, holding a water-pot and staff, wearing a pair of fine saffron garments, the likeness of the dense radiance of lightning — like the best of golden mountains, lofty-peaked and clad in red ochre.

*Note.* A play on words: *guru-śikha* means "with a lofty peak" (of a mountain) and also "with a long tuft of hair"; *gairika* is the red ochre on a mountain's slopes and also the ochre (saffron) cloth of a sannyasi.

**66.** "Having taken up this devotion to the Supreme Self, practised by the most ancient great sages, I shall cross the darkness whose far shore is hard to reach, by service to the feet of Mukunda alone."

*Note.* A quotation: Srimad-Bhagavatam 11.23.57 (the song of the Avanti brahmana who accepted sannyasa). The metre is *upajāti*. In the edition the verse received a serial number. *Sa* — in the Bhagavata, "that [brahmana]"; in the Lord's mouth, "I".

**67.** Reciting this shloka again and again on every road, He danced most ardently, His body shaken by the flood of tears from His eyes. Seeing Him, they rejoiced like those long dead [regaining] the breath of life, and in the excess of their joy they fell to the ground and bowed down to the Lord.

**68.** Some with words, others with the nectar of His smile, others with a glance moved by mercy, and others joyfully with the art of His touch — thus the Lord greatly pleased those devoted to His lotus feet. Then, melted with exceeding bliss and exceedingly merciful, He went to Advaita's house.

**69.** Then Gauranga, seated on an excellent, pure seat, having embraced Advaita, whose body was bathed in the tears of His eyes, sang together with them, as they wept, most charming hymns shining with the Name, endowed with the weight and depth of [His] qualities.

**70.** Then He partook of the most sweet food offered by Advaita, and on the next day, in the morning, He joyfully said to each person: "I am going to the Kshetra to behold the feet of the Lord. You must perform constant sankirtana of Hari here."

*Note.* The Kshetra ("the sacred field") is Purushottama-kshetra, Puri, the abode of Jagannatha.

**71.** Having thus dismissed them all and embraced Advaita, who was utterly flooded with the tears of His eyes, the Lord made ready to depart. Then Haridasa, placing a blade of grass between his teeth, fell to the ground before the Lord's lotus feet, his mind boundlessly longing.

*Note.* A blade of grass between the teeth is a sign of utmost humility.

**72.** Seeing him thus, the Lord said: "For your sake I shall ceaselessly speak before Jagannatha, bowing down. So rise, rise, take comfort!" Having said this, He embraced him and let him go; and as He was thus going, the embodied Advaita then said to Him:

**73.** "What will become [of us] here at this departure of Yours? Tell [me] that, O mighty one! How shall I keep my life? How shall I cross [the ocean of] separation?" To Him, overcome with agitation, the Lord said: "If You act thus, then what [use] is My going?" — and, having spoken so, He was moved.

*Note.* *Calitaḥ* can also mean "set off"; but according to v. 74 the Lord stays several more days, so we translate "moved, shaken" (?). The Gosvami edition (GGM) has the same text.

**74.** Then, though He wished to go to the Kshetra of Jagannatha, [the Lord], yielding to His dear ones, out of love for Advaita and for the joy of Haridasa, who lay bowed down, spent several days eating together with His own people the incomparable food cooked by Shachi-devi.

**75.** Then, as the Lord was going thus — ah! — a certain excellent brahmana said [to Him]: "O best of lords, let me see this most sweet body of Yours." And the merciful Lord, drawing the cloth off His limbs, shone thus utterly spotless, like the moon when the clouds depart.

*Note.* Grammatically, it is the merciful Lord Himself who draws off the cloth (*sa … karuṇaḥ prabhuḥ*).

**76.** Placing Nityananda, joyful at heart, in front, the immensely merciful Lord went on, surrounded by those devoted to His lotus feet — Gadadhara, the brahmanas and others, together with Mukunda. Ah, all of them could then see Him only with difficulty, through their grief!

*Note.* *Gadādhṛk* ("bearer of the mace") is a synonym of the name Gadadhara. The compound *gadādhṛg-viprādyaiḥ* may also be read "by the brahmana Gadadhara and others".

**77.** Thus on the way Gauranga, chanting His own qualities and name together with those to whom His feet are dear, shone, weeping ceaselessly. And nowhere were they, rich in the steadfast mercy of the beautiful [Lord], stopped by the crowds of toll-collectors whose hearts rejoiced in the taking of tolls.

*Note.* *Dānin* is a collector of tolls at ferries and checkpoints. Cf. the episodes of the journey to Puri: Murari 3.5.7–9, 3.7 (the toll-collectors, the detention of Mukunda); Chaitanya-bhagavata, Antya 2.

**78.** Then [the Lord] saw that Gopinatha of great power whose residence makes Remuna shine. As He bowed down, His head touching the ground, a crown of flowers slipped from the head of that Bhagavan and, as all the people looked on, fell on the Lord's head.

*Note.* Remuna (near Balasore in Orissa) is the site of the temple of Gopinatha Kshirachora.

**79.** Then Sri Gauranga [came] to the district called Kataka, [where] He who has attained fame in the world as Sakshi-gopinatha [dwells]. The crowds of people at once beheld the two of Them: They were distinguished [only] by fair and dark lustre, but were not different in great power and the rest.

*Note.* Sakshi-gopala ("Gopala the Witness") is the Deity near Kataka (Cuttack). The syntax of the first half is elliptical. The Gosvami edition (GGM) has the same text.

**80.** On the way Sri Gauranga gave His staff into the hand of the avadhuta and Himself went ahead, His mind overwhelmed with bliss. And He [the avadhuta], walking behind, having pondered it again and again in His mind, broke that staff, greatly pleased with His playful prank.

*Note.* A famous episode: Nityananda breaks Chaitanya's sannyasa staff.

**81.** Then, when [the avadhuta] had come nearer, the Lord — ah! — said to Him in alarm: "Where is My staff? Tell Me!" And He in turn replied: "By chance My foot slipped on the ground, and so it broke. What [can be done]?" And after that the Lord grew very angry.

*Note.* The edition has *जगदेतेन*: by the metre (⏑⏑) this is not *jagāda* but *jagade tena*, "it was said by Him".

**82.** Thus agitated, and having pondered much in His mind, He went on, His face shining sweetly with the singing of songs of Hari's names. Joyfully beholding all the Deities along the way, He came to the holy, blessed, most lovely city of Yaja.

*Note.* Yaja-nagari is Yajapura (Jajpur) on the Vaitarani.

**83.** Then, in the sacred field of Ekamra, the Lord saw the Subduer of Smara, offered him praise a hundredfold and fell to the ground; and the god Shiva, delighted, as it were performed the rite of worshipping Him with essences of sandal and fragrant aguru and with other prasada.

*Note.* Ekamra-kshetra is Bhubaneshvara, the temple of Shiva Lingaraja. Shiva burned Kama (Smara) with the fire of his third eye.

**84.** Going on from there, He reached lovely Kamalapura, bowed to Kapalesha and here bathed according to rule in the Bhargi. Then He beheld that temple, lovely as Kailasa with its lofty spire, with its gleaming disc and its banner fluttering in the wind.

*Note.* The temple is not named. Grammatically it could be Kapalesha's; but the "gleaming disc" (the Nila-chakra) and the banner are the marks of Jagannatha's temple, visible from afar from Kamalapura (Atharanala). According to tradition, it was here that the Lord, seeing the banner of the temple, was overcome with ecstasy (?). The Gosvami edition (GGM) has the same text.

**85.** Falling to the ground together with all of them, He thoroughly bathed the surface of the earth with the water poured from His lotus eyes. Then, having come to the Kshetra, the beautiful [Lord], showing supreme devotion, entered [the presence of] the Best of lords and, beholding Him, bowed down.

*Note.* The Best of lords is Jagannatha.

**86.** Gazing again and again on the most sweet moon of His [Jagannatha's] face, He bathed His own body with the streaming tears of His eyes. And Jagannatha too, looking at Him with unblinking lotus eyes, became as if endlessly immersed in an ocean of love.

*Note.* Unblinking eyes are a mark of the gods and of the very form of Jagannatha.

**87.** Thus in the land of Sri Navadvipa He who flooded all regions with the sweetness arising from His splendid appearance performed, with the greatest ardour, freely and to His heart's content, the art of dancing together with His own people — with perfumes, garlands, essences of sandal and abundant streams of camphor.

*Note.* Verses 87–89 are in the *mandākrāntā* metre: a summary of the Navadvipa period.

**88.** When He sported quite freely in the land of Sri Navadvipa, in every house Lakshmi herself, embodied, was always present; in every place the abundance of happiness became as if embodied; and day after day everyone's love for the Lord was ever new.

**89.** By the mercy of the Lord no one at all, whoever he might be, had sleep, or fear, or hunger and thirst, or self-will, or fear of Yama's rod of Time and the like, while He thus sported most charmingly in the land of Sri Navadvipa.

*Note.* *Kāla-daṇḍa*, "the rod of Time", is the staff of Yama, the lord of the departed.

**Colophon:** *iti śrī-kṛṣṇa-caitanya-caritāmṛte mahākāvye ekādaśaḥ sargaḥ* — "Thus [ends] the eleventh sarga in the mahakavya 'Sri Krishna-Chaitanya-charitamrita'." (The colophon gives no title for the sarga.)

---

## Sarga Twelve — verses 1–134

**Title of the sarga:** none — the colophon of the edition gives no title for this sarga.
**Metre:** *upajāti* (a mixture of *indravajrā* ——⏑——⏑⏑—⏑—— and *upendravajrā* ⏑—⏑——⏑⏑—⏑——; 11 syllables per pada); verse 87 — *śālinī*; verses 133–134 — *mālinī*.
**Total verses:** 134 (verses 49 and 112 have six padas; 112 opens with a quotation of Bhagavatam 10.81.16 in the *anuṣṭubh* metre; 87 — *śālinī*; 120 — the chant "Rāma Rāghava…"; 133–134 — *mālinī*).

**Contents of the passage:** the Lord in Puri with Sarvabhauma; the departure for the south as far as the Godavari.
- 1–9 — the Lord in Puri with Sarvabhauma; the darshan of Jagannatha.
- 10–35 — Sarvabhauma's plan to instruct the Lord in Vedanta; the disputation; the Lord refutes the doctrine of non-duality; the four-armed form; hymns of praise.
- 36–58 — the manuscript in Gopinatha's hand with Sarvabhauma's verse containing the word "Krishna"; the Lord, in ecstasy, returns to Puri; Gopinatha on the mercy shown to Sarvabhauma.
- 59–77 — the mahaprasada: Sarvabhauma eats it without first washing; both are in ecstasy; Sarvabhauma is humbled.
- 78–93 — eighteen interpretations of two stanzas from the Eleventh Canto; the verses *vairāgya-vidyā…* and *kālān naṣṭaṁ…* (the Lord tears up the leaf; Mukunda had copied them onto the wall); "bhakti-pade" instead of "mukti-pade".
- 94–106 — eighteen days in Puri; the departure for the south; Sarvabhauma advises the Lord to meet Ramananda; Kurma-kshetra, the brahmana Kurma.
- 107–117 — the leper Vasudeva is healed by an embrace; Kurma's lament.
- 118–121 — Nrisimha-kshetra; the Lord's gait; the chant "Rāma Rāghava… Kṛṣṇa Keśava…".
- 122–129 — the forests by the Godavari: peacocks, deer, parrots, cicadas, fearsome thickets and torrents.
- 130–132 — hesitation: whether to speak with Ramananda; the Lord passes by and goes on south in ecstasy.
- 133–134 (*mālinī*) — the wonder of the southerners: "A golden elephant? Meru set in motion? Lightning that does not fade?"

**1.** Having entered the holy Kshetra, He whose pastimes are boundless came to the house of Sri Sarvabhauma. And that brahmana, seeing the unexpected sannyasi who charms the world, rejoiced.

*Note.* The edition has *ज्ञानमनोज्ञं* ("charming by knowledge"), but the metre requires ⏑⏑ at the sixth and seventh syllables; we read *jagan-manojñam*. The Kshetra is Purushottama-kshetra (Puri). Sarvabhauma Bhattacharya was a celebrated Vedantist and logician at the court of Puri.

**2.** Rising, the brahmana with devotion offered water for [washing] the feet and the rest, set an excellent seat before [Him] as well, and, having made obeisance, very composedly and humbly asked about everything.

**3.** "From where have You come, and where are You going? You are charming, wholly peaceful and self-controlled." When the Lord had been asked thus by him in earnest, those whose only Master He was related all of it.

**4.** Having learned all that just as it was, and delighted [at the thought] "Now the unknown has become known", that brahmana rejoiced before Him whose lotus feet are worshipped by the host of the gods.

*Note.* The edition has *तदाज्ञातम्*; we read *tadā 'jñātam*, "now the unknown [has become] known" (the metre is not affected).

**5.** Then, having understood His intention, he at once, with due respect, himself sent the Lord, who wished to see Jagannatha, straight to the Blue Mountain together with his own son.

*Note.* *Sitetarādri* ("the mountain other than white") is Nilachala, the "Blue Mountain", the hill of Jagannatha's temple.

**6.** Having come there together with him, and then joyfully and freely entering, He beheld the crest-jewel of Nilachala, and was then immersed in an ocean of the greatest bliss.

**7.** Beholding [Him] again and again with obeisances and hymns, having bathed His own form with the waters of His eyes, and having circumambulated five times, He came out from there with difficulty.

*Note.* *Svām mūrtim*, "His own form", may mean His own body, drenched with tears, or Jagannatha, the Lord's own Deity form (?).

**8.** Thus the Lord rejoiced, beholding there the lovely crest-jewel of Nilachala. Having made a necklace of the jewel of His own name, and ever wearing it on His neck, He shone.

*Note.* That is, He chanted the Name unceasingly.

**9.** With Mukunda Datta and others of His own, the Lord spent some days there beholding the jewel of the Blue King of mountains, His heart filled with eagerness.

**10.** Once Sarvabhauma, the foremost of the brahmanas, reflected in his mind, not knowing, because of [His] human condition, all this power and majesty of the Merciful One.

*Note.* Verses 10–16 are Sarvabhauma's reflections.

**11.** He by whose mighty masses of billows, by whose streams of waves from the ocean of unbroken learning, Brihaspati himself was utterly deafened and fell into a stupor —

*Note.* *Yasya* ("he by whose") is taken up by *sa eva* ("that very one") in verse 12, which points to Sarvabhauma (?). Brihaspati is the preceptor of the gods, the very model of learning. The Gosvami edition (GGM) has the same text.

**12.** — he, that very one, a heap of esteemed pride, deep of intellect, did not know the lotus feet of the Lord. That is no wonder at all: he does not know even a particle of His learning, lineage and so on.

*Note.* The edition has *yan na … na veda*, a double negative; the Gosvami edition (GGM) reads *yat prabhu-pāda-padmaṁ na veda*, and so we read: "that he did not know the feet of the Lord is no wonder".

**13.** "This great soul, the foremost among men, has embraced renunciation while still young. If such is His heart, then enough of our deliberations. But for You this is hard!

*Note.* The second half is obscure. The edition has *स्वान्तरलं तदालं*, perhaps corrupt (?). The Gosvami edition (GGM) reads *yad īdṛśaṁ svāntar alaṁ tadā laṁ / vicintitair na tvayi kaṣṭam etat*, with *na*: "this is not hard for You".

**14.** He who by the manifold marks of a jewel among men charms the people of the whole world — how indeed will He pass this time in observing the duties of sannyasa?

*Note.* In the first pada the fifth syllable (*pu-* of *puruṣa*) is light, where both *indravajrā* and *upendravajrā* require a heavy one; perhaps the poetic form *pūruṣa* was intended (?).

**15.** He is born of a great family, He is magnanimous, and His youth has scarcely blossomed. How will He get through, in [the age of] Kali, the most arduous asceticism befitting it? Alas, how exceedingly hard!

**16.** Therefore, by making Him, whose heart is wholly at peace, hear the Vedanta unceasingly, I shall make Him a wayfarer on the road to liberation, through the taste of detachment and through whole-hearted devotion to radiant knowledge."

*Note.* Sarvabhauma means to instruct the Lord in Advaita Vedanta. Cf. Chaitanya-charitamrita, Madhya 6: for seven days Sarvabhauma expounds the Brahma-sutras.

**17.** Having perceived this design of his heart, the Lord, whose face was charming as a full-blown lotus, and whose heart was stirred by a compassion that flashed forth and pervaded the three worlds, laughed inwardly.

**18.** On the next day, His splendour shining like the array of the rays of the fierce, unrestrained sun, His face charming with the form of the moon, the Lord, content, went to his house with His own [devotees], who were devoted to His feet.

*Note.* *Doṣākara* ("maker of the night") is the moon.

**19.** Seeing the Lord, he rose together with the crowds of his disciples and bowed down again and again. Then, having offered Him a fine seat, he himself settled there on a seat.

**20.** The brahmana humbly said to the Lord: "Here the Vedanta is studied by these [disciples]. Such as You are the most worthy [of it]. Listen, then, for by it the impurity of the mind quickly goes away."

*Note.* *Kaṣāya*, literally "astringent, dark", is in Yoga and Vedanta the residual impurity of the mind.

**21.** "This I have thoroughly studied and taught many times" — and so the foremost of the brahmanas heedlessly set about teaching it once again in the presence of this Lord.

*Note.* *Pramatta*, "intoxicated, heedless": Sarvabhauma does not know whom he is teaching.

**22.** This Brihaspati of the earth in person expounded this and that as he understood it, with speech full of flashing boldness. Having heard him, the Lord then slowly began, in due order, to raise objections.

*Note.* *Udgrāha* in debate is the putting forward of a thesis or an objection. *Yathādhi* — "according to [his] understanding" (*yathā* + *adhi*) (?). The Gosvami edition (GGM) has the same text.

**23.** "What is being asserted here? What indeed is the initial thesis? Or what do you set forth as its conclusion? This is not the meaning of the Vedanta scripture. So hear what I shall set forth."

*Note.* *Pūrva-pakṣa* is the opponent's thesis, *siddhānta* (*rāddhānta*) the final conclusion: terms of Sanskrit disputation. *Nirūpayāmaḥ* is plural ("We shall set forth").

**24.** Having spoken thus, He made ready a thesis of His own in the form of a counter-thesis to his thesis, refuted the doctrine of non-duality and expounded His own view, which establishes bhakti.

**25.** Thus, by all the means of valid knowledge and by the power of words — by intention (*tātparya*), by indication (*lakṣaṇā*) and by the figurative sense (*gauṇī*), by the primary sense (*mukhyā*), and by indication that abandons its own meaning, that does not abandon it, and that is mixed — He expounded His own view.

*Note.* The categories of Sanskrit semantics are listed here: *mukhyā vṛtti*, the primary sense; *gauṇī*, the figurative sense based on similarity; *lakṣaṇā*, indication, of which there are three kinds: *jahat-svārthā* (the word's own meaning is abandoned), *ajahat-svārthā* (it is retained) and the mixed *jahad-ajahat-svārthā*; *tātparya*, the overall intention of a statement. *Śakti* is the denotative power of a word. The form *mukhyā* at the beginning of a compound is unusual (?). The Gosvami edition (GGM) has the same text.

**26.** Though his intellect had been refuted, the brahmana then put forward a thesis [again] by cavilling, quibbles, "points of defeat" and the like; and he was quickly refuted by the Lord, whose conclusion is self-established.

*Note.* *Vitaṇḍā* (cavilling: disputation without a thesis of one's own), *chala* (verbal quibble) and *nigraha-sthāna* (a ground for declaring the opponent defeated) are terms of Nyaya.

**27.** The first was a proponent of non-duality, and the Lord, the one ocean of genius, a proponent of the lotus feet. Attended by their adherents, the two of them spent a very long time in disputations, each [arguing] in his own way.

*Note.* *Padābja-vādī* ("proponent of the lotus feet") — a defender of personal devotion to God.

**28.** Then the foremost of the brahmanas, his mind astonished, troubled in the depth of his heart, said [to himself]: "Who is He who has descended here to shatter my genius? Can He be Brihaspati?

**29.** My reasoning has always been this: Brihaspati, though ever striving in the boat of his intellect, will never reach the farther shore of the ocean of my genius.

**30.** But He is only of the age of boyhood: how much can He have studied? Say, what has He taught? Even so, my power did not serve to defeat Him at all; it served only for my own [defeat].

*Note.* *Sā* (feminine) refers back to *śaktiḥ*: the power meant to overcome the Lord has served only to overcome Sarvabhauma himself. The Gosvami edition (GGM) has the same text.

**31.** So He is surely Krishna, and nothing else: this conduct is the proof of it." Having reflected thus in his heart, he, his body covered with horripilation, bowed down to the Lord of hearts.

**32.** With streams flowing from his trembling eyes, he rejoiced; with his hair standing on end, he propitiated the Almighty with praises and obeisances. And He, the one ocean of mercy, then became gracious [to him].

*Note.* *Samudgama* (literally "rising") here probably means the bristling of the hair on the body. The Gosvami edition (GGM) has the same text.

**33.** He revealed His four-armed form, shining like a thousand million suns. Thereupon the brahmana rejoiced still more, and composed a hymn more exalted still.

*Note.* In the Chaitanya-charitamrita (Madhya 6.202) the Lord shows Sarvabhauma first His four-armed form and then His own form, dark-complexioned and holding the flute; in the Chaitanya-bhagavata (Antya 3) He shows him a six-armed form. Karnapura speaks of the four-armed form.

**34.** Whatever [hymns] that chief of the assembly of brahmanas, delighted and most eloquent, offered in praise, not even Vachaspati, capable though he is, could master, even striving again and again.

*Note.* The edition has *प्रभवेद्धविष्णुः*; perhaps a corruption of *prabhaviṣṇuḥ*, "capable" (?). The Gosvami edition (GGM) has the same text.

**35.** Then, having spent some days [there], the Lord resolved to set out to the south. And all went along, following Him, with the name of Hari [on their lips].

*Note.* In the edition the lines are wrongly divided (the first pada runs on to *nītvā*). By the metre, *nītvā* opens the second pada.

**36.** Having gone some distance, the merciful One then dismissed them all. Meanwhile, on the road, a brahmana named Gopinatha bowed down [to Him].

*Note.* In the edition the lines are again wrongly divided (*so 'pi / gopīnātha…*). Gopinatha Acharya, Sarvabhauma's brother-in-law, was a devotee of the Lord.

**37.** Seeing a manuscript of hymns in his hand, the Lord affectionately snatched it and took it. Then, as He went on, all [the others] came up, following Him.

**38.** When all had gone, the Lord, walking alone, sat down comfortably at the foot of some tree, untied the manuscript and looked at it for a long time with delight.

**39.** Examining it all round, the Lord saw there a poem of Sri Sarvabhauma, and then among its words He noticed the word "Krishna".

*Note.* Cf. Chaitanya-charitamrita, Madhya 6.252–255: Sarvabhauma's verses glorifying Chaitanya as Krishna (*vairāgya-vidyā-nija-bhakti-yoga…*); Mukunda copied them onto a wall, and the Lord, having read them, tore up the leaf.

**40.** Seeing that word "Krishna", He at once, His soul overwhelmed by an excess of love, fell motionless to the ground, the creeper of His body washed by a flood of streams of tears from His eyes.

**41.** Lying just so on the ground at the foot of the tree, the Merciful One spent what remained of the day and the whole night, wishing to show mercy to Sri Sarvabhauma.

*Note.* The edition has *यश्च*; by the sense, *yac ca*.

**42.** Awaking in the morning, His soul deeply overwhelmed, He went on, His throat choked and His voice faltering: "Ah, I have committed a great offence against the great-souled Sarvabhauma!

*Note.* The Gosvami edition (GGM) also reads *jagāma*, "went"; the words of verses 42–44 are spoken as He goes.

**43.** How indeed can I abandon him out of delusion and go on pilgrimage under the sway of pride alone? I shall go back to the Kshetra and serve him, for he is indeed a great soul.

**44.** Apart from the rite of serving him, I shall diligently do nothing whatever." Resolving thus, the ocean of mercy came back again to the Kshetra within a single watch.

*Note.* A watch (*prahara*) is about three hours.

**45.** Then He at once sent someone to fetch the best of acharyas. And that [man], hastening, came up to Gopinatha, that best of acharyas, and said:

*Note.* The subject is not named in the text. By the context it is the Lord, who has just returned to the Kshetra (44): the messenger announces that "Krishna-Chaitanya has come here" (46), and Gopinatha hastens "to Him" (48), while Sarvabhauma does not yet know of the Lord's return.

**46.** "O Acharya, come quickly: the divine Krishna-Chaitanya has come here!" — "What are you saying, fellow? Can it all be false? [Why,] He joyfully went off to the south!

*Note.* The Lord's sannyasa name, Krishna-Chaitanya, is mentioned for the first time in this sarga.

**47.** We ourselves accompanied Him and took leave of Him far from here — how could He be here?" Thus [Gopinatha] spoke, and the other told him again and again: "I am speaking the truth!"

**48.** Gopinatha, the best of worthy acharyas, hastened to Him. Seeing Him, the great soul rejoiced at heart and said with wonder and affection:

**49.** "What is this very great wonder, O Lord? How did You go, and how have You come back?" Then the graceful Lord, His crimson lips mingled with the lustre of the radiance of His teeth, which were like blossomed flowers, spoke with a play of words steeped in the juice of honey-wine:

*Note.* A verse of six padas. *Visūna*, "blossomed": the usual *prasūna* ("flower") would break the metre.

**50.** "O Acharya, I have now committed a great heap of offences against Sarvabhauma: for out of pride I abandoned him and wished to wander to the holy places.

**51.** That great soul is of the very nature of Bhagavan; he is devoted to the deliverance of the three worlds, and his aspirations are good: for from his mouth came forth that one faultless, charming verse with the name of Krishna.

**52.** Therefore I must render service only to him: for Me this is the only service to the Lord. Having considered the matter thus, I have come back again, though I had set out on the journey to the holy places."

**53.** Having pondered this supreme, inconceivable speech of His, worthy of inquiry and the very essence of the shruti and smriti, the brahmana at once laughed, displaying a row of teeth.

**54.** "Ah, see the conduct of the supremely compassionate Lord, merciful to wretched people! Who can know His greatness, so very hard to attain? These people are indeed like worms.

**55.** Ah, see the favour now [shown] to Sarvabhauma by the supremely compassionate [Lord], who wishes to fill the world with a flood of mercy — [a favour] hard to attain even for those like the lord of the gods!

**56.** That Sarvabhauma, the emperor in the circle of Vedantists, in whom there was not even the scent of devotion, has become the supreme vessel of Your mercy [merely] because by destiny the name of Krishna appeared in his verse!

*Note.* A play on words: *sārvabhauma* means "emperor, sovereign of the whole earth", and it is also the name (an honorific title) of Sarvabhauma Bhattacharya.

**57.** Ah, what foolish person would not serve Him, the supremely compassionate? Disregarding many faults, He accepts a particle of virtue and shows compassion again and again.

**58.** From whose mouth does the name of Krishna not come forth? What more than that was there in him? Then I understood: now You will show very great compassion to Sarvabhauma."

**59.** Having heard his speech, blossoming with the rasa of wonder and enthusiasm, the Lord said: "Do not speak so, O great soul! Here I must only render service to him."

**60.** Having spoken thus, He spent that day; at the end of the night He rose from His bed to see the first opening [of the temple], and then, together with His people, performed the daily observances.

*Note.* *Prathamāvakāśa* is the first opening of the temple at dawn (*maṅgala-ārati*).

**61.** Then, having put on the outer cloth and the waist-cord, His throat eager to repeat the name, the Lord entered the temple, as the autumn moon [ascends] the Mountain of Rising.

*Note.* The *bahirvāsa* (outer cloth) and the *kaṭi-sūtra* (waist-cord with loincloth) are the garb of a sannyasi.

**62.** Standing like a golden pillar behind the splendid pillar of the King of birds, He beheld the crest-jewel of Nilachala, His body washed by a flood of tears.

*Note.* The Garuda pillar (*garuḍa-stambha*) is the pillar bearing the image of Garuda in the temple of Jagannatha. Chaitanya usually stood behind it during darshan.

**63.** Then, having remained there until the incense [offering] and watched His [Jagannatha's] morning rites, He took a little most delightful mahaprasada food and came outside.

**64.** Just so, the Lord went to his house to see Sarvabhauma. He, however, as it happened, had not yet risen from his bed at dawn and was still lying there.

**65.** Then, when one of his servants, having seen the Lord, was about to [go and] wake him, the Lord stopped him and hid Himself right inside the bedroom.

*Note.* The form *jagante* is unclear (?); the sense "was about to [go and] wake him" is taken from the context.

**66.** Then, as [Sarvabhauma] turned from one side [to the other], the Lord heard "Sri Krishna, Krishna!" — words half awake, half submerged [in sleep] — and attained unfeigned, manifold bliss.

*Note.* In the edition the lines are divided wrongly (*…vāṇīṁ jagāma / nirvyājam…*); by the metre *jagāma* opens the fourth pada.

**67.** Then Sarvabhauma, the lion among the gods of the earth, awoke and saw beautiful Gaurachandra, the crest-jewel of the circles of ascetics.

**68.** Greatly flustered, he hastily sprang up from his bed and joyfully bowed down. And then, in varied conversation, that time of the two of them was full of great joy.

**69.** Then the Lord, compassionate at every moment to all people, the great ocean of rasa, drew the sanctified food out from the end of His garment and took it in His lotus hand.

**70.** Raising His arm — a wish-fulfilling tree covered with the miraculous herb of mahaprasada — He said: "When you have performed your daily rites, you will partake of this in due time," and gave [it to him].

**71.** He rose and with great longing hastily took the precious mahaprasada in his hand: "If there is delay upon receiving prasada, then enough, enough indeed of learned niceties!"

*Note.* Sarvabhauma eats the prasada at once, before he has washed, breaking the rules of morning purity. Cf. Chaitanya-charitamrita, Madhya 6.

**72.** Thus [resolving], he at once, covered with waves of hairs standing on end, put that mahaprasada into his mouth. Then the Lord, His soul brimming with great joy, embraced him with both arms and rejoiced.

**73.** The Lord and Sarvabhauma sighed long, gazing at each other (?); their bodies were adorned with hairs standing on end and drops of sweat, and their hearts were sated by swimming in the ocean of bliss.

*Note.* The beginning of the verse (*°śvasitākṣiṇīva*) is corrupt or obscure and is translated by the sense (?). *Akṣiṇī* may also hint at tears: "adorned with long sighs for each other, [tears of] the eyes, hairs standing on end and sweat".

**74.** The pupils of their eyes were hidden by flowing water, and their bodies by masses of hairs standing on end. Then, from the bath they had taken in the river of love, utmost numbness came over them both.

*Note.* A play of images: after bathing in a cold river the body grows numb (*jāḍya*). Stupor (*stambha*) is one of the *sāttvika* symptoms of ecstasy.

**75.** Thus by the rasa of great mercy the Lord brought under His sway the foremost of the assembly of brahmanas. And then that heart [of his] became fully permeated with the rasa of compassion.

**76.** From then on, having become attached with body, speech and mind to the lotus feet of supremely merciful Gaurangachandra, he cast off the whole burden of pride.

**77.** And so, on the next day, at the end of the incense [offering], this foremost of the twice-born, freed from the intoxication of learning and humble in disposition, went to see the supremely merciful Lord Gaurachandra.

**78.** Seeing Him, the great soul bowed down, his head shining at the very ground, and also offered a hymn of praise. Then the brahmana, very timidly, his palms joined in the form of a cup, at once said:

**79.** "O Lord, out of compassion for me, explain this one verse: I am afraid to expound it. We do explain it, but now there is no conviction at all in [my] heart about it."

**80.** Having spoken thus, he joyfully recited a pair of verses from the Eleventh Canto. Having heard it, the foremost of the compassionate gave an explanation of most profound meaning.

*Note.* In Chaitanya-charitamrita (Madhya 6.186–193) the Lord explains the *ātmārāma* verse (Srimad-Bhagavatam 1.7.10); in Karnapura it is a pair of verses from the Eleventh Canto, and which ones is said neither here, nor by Murari, nor in the *Chaitanya-chandrodaya* (Act 6).

**81.** He explained each of the two verses separately, one after the other, in nine ways. Hearing the eighteen meanings of both, the brahmana was utterly amazed.

**82.** Exceedingly amazed, the great soul praised [Him], censuring himself all the more: "Ah, O Lord, a fool like me — a beast in human form — did not understand Your greatness!"

**83.** Having thus offered praise to his heart's content, he took one of the Lord's associates with him and went home. And thereafter, having written a leaf with faultless verses…

**84.** …and having given him mahaprasada food seen by no one else, as alms for that supremely merciful [Lord], [saying]: "And the leaf is to be given to the Lord," the brahmana sent him off and rejoiced.

*Note.* *Ananya-dṛṣṭa*, "seen by no one else", that is, untouched and pure.

**85.** Then Mukunda Datta, seeing the leaf, read the two shlokas on it, wrote them on the wall as well, and gave [the leaf] into the Lord's hand. And He, looking at it, softly read:

*Note.* According to Chaitanya-charitamrita (Madhya 6.253 ff.), the Lord tore up the leaf, but the shlokas survived because Mukunda had managed to copy them onto the wall.

**86.** "To teach detachment, knowledge and bhakti-yoga directed to Himself, the one primeval Person has assumed the body of Sri Krishna-Chaitanya. In Him, who is the ocean of mercy, I take refuge.

*Note.* The celebrated verses of Sarvabhauma, quoted also in Chaitanya-charitamrita (Madhya 6.254–255).

**87.** He who has appeared under the name Krishna-Chaitanya to manifest again His own bhakti-yoga, lost through time — in the lotus of His feet may the bumblebee of my mind be absorbed ever more and more deeply!"

*Note.* The metre is *śālinī*.

**88.** Having read this, the ocean of mercy laughed and tore it up with both hands. Then all the people, seeing [the verses] on the wall, at once placed them on their necks like a jewel.

*Note.* "Placed them on their necks", that is, learnt them by heart.

**89.** Such is the mercy of Sri Gaurachandra — how [can it be described] in words? From a mere trace of contact with Him the cast of the mind truly becomes quite different — as if [it were] another birth and another destiny.

*Note.* *Adṛṣṭa* ("the unseen") is accumulated karma, destiny.

**90.** For this chief of brahmanas, [once] the sole traveller on the path of abstract spiritual knowledge, from contact with the Lord's feet now does not let even the name of liberation come onto the path of his ears — such is the mercy of Gaura, the Almighty!

**91.** Once, as the occasion arose, he recited in the Lord's presence a verse from the Bhagavata, and in the words *mukti-pade sa dāya-bhāk* ("he is an heir in the abode of liberation") he read "bhakti" [in place of "mukti"] — and rejoiced.

*Note.* Srimad-Bhagavatam 10.14.8: "…one who lives thus is an heir in the abode of liberation (*mukti-pade*)." Sarvabhauma replaced *mukti* with *bhakti*. The episode is also found in the Chaitanya-charitamrita (Madhya 6.260–266).

**92.** Hearing this, the Lord — God Himself — at once put another meaning into the word "mukti" and upheld [the original reading]. Even so, he, anointed with His lordship, said:

**93.** "Even so, because it calls something indecent to mind, I say: this [word has] the fault of indecorum." That Sarvabhauma, whose sweet sayings such as this are well known, cannot be described by any account.

*Note.* *Aślīla-doṣa* is, in poetics, the fault of a word that evokes shameful, repulsive or inauspicious associations. For a devotee "mukti" (merging into Brahman) is such a word. Cf. the Chaitanya-charitamrita, Madhya 6.266–268.

**94.** Having spent eighteen days there, beholding that God [Jagannatha] with exceeding joy, the Lord set out on His wanderings, bewildering some with [the pain of] separation.

**95.** Having beheld that Mahaprabhu Jagannatha and having first received His permission, Mahaprabhu — the fair moon whose rays are nectar — joyfully set off to the south.

*Note.* The title *Mahāprabhu* ("the Great Lord") is applied here both to Jagannatha and to Chaitanya.

**96.** But Sarvabhauma, his heart distraught with grief, piteously said to Him as He was thus leaving: "How [can this be], O Lord? Having scorched me with great sorrow, where are You going so resolutely?

**97.** Better that I had suffered grief for a son, better that my body had fallen! Having seen the pair of Your lotus feet, I am unable to bear separation from You.

*Note.* Literally, "Why did grief for a son not come to me? Why did the fall of my body not come to me?" — that is, any other sorrow would have been easier.

**98.** Alas, where will You go, and by what road? How then will You bear the hardships of the way? If You must go, then, O Merciful One, come to the land on the banks of the Godavari.

**99.** There lives a certain supreme great soul, a bee intoxicated at the lotus feet of Sri Krishna. Do not disregard him, thinking him a worldly man — Ramananda, the jewel among the sons of Bhavananda."

*Note.* Ramananda Raya was the governor on the Godavari (the viceroy of King Prataparudra), later one of Chaitanya's closest associates. Hence the warning: "do not take him for a worldly man".

**100.** Saying "So be it", the merciful Bhagavan joyfully went first to Kaurma, and there He bowed down [to Kurma], revealing His own devotion — and that indeed was His mercy.

*Note.* Kaurma is Kurma-kshetra (Shrikurmam, Andhra Pradesh), the temple of Vishnu in the form of the Tortoise.
