**101.** Having long beheld that avatara of His own and bowed down again, the skilful and grateful [Lord], assuming the role of a teacher who instructs [by example], performed the midday rite of honouring Him [Kurma].

**102.** In that holy place there was a very wise great soul named Kurma, born in a brahmana family. Seeing Him and bowing down again and again, he said sweetly and very timidly:

**103.** "Today, then, this birth [of mine] will bear fruit; this very day everything of mine will become fruitful, since the dust of His two lotus feet will fall in this house!"

**104.** That foremost of excellent brahmanas named Kurma, who had gathered a heap of merit in many ways, clasped His feet, led [Him] to his own house and washed those [feet] with water.

**105.** When this had been done, He, the supremely merciful, rejoiced in that man's auspicious house; right there the Lord accepted the alms then offered [to Him], and from there He set out.

**106.** Hearing that Mahaprabhu had gone south from Sri Purushottama, a certain brahmana named Sri Vasudeva then suddenly came [there], somehow, with difficulty.

*Note.* Vasudeva is the leper brahmana healed by the Lord (Chaitanya-charitamrita, Madhya 7.136 ff.).

**107.** That magnanimous man of revered greatness, whose reed-thin body was continually decaying with leprosy, though gravely ill, went to the house of the excellent brahmana named Kurma.

*Note.* *Śvitra* — white leprosy.

**108.** Having arrived, the steadfast [Vasudeva] approached the one named Kurma and asked about that Mahaprabhu; and he told that magnanimous man all this about the Abode of mercy:

**109.** "The Lord stayed right here: He accepted alms and showed mercy to one such as me. Had you come just a moment sooner, you would have seen the Lord right here."

**110.** Hearing all this, the great soul cried out in distress, "He has gone!" and fell to the ground in a swoon. Then the Lord turned back and came there again.

**111.** Coming [back], He embraced the brahmana with both arms and dispelled his swoon together with the leprosy. Having obtained a body restored to consciousness and far more beautiful, he, filled with joy and sorrow, bowed down to Him.

**112.** He recited the verse beginning "Where am I, a poor sinner, and where is Krishna, the abode of Sri?" and said: "Alas, this is not mercy — this is punishment for me!" "Do not give way to dejection: free from egoism, [you] will come to Me," said the Lord, and vanished.

*Note.* The verse has six padas: the first two are the opening of Srimad-Bhagavatam 10.81.16 (the words of Sudama, in the *anuṣṭubh* metre), the rest are *upajāti*. The same quotation appears in Sarga Seven. Vasudeva fears that his new beauty will breed pride in him. *Eṣyati* ("will come", third person) may be taken with an implied respectful "you" (*bhavān*) or generically: "one who is free from egoism will come to Me". Cf. the Chaitanya-charitamrita, Madhya 7.143–146. The edition has *चोचे*; the sense requires *cūce* (*ca ūce*).

**113.** Seeing him in such a state, Kurma too fainted, pain lodged in his heart. Rising again, that foremost of Vaishnavas wove a piteous garland of laments:

**114.** "Right here such a dawning of fortune took place: that Mahaprabhu, the Lord of the whole world, came and stayed — and yet not even for a single moment was He known [by me] as God!

**115.** Ah, man is utterly foolish-minded, petty, cruel, a doer of great sin! When a priceless gem had come into his very hand, he did not keep it, alas, through mere carelessness.

**116.** A beast, dull by nature and eating nothing but grass, does not know the relish of tasting nectar; and the bewildered one, even when he has touched the touchstone many times, does not know that it is a great gem."

*Note.* *Sparśa-maṇi*, "the touchstone", is the stone that turns iron into gold.

**117.** Alas! He could not bear this unbearable grief of separation from that greatly compassionate Lord of the world; utterly bewildered, he fainted again and again.

**118.** Then He, the supremely merciful, a lion among men, went on from there; arriving at the holy place of Nrisimha, He bowed down to Lord Nrisimha and also offered Him a hymn of praise.

*Note.* A play on words: *nṛsiṁhaḥ*, "a lion among men" (that is, Chaitanya), comes to *Nṛsiṁha-deva*. Evidently Jiyada-Nrisimha (Simhachalam) is meant; cf. the Chaitanya-charitamrita, Madhya 8.3–5 (the Lord comes to Jiyada-Nrisimha and glorifies Nrisimha).

**119.** Always moving like a lordly elephant maddened by musth, the great Sportive One with beautiful, mighty arms went on, flooding the earth with the streams of a river of nectar flowing from the moons of His toenails.

*Note.* In the edition the lines are divided wrongly: *नखेन्दुपीयूषनदीप्रवाहधारा- / भिराप्लाव्य रसां जगाम*. By the metre, the pada boundary falls after *pravāha-*. *Rasā* — "the earth".

**120.** "O Rama, O Raghava, O Rama, O Raghava, O Rama, O Raghava, protect me! O Krishna, O Keshava, O Krishna, O Keshava, O Krishna, O Keshava, save me!"

*Note.* This is a chant, not an *upajāti* stanza: its padas have 10 and 8 syllables. It is close to the Chaitanya-charitamrita, Madhya 7.96 (where *rakṣa* and *pāhi* stand in the reverse order); according to the Chaitanya-charitamrita, the Lord sings this chant on His way from Kurma-kshetra to the south.

**121.** Thus He chanted on the way, loudly, unceasingly and to His heart's content, His body covered with hairs standing on end; and somewhere, seeing a fearsome forest, the Supreme Lord wept bitterly in a plaintive voice.

*Note.* The correct form is *pariroditi* (root *rud*, second class; cf. *roditi* in v. 132), as in the Devanagari; the IAST line has *parirodati*. The metre is the same either way.

**122.** Seeing the depths of the forest, filled here and there in abundance with breezes that were cooled by the high waves of the Godavari and embraced the clusters of creepers, the Lord rejoiced.

**123.** In the avenues of kadamba trees [the peacocks], their mridangas resounding, joyously danced the tandava with their beautiful tails [spread], and the deer with their mates trustingly raised their eyes [to Him]; and the Merciful One rejoiced again.

*Note.* The text does not name the peacocks: they are indicated by *kalāpa* ("a peacock's tail"). Peacocks dance at the rumble of rain clouds; the "mridangas" are perhaps that rumble.

**124.** In some places the stretches of forest land were silent, without the chirping of birds; in others all their quarters were seized by the echo of fierce cries; and in others they were heated by the fiery breath of huge, fearsome beasts lying asleep there…

*Note.* *क्वच* (twice in the edition) is evidently *kva ca* ("and somewhere"), a variant of *kvacit*; the metre allows this reading. The verse is linked syntactically with the next (*yugmaka*).

**125.** …[stretches] full of the great roar of the Godavari's rapids, [full of] mountain torrents, fearsome with their din — [these] utterly robbed the most tender heart of Sri Gaurachandra of its composure.

*Note.* The subject of *vitenuḥ* is the stretches of forest land of v. 124. The plural forms in *-āḥ* can be taken as qualifying them ("[having] the great roar of the Godavari's rapids", "[having] mountain torrents"), or as further subjects: the great roar of the Godavari's rapids and the mountain torrents.

**126.** In the forest on the bank of the Godavari He took delight in the parrots "kissing" split pomegranates — sated, heaps of seeds tumbling from their beaks, their feet slipping every moment and their wings fluttering.

*Note.* Verses 126–129 form one description: the qualifiers in the locative case in vv. 127–129 refer to the "forest" (*vane*). *Skhalat-pāda* ("with slipping feet") is taken as referring to the birds.

**127.** [That forest was] exceedingly delightful: fierce, screeching "saws" loudly tore through the clusters of betel-vine leaves, and the ceaseless, long-drawn, enchanting chirring of cicadas [filled it].

*Note.* The edition has *क्रकचैरसद्भिः* (*krakacair asadbhiḥ*, "non-existent"?). We read *krakacai rasadbhiḥ* ("screeching"), cf. the same word in v. 128; the script allows this division as it stands. *Krakaca* means "a saw"; evidently parrots are meant, their beaks likened to a saw.

**128.** [It was] frequented by rows of tamalas, by arjunas and kovidaras that touched the hosts of stars and resembled clouds, by crying birds of many kinds, by herds of chamuru deer and by yaks.

*Note.* The edition has *जुष्टैः*; we read *juṣṭe*, a locative agreeing with "forest" (v. 126), like *nikāma-ramye* in v. 127. *Patra-ratha* ("whose chariot is wings") is a bird.

**129.** Its ground, free from the scorching of the sun's rays, was dense [with growth], moist, very pleasantly cool and lovely; the roots [of its trees] were saturated with a natural unguent; and step-wells, ponds and the like filled it without a break.

*Note.* *Pakva* ("ripened, baked") here means "scorched" by the sun.

**130.** Then, having come to the Godavari, He began to waver in His mind: should He speak with the great-souled son of the illustrious Bhavananda, or not?

*Note.* The edition has *संभाषिव्यः* — a misprint; the metre requires *saṁbhāṣitavyaḥ*. The son of Bhavananda is Ramananda Raya (see v. 99).

**131.** And yet the Almighty, displaying detachment, went on to the south without even seeing him. His heart softened by the sight of the various forests, at times He would enter [a thicket] and weep beyond measure.

*Note.* In Krishnadasa Kaviraja (Chaitanya-charitamrita, Madhya 8) the conversation with Ramananda on the Godavari takes place on the way south. Karnapura in the Mahakavya transfers it to the return journey: 13.34–49 (Ramananda recites verses, the Lord answers "this is external" (*bāhya*), then the Bengali song *pahilahi rāga nayana-bhaṅga bhela* is sung — the same as in the Chaitanya-charitamrita, Madhya 8.192–193, in the numbering of the project's edition). Cf. Karnapura's own drama *Chaitanya-chandrodaya*, Act 7: there, as in the Chaitanya-charitamrita, the meeting with Ramananda takes place on the way south (with the same answer "this is external"), so the Mahakavya diverges even from Karnapura's own drama.

**132.** Here and there He sang full-throated, here and there He Himself danced, here and there He wept, the hairs of His body standing on end; and as He went on He did not distinguish night from day at all.

**133.** "Is this a golden king of elephants, freed from long bondage? Or is this Meru, suddenly set in motion, that shines [here]? Or else is this a mass of lasting light that flashes so freely — a play [of lightning] that lasts? Or what indeed is this marvel?"

*Note.* The metre is *mālinī* (⏑⏑⏑ ⏑⏑⏑ ——— ⏑—— ⏑——). A play on words: lightning is *acira-ruc*, "light of a brief moment", whereas here we have *cira-rociḥ*, "lasting light" — lightning that does not fade.

**134.** Thus [thinking], all the southern folk drank Him in with their eyes, with delight, constantly and without blinking; their minds numbed with stupor, even when the Lord, the moon among ascetics, had gone far from there, they stood there like posts.

*Note.* *Mālinī*. The edition has *सन्तोषं*, but the metre requires a short syllable here (⏑—— at the end of the pada): *satoṣam*. *Vinimiṣam* means "without blinking"; one would expect *animiṣam*, and the edition's form may be a corruption (?).

**Colophon:** इति श्रीकृष्णचैतन्यचरितामृते महाकाव्ये द्वादशः सर्गः । (*iti śrī-kṛṣṇa-caitanya-caritāmṛte mahākāvye dvādaśaḥ sargaḥ*) — "Thus [ends] the twelfth sarga in the mahakavya 'Sri Krishna-Chaitanya-charitamrita'." (The colophon gives no title for the sarga.)

---

## Sarga Thirteen — verses 1–148

**Title of the sarga:** none — the colophon of the edition gives no title for this sarga.
**Metre:**
- 1–63 — *upajāti* (a mixture of *indravajrā* ——⏑——⏑⏑—⏑—— and *upendravajrā* ⏑—⏑——⏑⏑—⏑——; 11 syllables per pada); 12–13 — *anuṣṭubh* (8 syllables per pada; a quotation from the Kurma Purana); 39 — *mandākrāntā* (————⏑⏑⏑⏑⏑——⏑——⏑——; 17 syllables); 42 — *vasantatilakā* (——⏑—⏑⏑⏑—⏑⏑—⏑——; 14 syllables); 46 — Ramananda's Bengali song (*rāga bhairavī*).
- 64–114 — *svāgatā* (—⏑—⏑⏑⏑—⏑⏑——) intermixed with *rathoddhatā* (—⏑—⏑⏑⏑—⏑—⏑—); 11 syllables per pada.
- 115–125, 128–134, 138–144 — *viyoginī* (odd padas ⏑⏑—⏑⏑—⏑—⏑—, 10 syllables; even padas ⏑⏑——⏑⏑—⏑—⏑—, 11 syllables); 119, 126–127, 135–137, 147 — *upajāti*.
- 145, 148 — *śārdūla-vikrīḍita* (———⏑⏑—⏑—⏑⏑⏑——⏑——⏑—; 19 syllables); 146 — *vasantatilakā*.

**Total verses:** 148.

**Contents of the passage:** the southern pilgrimage; the conversation with Ramananda; the return to Puri; the gathering of the devotees.
- 1–8 — the way south; Shrirangam: the chaturmasya in the house of Trimalla Bhatta; autumn.
- 9–13 — a brahmana devoted to Rama grieves over the abduction of Sita; the Lord shows him the verses of the Kurma Purana about the shadow Sita.
- 14–16 — the meeting with Paramananda Puri; he goes to Puri, the Lord to the Setu.
- 17–19 — seven palmyra palms are embraced and go to heaven.
- 20–30 — heretics try to allure the servant Krishnadasa; the Lord wins him back.
- 31–33 — Rameshvara at the Setu; the Lord turns back.
- **34–49 — the conversation with Ramananda on the way back, on the Godavari.** The verse on renunciation — "this is external"; the verse *nānopacāra…* — "external"; the song *pahilahi rāga…* on the highest oneness of the Two; the embrace; several days of conversation "on the path of realisation".
- 50–59 — Puri: the Snana-yatra; the anavasara; the Lord goes away to Alalanatha, and the devotees search for Him.
- 60–61 — **again on the Godavari**: four more months with Ramananda; at the onset of winter the Lord returns to Puri together with him.
- 62–71 — Kashi Mishra (he sees the Lord four-armed), Vishnudasa, Pradyumna Mishra.
- 72–77 — a grove reminiscent of Vrindavana: the creepers as dancers; the Lord's dance.
- 78–88 — Prataparudra in the grove: he glorifies the rasa dance; the embrace; "Who are you?" — "Your servant."
- 89–109 — Shikhi Mahiti, his brother Murari and his sister Madhava-devi (Madhavi-devi); Shikhi's dream; the meeting in the Jaganmohana; the embrace.
- 110–126 — the arrival of the Bengalis. The line of Madhavendra Puri and Ishvara Puri; Paramananda Puri in Navadvipa with Shachi and Acharyaratna; Kamalananda; the journey to Puri with Gadadhara and Jagadananda; the Lord bows to Paramananda; Vidyanidhi, Acharyaratna.
- 127–136 — Shivananda with Murari Gupta; Raghava; Govinda becomes [the Lord's] servant; Bhavananda ("you are like Pandu") and his five sons; Vaninatha.
- 137–144 — Purushottama Acharya becomes Svarupa Damodara.
- 145–148 — Vakreshvara, Vasudeva Datta, Bhagavan Acharya; the Lord's fame spreads; Satyaraja, Raghunandana, Narahari and Mukunda arrive.

**1.** Thus, eager to wander to the holy places, He — the only friend of the wretched, the one ocean of compassion — went from there to the fortunate south, longing for the festival of [people's] accepting the jewel of His own name.

**2.** Seeing Him on the road, the black antelopes took courage, as if to drink again and again the nectar of His form with their thirsting pairs of eyes, and all together came close to Him from every side.

**3.** The Merciful One reached Sri Ranga-kshetra, an elevated place encircled by the Kaveri. Beholding the Deity there, He, the mine of love, attained lofty joy.

*Note.* *Priyākara*, literally "a mine of what is dear", that is, a treasury of love; or perhaps "one who does what is pleasing" (?). Shrirangam stands on an island between the arms of the Kaveri. The first pada has only ten syllables (*śrī-raṅga-kṣetram asau dayāluḥ*: ————⏑⏑—⏑——) instead of the eleven of the *upajāti*; a syllable seems to have dropped out (?).

**4.** Settling in the house of the noble-minded Trimalla Bhatta, the Merciful One spent the chaturmasya there with eager delight, performing the obligatory duties.

*Note.* In the Chaitanya-charitamrita (Madhya 9.82–86) the host is Venkata Bhatta, but in the brief summary (Madhya 1.108–109) the Lord spends the chaturmasya with Trimalla Bhatta, as in Karnapura; according to Gaudiya tradition, Trimalla and Venkata were brothers (the third being Prabodhananda Sarasvati). The chaturmasya is the four months of the rainy season, during which sannyasis do not travel.

**5.** Bathing in the Kaveri, He multiplied its purifying power. Beholding the Deity named Sri Ranga, the Merciful One spent four months [there].

*Note.* *श्रीरङ्गसङ्गं* in the edition is unintelligible. We conjecture *śrī-raṅga-saṁjñam*, "called Sri Ranga" (?). The Gosvami edition (GGM) has the same text.

**6.** That season lying between the rains and autumn kissed Autumn as she embraced him — like a lover lying between two beloveds who, waking and turning onto his side, [kisses the one who embraces him].

*Note.* That is, autumn set in. The edition has *स काल* without the visarga.

**7.** Then Autumn — her face a freshly blossomed lotus, her eyes fresh blue lotuses, the film of mud gone from her, her waters pure — served the Lord like a maidservant who desires to attain His mercy.

*Note.* A double meaning (*śleṣa*): *paṅka* is both "mud" and "sin", *jīvana* both "water" and "life". The maidservant is free of sin, lives righteously and serves her master for the sake of his favour. The final *tat* is unclear; one would expect *tam* (?). The Gosvami edition (GGM) has the same text.

**8.** Then Mahaprabhu, immersed in a mass of bliss, went on from here, rejoicing, in the southern direction, His face charming with the unceasing loud chanting of songs about His own qualities.

*Note.* "His own qualities", that is, the qualities of Krishna.

**9.** Somewhere there He saw a best of brahmanas, a devotee of Sri Raghunatha, peaceful in mind, whose life, from grief that Sita had been abducted by the Ten-faced one, seemed to be departing.

*Note.* Cf. Chaitanya-charitamrita, Madhya 9.179–215: in Southern Mathura a brahmana worshipper of Rama (Ramadasa) grieves over the abduction of Sita; later the Lord brings him the leaves of the Kurma Purana. The Ten-faced one is Ravana.

**10.** Understanding his mind — "She is Lakshmi, and she has fallen into the hands of a Rakshasa? What is this?" — He comforted him and said: "O [brahmana], not so! Hear the truth, which I shall tell you.

**11.** Or if you will not have faith in My word, then look at this now." So saying, He suddenly drew out from the hem of His cloth two verses from a Purana and showed [them to him].

*Note.* In the Chaitanya-charitamrita the order is different: the Lord hears the Kurma Purana in Rameshvaram, takes the old leaves with these verses and, returning to Southern Mathura, gives them to the brahmana Ramadasa (Madhya 9.200–215). In Karnapura the leaf is already with the Lord.

**12.** "Agni, propitiated by Sita, produced a shadow Sita. Her the Ten-necked one carried off, while [the real] Sita went to the abode of Agni.

*Note.* Metre *anuṣṭubh*. This is a quotation: verses 12–13 are cited in the Chaitanya-charitamrita (Madhya 9.211–212) as verses of the Kurma Purana (confirmed; the last pada of 13 there is different — see the note on 13).

**13.** At the time of the ordeal the shadow Sita entered the fire, and Agni, bringing [the real] Sita from his own abode, led her forth [to Rama]."

*Note.* The edition has *उदनीनमत्*; the Gosvami edition (GGM) reads *sva-purād udanīnayat*, "led out from his abode" (the aorist of the causative of *nī*), and so we read. In the Chaitanya-charitamrita (Madhya 9.212) the last pada is different: *tat-purastād anīnayat*, "brought [her] before him", that is, before Rama.

**14.** Then here the Master saw a certain best of ascetics, magnanimous and very joyful at heart, [whose name had] "Parama" at the beginning, "ananda" in the middle and "Puri" at its end.

*Note.* A riddle-name: Paramananda Puri, a disciple of Madhavendra Puri. Cf. Chaitanya-charitamrita, Madhya 9.168–174: the Lord meets Paramananda Puri at Rishabha hill; he goes on to Puri, and the Lord to the Setu. In our edition the first pada has twelve syllables (*athātra kathañcid*); the Gosvami edition (GGM) reads *athātra kañcid*, "a certain [best of ascetics]", which is metrically correct, and so we read.

**15.** Having seen each other and conversed, the two, endowed with supreme power, were filled with joy. Under the sway of mutual love, both merciful, they resolved to depart from there.

*Note.* The edition has *कृपालु*; the dual *kṛpālū* is required.

**16.** The one, the golden moon, went south, and the other set out for Purushottama. And He whose eyes are long like lotus petals shone as He walked on, heading for the Setu.

*Note.* In the edition, as in the Chaitanya-charitamrita, Paramananda Puri goes to Puri. The Setu (Setubandha) is Rama's "bridge" in the south, near Rameshvaram.

**17.** Going along the road, His heart broken by love, He laughed abundantly and wept, utterly afflicted. His composure shattered, He moved on and then saw seven tall palmyra trees.

*Note.* The "Sapta-tala" are the seven palms connected with the story of Rama and Sugriva. Cf. Chaitanya-charitamrita, Madhya 9.312–315 (the Lord embraces the seven palms and they vanish, "sent to Vaikuntha"); there this episode takes place on the way back.

**18.** Seeing those palm trees, the Merciful One, seized with joy, embraced each one of them. At that very moment they went to heaven, and that ground suddenly became empty.

**19.** What wonder is this, then, for Gauranga Mahaprabhu, whose manifestations in the world are wondrous and manifold? The greatness of the Merciful One is inconceivable. A wonder [indeed] — yet what is impossible for mercy?

*Note.* *Tat* at the end of the pada, before a vowel, stands in the edition without sandhi.

**20.** Then, going on through the southern region and observing curious customs, He saw crowds bearing sectarian marks of various kinds, who had entered upon the path of utter heresy.

*Note.* *Liṅga* — the outward marks of a sect: dress, tilaka, staff and so on.

**21.** Those heretics, great sinners abiding on an utterly perverse path, saw the Master and constantly tried to allure Him with their own doctrines of various kinds.

**22.** By the mere unfolding of His maya they do not see that they themselves have set out on the path of extreme heresy; how then could they bewilder Him with their various tricks?

**23.** Then those extremely dull-witted people saw a man of unsteady mind named Krishnadasa, who was walking in the company of the Lord of the world, and began to allure him.

*Note.* This is Kala Krishnadasa, the Lord's servant on the southern pilgrimage. In the Chaitanya-charitamrita (Madhya 9.226–231) he is lured away by the women of the Bhattatharis in Malara-desha, and the Lord rescues him.

**24.** "Hey, where are you going? [There] only suffering is to be gained. So make friends with us, and then you will go to heaven in this very little body of yours — there is no doubt about it.

*Note.* In the edition *duḥkha-mātra-sādhyam* agrees with nothing; the Gosvami edition (GGM) reads *duḥkha-mātraṁ sādhyam*, "[there] only suffering is to be gained", and so we read.

**25.** There is a certain path here, a little way off, inaccessible to all people. So come: by that very path we shall surely bring you from here to heaven."

**26.** Thus beguiled by those who had entered upon the path of heresy, his mind wavering, he grew bewildered and, as the Lord went along the road, gradually began to lag somewhat behind.

**27.** Perceiving the machinations of those wicked people and his fickleness, the Lord — the one ocean of mercy, the one friend of the world — entered into a dispute with those evil-minded men.

**28.** "Hey, renunciants, what is this? He is My servant, a mere boy — where are you leading him, having enticed him? This bodes no good for you, nor is it the conduct of the virtuous. So leave him and keep far away!"

*Note.* *Ādhvam* is the imperative, second person plural, of *ās*, "to sit, to stay".

**29.** Thus, after disputing for no long time, the ocean of mercy by His own power somehow or other turned him away [from them]; for so it happens when destiny is well pleased.

*Note.* *Tam* is singular, so Krishnadasa is meant, not the heretics (?). The syntax of *na ciraṁ kṛtena* is unclear. The Gosvami edition (GGM) has the same text.

**30.** Seeing such misconduct on the part of the one who had strayed from the road, He only smiled slightly, said nothing at all to Krishnadasa, and went on from there, heading for the Setu.

**31.** On the way the Lord unceasingly performed kirtana of His own qualities and names; His whole body washed by tears of love, He purified the whole forest.

**32.** Thus the Merciful One, who had flooded all the lands with mercy, came to the Setu. Seeing that Rameshvara worshipped by Rama, He bowed down and offered a hymn as well.

*Note.* Rameshvara is the Shiva-linga that, according to tradition, Rama worshipped before His campaign against Lanka.

**33.** Having seen the Setu, the glory of Raghunatha, the beautiful Gaurachandra, the ocean of mercy, whose power is supreme, then resolved there to turn back from the Setu.

**34.** [Returning] by that very same road, He saw the Deity Sri Ranga again, His heart melting; and, having come to the Godavari, He likewise had a meeting with Ramananda.

*Note.* Whether *tathaiva*, "likewise", implies that a meeting had already taken place on the way south (contrary to 12.131: *na taṁ vilokyaiva*) is not clear from the text (?). More probably *tathaiva* simply means "likewise [as He had seen Sri Ranga]". The Gosvami edition (GGM) has the same text.

**35.** Having come to the Godavari, the Master joyfully went to [Ramananda's] house to see him — like the moon [going] to the Mountain of Rising at the end of the season of clouds.

**36.** And he, whose mind was [absorbed] in Krishna, seeing the Master, fell to the ground out of joy and bowed down. Thereupon he bore a wealth of delight that had arisen [in him], increased ten-millionfold.

*Note.* In the edition the verse number lacks the second danda: *॥३६*.

**37.** And the Lord, at the mere sight of him, melted in heart under the weight of joy. Then He, the sole beauty of the world, lovely as a host of Kandarpas, shone forth on a raised [seat].

**38.** In a voice deep as thunder, and with guile, He said a few words: "O [friend], recite some poetry!" Hearing that, the great knower of rasa recited a verse rich in the flavour of renunciation.

*Note.* The edition clearly has *सकैतवं*, "with guile", that is, to test him. The Gosvami edition (GGM) has the same text.

**39.** "If [a thing] abundantly produces renunciation, let it be even sin; may merit be ours [only] if dense attachment does not arise from it. Through renunciation the mind is gladdened; [but if one] comes to attachment, then through attachment even a brahmana languishes in the hollow of a woman's womb."

*Note.* Metre *mandākrāntā*. The rendering of the syntax is conjectural (?); the second half might also be construed "one whose mind is gladdened by renunciation comes to *rāga*", with *rāga* in the third pada understood as love [for the Lord], in contrast to the worldly *rāga* of the fourth (?). The sense: better a sin that leads to renunciation than a merit that breeds attachment, for attachment leads to a new birth. This is the first, "external" stage of the conversation; cf. the stages in the Chaitanya-charitamrita (Madhya 8.57–67): varnashrama, offering the fruits of action, giving up one's own dharma, devotion mixed with knowledge — each of which the Lord calls "external". The Gosvami edition (GGM) has the same text.

**40.** Hearing this, Gaurachandra [said]: "This is external — external, exceedingly external, alas, external!" And He who puts an end to the rising of the heat born of the wealth of flashing eloquence did not attain great joy.

*Note.* The compound in the third and fourth padas is unclear (?). Cf. the famous *eho bāhya, āge kaha āra* ("this is external; speak further"), Chaitanya-charitamrita, Madhya 8.59–66, where the Lord repeats this after each of Ramananda's answers. The Gosvami edition (GGM) has the same text.

**41.** And then Ramananda, his mind purified, his body flooded with great bliss, recited a wholly lovely poem of his own that sets forth bhakti.

**42.** "Worship of the Friend of the afflicted performed with various offerings — [in it] the devotee's heart melts with happiness only through love. For as long as there is hunger in the stomach and keen thirst, just so long do food and drink give pleasure."

*Note.* Metre *vasantatilakā*. In the Chaitanya-charitamrita (Madhya 8.70) this stanza is quoted by Ramananda; it is also found in Rupa Gosvami's *Padyavali* (13). Karnapura calls it Ramananda's own stanza (*svakīyām*, v. 41).

**43.** Having heard this too, the Lord said likewise: "External — this too is external! Recite further." Then [Ramananda] fell [at His feet], entwining the Lord's feet with his very long hair.

**44.** His body languid under the weight of deep bewilderment, he bowed to the Lord — golden as gold, charming as Ananga — then fell at the base of His lotus feet, rose, and rejoiced.

*Note.* *Gāṅgeya* ("born of the Ganga") is gold.

**45.** Then he sang a song which the sakhis who relish rasa drink in. By setting forth the utmost limit of the supreme love of the two clever lovers, it sets forth the supreme oneness of the Two.

*Note.* "The oneness of the Two" — cf. *prema-vilāsa-vivarta* in the Chaitanya-charitamrita (Madhya 8.192–194; 8.190–193 in the numbering of the project's edition), the summit of the conversation with Ramananda.

**46.** (Raga *bhairavī*.)
At first attraction arose from the play of glances;
day by day it grew and knew no limit.
He is not the lover, nor am I the beloved:
the god of love ground our two minds into one — this I know.
O friend, all this tale of love
tell to Kanu, as to one who has forgotten.
I sought no messenger, I sought no one else:
the go-between at our meeting was the Five-Arrowed one.
And now, with that estrangement, you have become the messenger —
such is the way of love for a worthy man.
May the honour of King Rudra increase! —
so says the poet Ramananda Raya.

*Note.* This song is quoted in the Chaitanya-charitamrita (Madhya 8.194; 8.193 in the numbering of the project's edition), but without the last couplet, the poet's signature (*bhaṇitā*): there it ends with the line about the messenger. Readings of the edition: *पेशल* (the Chaitanya-charitamrita has *peṣala*, "ground"), *मधत* (= *madhya ta*). The signature line is understood tentatively. "King Rudra" is apparently Prataparudra, the king of Orissa, under whom Ramananda served as governor.

**47.** Then, having heard that [song], higher than the highest, the Lord — the pair of His lotus eyes opened wide, His inmost self stirred by the power of love — embraced him in deep joy.

**48.** Thus, for the two whose hearts were tossed by the billows of the manifold arts of firm embrace, that time became something indescribable — pervaded with festivity, with masses of waves of the ocean of happiness.

*Note.* The edition has *दृढाश्लेषकला कलाप-*; the space is erroneous. *Parvatā* — "festivity" (from *parvan*, "festival").

**49.** Thus, with the nectar of a natural love that had come down from long ago, He discussed with him the path of realisation; and having spent some days [there], He became eager to see Jagannatha.

*Note.* In the Chaitanya-charitamrita (Madhya 8) the conversation lasts ten days and takes place on the way south. On the return journey (Madhya 9.328–335) the Lord again spends a night in conversation with Ramananda, and Ramananda, having written to the king asking to be relieved of his post, prepares to follow Him to Puri.

**50.** Then the golden nectar-rayed moon came to the Kshetra, richly adorned and removing [all] weariness; and then, before the great bathing festival, He beheld the jewel in the crown of Nilachala.

*Note.* The bathing festival is the Snana-yatra of Jagannatha. "The jewel in the crown of Nilachala" is Jagannatha.

**51.** When Gaurachandra rose over Nilachala, the ocean raised a high tide; and the people, the darkness of their grief dispelled, had the lotuses of their eyes blossoming to their corners.

*Note.* The image rests on poetic conventions about the moon: its rising swells the tide of the ocean, dispels darkness and opens the night-blooming lotuses (*utpala*).

**52.** Some from beholding Jagannatha, some from bowing down and worship, others from circumambulation and service — all together gathered close around Him.

**53.** And the Lord — some with laughter, some with an affectionate, smiling glance, others with the sweetness of His embrace — made the hearts of all blossom with [the fulfilment of their] desires.

*Note.* The edition has no sandhi before *vilokanena* (*kāṁścit vilokanena*).

**54.** Then the Lord, in front of them, having made [them] witnesses, said "Go!" to Krishnadasa, whom He had brought to that Kshetra with great effort, and there dismissed him for good.

*Note.* "With great effort" recalls vv. 20–30, where the Lord wins Krishnadasa back from the heretics. Cf. the Chaitanya-charitamrita, Madhya 10.62–70: the Lord sends away Kala Krishnadasa, and the devotees send him to Bengal with the news of the Lord's return.

**55.** Gazing at the jewel in the crown of Nilachala, lovely with a hundred jewels, Gaurangachandra, overjoyed, again bathed His own body with the stream of water from His own eyes.

**56.** Then He beheld the beautiful great bathing festival, hard for even the gods to attain, as though He were beholding on the ocean shore another, lofty ocean — of abounding bliss.

*Note.* *Īkṣyamāṇaḥ* is passive in form, but with the accusative *samudram* it must be taken actively ("beholding").

**57.** Then, at daybreak, the Merciful One went as before to see Him; but not seeing Him there, hidden as He was, He became sorrowful and shed tears.

*Note.* After the Snana-yatra Jagannatha is hidden from view for two weeks (the *anavasara*). Cf. the Chaitanya-charitamrita, Madhya 1.122 (in separation from Jagannatha during the anavasara the Lord goes to Alalanatha); 11.61–63. The word order *vilokya nāsau* stands for *na vilokya asau*.

**58.** Going out in haste, in great longing, wondrous in His behaviour, beautiful as an exhilarated lion, He went off to Alalanatha; and they likewise went in search of Him, distressed in body.

*Note.* Alalanatha is a temple of Vishnu at Brahmagiri, south of Puri, to which the Lord would withdraw during the anavasara.

**59.** Having searched there and not finding Him at all, they, utterly dejected, were sorely tormented. Then they came [back] to the Kshetra in exceeding sorrow, and a single moment seemed to them like kalpas.

*Note.* A *kalpa* is a "day of Brahma", an immense span of time.

**60.** And the Lord, most excellent and merciful, went thus by that same road to the Godavari, and together with that same [Ramananda], in dear conversation, spent another four months.

*Note.* An important divergence from the Chaitanya-charitamrita (checked against Madhya 1.122–123 and 11). In Karnapura the Lord goes from Alalanatha (or from Puri after the anavasara) once more to the Godavari and spends another chaturmasya with Ramananda. In the Chaitanya-charitamrita (Madhya 11) Ramananda himself comes to Puri, and the Lord remains there.

**61.** Then, in the season of early winter, the Most Excellent, likewise together with him, spreading mercy all around, came to the best of Kshetras. Who can know His wondrous deeds?

*Note.* *Hemanta* is the season after autumn (November–December).

**62.** Having arrived at Nilachala, He — like a golden mountain, His body lovely — eagerly and constantly beheld that Mahaprabhu Jagannatha and passed the time in joy.

**63.** Hearing that He had arrived, Kashi Mishra, whose darkness — the veil of his sins — had been destroyed, saw [Him], the longed-for [Lord], endowed with four arms, bowed down, and rejoiced to his heart's content.

*Note.* Kashi Mishra was a priest and superintendent of the Jagannatha temple; in his house (the Gambhira) the Lord lived in Puri.

**64.** Kissed by His mercies, coloured by the dust of His beautiful lotus feet, his body bristling with thrills of rapture, overpowered by dense happiness, he shone.

*Note.* Here the metre changes: vv. 64–114 are in *svāgatā* (—⏑— ⏑⏑⏑ —⏑⏑ ——) alternating with *rathoddhatā* (—⏑— ⏑⏑⏑ —⏑— ⏑—).

**65.** By whom can the greatness of that most worthy man be measured, who by His very great mercy held in his own control the Lakshmi of the house of [Him who is] the tilaka of the Blue Mountain?

*Note.* "The tilaka of the Blue Mountain" is Jagannatha. *Ālaya-lakṣmī* — "the Lakshmi (wealth) of the house", that is, the temple's estate.

**66.** He carries out every command of Gaurachandra's two feet, discerning [His] wish: can this Kashi Mishra be known through any account?

**67.** In arranging the great festivals he, having discerned the Lord's mind, carried out various special arrangements, mostly of his own conception: can he be understood by ordinary people?

*Note.* The edition marks the verse number with a dash: *॥६७–*.

**68.** Also, a certain exalted great soul of pure intelligence, named Vishnudasa, abandoning everything, beheld the pair of lotus feet of the son of Sri Shachi.

*Note.* Cf. the Chaitanya-charitamrita, Adi 10.151 (Vishnudasa is among the Lord's associates who lived in Puri).

**69.** At once he, blessed through his store of pious deeds, was pervaded by His mercies, and then his whole body was washed by the streams of water pouring from his two eyes.

**70.** A certain fortunate man of abundant merit, an ocean of good fortune, named Pradyumna Mishra, joyfully made the pair of Gaurachandra's lotus feet the guest of his eyes.

*Note.* The edition divides the first two padas wrongly (10 and 12 syllables); in the Gosvami edition (GGM) the boundary falls within the name — *subhagaḥ pra- / dyumna-miśra* — and both padas are correct (11 syllables each); the edition's *सुभयः* is apparently a misprint for *subhagaḥ*. Cf. the Chaitanya-charitamrita, Antya 5.4–8: Pradyumna Mishra asks the Lord to speak about Krishna, and He sends him to hear Ramananda.

**71.** Thus, merely by being the guest of his eyes, His compassion was manifested upon him: for the stream of water that came to his eyes was truly a rain-laden cloud of the month of Shravana.

*Note.* *Shravana* is July–August, the season of the heaviest rains.

**72.** Once the Lord, remembering His own particular pastimes, went impetuously into charming gardens that awaken remembrance of Vrindavana.

**73.** Entering that grove — which in Him had found its lord — and beholding the charm of its supreme splendour, the Lord became exceedingly full-hearted with love.

*Note.* A play on words: the *nāthaḥ* ("Lord") enters the grove, and it becomes *sanātham* — "possessed of a lord".

**74.** [He beheld that row of creepers,] its clusters of flowers assailed by bees, its rows of young shoots trembling — as if it were a beloved shaking the tender shoot of her hand at her darling who delights in biting her lip…

*Note.* Verses 74–75 form a pair (*yugmaka*; so marked in the edition too). Metre *rathoddhatā*; in the first pada *-ta pra-* is long by position — a licence. The accusative *priyām* can stand: it is in apposition to *āvalīm* (v. 75), the row of creepers being itself the "beloved"; one could also read *priyā* (nominative) (?).

**75.** …He beheld that row of creepers, lovely and slender-limbed, with their charming bees — like a company of dancing girls possessed of rhythm, measure, tempo and expressive gestures.

*Note.* *Va* = *iva* ("like"). Double meanings: *bhṛṅga* — "bee" and "gallant"; *tāla* — "rhythm" and "palm tree".

**76.** Thus for a long time, setting down the pair of His lotus feet here in a light dance, and here and there forgetting His own movements as He remembered the dance of the playful [gopis],

*Note.* The verse has no finite verb: the sentence closes with *vireje*, "He shone", in v. 77.

**77.** …His captivating chest resplendent with the beauty of a necklace formed by His streaming tears, the creeper of His body bristling with rapture to its very tips, His face like the full moon — He shone.

**78.** While He was thus sporting here, the Gajapati, drawn by Sarvabhauma's accounts, eagerly and daringly came here quickly into His presence.

*Note.* Gajapati ("lord of elephants") is the title of the kings of Orissa; here it is Prataparudra. In the Chaitanya-charitamrita (Madhya 14.4–16) this scene follows the Ratha-yatra: on Sarvabhauma's advice the king, dressed as a Vaishnava, enters the garden where the Lord is resting and recites to Him verses about the rasa-lila; at the stanza *tava kathāmṛtam* the Lord rises and embraces him.

**79.** When good fortune comes, first of all one gains what one longed for, one's wishes bearing fruit; but what the king did daringly brought him happiness ten million times over.

**80.** By whom, however exalted in spirit, could a full measure be taken of the ocean of good fortune of that king, exceedingly glorified by his shining heap of merits?

*Note.* *Pariṇati* is taken here as "measuring, reckoning"; the translation is tentative.

**81.** Then the king, glorified by great fortune, entered that excellent grove and saw Him, the ocean of compassion, shining like a mountain of molten gold.

**82.** Falling to the ground like a rod and clasping the pair of [His] lotus feet, the great-souled [king], his tears streaming, artlessly praised [Him], describing in particular the rasa dance.

*Note.* Cf. the Chaitanya-charitamrita, Madhya 14.8–13, where the king recites the Gopi-gita (Srimad-Bhagavatam 10.31, from *jayati te 'dhikam* to *tava kathāmṛtam*). In Karnapura he describes the rasa dance.

**83.** As he was thus praising, he was then lifted up by the beautiful, supremely Merciful One, who clasped him tightly with His two arms, like the trunk of a maddened elephant.

*Note.* *Samudāse* is unclear: perhaps the perfect middle of *sam-ud-as* ("to toss up, to raise"), here with passive sense and the instrumental of the agent (*śrīmatā parama-kāruṇikena*) (?). The Gosvami edition (GGM) has the same text.

**84.** Adorned with streaming tears and with the thrill of his hairs standing on end, that lord of elephants — the king, though as strong as a king of wrestlers — crushed in His arms, became as [a mere] nothing.

*Note.* *Gajeśa*, "lord of elephants", renders the title Gajapati borne by the kings of Orissa.

**85.** Releasing him, the Lord, His body all moist [with tears], then asked: "Who are you?" And he answered: "This person is indeed Your servant. Grant [him] this — service!"

**86.** The Lord said: "O sir, by one such as you I ought not to be named at all!" And Rudradeva, greatly delighted beyond measure, eagerly spoke in turn.

*Note.* The sense of the Lord's words is unclear (?). It is not fitting for a sannyasi to associate with a king; cf. the Chaitanya-charitamrita, Madhya 11.6–8: for a renounced sannyasi, meeting a king is like willingly drinking poison. Rudradeva is Prataparudra. The Gosvami edition (GGM) has the same text.

**87.** His heart joyful, rich with the weight of abundant joy, [the king] quickly went out from there. When destiny is favourable, what is unattainable for the fortunate, whose good deeds are very abundant?

**88.** The supreme mercy that the Lord, the one ocean of compassion, extended to every person — ah, could [even] the lord of speech be able to tell of it? How then these others?

*Note.* *Gīṣpati*, "the lord of speech", is Brihaspati, the preceptor of the gods.

**89.** There, in the land of Purushottama, lives a pure man named Shikhi Mahiti — a great soul who, as His servant, has obtained the mercy of the tilaka of the Blue Mountain.

*Note.* "The tilaka of the Blue Mountain" — Jagannatha, the ornament of Nilachala. Shikhi Mahiti was a scribe of the Jagannatha temple; cf. the Chaitanya-charitamrita, Adi 10.135–136 (Shikhi Mahiti and his brother Murari Mahiti are constant companions of the Lord in Puri) and Antya 2.104–106. The rendering of *dāsavat karuṇatāṁ samupetaḥ* is conjectural (?). The Gosvami edition (GGM) has the same text.

**90.** He has a younger brother named Murari, and after him, likewise, the youngest, a sister of pure intelligence, Madhava-devi. Thus these siblings lived there.

*Note.* The brother is Murari Mahiti, not Murari Gupta. In the Chaitanya-charitamrita (Antya 2.104–106) the sister is Madhavi-devi, an aged ascetic, whom the Lord counted among the associates of Radharani; the "three and a half" intimate associates [of the Lord in Puri] are Svarupa Damodara, Ramananda, Shikhi Mahiti and, as the "half", Madhavi.

**91.** These two, however, the dear younger brother and sister, became devoted to Gaurachandra; for a steadfast, auspicious, innate inclination never admits of forgetfulness.

**92.** "This Master, the supreme treasury of mercy, has come forth to manifest love. This Beloved, made of loveliness, is the moon [risen] from the ocean of Sri Shachi's womb.

*Note.* The edition numbers this verse *॥८२॥*, a misprint for 92. Verses 92–93 give the thought of the brother and sister.

**93.** The Moon of Vrindavana has now risen here on the earth as Gaurachandra." Such was the auspicious conviction of these two, who constantly cherished an abundance of love.

**94.** And these two made great efforts that their elder brother, the loving servant of the Lord of the Blue Mountain, should worship Gaurachandra; but he did not become inclined to it.

**95.** The next day, at his younger brother's instruction, he was constantly [turning over] many thoughts in his mind; and when the last part of the night had come, he was overwhelmed by a vision in a dream.

*Note.* *Bahu-manaḥ-kathā-cyutaḥ* is unclear: perhaps read *-yutaḥ* ("occupied with many reflections") (?); or *cyutaḥ* may mean "lapsed [into sleep]" amid many reflections (?). The Gosvami edition (GGM) has the same text.

**96.** And his younger brother and sister, who delighted in the lotus feet of Gaurachandra, were waking their brother at that very moment, and they saw him startled by what he had seen in the dream.

**97.** With floods of thrill arising from the wondrous vision, his hairs standing on end, he became twice [his size] for joy. Slowly he opened his eyes, filled with tears, and then saw the two of them.

**98.** Seeing close beside him the two great-hearted ones, who had come to wake him, he, overjoyed, embraced them tightly; and then the two were astonished.

**99.** "O brother and sister, hear what I saw in a dream: it is a wonder indeed! The son of Shachi is of immeasurable greatness — of this I have today become fully convinced.

**100.** Gazing at the tilaka of the Blue Mountain, He enters there [into Him] at every moment; then, coming outside again, He looks [at Him]. Just so He did again and again.

*Note.* That is, the Lord enters into Jagannatha and comes out of Him again. The dream of Shikhi Mahiti is not in the Chaitanya-charitamrita (Antya 2.104–106 merely names him and his sister among the intimate associates); it is apparently known only from Karnapura.

**101.** A wonder, a great wonder indeed! Even now I see the Lord in that state. My two eyes seem bewildered from beholding [His] supreme play.

*Note.* The syntax of the second half is unclear (?). Perhaps the pair of eyes is the Lord's own: "[His] two eyes as if bewildered from beholding the supreme grace [of Jagannatha]" (?). The Gosvami edition (GGM) has the same text.

**102.** And when I came near Him, the ocean of boundless mercy called me by name and embraced me with His pair of beautiful arms — long, mighty, gracefully reaching to the knees."

**103.** Thus, his body thrilling with hairs on end, his voice faltering with love, in great agitation, his two eyes streaming with tears, he did not cease repeating this.

**104.** Hearing this, the two were filled with happiness and bade [him] go there to see the Master, who had gone to behold the Lord of the Blue Mountain.

**105.** Saying "So be it," the three siblings, exceedingly great-hearted, who had set their longing on the feet of Gaurachandra, quickly went to behold the Lord of the Dark Mountain.

**106.** There the two, their hearts joyful, first of all saw the son of Shachi in the Jaganmohana; and seeing Him, with streams of tears flowing from their eyes, they attained joy.

*Note.* The Jaganmohana is the hall in front of the sanctuary of the temple. In the edition the lines are wrongly divided: *तत्र तौ मुदितमानसौ– / जगन्मोहने…*; by the metre the boundary falls after *jagan-*.

**107.** And the elder brother, Shikhi by name, seeing Him in every respect just as he had seen Him in the dream, became thrilled at heart with love.

**108.** And He, the abundantly merciful, then said, "You are Murari's elder brother," and embraced him with both arms; and he, his mind fixed on Him, became [as it were] the embodied gathering of a mass of happiness.

**109.** From that time on, having forgotten everything through a particle of the fragrance of His pair of lotus feet, he always, every day, serves Him as his own Deity — so great is his fortune.

*Note.* The edition gives the number without the second stroke: *॥१०९*.

**110.** Just so the ocean of compassion, His heart drawn by affection, suddenly drew to the land of Purushottama His own people who dwelt on the banks of the river of the gods.

*Note.* "The river of the gods" is the Ganga; that is, [He drew His devotees from] Bengal.

**111.** There was a certain one named Madhava Puri, who preceded the descent of the son of Sri Shachi — a great-minded one on the earth, the very rasa of bhakti for Vishnu embodied.

*Note.* Madhava Puri is Madhavendra Puri, the guru of Ishvara Puri. The form *-vatāraṇa-* (for *avatāraṇa*) is for the sake of the metre.

**112.** A great soul became his disciple — of spotless splendour like a myriad suns, truthful in speech, most pure, with a heart full of rasa, unfathomable, deeper than the ocean…

**113.** …Ishvara [Puri], an avatara of the lord of serpents; he was as if the rasa of bhakti embodied. He became a worshipper [of the Lord], and before [Him] he also spread renunciation across the lands.

*Note.* Ishvara Puri is the guru of Sri Chaitanya. "An avatara of the lord of serpents" (Shesha, Ananta): we have not found such an identification in the other sources available to us. The Gosvami edition (GGM) reads *sa pūrvaṁ bhūmiṣu* (*pūrvam* as a separate word, not "in the eastern lands"); we accept *pūrvam* — "before", "first". Verses 111–114 form one connected group.

**114.** With him the best of Lords had in the south a meeting full [of joy]. Cool [of temper], steady of mind — was he not the very mass of forbearance embodied?

*Note.* Grammatically the verse might continue the description of Ishvara Puri. But the meeting in the south (13.14–16) was with Paramananda Puri. Perhaps verses 111–114 speak of the line of Madhavendra Puri, and "he" here is already Paramananda, who is named in verse 115. The Gosvami edition (GGM) has the same text.

**115.** Then the master called Paramananda Puri, the supreme beloved of the worlds, in [the course of his] wandering came at that time by chance to the bank of the river of the gods.

*Note.* From here to v. 125, except v. 119, the metre is *viyoginī* (⏑⏑—⏑⏑—⏑—⏑— / ⏑⏑——⏑⏑—⏑—⏑—). "The river of the gods" (*sura-dīrghikā*) is the Ganga.

**116.** Then, full of longing, he reached Navadvipa, adorned with the pastimes of the Master, and eagerly took his rest in the house of the Supreme Lord.

**117.** She who is the mother of the three worlds, truly as forbearing as myriads of earths, purifying beyond the river of the gods, full of constant affection, great-hearted —

*Note.* Verses 117–118 form a pair (*yugmaka*; so marked in the edition). They speak of Shachi.

**118.** — is she not the nectar of bhakti embodied? Is she love itself? Is she not made of sweetness? Seeing him, the great-minded one, she at once honoured him with alms, feeling for him as for [her own] son.

*Note.* The first pada has 11 syllables instead of 10: *kiṁ* is superfluous or corrupt (?). The Gosvami edition (GGM) has the same text.

**119.** On another day this most magnanimous one, joyful, took alms from Acharyaratna, dwelling in the very house of that beloved of the Lord and again and again bestowing joy on him.

*Note.* Acharyaratna is Chandrashekhara Acharya, the Lord's maternal uncle. Metre *upajāti*.

**120.** Then a certain one — who, from bathing deeply and many times in the Sarasvati, the river of the nectar of love for the feet of Gaurachandra, was constantly filled with it within and without —

**121.** — dear to Him, great and great-minded, named Kamalananda, came there in haste, joyfully, to see that Mother.

*Note.* Kamalananda is named in the Chaitanya-charitamrita (Adi 10.149) among the Bengali servants of the Lord who later settled with Him in Puri (together with Singabhatta, Kamabhatta and Shivananda); nothing more is known of him.

**122.** Having seen the Mother, he then in turn saw that master, Paramananda Puri, and from the sight of him he became supremely tender-hearted.

**123.** Having spent some days there, they set out together — and with them that Gadadhara Pandita, and also this noble Jagadananda.

*Note.* In the Chaitanya-charitamrita the Lord's companions on the way to Puri are Nityananda, Jagadananda, Damodara and Mukunda (Madhya 3.209); Gadadhara is named among the Bengali devotees who came for the first Ratha-yatra (Madhya 11.84), and afterwards he stays with the Lord in Puri (15.183). The Mahakavya itself, in 11.76, already names Gadadhara among the Lord's companions on the way to Puri, and here he comes again — with Paramananda Puri.

**124.** That king of ascetics was full of eagerness for the good fortune of seeing the Lord Gaurasundara; [they all] went to the supreme Purushottama and, having arrived, then saw the Lord.

*Note.* The subject is singular, but the verbs are plural: *yayuḥ*, *adadṛśuḥ*.

**125.** Then the king of ascetics beheld the pair of lotus feet of Gaura Mahaprabhu. And the Lord Himself even bowed down to him, showing him abundant respect as an elder.

*Note.* In the edition there is a superfluous danda at the end of the verse: *। ॥१२५॥*.

**126.** Acharya Vidyanidhi too came likewise — a boundless ocean of virtues, full of love, blissful of soul — and also the honoured, magnanimous Acharyaratna, the great soul.

*Note.* Vidyanidhi is Pundarika Vidyanidhi. The edition reads *आचार्यरत्नं* (accusative) — apparently a misprint for *ācāryaratnaḥ*. The metre of vv. 126–127 is *upajāti*. The Gosvami edition (GGM) has the same text.

**127.** The glorious one known as Shivananda, having come together with Murari Gupta, then saw that Lord for the first time — as if the heap of his own good fortune embodied.

*Note.* Shivananda Sena is the father of Kavi Karnapura.

**128.** And He, His heart softened with compassion for the wretched, again and again touched his head with the petal of His big toe, and also said this: "Surely I know you."

*Note.* Here *viyoginī* resumes. Cf. Chaitanya-charitamrita, Antya 1.15–32: every year Shivananda Sena leads the Bengali devotees to Puri and takes care of everyone on the way, even of a stray dog.

**129.** After that the righteous Raghava, who had gathered a store of merits, full of love, impetuously [came and] saw Him; and He, softened with mercy, at once showed him mercy.

*Note.* Raghava Pandita of Panihati.

**130.** Then a noble one of pure mind named Govinda, a very great ocean of merit, after wandering abroad through many holy places, came —

*Note.* Verses 130–131 form a pair (*yugmaka*). Govinda is the Lord's personal servant. In the Chaitanya-charitamrita (Madhya 10.131–148) he comes at the order of his guru Ishvara Puri. The first pada has 11 syllables: metrically *sa* belongs to the beginning of the second.

**131.** — to Purushottama. There he saw that Beloved, Gaura, the great treasure-house of mercy, and became constantly devoted to the service of His lotus feet.

**132.** And this very fortunate one too, from then on staying here near the lotus feet of the Lord and having given up [all other] activities, rendered [Him] service day and night.

**133.** Then a noble one of pure mind, virtuous and of righteous conduct, named Bhavananda, adorned with floods of happiness, beheld the Lord.

*Note.* Bhavananda Raya is the father of Ramananda.

**134.** And the Lord firmly embraced with both arms [him] whose heart was very pure, and spoke sweet words: "O, you are as fortunate as Pandu!"

*Note.* Cf. Chaitanya-charitamrita, Madhya 10.51–53: the Lord calls Bhavananda Pandu, his wife Kunti, and his five sons the Pandavas.

**135.** And his five sons too, Ramananda and the others, staying at the side of that greatly Merciful One and rendering [Him] service, truly became very dear to Him.

*Note.* The metre of vv. 135–137 is *upajāti*.

**136.** Gentle, a great soul, supremely dear, peaceful, ever the friend of all people, devoted to the feet of Chaitanya-chandra — [such was] Vaninatha, who served Him.

*Note.* Vaninatha Pattanayaka is one of Bhavananda's sons.

**137.** A certain great-minded one of lovely character, named Purushottama and bearing the title of Acharya, having heard of His deeds, came with effort, eager at heart, to see Him.

*Note.* The future Svarupa Damodara (see v. 143). Cf. Chaitanya-charitamrita, Madhya 10.102–125.

**138.** Having come to Purushottama, he then, overwhelmed, beheld the feet of the treasure-house of mercy. And from the mere delight of seeing [them] he became — who can say what?

*Note.* *Sammataḥ* at the end is unclear (?). Perhaps: "he became esteemed [by the Lord] in a way beyond words" (?). The metre of vv. 138–144 is *viyoginī*. The Gosvami edition (GGM) has the same text.

**139.** Ah, even [his] body was forgotten! Only rasa, only happiness was seen [by him]. And from the sight of the Lord of his life he was always overcome by stupor.

**140.** Then he bore eyes filled with cascades of tears, a body adorned solely with hairs rising on end, and a heavy pair of thighs breaking and giving way under violent trembling.

*Note.* The first pada has 12 syllables instead of 10. Without *atha* the metre is correct: *nayane jala-nirjharākule* (?). The end of the verse (*गुरुमूरुद्वितयं*) is *gurum ūru-dvitayam*; so too in the Gosvami edition (GGM).

**141.** Filled with emotion, he became the very beloved [maiden] filled with emotion by her lover's glance. And He too, the sole ocean of love, became very tender through [these] displays of love.

**142.** Beholding [in him] such sweet beauty [of emotion], manifold, the Lord at once became tender; and He, the ocean of great rasa, showed him a deep and renowned love.

**143.** Then he, of good disposition and of no small fortune, accepted sannyasa and attained the very nature (*svarūpa*) of rasa; here he is called Damodara.

*Note.* A play on words: *rasa-svarūpatā* ("the very nature of rasa") hints at the name Svarupa Damodara. According to the Chaitanya-charitamrita (Madhya 10.103–109), Purushottama Acharya took sannyasa in Varanasi from Chaitanyananda Bharati without putting on saffron cloth, changing only his name (to Svarupa), and then, with his guru's permission, came to Puri.

**144.** Thus, staying constantly near the lotus feet of the Lord, he could not bear even the blinking of his eyes, gazing [at Him] as if drinking [Him] in with thirst.

*Note.* *Tena* is syntactically superfluous; perhaps a corruption (?). The Gosvami edition (GGM) has the same text.

**145.** Sri Vakreshvara Pandita — a certain most sweet great soul, ever an ocean of the nectar of the rasa of dense bliss, and so the abode of the love of Sri Prabhu — came, beheld [Him], and became one at the very outset of whose dancing Gaura Mahaprabhu Himself would always be tenderly inclined [toward him].

*Note.* Metre *śārdūla-vikrīḍita*. Vakreshvara was renowned for his dancing at kirtanas; cf. Chaitanya-charitamrita, Adi 10.17: he could dance in unbroken ecstasy for seventy-two hours.

**146.** Sri Vasudeva by name, the one jewel of the Datta family, having seen Gauranga-chandra, at once became wholly immersed in the ocean of love for Him and truly became for ever no different from [His] very life.

*Note.* Metre *vasantatilakā*. *Jīvana-nirviśeṣaḥ* is understood tentatively: "equal to [His] life", that is, as dear as life. *Ratnaṁ* is a neuter nominative in apposition to the subject (in the neuter the nominative and accusative coincide). Vasudeva Datta is famed for having asked the Lord to let him take upon himself the sins of all living beings (Chaitanya-charitamrita, Madhya 15.159–180). The Gosvami edition (GGM) has the same text.

**147.** And another, a certain great soul known here as Bhagavan, the best of true acharyas, constantly bowed in devotion to Sri Gaurachandra, served the glorious Lord Jagannatha.

*Note.* Bhagavan Acharya; cf. Chaitanya-charitamrita, Antya 2.84–100 (a learned man and great devotee who lived with the Lord in Puri; his brother was Gopala Bhattacharya). Metre *upajāti*.

**148.** Thus, while Sri Purushottama abode [there], the report [of Him] spread in every direction, and the people of all the quarters and intermediate quarters came with longing. And others came too — the good-minded Satyaraja, his brothers, sons and others; and others again — Raghunandana, Narahari, the glorious Mukunda and the rest.

*Note.* Metre *śārdūla-vikrīḍita*. *Śrī-puruṣottame sthitavati* (locative absolute) can be understood both as "while [the Lord] abode in Sri Purushottama" and as "while Sri Purushottama — the Supreme Person, the Lord — abode [there]". The edition has *प्रत्यासम्*; we read *pratyāśam* ("in every direction") (?). *Tad-bhrātṛ-putrādayaḥ* may also be read as "his brother's sons and others". Satyaraja Khan is from Kulinagrama. Mukunda, his son Raghunandana and his brother Narahari Sarakara are from Shrikhanda. Cf. Chaitanya-charitamrita, Madhya 10.89–90 (the residents of Kulinagrama and Mukunda, Narahari and Raghunandana of Khanda join Advaita) and 11.91–92. The Gosvami edition (GGM) has the same text.

**Colophon:** इति श्रीकृष्णचैतन्यचरितामृते महाकाव्ये त्रयोदशः सर्गः । (*iti śrī-kṛṣṇa-caitanya-caritāmṛte mahākāvye trayodaśaḥ sargaḥ*) — "Thus [ends] the thirteenth sarga in the mahakavya 'Sri Krishna-Chaitanya-charitamrita'." (The colophon gives no title for the sarga.)

---

## Sarga Fourteen — verses 1–135

**Title of the sarga:** none — the colophon of the edition gives no title for this sarga.
**Metre:**
- 1–129 — *anuṣṭubh* (*śloka*; 8 syllables per pada: odd padas ××××⏑——×, even padas ××××⏑—⏑×; the odd padas also admit the usual *vipulā* variants). Verses of six or more padas: 5, 37, 77, 81, 87, 102, 108, 109, 117; verses of two padas: 69, 82, 99. In the printed text the first line of 50 and the second line of 93 are one syllable short (in 93 the reading of the Gosvami edition, *stavana-*, restores the metre).
- 130–132, 134–135 — *śārdūla-vikrīḍita* (———⏑⏑—⏑—⏑⏑⏑———⏑——⏑—; 19 syllables per pada).
- 133 — *upajāti* (a mixture of *indravajrā* ——⏑——⏑⏑—⏑—— and *upendravajrā* ⏑—⏑——⏑⏑—⏑——; 11 syllables per pada).

**Total verses:** 135. The Gosvami edition (GGM) has 141: there the long stanzas of this edition (e.g. 37, 87, 108–109, 117) are divided into couplets, and number 71 is skipped; the text is the same, and nothing is missing.

**Contents of the passage:** the first coming of the Bengali devotees for the festival of the chariots; the Snana-yatra.
- 1–10 — the Lord calls Advaita and Srivasa to come every year for the chariot festival.
- 11–35 — the preparations. Srivasa, Vasudeva and Shivananda fix the day; with Shachi in Navadvipa; Advaita, Haridasa, Rama Pandita, Mukunda, Murari; the journey with kirtana as far as Remuna.
- 36–41 — Gopinatha in Remuna; the stream of devotees — "an ocean without shores" (*kulaka*).
- 42–56 — **Sarvabhauma in Jayapura.** He is going to Kashi to preach bhakti against the Lord's prohibition; he glorifies Advaita and Haridasa.
- 57–66 — Jajpur and the Vaitarani. After a dream Prataparudra summons Advaita to Cuttack (Advaita fears the Lord's displeasure); the others go by way of Hamseshvara; all are together again at Kamalapura.
- 67–82 — the temple of Jagannatha in the distance; garlands from the Lord; at the Narendra a message: a fast, and the meeting in Mishra's house; Murari, in his humility, stays behind by the tank.
- 83–114 — the darshan of Jagannatha and the meeting with the Lord. The description of His form (*kulaka*, 85–92); the mutual obeisances with Advaita (Bhagavatam 10.14.1); Srivasa, Rama Pandita, Vasudeva; two vessels of Ganga water; Murari comes with a blade of grass between his teeth.
- 115–135 — the wedding festival on the Ekadashi; the Snana-yatra on the full moon: the platform; the procession of Baladeva, Subhadra and Jagannatha; the bathing; the hyperbole of "the hero's march".

**1.** Once the Lord said to His own people, headed by Advaita, who had come there to see His lotus feet:

**2.** "O Acharya, great in intellect! O noble-hearted Pandita! Hear what I shall say about the doings of the glorious Jagannatha.

*Note.* The Acharya is Advaita, the Pandita is Srivasa (cf. verses 11, 24).

**3.** This Lord Sri Jagannatha, ever the shelter of all rasas, makes the journey to the Gundicha with a mind intent on play.

**4.** This blossoming garden, full of loveliness, which [lies] here on both sides of the road on the way to the Gundicha temple —

*Note.* The relative clause (*ya eṣa…*) is taken up by *enam*, "it", in verse 5.

**5.** — know that it especially awakens the remembrance of Vrindavana. Going there, Lord Jagannatha stays in the pavilion of the Gundicha for eight days and one more, sporting there.

*Note.* A verse of six padas. "Eight days and one more" means nine days.

**6.** Therefore people such as you should surely come here every year to see this supreme procession, hard to attain even for the gods and others."

**7.** Thus Mahaprabhu, the ocean of mercy, spoke to them on the pretext of the Ratha-yatra, [so that they might come] to see His own pastimes.

*Note.* The edition's *कृपाम्भोधी* is the regular sandhi form of *kṛpāmbhodhiḥ* before *r*.

**8.** From then on they have gone thus every year at the time of the chariot, longing to see Him, the Lord Sri Gaurachandra.

**9.** The story of how they go forth every year to see the beautiful Gauranga — could even a hundred Brihaspatis, preceptors of the gods, tell it?

*Note.* The preceptor of the gods is Brihaspati, the type of eloquence.

**10.** Nevertheless, out of constant longing, setting it forth in a general way, we shall describe one such journey in full.

*Note.* *Aviśeṣataḥ*, "without distinction", i.e. in general: a single journey is described as representative of all of them, while *samantāt* means "in full", from beginning to end. The Gosvami edition (GGM) has the same text.

**11.** The next year the divine Advaita Acharya and the glorious Srivasa Pandita, taking many people with them, became eager to set out.

**12.** When the month of Madhava had begun, when the Malaya breeze was blowing and [the air] resounded charmingly with cuckoos, bees and the rest, they made ready to go.

*Note.* Madhava is the month of Vaishakha (April–May). The Malaya breeze is the spring wind from the Malaya mountains in the south. Note the sound-play *-mārute | rute … cāru te*.

**13.** First the glorious Srivasa Pandita, joyful at heart, his mind tender and overflowing with love for Sri Gaurachandra…

*Note.* Verses 13–15 form one sentence.

**14.** …joyfully spoke to Sri Vasudeva Datta and Sri Shivananda Sena, gladdening his own heart and theirs too:

**15.** "That time of the chariot has come. So fix now an auspicious day for our setting out, and for yours too."

**16.** Then, having fixed the day of departure, all of them, full of the greatest longing, became very eager to go to Sri Navadvipa.

**17.** Those noble-minded ones saw the holy Sri Shachi, bhakti for Vishnu embodied, the mother of the whole world.

**18.** Having stayed there two days, made happy by the fullness of her affection, they saw the glorious Lord Advaita, greatly eager [for the journey].

**19.** Then the Lord Advaita Acharya said sweetly: "The day of departure that is auspicious for you is auspicious for me as well."

**20.** Then all of them, delighted, gave themselves there to dancing and kirtana, made blissful by their love for the feet of Gauranga.

**21.** The glorious Lord Advaita too set out, supremely eager — as if the great boundary mountain of the rasa of the pastimes of bhakti.

*Note.* In the edition the verse number lacks the second danda: *॥२१*.

**22.** Then Sri Haridasa, immersed in the great ocean of the pastimes of bhakti like the great mountain Mainaka in the ocean…

*Note.* Mainaka is the mountain that hid in the ocean from Indra when he was cutting off the wings of the mountains. Verses 22–23 form one sentence.

**23.** …he who incessantly and longingly sang His qualities, drawing them from the ocean of His greatness — he too agreed to this very [journey].

*Note.* In the edition the verse number lacks the second danda: *॥२३*.

**24.** Then these great souls, Haridasa and the others, placing the Acharya and the Pandita at their head, went on their way happily.

**25.** Sri Vasudeva Datta too and Sri Shivananda Sena, deeply attached to each other, went joyfully in their company.

**26.** There came Srivasa Pandita's younger brother Rama Pandita, by whose singing Gauranga was always brought under his sway.

**27.** The pure, gentle-minded, glorious Mukunda, supremely dear, sweet, peaceful, consoling in speech, exceedingly tender…

*Note.* Verses 27–28 form a pair (*yugmaka*, so marked in the edition). Mukunda (Datta) is a singer, the Lord's friend from His youth.

**28.** …and then Murari Gupta, an ocean of the rasa of loving bhakti, as if [Mukunda's] second self, went along in joy as a companion in his company.

*Note.* A play on *dvitīya*, "second" and "companion".

**29.** Then, overwhelmed by love for the feet of Srila Gauranga, they went on in joy, chanting His qualities, names and so on — His alone.

**30.** Beginning the kirtana in the morning, they would rest [only] at dusk or at night, and after that [attend to] what had to be done on the road.

**31.** Thus, spending the day in kirtana and dancing, the noble-hearted ones went along the road, their hearts supremely eager.

**32.** Of each of those days nothing can be described: there was simply an ocean of happiness, flooding all the quarters.

**33.** Thus, their hearts tossed by the billows of the ocean of joy, longing for the feet of Gaura, they joyfully came to Remuna.

*Note.* Remuna is a town near Balasore, where the temple of Gopinatha Kshirachora stands.

**34.** The sun, laying all its rays upon the crest of the mountain of sunset, grieved again and again at not obtaining their glance.

*Note.* A play on *kara*, "ray" and "hand": the sun in despair "props its head on its hand" — and sets.

**35.** There, in that town, to behold the glorious Gopinatha, they entered the lovely city, their slender bodies covered with thrills of rapture.

**36.** Seeing the moon of His face, they attained the highest joy; and, having bowed down, the great souls came out [again] only with difficulty.

**37.** In the morning they all set out, the noble-hearted ones, their hearts ever eager, pining for the sight of the feet of Sri Gaurachandra. That great stream of theirs, constantly filled with happiness, shone like the ocean (*pārāvāra*) — but an ocean without either shore.

*Note.* A verse of six padas. A play on words: *pārāvāra*, "the ocean", literally "[that which has] a further and a nearer shore", while *pārāvāra-vivarjitaḥ* means "devoid of a further and a nearer shore". Verses 38–41 develop the comparison (*kulaka*, so marked in the edition after verse 41).

**38.** Advaita was its treasure, Srivasa the mountain of bhakti, the kirtana its nectar, Haridasa its great jewel.

*Note.* The images come from the churning of the ocean: the treasures, Mount Mandara, the nectar, the Kaustubha jewel.

**39.** Their mutual love was the supreme Lakshmi; the heap of their glories, the foam; and their splendour, the submarine fire.

*Note.* Lakshmi too rose from the churned ocean; glory is white in poetic convention, hence the foam. *Baḍavānala*, "the mare's fire", is the fire at the bottom of the ocean.

**40.** The cries of "Jaya!" were its billows, their unrestrained leaping [in the dance] its waves, their toes its fish, and the rows of their toenails its pearls.

*Note.* *Āpluti* is taken as "leaping" (from *ā-plu*, "to leap; to plunge"); it may also be understood as "overflowing, flood" [of rapture].

**41.** Their arms were serpents, their chests clusters of islands, and their shining faces wondrous lotuses.

*Note.* The edition apparently reads *वक्षांसि*, "chests".

**42.** Then to that very place, the village of Jayapura, came the great-minded Sarvabhauma, supremely eager to meet [them].

*Note.* The episode of Sarvabhauma on his way to Kashi (verses 42–56) is not in the Chaitanya-charitamrita. Sarvabhauma's departure for Varanasi to preach bhakti is found in Karnapura's own drama (Chaitanya-chandrodaya, Act 10), but there it comes later.

**43.** Shedding water from his eyes — that was his very love for them — his body overwhelmed all over by a host of thrills of rapture…

**44.** …seeing Advaita, the great soul, there, he — noble-hearted, a true poet — praised Him with a verse composed by his own poetic gift:

**45.** "Obeisance to You, Advaita, Mahesha, the great soul, by whose grace love for the feet of Gauranga is born!"

*Note.* Advaita is honoured as an incarnation of Maha-Vishnu and of Sadashiva (Mahesha, "the great Lord").

**46.** Having spoken thus, he — a great soul, an ocean of good fortune — numbed by love, his hairs standing on end, fell to the ground like a rod.

**47.** Seeing Haridasa, the great man was filled with reverence and, joyful, his hairs standing on end, fell to the ground like a rod.

**48.** With bowed neck the illustrious one made obeisances again and again: "Obeisance to you, Haridasa, [whose greatness] does not depend on family or caste!"

*Note.* Haridasa was born in a Muslim family; Sarvabhauma, a brahmana, bows down to him.

**49.** Then the best of brahmanas, his weariness gone, his body bristling with hairs standing on end, spoke in a faltering voice:

**50.** "Even without the leave of the lotus feet of Sri Gaurachandra — to give Vedanta another meaning and to deliver its knowers…

*Note.* Verses 50–51 form one period. In the printed text the second pada has only seven syllables (the first line has fifteen); probably *kamalasyāpy anājñayā* ("without the leave") should be read: this restores the metre and agrees with v. 52–54, where the Lord forbids the journey. The text is left as printed.

**51.** …and to revive with this nectar of bhakti those whose throats have long been parched by meditation on the yoga of the Self — with this [aim] I am going.

**52.** Here, O Master, after hearing my vow, He [said] thus, very plainly: 'Do not engage in a play of words and vain labour.'

*Note.* Sarvabhauma addresses Advaita (*prabho*). The syntax is compressed: after *yathā* "said" is understood.

**53.** And when, out of eager longing, I still wished to go, the Treasury of Mercy replied to me: 'In no way will you have the strength [for it].

**54.** Do not go; do not undertake this useless labour, O brahmana. What can you make a man do who has no good fortune?'

**55.** Even so, utterly shameless, out of eager longing I am going to Kashi. Therefore show [me] mercy, so that my desire may bear fruit."

*Note.* Kashi (Varanasi) was the stronghold of the Mayavadi sannyasis; cf. the Chaitanya-charitamrita, Adi 7 and Madhya 25, where the Lord Himself converts the Mayavadis of Kashi headed by Prakashananda.

**56.** Having spoken thus, Sarvabhauma, the learned one among the gods of the earth, most fortunate, bowed down and went away, filled with happiness.

*Note.* "The gods of the earth" are the brahmanas.

**57.** Then these great souls came to charming Yajapura (Jajpur) and, having bathed in the Vaitarani, went through the middle of the town.

*Note.* In the edition *ततः एते* stands without sandhi.

**58.** Then the great-souled Prataparudra, having seen a dream, sent his man; that man had a palanquin raised and brought Advaita.

**59.** Realizing, "I am to go and converse with the king," He became troubled with anxiety: "What will the Master say?"

*Note.* Verses 59–60 form a pair (*yugmaka*). It does not befit a sannyasi or a Vaishnava to seek the company of a king; cf. the Chaitanya-charitamrita, Madhya 11.6–8: for a renounced sannyasi meeting a king is like drinking poison of one's own accord.

**60.** Though He is Himself Ishvara, He trembled with fear of Gauranga-chandra and at once took Sri Vasudeva Datta with Him as His companion.

*Note.* The edition has *भीताशु* — apparently for *bhītyāśu*. The Gosvami edition (GGM) has the same text.

**61.** Some people, followers of Advaita who had taken shelter at the feet of Sri Gaura, went together with Him by the road through Kataka (Cuttack).

*Note.* Kataka (Cuttack) was the capital of Prataparudra.

**62.** But the others, noble great souls — Haridasa and the rest — placing Srivasa in front, went by the roads through Hamseshvara.

**63.** Having spent that day there and beheld that Lord of Uma, they rose in the morning and, happy, joyfully went on.

*Note.* "The Lord of Uma" is Shiva, here Hamseshvara. The edition has *सुखिता* for *sukhitāḥ*.

**64.** The people headed by Srivasa waited some distance away; and as they drew near, their longing doubled.

*Note.* *Tiṣṭhan* is apparently for *atiṣṭhan* (imperfect plural). The Gosvami edition (GGM) has the same text.

**65.** "The splendour of the moons of Gauranga's toenails is soon to be seen!" — thinking thus, the great-minded Advaita too then joined them there.

**66.** Having joined together in one place, they went to the town of Kamalaka, filled with supreme joy and chanting [the Name] everywhere around.

*Note.* In the edition the last pada has seven syllables; the Gosvami edition (GGM) reads *kīrtayanto 'bhito 'bhitaḥ*, and so we read. Kamalaka is Kamalapura (cf. 11.85).

**67.** Having reached the river and bathed well, they beheld again and again the temple, which by its height seemed to knock the sun down from the sky —

*Note.* Verses 67–69 form one period. The edition has *प्रसादं* for *prāsādam* (so in v. 68).

**68.** — in splendour like myriads of suns and endowed with nectar: that temple of the Lord of the Blue Mountain, a joy to behold,

*Note.* A play on words: *sudhā* means both "nectar" and "lime plaster" (whitewash).

**69.** giving joy to all beings, was seen by those noble ones.

*Note.* A verse of two padas; verses 67–69 together make six padas.

**70.** When they saw the lofty temple, their hairs stood high on end in masses, and a joy arose in them as greatly lofty as the temple itself.

**71.** Beholding it, the great souls, devoted to the kirtana of Hari, overflowing with an abundance of joy and overwhelmed by the [inner] vision, bowed down.

**72.** Then the great soul, Lord Advaita, receiving a supremely purifying garland sent by Sri Gaurachandra, rejoiced.

*Note.* Cf. the Chaitanya-charitamrita, Madhya 11.74–78: the Lord sends Svarupa and Govinda to meet the Bengalis with prasada and garlands; Svarupa first places a garland on Advaita.
