**73.** As the great souls, exulting in love, chanted without end, Advaita too, possessed by happiness, began to dance.

**74.** So — He dancing and they chanting, longing for Gauranga — they reached the shore of the lake called Narendra and were happy.

*Note.* Narendra-sarovara is a sacred pond in Puri (cf. the Chaitanya-charitamrita, Madhya 11.68: the Bengali devotees halt at Narendra Lake; Madhya 14: the water pastimes there).

**75.** Then, receiving once more a purifying garland, from Govinda, Advaita, intensely eager, heard His message:

*Note.* Govinda is the Lord's servant in Puri.

**76.** "This is the instruction of Mahaprabhu, who is staying on the shore of the ocean: a fast is enjoined, and you are not to come here.

*Note.* Verses 76–77 form one speech.

**77.** For [the darshan] will be right there: behold the Lotus-eyed One. I shall certainly come to that very place after a while, and we shall converse there, in Mishra's house."

*Note.* A verse of six padas. The Lotus-eyed One is Jagannatha. Mishra's house is the house of Kashi Mishra (cf. 13.63). The first pada is syntactically incomplete.

**78.** Hearing this, Lord Advaita surmised, "This is a stratagem," yet He gave His consent just so, since He Himself is under [the Lord's] control.

*Note.* *Tad-vaśo 'sau* can also be taken the other way: "since He [the Lord] Himself is under [Advaita's] control" (?). Grammatically both readings are possible; we keep ours. The Gosvami edition (GGM) has the same text.

**79.** Then Murari Gupta, his mind full of great despondency, fell to the ground like a rod and, weeping, said this:

**80.** "This wretched, most miserable, utterly vile creature has been brought so far by you, worthy souls.

*Note.* Verses 80–82 form one speech.

**81.** I am not able to go; I have no strength; nor have I as yet the courage to see the Supreme Lord of the world. Afterwards, when you have made [me] known [to Him], I shall have the strength to go."

*Note.* A verse of six padas.

**82.** Having spoken thus, greatly dejected and sorrowful, he remained motionless right there.

*Note.* A verse of two padas. Cf. the Chaitanya-charitamrita, Madhya 11.152–157: Murari does not go to the Lord at once but bows down from afar, in the street; later he comes with two bunches of grass between his teeth and draws back: "Do not touch me."

**83.** After that, those noble ones headed by Advaita beheld the pair of lotus eyes of the Lord of the world.

**84.** Beholding that Jagannatha — broad-chested, mighty-armed, with wide, long eyes — they attained the greatest joy.

**85.** Then Sri Sri Gaurachandra rose [before them], shining brilliantly like myriads of moons, possessed by happiness, bathed in a flood of streaming tears.

*Note.* Verses 86–92 form one descriptive period (*kulaka*); its subject is the Lord.

**86.** Crushing the earth with His footfalls, with the gait of a maddened king of elephants, with the great exultation of a maddened lion, His two shining arms reaching to His knees;

*Note.* The edition has *पञ्चीन्द्र* — a corruption; the Gosvami edition (GGM) reads *padmīndra*: *padmin* is an "elephant" (with lotus-like spots on its skin), *padmīndra* — "the king of elephants".

**87.** a moving golden mountain; as if the moon in person; with a mass of cascades and waterfalls from the downpours of streams of flowing tears; [like] myriads of moons risen at once and become one, pouring forth a lake of liquid nectar in unceasing downpours;

*Note.* A verse of eight padas.

**88.** beautifully adorned with a loincloth and an outer garment red as vermilion; His pair of thighs outdoing the beauty of a pair of plantain trunks;

**89.** and — an ocean of rasa — illuminating the blessed earth with the radiance of the nectar of the beautiful light of the moons of His toenails;

*Note.* A play on words: *rasā* ("the earth") — *rasa* ("taste").

**90.** bathing the faces of the quarters with the soft, dense light of the moon of His face; truly another ocean of happiness, embodied; with a neck like a conch…

**91.** …with the neck of a lion, captivating with His very massive chest, charming with the girdle that clung to His slender waist —

**92.** — Mahaprabhu, reciting Brahma's hymn of praise that begins "I praise You, O worthy of praise, whose body is like a cloud", Himself bowed down to Lord Advaita.

*Note.* The opening of Brahma's prayer, Bhagavatam 10.14.1.

**93.** And Advaita, overcome with happiness, the hairs of His body standing on end, bowed down to Him. And the two could not [stop] even for a moment in their praises and obeisances to each other.

*Note.* The edition has *dvayos tava-*, and the second line is one syllable short (15 syllables); the Gosvami edition (GGM) reads *dvayoḥ stavana-natyādau* ("in the praises and obeisances of the two"), which restores the metre; the sense is confirmed.

**94.** The streams of tears flowing from the two were again and again strings of a hundred thousand pearls. With obeisances and praises there passed a time of indescribable bliss.

**95.** Then Mahaprabhu clasped the lotus feet of Srivasa and, greatly overwhelmed, offered [him] an excellent hymn of praise.

**96.** And he, the foremost of brahmanas, was shaken, as if he wished to die. Rich in pious merit, he bowed down and fervently praised [Him] with words.

**97.** Then his younger brother, the very noble-hearted Rama Pandita, and Sri Vasudeva Datta bowed down to the Lord together.

**98.** Mahaprabhu clasped the two with the pair of pillars that were His arms. After them Sri Shivananda Sena too joyfully bowed down…

**99.** …and then set before Him two vessels of Ganga water.

*Note.* A verse of two padas.

**100.** Seeing this, the ocean of mercy sang aloud the glory of the Ganga, and then, His lips lit up by the gleam of His teeth, He spoke sweetly:

**101.** "Let this one be given for the bathing festival, and one to Me." Those two [vessels] belonged separately to Sri Vasudeva and Sri Shivananda, [one to each].

*Note.* The second half is understood tentatively (?). The Gosvami edition (GGM) has the same text. In the next verse the Lord divides the water, knowing the wish of both; so the vessels belonged to the two of them, one each.

**102.** Knowing the wish of both, the Lord spoke again: "Divide them: first let half be given to Jagannatha, and then let the other half be kept right here."

*Note.* A verse of six padas.

**103.** Then the glorious ocean of mercy, filled with wonder, asked: "Where is Murari? Where is Murari? Where is he? Bring him quickly!"

*Note.* The edition has *प्रपच्छ* for *papraccha*; the number lacks the second danda: *॥१०३*.

**104.** Hearing this, [people] by the hundred, extremely eager, ran off and, quickly going there, on the shore of Narendra Lake…

*Note.* Verses 104–107 form one period (in the edition 106–107 are marked as a *yugmaka*).

**105.** …saw him, overwhelmed, lying on the ground, weeping, his heart downcast; and so they said: "Come quickly!"

**106.** Hearing their words thus, Murari, extremely eager, overwhelmed, ceaselessly drenched with tears, grey with dust…

**107.** …sobbing just so, with many plaintive utterances, the noble-hearted one, utterly despondent, went to see the Lord of his life.

**108.** From stupor and sweat his feet kept stumbling, and he kept falling. The edge of the cloth he was wearing he had half tied round his neck; he had put many blades of grass between his teeth and went along [humble] as a blade of grass; on his chest he wore a necklace of pearls made of the water of his flowing tears. As if blinded by love, he gazed right there at the Lord for a long time.

*Note.* A verse of ten padas. A cloth round the neck and grass between the teeth are signs of complete submission. Cf. Chaitanya-charitamrita, Madhya 11.152–157 (Murari with two bunches of grass between his teeth).

**109.** His throat choked with tears, he could not say anything even for a moment. Yet, uttering a hundred thousand plaintive words in faltering cries, utterly humble in mind, he clasped the lotus feet of the Lord, and again and again he bathed that pair of His lotus feet with masses of streams of tears issuing from his two eyes.

*Note.* A verse of ten padas. The edition has *-samuccaye*; we read *-samuccayaiḥ* (instrumental) (?). The Gosvami edition (GGM) has the same text as the edition.

**110.** And the Lord, the pair of His long lotus eyes reddened, bathed his back with the waters that sprang from His eyes.

**111.** All the people present there, at his weeping and plaints, wept as if they were like him; that time became wholly filled with him.

**112.** And the Lord, seeing that most intense weeping and hearing those plaintive words, could not bear it even for a moment and was shaken.

**113.** Then the Lord shone there, accompanied by Advaita and the others, gentle, like the moon of the full-moon night adorned with stars…

**114.** …His moonlight tinged by the smile of His mouth, red as gleaming coral; with the radiance of the moonlight of His own body He ceaselessly bathed the faces of the maidens of the quarters.

**115.** Then they, greedy for the intoxicating drink of the feet of Krishna-Chaitanya, became ceaselessly eager to see the Snana-yatra.

**116.** On the Ekadashi they eagerly watched the wedding festival, and then, on the full moon, the purifying Snana-yatra.

*Note.* The wedding is apparently the festival of Rukmini-harana, which in Puri is celebrated on the Ekadashi of the bright fortnight of Jyeshtha, four days before the Snana-yatra (on the full moon); not confirmed in our sources.

**117.** There, on the lovely Blue Mountain, in the delightful, joy-bringing city of exalted beauty, with its mansions, towers and gateways, with its splendid white temples that lick the clouds, a certain bathing platform, adorned with whitewash (and as if steeped in nectar), moves along, as it were.

*Note.* A verse of eight padas. *Sañcarati* ("moves") is understood thus: the platform is stationary, but it seems to move. *Sudhā* means both "whitewash" and "nectar". The Gosvami edition (GGM) has the same text.

**118.** Then, on the day before, when the sun was going to the mountain of its setting, the zealous people began to adorn that platform beautifully.

**119.** Likewise there the beloved of the night — the moon — rose, lacking one digit yet as if full, and shone, beautifying that great city.

*Note.* The evening of the fourteenth day, the eve of the full moon.

**120.** And the lovely moon, full of nectar, swept the bathing platform clean with its rays — its hands — as if intent on the Lord's service.

*Note.* The edition has *करौ* (dual). We read *karaiḥ* (?). A play on words: *kara* is both "ray" and "hand"; *sudhā* — "nectar" and "whitewash". The Gosvami edition (GGM) has the same text as the edition.

**121.** With a great, wondrous net with arches, with the ringing jingle of glittering little bells, with garlands of flowers of many kinds —

*Note.* Verses 121–122 form one period. The edition has *दीव्येन*, a misprint for *divyena*.

**122.** — when the bathing pavilion had been [so] adorned, at the full rising of its beauties there arose something — how could it be described? — that delighted the minds of the people of the whole world.

**123.** Then, at the command of Gauranga-chandra, the noble-hearted ones, longing to see the bathing, settled firmly on top of the enclosure wall…

*Note.* Verses 123–124 form one period.

**124.** …and shone like the gods standing in the sky before Hari, their chests adorned with sandal paste applied by the hand of Sri Gauranga.

*Note.* *Hari* here is probably Indra, the king of the gods.

**125.** When the last part of the night had come, the dayitas and others fastened the spotless silk girding-band on the beautiful body [of Jagannatha].

*Note.* The dayitas (daitapatis) are the temple servitors who carry the Deities.

**126.** Then first the Plough-bearer, showing His readiness for the triumphal march, descended from the throne — the almighty Lord shone like millions of moons.

*Note.* The Plough-bearer is Balarama (Baladeva). The edition has a superfluous syllable: *बभौ क कोटीन्दुवद्*; the metre requires *क* to be omitted.

**127.** Then the holy goddess Subhadra, and then the Lord of the world, Jagannatha, too, descending [from the throne], attained a wondrous splendour.

**128.** And then the golden Moon, going ever ahead, watched that triumphal march of the Three along the way, one after another.

*Note.* *Vijaya* (Oriya *pahaṇḍi vijaya*) is the solemn procession of the Deities.

**129.** Shaking the earth with His steps, passing from cushion to finer cushion, Jagannatha shone like the moon passing from star to star.

*Note.* During the procession the Deities are carried along cotton cushions (*kaśipu*) laid out on the way.

**130.** Onto that pavilion — spotless, clear in radiance with its rows of steps, stealing the beauty of the ocean of milk that sparkles with a succession of dancing waves, fully adorned with masses of nets and with cries of "Jaya!" marked by the jingling of bells — the Lord, the crest-jewel of the Blue Mountain, ascended.

*Note.* The metre of verses 130–132 and 134–135 is *śārdūla-vikrīḍita*.

**131.** Sri Chaitanya Mahaprabhu, surrounded by devotees, [stood] in front, bearing on His chest the stream that flowed ceaselessly from His two lotus eyes; and Jagannatha Himself too, being bathed, gleamed with streams [of water]. Beholding in each other the same play, [each] shone, overwhelmed with joy.

**132.** With loud cries of "Jaya, jaya!" ringing out on high, rising all together, and with even showers of heaps of flowers, the splendid great festival of the glorious jewel of the Dark Mountain — hard to attain even for Brahma and the others — now plainly became a nectar of bliss for the eyes of all the people.

*Note.* The edition has *दुर्लभो सितगिरि-*, without the avagraha. We read *durlabho 'sita-giri-* ("of the Dark Mountain", that is, of Nilachala).

**133.** Drenched by the streams of bathing water, this Lord of the Blue Mountain shone, weeping in the mood of separation from the golden Moon, [as if thinking]: "For a long time I shall be hidden."

*Note.* The metre is *upajāti*. The streams of bathing water are fancied as tears. A hint at the anavasara (cf. 13.57). The edition numbers the verse *॥२३३॥*, a misprint for 133.

**134.** Thus He — the lovely tilaka on the crown of Sri Nilachala, His broad chest anointed with the nectar-liquid of the great bathing festival — stood [there] a moment in festive joy; then, splendid, He began again to move over the cushions in an auspicious clockwise circuit, the pair of pillars that were His arms held by throngs of servants.

**135.** At the setting out of the victorious Enemy of Mura, the crest-jewel of the Blue Mountain, the Tortoise sinks, this Shesha is shaken with all the circles of his hoods, the earth quakes, the mountains are split, the egg of Brahma is cracked, the ocean has overstepped even its bounds, and the sun yonder has taken to flight!

*Note.* The classical hyperbole of "the hero's march". The Tortoise and Shesha uphold the earth. The Enemy of Mura is Krishna.

**Colophon:** इति श्रीकृष्णचैतन्यचरितामृते महाकाव्ये चतुर्दशः सर्गः । (*iti śrī-kṛṣṇa-caitanya-caritāmṛte mahākāvye caturdaśaḥ sargaḥ*) — "Thus [ends] the fourteenth sarga in the mahakavya 'Sri Krishna-Chaitanya-charitamrita'." (The colophon gives no title for the sarga.)

---

## Sarga Fifteen — verses 1–110

**Title of the sarga:** none — the colophon of the edition gives no title for this sarga.
**Metre:**
- 1–104 — *puṣpitāgrā* (odd padas ⏑⏑⏑⏑⏑⏑—⏑—⏑——, 12 syllables; even padas ⏑⏑⏑⏑—⏑⏑—⏑—⏑——, 13 syllables).
- 105 — *sragdharā* (————⏑——⏑⏑⏑⏑⏑⏑——⏑——⏑——; 21 syllables per pada).
- 106 and 108 — *śārdūla-vikrīḍita* (———⏑⏑—⏑—⏑⏑⏑———⏑——⏑—; 19 syllables per pada); 106 is a verse of six padas.
- 107 — *pṛthvī* (⏑—⏑⏑⏑—⏑—⏑⏑⏑—⏑——⏑—; 17 syllables per pada).
- 109 — *hariṇī* (⏑⏑⏑⏑⏑—————⏑—⏑⏑—⏑—; 17 syllables per pada), with rhyme at the ends of the padas.
- 110 — *mālinī* (⏑⏑⏑⏑⏑⏑———⏑——⏑——; 15 syllables per pada).

**Total verses:** 110.

**Contents of the passage:** the anavasara; the cleansing of the Gundicha temple; the Netrotsava; Jagannatha mounts the chariot.
- 1–11 — the anavasara: Jagannatha remains hidden with Lakshmi; the Lord's longing (the separation of the gopis); His consolation — the Lord of the gopis on the swing, with the dancing of the devadasis (9–10, *yugmaka*).
- 12–23 — the preparations: the brooms, the bath before dawn, the crimson cloth; the Lord gives out a hundred brooms; the run to the Gundicha, like an elephant broken loose.
- 24–34 — the sweeping: the devotees forget themselves; "you — here, you — there"; blows with brooms for latecomers; the Lord cleans the throne and is covered with dust; "Krishna, Krishna, Krishna".
- 35–54 — the washing: water from the well passed along a chain, "a game of ball with pitchers"; the Lord washes the veranda; the drenched crimson cloth ("Meru in the evening clouds").
- 55–65 — gathering blades of grass and pebbles in the courtyard, a contest of who gathers more (57–58); kirtana and dancing.
- 66–70 — bathing in the tank; obeisance to Nrisimha.
- 71–77 — **the Netrotsava**: the first darshan after the anavasara; the Lord and Jagannatha are "alike": neither of them blinks; at the bhoga-mandapa.
- 78–90 — Jagannatha with Lakshmi; the journey to the Gundicha as "a journey to Vrindavana"; the descent from the throne over cushions.
- 91–99 — the procession: the "game of ball" of Jagannatha and Gaura; Prataparudra sweeps with a golden broom (95–96); Jagannatha mounts the chariot.
- 100–110 — the Lord bathes Advaita, Srivasa and the singers and anoints them with sandal paste (the list, 105–106); the setting out; He falls prostrate before the chariot of Baladeva.

**1.** Then, when the anavasara has come, this Lord of the Dark Mountain, together with Lakshmi, the attendant of the inner apartments, dwells in hidden guise, far away from the eyes of the people.

*Note.* The *anavasara* is the fortnight after the Snana-yatra during which Jagannatha is not shown to the people. The Lord of the Dark Mountain is Jagannatha (the Dark Mountain is Nilachala).

**2.** Whether desiring to give the devoted people who dwell on the Dark Mountain over to boundless anguish, or else to sport with Lakshmi in seclusion, this God hid Himself and shone in secret.

**3.** Then those great souls who dwelt on the Dark Mountain, bearing hearts utterly agitated by the great sorrow of not seeing Him, were intensely tormented, deprived of the sight of the Lord.

**4.** And the Lord, the son of Shachi, not seeing Him, then became sorrowful and greatly distraught. And under this pretext He reveals the sorrow of separation of the beautiful women of Vrindavana.

**5.** Under this pretext He experiences the pains of separation of the beautiful women of Vrindavana, which abide unceasingly in His heart; for the agitation of the heart rises up as soon as it has found an opportunity.

**6.** Rich with the charm of a necklace well formed on His chest by the streams of tears flowing unceasingly, so wasted that only His name was left, Gaurachandra languished from long separation.

**7.** Heavily He sends forth the very long, hot wind of sighs, and water from His eyes, red as a pair of beautiful red lotuses. Thus, day after day, His body grew thin.

**8.** The ocean of mercy, whose forest fire of sorrow was doubled by not seeing the Lord of the Dark Mountain, attained some steadiness of heart on beholding there the procession of the Lord of the gopis.

*Note.* Evidently during the anavasara the Lord watched the festival of Jagannatha's representative — Krishna with the flute on the swing (verses 9–10). In the Chaitanya-charitamrita (Madhya 11.62–63) the Lord goes to Alalanatha during the anavasara; such a festival is not mentioned in our sources. *Vijaya* can also mean "triumph".

**9.** With a very graceful flute in His hand, mounted on a most charming swing, He shines, and at the three junctures of the day He delights unceasingly in the art of dance of the finest temple dancers…

*Note.* Verses 9–10 form a pair (*yugmaka*). The dancers are the devadasis of Jagannatha's temple.

**10.** …and He sports to drums, excellent kettledrums, the rolls of sweet mridangas and charming songs, in the middle of a hall whose floor is thickly whitened by an unceasing shower of masses of flowers.

**11.** Thus, His mind dejected by separation, and gaining only a little steadiness from the sight of His own people, Gaurachandra passed some days weeping unceasingly and aloud, with open throat.

**12.** Then the golden Moon, together with the devotees exclusively devoted to His own lotus feet, made ready to perform a complete cleansing of the temple known as the Gundicha.

*Note.* Cf. Chaitanya-charitamrita, Madhya 12.79–151 — the famous scene of the cleansing of the Gundicha (the brooms — 12.80).

**13.** And on the previous day the son of Shachi, having made a plan with all the people, quickly prepared, with an elated heart, a multitude of beautiful brooms for the cleansing of the temple.

*Note.* The even pada as printed has 12 syllables instead of 13, and with the short *śaci-* it does not scan in any case. It becomes regular if the *a* of *ahani*, elided across the pada boundary (*pūrve 'hani*), is counted and *śacī-* is read with a long *ī*: *ahani śacī-tanujo vidhāya yuktim* (⏑⏑⏑⏑—⏑⏑—⏑—⏑——). The edition writes *-समूहमुदितमना* continuously; we divide *samūham udita-manā(ḥ)* (the visarga is dropped by sandhi before *bh-*); *mudita-manāḥ*, "joyful at heart", is also possible, with the same sense. The text is the same in the Gosvami edition (GGM).

**14.** Then, before the time of the night's end, the Lord, under the sway of eagerness, rose from His bed and made ready to bathe with an abundance of pure water.

*Note.* In the first pada the fifth syllable (*-nī-*) is long, whereas the metre requires a short one; with the stem *rajani-* (an equally correct form) the pada would be regular.

**15.** Having bathed with abundant pure, fragrant, cool water, He then put on another cloth, of bright crimson — as if Sumeru had eagerly and tightly embraced the evening glow.

*Note.* In the edition *सुरभी-*; the metre requires *surabhi-*. Even so the first pada has 13 syllables instead of 12; with *śīta-* for *śītala-* it would be regular (?). The golden Lord in crimson cloth is compared to the golden mountain Sumeru embracing the red glow of evening.

**16.** Having tied His garment very firmly with a very beautiful waist-cord and applied a tilaka of Malaya sandalwood, the great ocean of mercy attained an overflowing, lovely beauty.

**17.** Then, coming outside and looking at all the people with waves of reddish glances, He had a hundred brooms brought before Him and gave them out in turn, to each one separately.

**18.** And that host of devotees of the Lord's lotus feet, receiving from the lotus hand of the Lord this beautiful broom, like a cluster of blossoms of the tree of happiness, at once rejoiced exceedingly in secret.

*Note.* *Raho 'ti* is understood as "in secret, exceedingly" (?). The text is the same in the Gosvami edition (GGM). As printed, the fourth pada is a syllable short (12 instead of 13); the reading *rahasy ati* ("in secret, exceedingly") would make it regular — a conjecture (?).

**19.** Then He — moving like a line of maddened kings of elephants, like a golden mountain that had begun to move, the play of His heart stirred by supreme impetuosity, the foremost in sweetness — ran swiftly.

*Note.* Verses 19–20 form a single period.

**20.** Running like a maddened elephant long held back and suddenly set free, unchecked by the goad, He made the surface of the earth tremble continually with the great weight of the play of His lotus feet.

*Note.* The *aṅkuśa* is the elephant-driver's goad.

**21.** Then, going swiftly, His heart eager, [He came] to the vicinity of the Gundicha temple, the goal of the supreme Lord's journey, and entered that enclosure as if [plunging] into an ocean of happiness. But they came near only long after.

*Note.* *-gamya-* in the second pada is obscure (?). We take *prabhu-vara-gamya-* as "to be gone to by the supreme Lord", that is, the Gundicha temple as the destination of Jagannatha's journey (cf. 14.3–5); it may also be understood of Gaura Himself: "the best of lords [reached] the vicinity…". The verse is metrically complete: all four padas scan as regular *puṣpitāgrā*. The text is the same in the Gosvami edition (GGM).

**22.** Filled with exceeding joy, He entered the enclosure first, and then and there, with His own people, took up that broom and at once swept here and there, each place separately, in due order.

**23.** Then this crowd of people, eager for the cleansing and utterly enchanted by the Lord's kirtana, all at once swept with brooms every room, every wall, every veranda and every roof.

**24.** And some, enchanted by beholding the face of the Lord, would stand apart, broom in hand, their bodies washed by a torrent of tears, and for a long while seemed to have forgotten the work of sweeping.

**25.** And some, with hearts intent on hearing the Lord's fine words, their limbs bristling with intense thrill, also swept the room in the same way, but, lost in bewilderment, did not notice even the work already done.

**26.** And the Lord, enraptured by supreme joy, gave abundant directions to the people, making them happy again and again: "You — here, here! There, there — you! Sweep nicely!"

*Note.* The second pada has 10 syllables instead of 13 — the text is corrupt (?). The text is the same in the Gosvami edition (GGM).

**27.** Since, through the play of the Lord's words, they did the work here and there to their heart's content, they obtained a doubled load of happiness, and yet their satiety never came to completion.

*Note.* In the edition *विलासते* — a misprint for *vilāsato*.

**28.** And whoever came before Him late, the Lord, out of the fullness of the rasa of love, struck him very hard on the back with brooms, in great anger.

**29.** But that crowd of people attained happiness even from the pain of the very hard blows of the brooms. Such indeed is the fruit of an abundance of heartfelt love: it makes even no small pain dear.

**30.** In mighty joy the Lord now Himself sweeps gracefully, now watches the work of others, now has them work with affectionate instructions.

*Note.* As printed, the fourth pada has 14 syllables instead of 13; without the *ca* (perhaps repeated from the third pada) it would be regular (?).

**31.** Going up close to each and every one, He produced in them a load of extreme joy. With smiles, words, glances and touches He calmed the floods of all their griefs and gave them joy.

*Note.* *Abhimarśa* here means "touch".

**32.** And He Himself, with a few people, His heart wholly given [to the task], cleaned the throne all around in the fullness of supreme happiness, and then at once made ready to sprinkle [it with water].

*Note.* The throne (*siṁhāsana*) is the platform for the Deities in the Gundicha temple.

**33.** His body, like a golden king of mountains, covered by this dust that the brooms raised unceasingly and that settled again and again — whom did the son of Shachi then resemble?

*Note.* *Ka iva babhūva*, literally "like whom did He become?": He seemed someone else altogether, beyond any comparison.

**34.** And, unceasingly repeating "Krishna, Krishna, Krishna" in an incomparably steady, deep and beautiful [voice], the moon of His face full of sweet smiles, the Lord carried on the cleansing of the enclosure.

**35.** Then, to accomplish a thorough washing of this enclosure, all the people set about drawing water there from the well with multitudes of pitchers fitted with very strong ropes.

*Note.* A play of sounds: *ghaṭī-ghaṭābhir ghaṭayitum*.

**36.** In one place people, their upper garments tied very firmly about their hips, took up ropes and pots; some stood fully ready near them, and others likewise near those.

*Note.* The first pada has 11 syllables instead of 12 (?); the same in verse 41. The second pada, for its part, has 15 syllables instead of 13. The text is the same in the Gosvami edition (GGM).

**37.** Then, in that crowd of people, someone drew up a flood of water with a rope and a multitude of pitchers; someone somehow handed it to someone else, and someone carried it on in succession.

*Note.* In the edition the third pada reads *कथमभि … कस्यचिन्न*; the Gosvami edition (GGM) reads *kasyacic ca*, and the superfluous *na* disappears; *katham api* is our correction of *कथमभि*. The threefold *ko 'pi*, "someone", describes the chain of people passing the water along.

**38.** Some raised up the full pitchers, and some carried the empty ones down. Delightful indeed is this outcome for both: for nature surely does not turn into its opposite.

*Note.* A play on words: what is full rises, what is empty goes down.

**39.** By them, supremely rejoicing from the fullness of happiness, through the passing to and fro of multitudes of pitchers, again and again now empty, now full by turns, a game of ball played with pitchers was set going.

*Note.* Again a play of sounds on *ghaṭ-*: *ghaṭana-vighaṭanair ghaṭī-ghaṭānāṁ ghaṭamaya… anvaghāṭi*.

**40.** "Here, here, here! Bring it, bring it!" — this cry, arisen from relish and merriment, rich with thrilled, joyful, loud shouts of "hum" and kissed by the gurgling of the filling pitchers, boldly rang out [all around].

**41.** In some places they poured water from pots again and again on the veranda, in others on the many walls; some on the roof, some on the awnings. At the Lord's word their hearts were wholly immersed in happiness.

*Note.* The first pada has eleven syllables instead of twelve, as in v. 36 (?); the second pada, too, has twelve instead of thirteen (?). The Gosvami edition (GGM) has the same text. *Payo ghaṭān* may perhaps be taken as one compound, *payo-ghaṭān*, "pots of water".

**42.** "You — this way, this way, this way! You — here! And you, you — this way!" — thus, with sweetness of speech to each person, the Lord too had all the corners in every chamber cleaned.

**43.** Some people continually placed brimming pots into the pair of the Lord's lotus hands, while others poured floods of water on the ground all around His feet.

**44.** And the Lord, with a joyful heart, took a full pot there and gave up another, [empty one]. By fullness and emptiness in turn, that twofold [exchange] became an illustration for the two.

*Note.* That is, as the pots passed between them, now the devotees, now the Lord were in turn "full" and "empty".

**45.** Some — all their limbs covered with masses of hairs standing on end, their bodies swaying with the violent trembling caused by the fullness of happiness — could find no satiety at all in giving pots into the lotus hand of the Lord.

**46.** Some, eager [for Him], secretly poured water copiously on the pair of the Beloved's lotus feet alone. Where, then, did the business of washing the chambers remain for them?

*Note.* The rhetorical question means that they quite forgot about washing the chambers. The Gosvami edition (GGM) has the same text.

**47.** As the host of His own people poured water from pots near His feet again and again, a hundred times over, the Lord shone still more splendidly in His bright red cloth, wet up to the knees.

**48.** The moon of His face shining with the glistening of droplets of sweat, the border of His red garment wet, drenched by the water splashed from every side, the Lord shone as though He had just come out of a sport of bathing.

**49.** And He Himself, taking from the lotus hand of His devotee a pot full to the brim with water, impetuously poured and poured, and flooded the veranda densely, like a raincloud.

*Note.* *Praghaṇa* is a covered veranda before the entrance.

**50.** Here His body kissed by drops of water, there enraptured by play with the mud, He shone then like an elephant in the water that has come out after churning up a fresh pond.

**51.** The beauty of His hips shining through the bright red cloth, wet through from the drenching with masses of water, He shone then like Mount Meru enveloped in evening clouds that have taken shelter [on it] out of fear of the sun.

*Note.* The edition has *सेकतोऽभिताम्यत्*. By sense we read *-'bhitimyat*, "wet through"; cf. v. 47 (?). Metrically the two readings are equivalent. The Gosvami edition (GGM) has the same text as the edition.

**52.** How many pots broke then, how many never came back there again, how much water was brought — and how many rivers sprang up here and there!

*Note.* *चाहृतानीत* = *cāhṛtāni + ita* (sandhi across the pada boundary).

**53.** Ceaselessly pouring water from pitchers and from their eyes out of the fullness of overflowing joy, and now and again impetuously raising loud cries of "Jaya!", they shone like [thundering] clouds.

**54.** Their many hands wet from the ceaseless drenching with water, they shone by the enclosure near the Lord like noble elephants with their trunks; and the mass of their happiness could not be contained in the world.

*Note.* A play on words: *kara* means both "hand" and "trunk".

**55.** Then, having cleaned the whole enclosure and washed the throne thoroughly, the Lord — He who ever delights in rasa — went outside, into the courtyard.

**56.** Then the Lord sat down in the courtyard among [the devotees] seated in well-arranged rows and, starting from one side, in order, removed blades of grass and pebbles here and there with His very tender fingers and hands.

**57.** Rich in pastimes, He hastily spread His graceful outer garment on the ground and, wishing to win the prize of the fixed wager at all costs — how many blades of grass and pebbles did He not gather!

*Note.* The edition has *नैकति*; the Gosvami edition (GGM) reads *na kati* ("how many … not"), and so we read. The second pada is metrically correct; it is the fourth pada that is one syllable short (twelve instead of thirteen), with either reading (?). Cf. Chaitanya-charitamrita, Madhya 12.89–91: the devotees gather the dust and rubbish into their cloths, and the Lord, looking at the heaps, judges who has worked best.

**58.** Inspecting in turn the heaps of grass and clods gathered here and there by all the people, the Lord sported [with them]: "Since you have [gathered] only so much, so much, so much — you have lost here!"

**59.** Having thus cleaned the inner courtyard of the whole temple, every gate of the enclosure and the road, He — His inner self wearied by the fullness of His great eagerness — began His own kirtana with His own people.

*Note.* The second pada has fourteen syllables instead of thirteen (?).

**60.** How these [singers], by nature supremely sweet-voiced, sang then before the Lord as He danced — nobly, beautifully, majestically — ah, what person here could describe it?

*Note.* The edition has *प्रभुनर्त्तने*.

**61.** Filling the ears of the hosts of kinnaras with exceedingly graceful, long, very long notes, overcome by rapture, their hairs standing on end, wholly filled with happiness, they sang with one mind during the Lord's dance.

*Note.* The kinnaras are celestial musicians.

**62.** In the fullness of His great eagerness He flung up His knees and His chest, and a long thrill of the hairs spread over His body; His whole body washed by streams of ceaselessly flowing tears, Gaura danced, exulting.

**63.** In the fullness of great joy He beautifully uttered hundreds of graceful, soft and deep cries of "Hum!", and danced, whirling now lightly, now swiftly, now slowly.

**64.** Now He made the people, no different from His own body, dance with unceasing rasa; [now] He sang in a deep voice, making the quarters resound with the sweetness of the soft sound of His clapping palms.

**65.** Now, delighted, He looks on; now He sings, now He dances. Having gained splendour from the mingling of sweat, of tears from His eyes and of mire [caused] by the heat, the Lord shone.

**66.** Thus, at the end of the cleaning of the enclosure, having also displayed the art of dancing, Gaurachandra then wished to sport in the pond; the slender stem of His body was exhausted under the burden of weariness.

**67.** Then, to dispel His weariness, Gaurachandra eagerly sat down for a while with the company of His own people in a soft, cool spot and, His face sweet, delighted in good talks.

**68.** Then, with the desire to sport in the water, He walked slowly, slowly to the pond together with the throngs of His devotees, His body languid with weariness, and gave happiness to the eyes of the onlookers.

*Note.* *Puraḥ sarasyām* is unclear (?): *puraḥ* may mean "first" or "in front". Cf. Chaitanya-charitamrita, Madhya 12.151–153: after the cleaning and the kirtana the Lord goes with the devotees to the pond, sports in the water, and then bows down to Nrisimhadeva. The Gosvami edition (GGM) has the same text.

**69.** Then, after a long, playful sport in the water, the slender stem of His body thoroughly cooled, He came with delight to the bank together with the crowd of His own people and put on two fine garments.

**70.** After that Gaurachandra came to Lord Nrisimha and, delighted, bowed down [to Him]. Then He made ready to go on, and at every step His lotus feet were just as exultant.

*Note.* The temple of Nrisimha is near the Gundicha; cf. Chaitanya-charitamrita, Madhya 12.136 (the Lord cleans the temple of Nrisimha too) and 12.152 (having changed His clothes on the bank of the pond, He bows down to Nrisimhadeva, whose temple is close by).

**71.** Then, together with the people, He beheld the lotus face of the Lord who is the tilaka of the Dark Mountain — the face that makes a festival for the eyes of the people of the whole world, which had been hidden [from Him] for a fortnight.

*Note.* A play on words: *netrotsava*, "a festival for the eyes", is also the name of the festival of the first darshana after the anavasara — the Netrotsava. Cf. Chaitanya-charitamrita, Madhya 12.204–211 (the Netrotsava falls on the day after the cleaning; the Lord's eyes, like bees, drink the honey of Jagannatha's lotus face).

**72.** With eyes utterly tormented by the thirst of the fast caused by long separation, the Lord gazed without blinking; yet the mere moon of His [Jagannatha's] face could not satisfy Him at once.

**73.** The Jewel of the Dark Peak and Gaurachandra secretly became alike then: the forms of both were lovely with fresh, thick colour — with love — and the lotuses of the eyes of both were thirsting and unblinking.

*Note.* A double meaning: *rāga* is both "colour" and "love". For the Netrotsava Jagannatha is freshly painted, and His eyes never blink.

**74.** With the lovely sounds "Jaya, jaya, O Lord, jaya!" issuing on all sides from the mouths of all the people, and with the swell of the waves of hands raised again and again, another ocean, as it were, arose there.

**75.** Surpassing again and again the whole crowd of people with the beauty of the incomparably tall stem of His body, He stood on a clean stone at the edge of the hall of offerings before the Supreme Lord, beholding Him.

*Note.* The edition has *विमलदृशदिभोग-*; we read *vimala-dṛṣadi bhoga-*. The bhoga-mandapa is the hall of food offerings in the Jagannatha temple.

**76.** With torrents of tears He purified the moons of the nails of the pair of lotus feet. Was it [with the thought] "For in the world there is nothing else so hard to attain as this" that He then bathed the lotus feet?

*Note.* Whose feet are meant — His own or Jagannatha's — is unclear (?). The Gosvami edition (GGM) has the same text.

**77.** As He, desiring nothing else, secretly beheld the moon of the face of the Moon of the Dark Mountain, the pair of His eyes took on the beauty of red lotuses, and then His body became very much like a bud.

*Note.* "Like a bud": the body contracted.

**78.** Thus the Lord of the world, of charming form, at the end of fifteen days on the Dark Mountain, perceiving the opportunity, spent one day with Rama, wishing to sport [with her].

*Note.* Rama is Lakshmi.

**79.** On the next day this Blue Moon — His body gladdened and nourished by doubled meals, captivating with supreme splendour — shone, eager for the coming festival of the chariot procession.

**80.** This blue jewel of the Dark Mountain — the great sportive Lord who has tasted all the rasas, who re-enacts the pastimes of all the incarnations, ever gracious toward His own people…

*Note.* Verses 80–81 form one period. In the second pada the sixth syllable (*svā* in *rasāsvādito*) is heavy where the metre requires a light one (?).

**81.** …softened by good mercies toward His own people, Himself goes every year to the Gundicha, having propitiated Lakshmi there, in secret, alone together [with her], after the passing of fifteen days.

*Note.* The syntax of the second half is unclear. The Gosvami edition (GGM) has the same text. The sense seems to be that the Lord spends the fifteen days of the anavasara alone with Lakshmi and, having won her over, sets out for the Gundicha; cf. v. 78.

**82.** On both sides of the path, lovely with heaps of soft sand, [stretches] that very cluster of groves, lovely with trees of many kinds and so on, which enchants the world and awakens the highest remembrance of Vrindavana.

*Note.* Verses 82–83 form one period; cf. 14.4–5.

**83.** Thus, under the pretext of the chariot procession, [Jagannatha] sets His mind on re-enacting the journey to Vrindavana and is seized by the desire to sport in the cluster of groves — as this Gaura had said before.

**84.** The Supreme Lord sports by the Ratha-yatra; and Gaurachandra, having experienced that happiness, then displays here and there in the groves the countless delights of Vrindavana.

**85.** While the Jewel of the Blue Mountain stays for nine days within the Gundicha temple, the golden Moon, an ocean of rasa, refreshed by the wafting of the breeze in the groves, sports [there].

**86.** Then at the close of night the Blue Moon, eager for the joy of the procession, clad in [His] array, with fitting splendour, [came down], under the guise of a descent, from His lovely great throne, like the moon [setting] from a mountain.

*Note.* The verb is omitted. The Gosvami edition (GGM) has the same text.

**87.** In the midst of the lovely descent that was being performed, the Victorious One, [moving] with His natural gait, perceived Gaurachandra, who stood before Him, as a golden best of mountains.

*Note.* *क्षितिक्षिद्* is apparently a corruption of *kṣitibhṛd* ("earth-bearer", i.e. mountain). The Gosvami edition (GGM) has the same text.

**88.** Then, having gradually come down to the ground and making leaps all around over the heaps of cushions, He shone like the moon going from star to star, dispelling the darkness by the abundance of His radiance.

*Note.* Cf. 14.129. The first pada has 11 syllables instead of 12 (?): instead of the six light syllables with which the pada opens, there are only five (*a-tha-dha-ra-ṇi-*), while the rest of the pada (*-ṣu kramād upetaḥ*, —⏑—⏑——) is regular.

**89.** As the Victorious One gradually set His steps here and there on the cushions, He at once fully surpassed thereby the beauty of a mass of waves on an ocean of molten sapphire.

*Note.* *Surapati-ratna* ("the jewel of Indra") is the sapphire (*indranīla*).

**90.** Victorious is He, drawn by the servants who stretched taut the pair of silk ropes tied about the slope of His waist: is this perhaps the assembly of creators, born from a pair of lotus-stalks of His navel, [gathered] all around [Him] in private?

*Note.* The image is understood tentatively. The ropes by which the Deity is drawn are compared to the stalks of the lotus from Vishnu's navel, and the servants to the Brahmas born from it. *Avahṛṣṭaḥ* is understood as "drawn" (perhaps for *avakṛṣṭaḥ*) (?). The Gosvami edition (GGM) has the same text.

**91.** By the multitude of parasols held up above [Him] — moons, as it were, devoted to serving the moon of His face — and by the unceasing rain of masses of flowers, even the Lord of the Blue Mountain [became] a white field of battle.

*Note.* The meaning is unclear (?). The play is on colours: the blue Lord, covered by white parasols and white flowers, becomes white; the procession is the victor's "march" (*vijaya*), hence the "field of battle". The Gosvami edition (GGM) has the same text.

**92.** As the Dark Moon moves forward, so the son of Shachi draws back a little. That [movement] of the two, approaching face to face, bore the semblance of a graceful game of ball.

**93.** As the Lord of the Dark Mountain was surrounded by His servants, just so was Gaurachandra. The two of them, with the lustre of a sapphire and of a golden jewel, were visible to the throngs of people.

**94.** At times He too, [Jagannatha], becomes golden-hued by the radiance of Gaurachandra, and likewise that One too [becomes blue]. Thus the lordship of both over the not-white Mountain was manifested to the world all around.

*Note.* The "not-white Mountain" is Nilachala. The first sentence is compressed: [Gaurachandra becomes blue from the radiance of Jagannatha].

**95.** Prataparudra — who with the staff of the trunk of the king of elephants hacked all his foes to pieces, the remover of all obstacles, the lord of the host of kings, who, like the sun, ever blazes with might —

*Note.* Verses 95–96 form a pair (*yugmaka*). A play on words: *gaja-pati*, "king of elephants", is also the title of the kings of Orissa, the Gajapatis. The epithets "remover of all obstacles" and "lord of the host" (*gaṇa-pati*), together with the elephant's trunk, also suggest Ganesha.

**96.** …he, behaving like the humblest servant, a spotless golden broom in his hand, beholding the indescribable sporting pastime of those two, stood motionless, all his movements gone.

*Note.* Cf. Chaitanya-charitamrita, Madhya 13.15–16: the king sweeps the road with a broom with a golden handle; for this humility the Lord later shows him mercy (13.184).

**97.** By the manifold great glare of the great torches blazing constantly on both sides, the end of the night was forgotten; and by the multitude of drums, mandus, dindimas and the rest, that time was thus one of surpassing grandeur.

*Note.* The edition has *मरातप*, a corruption; the Gosvami edition (GGM) reads *mahātapa* ("great glare, heat"), and so we read. The *maṇḍu* and *ḍiṇḍima* are kinds of drums.

**98.** Thus, going near the chariot and gazing at [Jagannatha] in front, Gaurachandra shone, amid the cries of His servants: "From here, from here, from here! This, this, this! It must be seen from here!"

**99.** Then the Lord of the Blue Mountain, delighting in rasa, mounted the chariot and shone like the honey-sweet full moon on the edge of the eastern mountain when the clouds have passed.

*Note.* In the edition the number *॥९९॥* lacks the first danda.

**100.** Thus, when the fine sport on the way had been performed and the Lord of the Blue Mountain was mounting the chariot, Gaurachandra went with the throngs of His own people, wishing to sport in bathing.

**101.** Then they, refreshed by a quick plunge, gathered before the Lord; and He, having bathed Himself, abundantly anointed each of them with heaps of sandal paste.

**102.** First the joyful relisher of rasa repeatedly anointed with sandal juice, with the tender shoot of His hand, the chest of Him whose nature is non-duality, thrilling Him doubly, to the very hairs [of His body].

*Note.* "Whose nature is non-duality" (*advitīya-bhāva*) is a hint at the name Advaita.

**103.** Then [He anointed] the magnanimous one, the moon of the brahmana race, Narada's very self on earth; then his younger brother; and then likewise, in order, others from here on He anointed with sandal.

*Note.* Narada's very self on earth is Srivasa; his younger brother is Rama Pandita.

**104.** Then the Merciful One — the slender stem of His body languid under the full weight of joy, stirred by the art of dancing — thus specially anointed with that [sandal] the chest of each and every one of the singers.

*Note.* This is the last verse in *puṣpitāgrā*.

**105.** Namely: Srivasa and Rama, who surpassed the cuckoos with their voices; Vasudeva; Mukunda; the illustrious one named Damodara, renowned in the world as an ascetic, a mass of love; the illustrious Vakreshvara, with his celebrated host of virtues; that Srila Damodara, foremost of the brahmanas; then a certain most sweet one named Narayana…

*Note.* The metre is *sragdharā*. Verses 105–106 form one period. The ascetic Damodara is Svarupa Damodara (13.143); Srila Damodara is Damodara Pandita. Cf. Chaitanya-charitamrita, Madhya 13.32–47: the seven kirtana parties of the Ratha-yatra (the first led by Svarupa Damodara).

**106.** …Shrikanta, Makaradhvaja, the most sweet and pure Shubhananda, Kashinatha and Vallabha, the one named Haridasa, Raghu of pure mind — all these He Himself at once anointed with sandal juice. And then the beautiful Gauranga, having firmly tied His cloth on the slope of His beautiful waist — with His pair of stout arms reaching to both knees, His body adorned with gently thrilling hairs standing on end, wholly absorbed [in ecstasy] — set out with them.

*Note.* A verse of six padas; the metre is *śārdūla-vikrīḍita*. Note the passive *niryaye* (literally "there was a setting out [by Gauranga]"). The third pada has 20 syllables instead of 19 (?): without *sa* (*…liptvā svayaṁ śrīmatā*) it would be regular, and *sa* is awkward in any case beside the instrumental *gaurāṅgena* of the passive *niryaye*.

**107.** Their lotus hands well adorned with a multitude of loud karatalas and fine, lovely mandiras, their bodies thickly covered with the thrills of rapture, they went quickly after [Him]; at every step their lotus feet stumbled helplessly, and they were sunk in an ocean of happiness.

*Note.* The metre is *pṛthvī*. Karatalas and mandiras are hand cymbals.

**108.** Govinda quickly came up and, having drawn very near, standing at [His] side and constantly immersed in oceans of happiness, then set out [with the Lord]. And as for those splendid ones who had come, agitated by exultation at every step — the boundless ocean of the nectar of their joy [could] not be contained within the universe.

*Note.* The metre is *śārdūla-vikrīḍita*. Govinda is the Lord's servant. The ending (*'ñcitum*) is read tentatively. The Gosvami edition (GGM) has the same text.

**109.** Then Gaura — His gait playful and graceful [like that] of a troop of maddened lions, His steps scattering a bath of sweat that streamed from joy, His body adorned with hairs standing on end from the surge of incomparable happiness — went swiftly along the road, outshining with His effulgence the orb of the sun.

*Note.* The metre is *hariṇī*, with rhyme at the ends of the padas (*pāda-anta-yamaka*: *pada-kramaḥ / pada-kramaḥ; vigrahaḥ / ravi-grahaḥ*). The second pada is read according to the Gosvami edition (GGM): *…pracāyaka-pada-kramaḥ* (our edition has *-mada-kramaḥ*). In either reading the second pada has 18 syllables instead of 17 (?): at its end *-yaka-pada-kramaḥ* gives ⏑⏑⏑—⏑— where the metre requires ⏑⏑—⏑—, i.e. one light syllable is superfluous.

**110.** In front of the chariot of Baladeva, Gaurachandra — charming in the intoxication of joy, His body shining with beauty, [like] a mountain of molten gold — out of love again fell to the surface of the earth like a rod, together with [a flood of] tears.

*Note.* The metre is *mālinī*.

**Colophon:** इति श्रीकृष्णचैतन्यचरितामृते महाकाव्ये पञ्चदशः सर्गः । (*iti śrī-kṛṣṇa-caitanya-caritāmṛte mahākāvye pañcadaśaḥ sargaḥ*) — "Thus [ends] the fifteenth sarga in the mahakavya 'Sri Krishna-Chaitanya-charitamrita'." (The colophon gives no title for the sarga.)

---

## Sarga Sixteen — verses 1–49

**Title of the sarga:** *Guṇḍicā-nṛtyādi-varṇana*, "The Description of the Dance [on the Way] to the Gundicha and Other [Events]" (from the colophon).
**Metre:**
- 1–3, 5 — *mālinī* (⏑⏑⏑⏑⏑⏑———⏑——⏑——; 15 syllables per pada). In 5 the first pada is one syllable short.
- 4, 48 — *mandākrāntā* (————⏑⏑⏑⏑⏑——⏑——⏑——; 17 syllables per pada).
- 6–8, 33–36 — *vasanta-tilakā* (——⏑—⏑⏑⏑—⏑⏑—⏑——; 14 syllables per pada); 6–8 form a *viśeṣaka* (three verses, one sentence). In 34 the fourth pada has fifteen syllables (*mūrdhani*, where the metre requires *mūrdhni*).
- 9–11, 22–23, 26–27, 32, 49 — *śārdūla-vikrīḍita* (———⏑⏑—⏑—⏑⏑⏑———⏑——⏑—; 19 syllables per pada). In 9 the second pada has only seventeen syllables; in 10 the first pada has a light syllable (*saha*) in the sixteenth place.
- 12–16, 24, 28–30 — *sragdharā* (————⏑——⏑⏑⏑⏑⏑⏑——⏑——⏑——; 21 syllables per pada). In 28 the printed first pada has nineteen syllables (restored from the Gosvami edition); in 30 the fourth pada has a light syllable (*pu-* of *-purī*) in the seventeenth place.
- 17–19, 31 — *pṛthvī* (⏑—⏑⏑⏑—⏑—⏑⏑⏑—⏑——⏑—; 17 syllables per pada).
- 20–21 — *hariṇī* (⏑⏑⏑⏑⏑—————⏑—⏑⏑—⏑—; 17 syllables per pada). In 20 the fourth pada has a light syllable (*-la* of *lola-*) in the eighth place.
- 25 — *praharṣiṇī* (———⏑⏑⏑⏑—⏑—⏑——; 13 syllables per pada).
- 37–47 — *bhujaṅga-prayāta* (⏑——⏑——⏑——⏑——; 12 syllables per pada); 37–46 form a *kulaka*.

**Total verses:** 49.

**Contents of the passage:** the Ratha-yatra — the Lord's dancing before the chariot of Jagannatha on the way to the Gundicha.
- 1–5 — raising His arm, the Lord recites three verses: Mukunda-mala 2, Srimad-Bhagavatam 10.90.48 and Padyavali 63 (*nāhaṁ vipro…*); cf. Chaitanya-charitamrita, Madhya 13.
- 6–8 — a portrait of Gaura dancing (a *viśeṣaka*).
- 9–16 — the chariot: its height; "a new king of mountains"; the festival of the people of Puri; the earth beneath the wheels; the rivalry of the chariots; the dust up to the heavens; the sufferings of Kurma and Shesha; "but the dancing Gauranga has captured the hearts of all".
- 17–23 — the dance: the sound of the kirtana pierces the shell of the universe; whirling like a wheel, the tears form a ring like a halo; foam at the mouth; tears like a breaking string of pearls.
- 24 — three circles: the singers; the devotees headed by Kashi Mishra, holding one another's hands; Prataparudra outside.
- 25–29 — no one blinks; the Lord in a swoon, the chariot stands still; the devotees carry Him aside; He leans on Advaita; Prataparudra serves His feet (28).
- 30–33 — Paramananda Puri; a play on the name of Svarupa Damodara (31); Damodara embraces the Lord's feet; Kashishvara, Kashi Mishra, Govinda.
- 34–36 — the Lord places His foot on the heads of the devotees, presses His own foot to His heart and "tastes" it.
- 37–47 — the *kulaka* (37–46): an extended portrait of the Lord dancing; Jagannatha cannot blink.
- 48–49 — the miracle of the garland; immersion in the ocean of bliss.

**1.** Then, raising His mighty arm — which shone with a mass of hairs standing on end and resembled a golden tree resplendent with clusters of opening buds — He, like a golden mountain with the sky clinging to its peak, loudly and sweetly recited [these verses]:

*Note.* Metre — *mālinī*; verses 1–5 are in *mālinī*, except 4 (*mandākrāntā*). Before the chariot the Lord now recites three verses; cf. Chaitanya-charitamrita, Madhya 13.78–80, where the same verses are cited before His dance at the chariot (2 — from the Mukunda-mala, 3 — Srimad-Bhagavatam 10.90.48, 4 — Padyavali 63).

**2.** Glory, glory to this God, the son of Devaki! Glory, glory to Krishna, the lamp of the Vrishni dynasty! Glory, glory to Him who is dark as a cloud, whose body is tender! Glory, glory to Mukunda, the remover of the burden of the earth!

*Note.* A verse of Kulashekhara from the Mukunda-mala (v. 2); there the reading is *jayatu jayatu* ("may He be glorious"), here *jayati* ("He is glorious").

**3.** Glorious is He, the abode of all beings, whose birth from Devaki is only a manner of speaking; whose retinue are the best of the Yadus; who with His own arms casts away irreligion; the destroyer of the sin of all that moves and does not move; who with His beautiful smiling face increases the god of love in the women of Vraja and of the city!

*Note.* Srimad-Bhagavatam 10.90.48; the text is identical. *Yadu-vara-pariṣat* — "whose retinue are the best of the Yadus", or "surrounded by the best of the Yadus". We take *vraja-pura-vanitā* as "the women of Vraja and of Mathura and Dvaraka", following the widespread interpretation. The Gosvami edition (GGM) has the same text.

**4.** I am not a brahmana, nor a king, nor a vaishya, nor a shudra; I am not a brahmachari, nor a householder, nor a forest-dweller, nor an ascetic. But I am the servant of the servant of the servant of the lotus feet of the Lord of the gopis, the ocean of nectar full of surging, complete, supreme bliss.

*Note.* Metre — *mandākrāntā*. Padyavali 63, where it is ascribed to Sri Chaitanya Himself; in the Chaitanya-charitamrita, Madhya 13.80, the Lord recites this verse at the chariot.

**5.** Thus at the beginning of His artful dance, having set forth, deeply stirred, the supreme greatness of the Moon of Sri Vrindavana, He — whose body is exceedingly charming — softened by boundless compassion and spreading loving devotion, became filled with joy.

*Note.* The first pada has fourteen syllables instead of fifteen: after *śrī-* (the ninth syllable) a light syllable is missing, so that the heavy *vṛn-* falls in the tenth place, which requires a light one (?). The Sanskrit is given as printed.

**6–8.** Slapping the side of His left armpit with His hand; His body reddening; most lovely in His sweetness and tenderness; making the whole host of the directions glitter with the beautiful streams of radiance from the rays of the moon of His face, moving in play; again and again loudly crying out "Glory, glory!" at the top of His voice, together with the bristling of the multitude of hairs on His body; mocking the bandhujiva flower with the lustre of the crimson cloth gracefully tied about His slender waist, which a fist could measure; His golden body bathed in the water of His beautiful eyes; the moon of His face studded with fresh drops of perspiration; the bendings of His body agitated by the art of the unrestrained tandava — the beautiful [Gaura] then adorned the midst of His own people.

*Note.* Metre — *vasanta-tilakā*; the three verses form a *viśeṣaka* (so marked in the edition). Slapping the armpit (*āsphoṭana*) is a wrestler's gesture, a challenge before a bout; here it is a sign of exultation before the dance. *Jhalamalāyita* is a denominative from the onomatopoeic *jhalamala*, "glitter, twinkling". The *bandhujīva* is a crimson flower (*Pentapetes phoenicea*).

**9.** Majestically triumphs the chariot of the Enemy of Mura, full of splendour: by its loftiness it makes the expanse of the sky tremble; again and again it kisses the disc of the sun; it fully performs the rite of honouring the gods; and it inspires a festival of joy for the eyes of all that abides within the universe.

*Note.* Metre — *śārdūla-vikrīḍita*; verses 9–11 are in *śārdūla*. The edition prints औत्तुङ्गेन (*auttuṅgena*, which is not a word); we read *auttuṅgyena*, "by its loftiness" (the metre is the same). *Deva-sabhājana* may also be understood through *deva-sabhā*, "the assembly of the gods": "it brings full joy to the assembly of the gods". The second pada has only seventeen syllables instead of nineteen and does not scan from the ninth syllable on (*-na* of *-sabhājana-* stands where a heavy syllable is required): some two syllables seem to have dropped out (?).

**10.** Bowing Kailasa down in every way, [humbling] Meru together with all its pride, indeed throwing the paltry Vindhya into longing distress and dejecting the father of Gauri — "Has the Creator now made on earth some other king, as it were, of the mountains?" — [so people thought of] this chariot, which bore the nectar of the beautiful form of the Enemy of Mura.

*Note.* The edition prints राजेक (*rājeka*), which makes no sense; we read *rājeva* (= *rājā iva*), as does the Gosvami edition (GGM). *Meruṁ saha-nirbharaṁ* is obscure, and the sense given is conjectural; moreover, *saha* puts a light syllable in the sixteenth place, where the *śārdūla-vikrīḍita* requires a heavy one. The variant *meruṁ hasan nirbharaṁ*, "laughing heartily at Meru", scans correctly and gives a clear sense (?). The diminutive *vindhyaka* is contemptuous ("some Vindhya or other"). The father of Gauri (Parvati) is Himavan, the Himalaya.

**11.** Among the worthy [people of Puri] — whose souls were blessed by exultation, who with the cupped hands of their lotus eyes, their lashes raised, drank the nectars radiating from the beautiful form of the Crest-Jewel of the Blue Mountain, and who shone motionless with rows of hairs standing on end — there was indeed everywhere a great festival, with waves of the Mandakini of bliss.

*Note.* The Mandakini is the heavenly Ganga. *Utpakṣma*, "with lashes raised", i.e. without blinking.

**12.** The ground around the chariot, though almost all of it was tormented by the pressure of the wheels, with a gladdened heart seemed to long to colour itself with vermilion — with the lovely streams of sindura fallen especially from the foreheads of the many women who came again and again from all sides with eager hearts.

*Note.* Metre — *sragdharā*; verses 12–16 are in *sragdharā*. The edition has *saindūrī-* (the *vṛddhi* derivative of *sindūra*), which is admissible. Sindura on the forehead (in the parting of the hair) is the mark of a married woman.

**13.** Looking and looking to their hearts' content at Gaurachandra as He danced, charming in the placing of His pair of lotus feet, those brothers standing on the chariots grew eager at heart; and again, vying with one another — "I first!" — moving swiftly and eagerly forward, these swift-footed ones here and there, as it were, danced from the excess of their joyful intoxication.

*Note.* The "brothers" are Jagannatha, Balarama and Subhadra (the brothers and their sister). Grammatically the subject of the whole verse is one — *bhrātaraḥ … amī jaṅghālāḥ* — that is, the chariots themselves, overtaking one another (?); the second half may also be referred to the people running before the chariots, vying to go first. *Kurvata* is an epic form without the augment (= *akurvata*). The Gosvami edition (GGM) has the same text.

**14.** The stream of dust, raised and spreading from the crushing [of the ground] by the circles of the rolling wheels, spreads so that it blocks the openings of the trunks of the host of lordly elephants of the directions, makes the Lord of Airavana tearful in all His thousand eyes, makes the gods there shine with grey hair, and once again creates the illusion of a bridge over the ocean.

*Note.* The bridge over the ocean is the one built by Rama's army (the Setu). The edition has *payodhe* without the visarga; we read *payodheḥ*. Airavana (= Airavata) is Indra's elephant.

**15.** When the Ornament of the Blue Mountain eagerly set out for the Gundicha, the Tortoise felt pain in his vital parts; this lord of the serpents, his heads bowed, again and again moved the earth from head to head — [and each head] became worn; and the ocean, its waters tossing at the shore, seemed for a long time to have lost its bounds.

*Note.* The Tortoise (Kurma) and Shesha are the supports of the earth. *Jīrṇa-maṇḍam babhūva* is obscure (?): perhaps "[each head] became worn" (as if *jīrṇa-maṇḍala*), or "he grew exhausted". The metre confirms *-maṇḍaṁ*. The Gosvami edition (GGM) has the same text.

**16.** Let all the jewelled riches shine there at the triumphal setting out of Murari's chariot, again and again surpassing the lovely splendour of the assembly of the lord of the gods; let the beautiful Lord Himself, rich in such ornaments, display His splendour to the full — but it is this dancing Moon, Gauranga, who has captured the hearts of all the people!

*Note.* *Ratha-vijaya*, "the triumphal setting out", is the procession of the chariot.

**17.** May that indescribable glittering of the streams of pure radiance of the Moon, the son of Shachi, which spreads unceasingly through the directions, expand in your hearts — [the glittering] of Him who, again and again bending His body, has outdone the lovely lotuses by the playful steps of His feet.

*Note.* Metre — *pṛthvī* (⏑—⏑⏑⏑—⏑—⏑⏑⏑—⏑——⏑—); verses 17–19 are in *pṛthvī*. The edition has the nominative *kalānidhiḥ*, but the sense requires the genitive *kalānidheḥ* (the metre is the same); *-paṅkeruhaḥ* is then genitive too. *-Malaj-* is obscure (?); the conjecture *-mālā-*, "a garland of streams of radiance", would fit the sense but not the metre, which requires a light syllable followed by a heavy one here. The Gosvami edition (GGM) has the same text.

**18.** The sound of the singing, blending with the ringing of the loudest hand-cymbals and worshipped by the lotus faces of the maidens of the directions, who echo it, loudly and again and again pierces the partition of the last layer [of the universe] — and how far it goes, by whom then can that be measured?

*Note.* *Mandirā* (Bengali *mandira*) are small hand-cymbals (karatalas). *Uccavaccaram* in the edition is obscure. The Gosvami edition (GGM) divides the same letters as *uccavac carama-khaṇḍa-khaṇḍāntaram*, "the loud [sound, piercing] the partition of the last layer"; we accept this division. The metre confirms the syllables of the printed text.

**19.** Again and again encircled by a glittering stream [of tears] set swaying by His sweet whirling like a wheel — like the moon adorned with a halo — He triumphs here in the exertion of the dance, washing the faces of the directions all around with the floods of water from His eyes, formed into a ring.

*Note.* The image: as the Lord whirls, His tears fly out in a ring, like a halo around the moon. *-Vilolayāśleṣaṇaḥ* is obscure (?): we read *vilolayā* (agreeing with *dhārayā*) + *āśleṣaṇaḥ*, "embraced, encircled". The Gosvami edition (GGM) has the same text.

**20.** What could all the directions do, made to resound deafeningly by hundreds upon hundreds of very loud cries of "Glory, glory, may He be glorious!"? For He, unceasingly casting His reddened eyes on them, by the art of His dance made the surface of the world trembling and crimson.

*Note.* Metre — *hariṇī* (⏑⏑⏑⏑⏑—————⏑—⏑⏑—⏑—); verses 20–21 are in *hariṇī*. The edition prints *ninādaparaḥ śatair*; we read *paraḥśataiḥ* as one word, "more than a hundred, hundreds upon hundreds". In the fourth pada the light *-la* of *lola-* stands in the eighth place, where the metre requires a heavy syllable (?).

**21.** With the foam poured forth from the moon of His face He seems to laugh at the cool-rayed autumn moon, full of spots and ever defeated by [His] beauty; with the thickly sprouted masses of hairs standing on end He is like Sumeru, with sprouted shoots in hundreds beyond all counting: so He shines.

*Note.* Foam at the mouth is one of the signs of ecstatic love. The golden Lord with His hairs standing on end is compared to the golden mountain Sumeru covered with sprouting shoots.

**22.** May the joy of the worlds ever be brought about by the tear of the Lord, which first opens the eyelashes and floods them, then at once flows long in abundant streams down the slopes of His beautiful cheeks, then, reaching the path of His chest, spreads in three [streams and falls] on the ground like a breaking string of pearls.

*Note.* Metre: *śārdūla-vikrīḍita* (22–23).

**23.** May bliss be bestowed on the worlds by that flood of Lord Gaura's tears of bliss which, rolling there over the ground, as it were floods the universe; which on His chest eclipses the beauty of a lovely river through whose depths golden stones show; and which in His eyes has the beauty of a lovely lake with submerged lotuses.

*Note.* Gaura's chest is the golden stones on the bed of the river of tears; His eyes are the lotuses under the water.

**24.** When the first circle had been formed by those singing singers, and outside it [a second] had been ringed unbroken by the supremely right-minded [devotees] headed by Sri Kashi Mishra, who had taken refuge at His lotus feet and joyfully held one another's hands, and when outside that Sri Sri Rudradeva, with "Pratapa" before [his name], unobtrusively went round [them] here and there — the Lord shines.

*Note.* The edition has प्रमथ- (*pramatha-*), a misprint for *prathama-*, "first" (the metre is the same). The name of Prataparudra is split up for the sake of the metre: "Sri Sri Rudradeva, before whom [stands] 'Pratapa'". Cf. Chaitanya-charitamrita, Madhya 13.88–90: three circles around the dancing Lord — the first led by Nityananda, the second formed by the devotees headed by Mukunda and Kashishvara, holding one another's hands, and the third, outside, by Prataparudra with his retinue.

**25.** Whether Indra, or the Creator, or the god Isha — none of them was then worth regarding: while Sri Gaura was lovely in the attire of the play of dance, there was not even for a moment any closing of the eyelashes.

*Note.* Metre: *praharṣiṇī* (———⏑⏑⏑⏑—⏑—⏑——). The edition has विधि without the visarga; the metre requires *vidhiḥ*.

**26.** When He, made motionless by bliss, [lay] long on the ground, the chariot of the Lord of the Blue Mountain likewise stood still; and when it [again] began to approach, these [devotees], growing greatly anxious, took Him each into his own arms with their tender, shoot-like hands and gently carried Him some way off. Victorious is Lord Sri Gaurachandra!

*Note.* Metre: *śārdūla-vikrīḍita* (26–27). The chariot does not move while the Lord lies unconscious — cf. Chaitanya-charitamrita, Madhya 13.99 (the chariot stops, and Jagannatha gazes without blinking at the Lord's dance) and 14.48–55 (the chariot will not move, however it is pulled, until the Lord pushes it with His head).

**27.** Victorious is Lord Sri Gaurachandra, captivating with the exuberance of His dance: at every step He grows motionless from bliss, amid a clamour of roars of "hum"; His broad chest is moistened by the touch of the tender hand that Advaita offers [Him]; His staff-like arms and both His feet fall this way and that like a staff.

*Note.* *Advaitārpita-pāṇi-pallava* is either "the tender hand which Advaita offered [for support]" or "the tender hand which [the Lord] laid on Advaita"; *uru* may also be taken as *ūru*, "thighs" ("His thighs and chest").

**28.** When He, as if fallen into a swoon from the surge of bliss, was slow in movement and breath, Gaurachandra shines, the pair of His lotus feet served again with bliss by Rudradeva — whose body was shaken by floods of rising hairs standing on end, who was slowed by bliss, and whose pair of lotus eyes streamed with water.

*Note.* Metre: *sragdharā* (28–30). In the edition the first pada has nineteen syllables instead of twenty-one; the Gosvami edition (GGM) reads *mūrcchāgata iva bhavati* (*-gate iva* with sandhi), and the gap is filled from it. Rudradeva is King Prataparudra; cf. Chaitanya-charitamrita, Madhya 14.4–18 (the king, dressed as a Vaishnava and with the devotees' permission, serves the feet of the Lord, who lies in the garden with closed eyes — 14.6–7).

**29.** When this beautiful Moon, Gauranga, dances — His lotus eyes opening, the stem of His whole body trembling under a covering of hairs standing on end, outshining the moon with the radiance of His moonlike face that gleams with the flood of foam He spits forth, slowed by a succession of dense bliss, the moon [that brings] good fortune to the ocean of waves of sweetness — in whom does stupor not arise?

**30.** Paramananda Puri — whose wealth is the sweetness of the beauty of every limb washed by streams of an abundance of boundless supreme bliss, and whose heart yearns for loveliness — when, having drunk again and again the flood of the river of the rasa of the dance, he trembles with exultation and again and again becomes motionless, joyfully spreads bliss through the openings of his eyes.

*Note.* *Bobhavīti* is an intensive of *bhū*. Bliss "through the openings of the eyes" means tears streaming from the eyes. *Paramānanda-sandoha*, "an abundance of supreme bliss", echoes the name Paramananda. Paramananda Puri is a senior companion of the Lord, a disciple of Madhavendra Puri. In the fourth pada the seventeenth syllable (*pu-* of *-purī*) is light where the *sragdharā* requires a heavy one: the name does not fit the metre (?).

**31.** Is it because the Lord wore a waist-cord that this best of renunciants is proclaimed here "Damodara", as though he were His "Svarupa", His own form? — he who at the festival of the dance, intensely longing, with all the workings of heart, body and speech, as it were enters into the Moon that is the son of Shachi.

*Note.* Metre: *pṛthvī*. A play on words: *dāmodara*, "[he who has] a cord (*dāman*) round the belly (*udara*)", is a name of Krishna, who was bound by Yashoda; the Lord wears a waist-cord, and so His companion is called Damodara, as if he were His *svarūpa*, His "own form". Svarupa Damodara is meant.

**32.** Behold this Lord dancing: He freely sets on the ground, gleaming, the pair of His beautiful lotus feet, on which [sweat] appears like nectar, while this Damodara, melted by great love, out of joy, here and there, low down, firmly embraces His feet with the tender buds of his hands.

*Note.* Metre: *śārdūla-vikrīḍita*. The edition has *anunā*, a misprint for *amunā*, "by this"; the correction is made by sense, since metrically the two readings are equivalent. *Kuṭnala* = *kuḍmala*, "bud".

**33.** Kashishvara and the others, and Kashi Mishra — the sole vessel in which the fullness of joy rests — and this Govinda, their hearts eager, vying with one another, eagerly drink with their eyes the nectar of His dance.

*Note.* Metre: *vasanta-tilakā* (33–36). Kashishvara and Govinda are servants of the Lord (disciples of Ishvara Puri).

**34.** Victorious is He who is fair as a mountain of gold: dancing, He sets His lotus foot on the ground; with swaying head He happily embraces person after person with His arms, kisses them and, His love made manifest, places [His foot] on their heads.

*Note.* *Samupadiśya* ("having pointed out", "having directed") is strange in this context (?); the sense seems to be that, setting His feet on the ground in the dance, He places His foot on the heads of the devotees. The Gosvami edition (GGM) has the same text. In the fourth pada *mūrdhani* gives fifteen syllables instead of fourteen; the metre requires the equally correct locative *mūrdhni* (?).

**35.** "Apart from this there is nothing else here in the world so lovely, beautiful, fragrant, full of great mercy and hard to attain" — having considered thus, He presses His own lotus foot to His heart in the dance and constantly kisses it. Victorious is He!

*Note.* Cf. v. 36: Gaura, like Krishna, relishes the sweetness of His own feet (the motif of the infant Krishna sucking His toe). Another understanding: in the dance He places His feet in the hearts [of the devotees].

**36.** As if loving His own lotus foot at every step, as if placing it in the depths of His heart, as if drinking it with His pair of eyes, and as if again and again tasting it, the charming Gaura is unceasingly victorious in the dance.

**37–46.** As He sets down the pair of His lotus feet, their sweetness flashing forth washes away the beauty of the red lotus; His sturdy thighs have the grace of swaying, lovely plantain trees, and the rounded curves of His hips shine. With the redness of the pure splendour of His cloth, vividly red like clusters of opening hibiscus buds, He has coloured the three worlds red; the play of bliss has risen in Him, and in the flashing tandava His staff-like arms are raised in play. He is most lovely with the lustre of the beautiful waist-cord that closely clasps His quivering waist, which a fist could span; His chest is bathed in a heavy stream of sweat, and [over His body is] a beautiful, unrestrained flood of hairs standing on end. He carries a stream of that bliss resembling the ocean of milk, whose course brings transformations; with the relishes of the play of His eyes, graceful as the play of darting bees, He wonderfully makes for the people the heart of a lotus. In Him the manifestation of stupor, hairs standing on end and trembling is accompanied by the adorning manifestation of a swoon of bliss; from the excess of the manifestation of irresistible feeling, the flashing streams of the radiance of His body have covered the world. The nectar stream of His fame spreads over the three worlds; He has made manifest the propagation of loving devotion; the waves of His loveliness and youth are covered by the streams of radiance of His beautiful body, shining like molten gold. Accompanied by mridangas rumbling amid a host of ringing cymbals, by the surging waves of an ocean of great exultation, and by singers whose voices again and again are ardent in the modulations of charming melodies, He is ever full of bliss. Seeing Lord Jagannatha enchanted by His dances, He [is overcome] with tears of excessive joy, with sweat and with laughter; from the excess of rasa His beautiful body is exhausted, and the curves of the corners of His eyes are ever narrowed and reddening. Intoxicated by the Lord, the Crown of the Blue Mountain, who stands before Him, exceedingly restless in His impatience to behold His dance and unable even to blink His eyes — [intoxicated] by an abundant upsurge of joy; with the grace of the play of His mobile lotus face, with the shining manifestation of His hairs [standing on end] amid bursting hissing intakes of breath, giving in unprecedented measure the water of love to the three worlds, the Lord dances and dances [on the way] to the Gundicha.

*Note.* Metre of 37–47: *bhujaṅga-prayāta* (⏑——×4; 12 syllables per pada). Verses 37–46 are a *kulaka*: one sentence, with the verb *narīnartti* (an intensive of *nṛt*) in 46. In 38 the edition breaks *raktāṁ / śukaṁ* at the junction of the padas; we read the compound *raktāṁśuka-svaccha-…* (the metre is the same); *anuraktām*, "coloured red", also means "filled with love". In 40 *abja-garbham*, "the heart of a lotus", is unclear (?): perhaps His bee-like glances make the people, as it were, the heart of a lotus in which they sport; another understanding is that the devotees see His eyes as the heart of a lotus. *Rāma-rambhā* (37) — "a lovely plantain tree". In 45 the instrumentals (*purasthena … īśvareṇa … akṣameṇa*) are taken as the agent of *pramattīkṛtaḥ*, together with *bhūri-harṣodgamena*; *-avalokāsthirāty-asthireṇa* may also be divided *-avalokāt sthirāty-asthireṇa*. *Śītkṛta* (46) — drawing in the breath through the teeth, a sign of rapture. The Gosvami edition (GGM) has the same text.

**47.** Was it because He had seen His dance, full of playful sweetness, that He was unable to blink His eyes? For He, whose eyes are full-blown lotuses, was utterly immersed in it with His whole self.

*Note.* "He" is Jagannatha, whose eyes have no lids in any case: the poet plays on His unblinking eyes.

**48.** Dancing there in the circle, He whirls the incomparable garland like a wheel with His fingertips and, from the excess of joy, throws it so that it shines on the neck of the one whom He had wished for in His heart, even though that one stood far off. Ah, what a wonder this is!

*Note.* Metre: *mandākrāntā*. Another understanding: the garland falls on the neck of Jagannatha Himself.
