**49.** Having thus performed in many ways a lovely dance on the road in front of the Blue Ornament on the crown of Sri Nilachala, the son of Shachi, full of love, beheld the beautiful radiance of His moonlike face, cool as nectar, and sank deep into the ocean of bliss together with those to whom His feet are dear.

*Note.* Metre: *śārdūla-vikrīḍita*.

**Colophon:** इति श्रीकृष्णचैतन्यचरितामृते महाकाव्ये गुण्डिचानृत्यादिवर्णनं षोडशः सर्गः । (*iti śrī-kṛṣṇa-caitanya-caritāmṛte mahākāvye guṇḍicā-nṛtyādi-varṇanaṁ ṣoḍaśaḥ sargaḥ*) — "Thus [ends] the sixteenth sarga, 'The Description of the Dance [on the Way] to the Gundicha and Other [Events]', in the mahakavya 'Sri Krishna-Chaitanya-charitamrita'."

---

## Sarga Seventeen — verses 1–66

**Title of the sarga:** *Śrī-puruṣottama-vihāra-varṇanam upavana-vilāsaś ca*, "The Description of the Pastimes in Sri Purushottama, and the Pastimes in the Garden" (from the colophon).
**Metre:** in its second half the sarga is a "display of metres": from verse 43 almost every verse is in a metre of its own, and in 34 and 56 the name of a metre is woven into the text.
- 1–14, 16–24 — *mañjubhāṣiṇī* (*sa-ja-sa-ja-ga*: ⏑⏑—⏑—⏑⏑⏑—⏑—⏑—; 13 syllables per pada). In 5 the third pada has fourteen syllables (*tad-anusaṅgi-*; *tanu-saṅgi-* would scan); in 8 the third pada has a light syllable (*-va* of *iva*) in the ninth place (*ivāyayuḥ* would scan); in 19 the third pada, which by the metre ends with *vāsaso*, is one syllable short.
- 15 — *anuṣṭubh* (a quotation).
- 25–29 — *mandākinī* (*na-na-ra-ra*: ⏑⏑⏑⏑⏑⏑—⏑——⏑—; 12 syllables per pada).
- 30–42 — *candravartma* (*ra-na-bha-sa*: —⏑—⏑⏑⏑—⏑⏑⏑⏑—; 12 syllables per pada); in 34 the name of the metre, *candra-vartma* ("the path of the moon"), opens the verse. In 33 the fourth pada has thirteen syllables (*tāṇḍavākulita-*; *tāṇḍavākula-* would scan); in 40 the first pada has a heavy syllable (*-lī-* of *paṭalīkayā*) in the tenth place, where a light one is required; in 41 the fourth pada has a heavy syllable (*vas-* of *vastv*) in the sixth place, where a light one is required.
- 43 — *mattamayūra* (*ma-ta-ya-sa-ga*: —————⏑⏑——⏑⏑——; 13 syllables per pada). The third pada has twelve syllables (a light syllable is missing in the eleventh place, before *ramyaḥ*); the fourth has fourteen (*taṁ* is superfluous to the metre).
- 44 — *na-na-sa-sa-ga* (⏑⏑⏑⏑⏑⏑⏑⏑—⏑⏑——; 13 syllables per pada).
- 45 — *kalahaṁsa* (*sa-ja-sa-sa-ga*: ⏑⏑—⏑—⏑⏑⏑—⏑⏑——; 13 syllables per pada). The third pada has fourteen syllables (*mūrdhani*, where the metre requires *mūrdhni*).
- 46 — *bhramara-vilasita* (*ma-bha-na-la-ga*: ————⏑⏑⏑⏑⏑⏑—; 11 syllables per pada).
- 47 — *dodhaka* (*bha-bha-bha-ga-ga*: —⏑⏑—⏑⏑—⏑⏑——; 11 syllables per pada).
- 48–49 — *śālinī* (*ma-ta-ta-ga-ga*: —————⏑——⏑——; 11 syllables per pada).
- 50 — *upajāti* (padas of *indravajrā* ——⏑——⏑⏑—⏑—— and *upendravajrā* ⏑—⏑——⏑⏑—⏑——).
- 51–52 — *rathoddhatā* (*ra-na-ra-la-ga*: —⏑—⏑⏑⏑—⏑—⏑—; 11 syllables per pada).
- 53 — *vasanta-tilakā* (——⏑—⏑⏑⏑—⏑⏑—⏑——; 14 syllables per pada).
- 54 — *śaśikalā* (fourteen light syllables and one heavy: ⏑⏑⏑⏑⏑⏑⏑⏑⏑⏑⏑⏑⏑⏑—; 15 syllables per pada). The first pada has only fourteen syllables.
- 55 — a nine-syllable metre (*ma-ta-ya*: —————⏑⏑——).
- 56 — *līlākhela* (fifteen heavy syllables per pada); the name *vidyun-mālā* (a metre of eight heavy syllables) is woven into the text.
- 57–62 — *līlā* (*ma-sa-ma-bha-ga-ga*: ———⏑⏑—————⏑⏑——; 14 syllables per pada). In 57 the second pada has fifteen syllables (*cumbavad*, where the metre requires *cumbad*); in 58 the second pada has twelve (two heavy syllables are missing at the beginning); in 60 the third pada has a light syllable (*-pu-* of *kaśipunā*) in the thirteenth place, where a heavy one is required.
- 63–66 — *śārdūla-vikrīḍita* (———⏑⏑—⏑—⏑⏑⏑———⏑——⏑—; 19 syllables per pada). In 66 the printed text is faulty in the third and fourth padas; the readings of the Gosvami edition, adopted here, restore the metre.

**Total verses:** 66.

**Contents of the passage:** the Ratha-yatra (continued): rest in the garden, the meeting with Rupa, Sanatana and Anupama, dances in the garden, bathing, the return to the chariot.
- 1–2 — Gaura after His dance before the chariot; the images of Jagannatha along the road.
- 3–7 — the Lord enters a garden dear to Jagannatha (a *yugmaka*, 3–4); the breeze and the goddesses of the forest serve Him.
- 8–24 — **Rupa, Sanatana and Anupama** ("three oceans of rasa", 9):
  - they praise the Lord with the hymns of Brahma; concealing His feelings, He directs them to Jagannatha (12);
  - He recites the verse *na me 'bhaktaś catur-vedī* (15) and bows down to them;
  - with straws between their teeth they recite the words of the gopis;
  - the Lord has mahaprasada served from three pots called *ghasa*; the brothers withdraw.
- 25–29 — an impetuous dance in the garden; a secluded dance with Vakreshvara.
- 30–39 — the songs of Vasudeva (apparently Vasudeva Datta); the eight sattvika-bhavas; "the path of the moon" eclipsed (34).
- 40–42 — the pond; rest on its bank.
- 43–50 — the bathing:
  - Krishnadasa, a servant of Nityananda, pours water from the pond over the Lord;
  - Advaita and the Lord pour water over each other;
  - Govinda brings fresh cloth.
- 51–52 — mahaprasada with the devotees; a stroll through the garden.
- 53–58 — the return to the chariot: the Lord pushes it (53), claps His hands and holds the ropes; His companions sing of His own pastimes.
- 59–62 — sunset; the chariot halts; Jagannatha is carried into the temple over cushions (60); the Lord, with Govinda, beholds Him again.
- 63–66 — the close of the day: people grow motionless at the sight of Jagannatha and are stunned at the sight of Gaura; the Lord sports thus every year.

**1.** Then, at the close of the dance, His body shining with the water of abundant perspiration, wearing a beautiful crimson cloth, He shone again and again before the Lord of the Dark Mountain like a golden mountain with lovely streams of [red] minerals.

*Note.* Metre — *mañjubhāṣiṇī* (*sa-ja-sa-ja-ga*: ⏑⏑—⏑—⏑⏑⏑—⏑—⏑—); verses 1–24, except 15. The crimson cloth answers to the streams of red ore, the perspiration to the mountain torrents.

**2.** Again and again looking upon the images of this Lord of the worlds on both sides of the road, in the spaces between the chariots — did He, whose lustre is that of a golden mountain, thereby confer lordship on them here, as it were?

*Note.* In the edition this verse is numbered ॥६॥, a misprint for ॥२॥. Apparently the images of Jagannatha along the road are meant. The Gosvami edition (GGM) has the same text.

**3–4.** Having sported for a very long time before the chariot, He, to relieve His fatigue, entered a charming garden, beloved of the Tilaka on the crown of the Dark Mountain, with trees that gave cool shade — a garden of no small charm, surrounded on all sides by clusters of fresh jati, kunda, oleander, yuthika, navamallika and lovely madhavi, by bakulas, young mango trees and champakas.

*Note.* A *yugmaka* (so marked in the edition). *Jāti*, *yūthikā*, *navamālikā*, *mādhavī* are various kinds of jasmine and flowering creepers; *karavīra* is the oleander; *bakula* — *Mimusops elengi*. On the garden where the Lord rests during the Ratha-yatra, cf. Chaitanya-charitamrita, Madhya 13.194–204: at Balagandi, where food is offered to Jagannatha, the Lord enters a beautiful garden and lies down there to rest.

**5.** The breeze, embracing the abundance of flowers all around and carrying a multitude of fresh spray from the lakes, gathering up the drops of perspiration that clung to Him, served the Lord very gently for a while.

*Note.* The breeze has the three classical virtues: it is fragrant (with the flowers), cool (with the spray) and gentle. The third pada has fourteen syllables instead of thirteen: *tad-anusaṅgi-* gives ⏑⏑⏑—⏑ where the metre requires ⏑⏑—⏑—; the reading *tanu-saṅgi-* ("clinging to the body") would scan correctly (?). The Sanskrit is given as printed.

**6.** He who had assumed a golden form, His body gently and unceasingly fanned by the eager-hearted goddesses of the forest with charming fresh shoots and with chamaras of fresh shirisha flowers, shone as befitted [Him].

*Note.* The shirisha (*Albizia lebbeck*) has fluffy flowers resembling a chamara, the yak-tail whisk.

**7.** Is not Gaurachandra, as it were, bathing the world with a current of the nectar of the streams of rays from His sweetly radiant face, so that it may not be oppressed by the unbearable scorching heats of the threefold miseries?

*Note.* The threefold miseries are those arising from one's own body and mind (*ādhyātmika*), from the higher powers (*ādhidaivika*) and from other beings (*ādhibhautika*).

**8.** Then certain beloved ones of this Lord of the worlds, richly adorned with the fullness of supreme power, came like oceans of the essence of rasa to behold the pair of the Lord's lotus feet.

*Note.* "The Lord of the worlds" may also be referred to Jagannatha ("certain dear devotees of Jagannatha"). In the edition *prabho* at the end of the third pada lacks the visarga; we read *prabhoḥ*. In the third pada the ninth syllable (*-va* of *iva*) is light where the metre requires a heavy one; the reading *ivāyayuḥ* (*iva āyayuḥ*, "came") would scan correctly (?).

**9.** The treasure-house of mercy saw the three oceans of the rasa of devotion to His own lotus feet, which had taken the forms of Sanatana, Anupama and Rupa and were flooding the world with the sparkling waves of their feeling.

*Note.* A play on words: *sa-sanātanānupama-rūpa-rūpiṇaḥ* may also be read "possessed of an eternal (*sanātana*), incomparable (*anupama*) beauty (*rūpa*)". According to the Chaitanya-charitamrita the brothers came to Puri separately: Anupama (Vallabha) passed away suddenly in Bengal, and Rupa, delayed, came to Puri alone (Antya 1.37–39), while Sanatana came later (Antya 4). In Karnapura all three come together.

**10.** Then those three dear brothers, as if most closely akin to His ecstatic manifestations, came near to Mahaprabhu and, speaking plaintively, fervently praised Him with the hymns of the Lotus-born.

*Note.* The hymns of the Lotus-born are the prayers of Brahma (Srimad-Bhagavatam 10.14). *Anubhāva* — the outward signs of ecstatic feeling; *anubhāva-sodaratamāḥ*, literally "most closely related to [His] anubhāvas", seems to mean that the brothers share His ecstasy (?).

**11.** Then again, overpowered by the tears streaming from their eyes, by the multitude of their hairs standing on end and by the softness of their hearts, standing near Mahaprabhu, they cast off their fear and offered praise.

*Note.* In the edition the final visarga of *sādhvasāḥ* is missing; it is restored in brackets.

**12.** Having heard all this, the Lord, the treasure-house of mercy, concealing His feelings, again said this: "Here before you is the Moon on the crown of the Blue Mountain. Why do you not go up to Him and offer your praise?"

*Note.* *Avahitthā* — the concealment of one's feelings, one of the *vyabhicāri-bhāvas*. *Kuruta* is an imperative plural ("offer!"): the Lord redirects the praise to Jagannatha.

**13.** Since their firmness grew ever stronger through the layers of dense attachment, how could they slacken? Therefore, casting off fear, they offered praise with still greater effort.

**14.** When He, trying in various ways to turn aside their boldness, could not restrain them, then they were plunged in the massed floods of an ocean of excessive joy.

*Note.* *Avagāhitā vidadhire* may also be understood as "[He] made them plunge".

**15.** "A knower of the four Vedas without devotion is not dear to Me; My devotee, though a dog-eater, is dear. To him one should give, from him one should accept; he is to be worshipped as I am."

*Note.* Metre — *anuṣṭubh*. A well-known verse (quoted in the Chaitanya-charitamrita, Madhya 19.50, and in the Hari-bhakti-vilasa with reference to the Itihasa-samuchchaya). The edition prints नमे भक्तः without the avagraha; the standard reading is *na me 'bhaktaś catur-vedī*. Read as printed, the first quarter would mean "a knower of the four Vedas is not My devotee". The Lord recites this verse to the brothers, who considered themselves low because of their service at the Muslim court.

**16.** Having thus sweetly recited [this verse], Mahaprabhu fell to the ground like a rod and bowed down [to them]. Thereupon they, their hearts made very bold by the intoxication of mighty devotion, were not frightened by this.

*Note.* *Mattayā* stands for the expected *mattatayā* (the metre does not allow the extra syllable).

**17.** When the sweetly radiant words "Speak, speak!" came forth again and again from Mahaprabhu, they then saw His supreme form, the form they desired, resembling the radiance of the dense rays of a hundred moons.

**18.** And Gaurachandra, intensely eager, full of the honeyed sweetness of a doubled radiance, again and again fervently said "Speak, speak!", bathing the circle of the earth with the rays of His smile.

**19.** Then, abandoning the hymn of the Lotus-born, taking a bunch of straws between their teeth and tying the edge of their cloth about their necks, they, full of longing, offered praise still more fervently.

*Note.* A straw between the teeth and the cloth about the neck are gestures of utmost humility; cf. Rupa and Sanatana before the Lord at Ramakeli, "holding straws between their teeth and binding their cloth about their necks" (Chaitanya-charitamrita, Madhya 1.185). *Vidadhiretarām* is a verb with the intensive suffix *-tarām*. Metrically the third pada ends with *vāsaso*: *'ñcalam utsukā vidadhiretarāṁ stutim* forms a regular fourth pada. The third pada then has only twelve syllables, and its opening *adhikaṇṭham abhi-* (⏑⏑—⏑⏑⏑) does not fit the metre (⏑⏑—⏑—⏑⏑): a syllable seems to have dropped out (?). The Sanskrit is given as printed.

**20.** Having recited what had been spoken by the beautiful cowherd women — words agreeable to their own hearts and bestowing what is desired — they, adorned with the water of their eyes, offered praise; and they too were immersed in an ocean of happiness.

*Note.* Apparently the Gopi-gita (Srimad-Bhagavatam 10.31) or other prayers of the gopis (?). The Gosvami edition (GGM) has the same text.

**21.** Having thus fully taken in their most plaintive speeches as something sweet, as if made of nectar, He became intensely eager: "Bring it, bring it! Let the great mahaprasada be served quickly!"

**22.** Then they, trembling violently and all but collapsing, their whole bodies washed by torrents of tears, sat down on the ground near the pair of His lotus feet.

**23.** Then the almighty Lord, who gives joy to His own people, having graciously distributed mahaprasada from three lovely pots called *ghasa*, shone, beautiful with the moon of His sweetly radiant face.

*Note.* *Ghasa* is apparently a local (Oriya) name for an earthen pot of temple food (?). The Gosvami edition (GGM) has the same text.

**24.** Then they too — treasuries of great good fortune — their hearts at peace, eager to behold the splendour of that manifestation [of the Lord] which accorded with their heart's desire, their bodies bathed in the streams flowing from their eyes, departed.

*Note.* The last verse in *mañjubhāṣiṇī*.

**25.** Then in the garden the Ocean of joy, His heart stirred by an impulse to dance, performed a honey-sweet, impetuous dance full of rasa together with those like Him.

*Note.* Metre of verses 25–29 — *mandākinī* (*na-na-ra-ra*: ⏑⏑⏑⏑⏑⏑—⏑——⏑—).

**26.** And there, full of love, beautiful with streams of honey-sweet radiance, He ardently and unceasingly unfolded His play here together with Vakreshvara, the moon of the race of the twice-born.

*Note.* Vakreshvara Pandita, an associate of the Lord, was famed for his dancing; cf. Chaitanya-charitamrita, Madhya 14.98–101: in the garden the Lord dances beneath every tree while Vasudeva Datta sings, and then bids Vakreshvara dance and Himself sings with Svarupa Damodara.

**27.** Now the beautiful [Gaura] embraces Vakreshvara and ardently kisses him; now He shines, lightly setting down the pair of His charming, honeyed lotus feet.

**28.** Now, whirling gracefully all around again and again, He embraces him once more; now, illuminating [all] with the charming lustre of His smile, He sings very softly, sweetly and tenderly.

*Note.* *Sa ca parirabhate … tam* may also be understood as "and he [Vakreshvara] in turn embraces Him" (?).

**29.** Thus, performing in seclusion with Vakreshvara, the moon of the race of the twice-born, a dance full of rasa, the Ocean of rasa then set His lotus foot down all around.

**30.** At that very time, in the garden, the deeply sweet [singer] named Vasudeva, delighting his beloved [Lord] with the rasas of the delight of song, eagerly sang sweet songs in seclusion.

*Note.* Metre — *candravartma* (*ra-na-bha-sa*: —⏑—⏑⏑⏑—⏑⏑⏑⏑—); verses 30–42. Vasudeva is the singer; in verse 32 he is called "the best of physicians" (*bhiṣag-ṛṣabha*), that is, he was of the vaidya caste: Vasudeva Datta, the brother of Mukunda Datta. So too in the Chaitanya-charitamrita, Madhya 14.98–99, where Vasudeva Datta sings in the garden and the Lord dances.

**31.** Alone, in a soft voice, he sang a very sweet, most excellent, honey-sweet song: how then could this [Lord], overflowing with rasa, ever fail to [undergo] that transformation here? Ah, what more [need be said]?

*Note.* The sentence is elliptical: a verb such as "would undergo" is understood with *vikāram* (?). *Vikāra* — the transformation of the body and mind in ecstasy.

**32.** As that best of physicians named Vasudeva, deeply sweet, was sweetly singing here, [Gaura], His body helpless, sweet with the lustre of His body suffused with ecstatic feeling, impetuously broke into dance.

*Note.* *Gāyati* is the locative of the present participle (*gāyat*), in an absolute construction with *bhiṣag-ṛṣabhe … vāsudeve* (*vāsudeva iti* = *vāsudeve iti* by sandhi).

**33.** Glorious is He — with abundant tears, with His exceedingly sweet limbs covered with masses of hairs standing on end, with stupor, perspiration, laughter and the rest, His body unceasingly agitated by the tandava!

*Note.* The fourth pada has thirteen syllables instead of twelve: *tāṇḍavākulita-* gives an extra light syllable; the reading *tāṇḍavākula-tanuḥ* would scan correctly (?). The Sanskrit is given as printed.

**34.** As He whose radiance is sweet danced sweetly, the path of the moon was eclipsed by the radiance of His face, this Meru was conquered by the lustre of His body, and the lotus was indeed put to shame by His lotus feet.

*Note.* Metre: *candravartma* (*ra-na-bha-sa*: —⏑—⏑⏑⏑—⏑⏑⏑⏑—; 12 syllables per pada), as throughout 30–42. The name of the metre is woven into the verse itself (a *mudrā*): its very first words, *candra-vartma*, "the path of the moon" (that is, the sky), are both the image — the radiance of His face eclipses the moon's path — and the name of the metre in which the verse is written. *Apaghana* — "body, limb".

**35.** Whatever that [singer] sang, filled with great rasa — even whatever in it was not very graceful — He, [hearing it] from His own beloved, perceived as doubly imbued with feeling.

**36.** And He, displaying all at once on the surface of His beautiful body [the signs] filled with the eight feelings, danced impetuously, His body helpless, creating much sweetness for the one who sang.

*Note.* The eight feelings are the *sāttvika-bhāvas*: stupor, perspiration, standing of the hairs on end, faltering of the voice, trembling, change of colour, tears and fainting.

**37.** Thus in the garden the most sweet Moon from the ocean of Sri Shachi's womb, His body quivering with the rasa of the lovely tandava, spread out on every side the fullness of [His] charm.

*Note.* The moon was born from the ocean at its churning; Gaura is the moon born from the "ocean" of Shachi's womb.

**38.** Whoever beholds Him — his heart at that very moment is as if drunk up in one sip from the hollow of the hand; but his eye, forgetting to blink, clings firmly now here, now there [on His body].

*Note.* *Culukī-kṛ* — "to make into a palmful (*culuka*)", that is, to drink up without remainder (cf. Agastya, who drank up the ocean).

**39.** Thus the Lord, having created together with Vasudeva the most graceful rasa of dance, and sporting lightly all around, now here, now there, reached the bank of the pond.

**40.** With masses of lovely spray the pond served the Lord, who was fanned by the wind — [a wind] that, with a covering of pollen from the blossoming lotuses, made the swarm of bees white in lustre.

*Note.* The subject of *abhajat* is *dīrghikā*, "the pond" (nominative): the pond served with its spray the Lord (*prabhum*, accusative), who was fanned (*paridhutam*) by the wind. In the IAST line *dīrghikā* is joined by a hyphen to *rucira-śīkara-nikaraiḥ*, but it must be taken as a separate word (the Devanagari does not divide the words). The Gosvami edition (GGM) has the same text. Metre: in the first pada the tenth syllable (*-lī-* of *paṭalīkayā*) is heavy where the metre requires a light one (*paṭalikayā* would be regular).

**41.** There, on the cool bank, made most sweet by the widely spreading shade, He whose face is sweet at once took His rest: whom does a most exquisitely charming thing not delight?

*Note.* The last pada is an *arthāntara-nyāsa* (a general statement in support of a particular one). Metre: in the fourth pada the sixth syllable (*vas-* of *vastv*) is heavy where the metre requires a light one (?).

**42.** When the Most Merciful had comfortably sat down, some [devotees], their bodies deeply immersed in an ocean of good fortune, then gathered together, eager to behold His lotus feet.

*Note.* A rhyming play on *kāruṇikatare / atha katare* (*antya-yamaka*).

**43.** Among them a certain great soul, most fortunate, who had taken refuge at the lotus feet of Sriman Nityananda — "dasa" [whose name] begins with "Krishna", charming on earth — rejoiced on seeing Sri Gauranga there.

*Note.* Metre: *mattamayūra* (*ma-ta-ya-sa-ga*: —————⏑⏑——⏑⏑——; 13 syllables per pada). From 43 onwards almost every verse is written in a metre of its own. The third pada has only twelve syllables (a light syllable is missing before *ramyaḥ*), and the fourth has fourteen (one heavy syllable too many in the opening: without *taṁ* the pada would be regular). The name Krishnadasa is split up for the sake of the metre. In 44 he is called "the best of brahmanas" (*kṣiti-sura-vara*). Which Krishnadasa is meant is unclear: the episode is not in the Chaitanya-charitamrita, and there are several Krishnadasas among the associates.

**44.** Then, seeing here that sweet moon of the face of the moon Gaura, wet with the water of perspiration from the abundance of His impetuous dance, the best of brahmanas conceived in his heart the desire to bathe Him.

*Note.* Metre: ⏑⏑⏑⏑⏑⏑⏑⏑—⏑⏑—— (*na-na-sa-sa-ga*, 13 syllables per pada); all four padas are regular (in the second, with the hiatus *vara iha*).

**45.** That great soul, having taken a pot from somewhere, his body bathed in the water of his eyes and resplendent with rows of hairs standing on end, then quickly and eagerly poured a mass of water from the pond over the Lord's head.

*Note.* Metre: *kalahaṁsa* (*sa-ja-sa-sa-ga*: ⏑⏑—⏑—⏑⏑⏑—⏑⏑——; 13 syllables per pada). In the third pada *mūrdhani* gives fourteen syllables; the metre requires the by-form *mūrdhni* (cf. 16.34).

**46.** Thus, quickly bringing water, he then poured over [the Lord] water brought in a hundred pitchers. At that moment glorious Advaita arrived and shone before the Lord's face.

*Note.* Metre: *bhramara-vilasita* (*ma-bha-na-la-ga*: ————⏑⏑⏑⏑⏑⏑—; 11 syllables per pada).

**47.** Seeing Him [Advaita], the merciful moon Gaura, whose form is enchanting, took His hand with the petal of His own hand and then led Him to His side to enjoy the bath.

*Note.* Metre: *dodhaka* (*bha-bha-bha-ga-ga*: —⏑⏑—⏑⏑—⏑⏑——; 11 syllables per pada).

**48.** Advaita sat down for the bath beside Sri Gaurachandra, and He too in the same way poured the clearest water over Gaurachandra again and again.

*Note.* Metre: *śālinī* (*ma-ta-ta-ga-ga*: —————⏑——⏑——; 11 syllables per pada), 48–49.

**49.** Again and again, out of intense longing, the brahmana poured those very cool waters [over Gaura] — and there He Himself was bathed by the waters of [His own] eyes. A wonder, a wonder is the power of Gaurachandra!

*Note.* The edition has *suśītai- / ratyotkaṇṭhāt*: one may read *suśītair atyutkaṇṭhāt*, "out of intense longing", or *suśītai raty-utkaṇṭhāt*, "out of the longing of love"; metrically both readings are regular. The "brahmana" is Advaita. The Gosvami edition (GGM) has the same text.

**50.** Then Govinda, the great soul, full of bliss, came before Him holding a freshly washed garment; and then He took the cloth together with the waist-cord.

*Note.* Metre: *upajāti* (*upendravajrā* in the first and third padas, *indravajrā* in the second and fourth). The edition has समात्तोद्गमनाय- (*-udgamanāya-*) and आनन्दमयौ — misprints (the metre does not decide between *-udgamanāya-* and *-udgamanīya-*; the correction is made by sense). *Udgamanīya* — "a pair of washed garments".

**51–52.** Having thus taken the garment, the Lord then partook to His heart's content of the mahaprasada here and there together with His own people, with charming laughter and jests; and then, eager to behold the charm of the garden, feeling delight in His heart at every creeper and every tree, He shone, His every action supremely charming.

*Note.* Metre: *rathoddhatā* (*ra-na-ra-la-ga*: —⏑—⏑⏑⏑—⏑—⏑—; 11 syllables per pada).

**53.** Coming once more close to the chariot there and seeing the Lord of the world, charming with no small play, out of joy He approaches from behind together with His own people and pushes the chariot — victorious is He of supreme radiance!

*Note.* Metre: *vasanta-tilakā* (——⏑—⏑⏑⏑—⏑⏑—⏑——; 14 syllables per pada). *Kṣipyan* is an irregular form (one would expect *kṣipan*), but it is the one the metre requires (the first syllable must be heavy). Cf. Chaitanya-charitamrita, Madhya 14.48–55: the chariot would not move, however it was pulled, and the Lord pushed it from behind with His head.

**54.** Now, clapping with His pair of lotus hands, "Glory, glory, glory, glory, glory, glory — oh!" — thus He sang ceaselessly in sweet words, quite softly, on the ground around the chariot.

*Note.* Metre: *śaśikalā* (fourteen light syllables and one heavy: ⏑⏑⏑⏑⏑⏑⏑⏑⏑⏑⏑⏑⏑⏑—; 15 syllables per pada). The first pada has only fourteen syllables (the compound *-kalita-dhvani* runs on into the second pada): one syllable is missing (?). *Ati-rahasi* — literally "in great privacy"; in a crowded procession this probably means "to Himself, under His breath" (?). The Gosvami edition (GGM) has the same text.

**55.** Seizing the ropes of the chariot again and again with His lotus hands, Sri Gauranga, whose eyes were long like lotuses, shone, His body bending this way and that from the excess of joy.

*Note.* Metre: —————⏑⏑—— (*ma-ta-ya*, 9 syllables per pada); the name of the metre is not given. All four padas are regular.

**56.** His body resplendent with rows of hairs standing on end through jubilation and the excess of joy, while His own people sang the song of His very own pastimes, His beautiful radiance like the lustre of a flashing garland of lightning, Sri Gauranga shone, seizing those ropes again and again.

*Note.* Metre: *līlākhela* (fifteen heavy syllables per pada: two padas of *vidyunmālā*, eight heavy syllables each, less one syllable). Here too the name of a metre is woven into the verse: *vidyun-mālā*, "a garland of lightning" (third pada), is at once the image of His radiance and the name of the metre (*vidyunmālā*) from which this one is built. All four padas are regular (the final syllable of the second and third padas is free). His companions sing of His own pastimes.

**57.** To whom in this world does it not give joy — this beautiful foremost of chariots of the Moon on the beautiful crown of the Blue Mountain, whose banner, fastened to the pot-shaped finial on its lofty summit, kisses the disc of the sun?

*Note.* Metre: *līlā* (———⏑⏑—————⏑⏑——; 14 syllables per pada), 57–62. The edition has चुम्बवद् (*cumbavad*) — a syllable too many; the metre requires *cumbad-* (*cumbat*, "kissing").

**58.** Thus on the road, gazing and gazing, the most merciful Gauranga — for whom any playful movement is already a pastime, and whose beautiful form was unrestrained in the dance — drank with His eyes the beautiful procession of the chariot, wondrous in appearance to the three worlds.

*Note.* The second pada is shorter than the others (twelve syllables instead of fourteen): *mātra-vilāso…* scans as the third to fourteenth syllables of the pada, so that the two heavy syllables of the opening are lacking — probably an omission in the edition (?). The Gosvami edition (GGM) has the same text.

**59.** When the sun, having again and again dispelled the darkness lying over the three worlds, goes away to rest to the line of forests on the Mountain of Sunset, the foremost chariot of the Moon of the beautiful Blue Mountain, I suppose, came to a halt there: the people had no [more] zeal [to go on].

*Note.* Metre: *līlā*, as in 57–58. The edition has *-sthita-miśraṁ*, which makes no sense; the Gosvami edition (GGM) reads *trailokya-stha-tamisraṁ*, "the darkness lying over the three worlds", and so we read. *Tarke*, "I suppose", is also in the Gosvami edition. *Upaiti* stands where a locative of the participle would be expected (in agreement with *arke*). *Notsāho manujānām* is literally "[there was] no zeal of the people"; it may also be understood as "[but] the people's zeal did not [abate]" (?).

**60.** Having come to the vicinity of [His] abode and casting the people there into an ocean of happiness, in the evening the Moon of the beautiful Blue Mountain, advancing right along the road on cushions that were moved along from all sides, and held back by playful steps, made His entrance there.

*Note.* Evidently the *pahaṇḍi* is described: Jagannatha is carried, swaying, over cotton cushions (*kaśipu*) laid down before Him. The edition has *ānaya*; by sense we read *ālaya-* (?). *Kṣipyan* — the same irregular form as in 53. *Pāda-vihārai ruddhaḥ* — *-vihāraiḥ* with the visarga dropped before *r*. *Sañcāryaiḥ* (plural) does not agree with *kaśipunā* (singular); *kaśipu* is evidently meant collectively. Metre: in the third pada the thirteenth syllable (*-pu-* of *kaśipunā*) is light where the metre requires a heavy one. The Gosvami edition (GGM) has the same text.

**61.** Having entered the temple and taken up residence in His own place, the merciful, beautiful Moon of the Dark Mountain — charming with manifold graces, for whom every action is play — accepted there the offerings rich in abundant flavours, and shone.

*Note.* The temple is, by the sense, the Gundicha, where the chariot arrives. The Dark Mountain (*asita-śaila*) is Nilachala.

**62.** Then, as night drew near, the lotus-eyed moon Gaura came again together with Govinda and, seeing the moon of His face, shone with boundless actions, tears flowing from His eyes and His body adorned with hairs standing on end.

*Note.* *Niśāyā ante* may also mean "at the end of the night", that is, the next morning (?); we follow the sense of the preceding verses (the evening of the same day).

**63.** Thus, having seen and tasted the rasa of the Gundicha festival and spent almost the whole day in kirtana and dancing, Sri Sri Shachinandana — full of great jubilation, most sweet, captivating with the excess of His joy — stole the hearts of all, enchanted by supreme bliss.

*Note.* Metre: *śārdūla-vikrīḍita* (———⏑⏑—⏑—⏑⏑⏑———⏑——⏑—; 19 syllables per pada), 63–66.

**64.** As soon as they saw the Lord of the world — with the fine grace of His attire, resplendent with a mass of such and such excellent ornaments; with the hundred curves of His whole body, resplendent with heaps of such and such excellent garlands; full of the beauty of a festival of bliss spreading forth from such and such excellent splendour — the crowds of people at once fixed their hearts on Him alone.

*Note.* *Tat-tādṛk*, "those and such", repeated in three padas, means "those [well-known] and so [indescribable]".

**65.** If a person with effort directs his gaze intently on the Jewel of the Blue Mountain, then his heart here, on the son of Shachi, becomes as though painted in a picture; and if he fixes the pair of his eyes on Him Himself — wondrous is this behaviour! — then suddenly, without cause, a bewildering stupor arises again and again.

*Note.* The edition has मदाति (*madāti*), which makes no sense; by sense we read *dadāti*, as does the Gosvami edition (GGM).

**66.** Thus every year He sports there joyfully during the Ratha-yatra: together with all His own people He ardently glorifies [His] qualities, beholding Himself. By whom indeed can the tale of the wonders of all those pastimes of dance be sung? Even Brahma and the others have not, have not at all, such ability.

*Note.* *Svam avekṣya*, "beholding Himself": in Jagannatha Gaura beholds His own self, Krishna. *Pratyabdam*, "every year": the Lord came to the Ratha-yatra every year of the eighteen years of His life in Puri. In the edition the third pada has only eighteen syllables (*tal-lāsya-vilāsa*), and in the fourth *śakti-kathā* breaks the metre of *śārdūla-vikrīḍita*. The Gosvami edition (GGM) reads *tat-tal-lāsya-…* and *śaktis tathā tādṛśī*, which restores the metre in both padas; this text is adopted.

**Colophon:** इति श्रीकृष्णचैतन्यचरितामृते महाकाव्ये श्रीपुरुषोत्तमविहारवर्णनमुपवनविलासश्च सप्तदशः सर्गः ॥ (*iti śrī-kṛṣṇa-caitanya-caritāmṛte mahākāvye śrī-puruṣottama-vihāra-varṇanam upavana-vilāsaś ca saptadaśaḥ sargaḥ*) — "Thus [ends] the seventeenth sarga, 'The Description of the Pastimes in Sri Purushottama, and the Pastimes in the Garden', in the mahakavya 'Sri Krishna-Chaitanya-charitamrita'."

---

## Sarga Eighteen — verses 1–63

**Title of the sarga:** *Śrī-puruṣottama-vihāra-ratha-yātrā-samāptiḥ*, "The Pastimes in Sri Purushottama and the Conclusion of the Ratha-yatra" (from the colophon).
**Metre:**
- 1–62 — *viyoginī* (an *ardha-sama* metre: odd padas *sa-sa-ja-ga*, ⏑⏑—⏑⏑—⏑—⏑—, 10 syllables; even padas *sa-bha-ra-la-ga*, ⏑⏑——⏑⏑—⏑—⏑—, 11 syllables); in the edition each printed line is a half-verse. In 35 the printed fourth pada has twelve syllables (*satato 'dhikaṇṭhita-*; *satatotkaṇṭhita-* scans); in 40 the first pada has nine syllables (*na ca nirvṛte vilokya taṁ* gives ⏑⏑—⏑—⏑—⏑—: a light syllable is missing in the fourth or fifth place); in 61 the fourth pada has twelve syllables (*vyacakiraj*; the imperfect *vyakirat*, "scattered", scans).
- 63 — *śārdūla-vikrīḍita* (*ma-sa-ja-sa-ta-ta-ga*: ———⏑⏑—⏑—⏑⏑⏑———⏑——⏑—; 19 syllables per pada). The printed Devanagari has *alaṅkṛtimati*, with a light syllable in the twelfth place of the fourth pada, where a heavy one is required; the transliteration gives the metrical *alaṅkṛtimatīṁ*.

**Total verses:** 63.

**Contents of the passage:** the end of the Ratha-yatra and the yearly round of the Lord's life in Puri.
- 1–5 — eight days in the garden by the Gundicha; the longing for Vrindavana assuaged.
- 6 — the return procession of Jagannatha.
- 7–15 — the water pastimes in Narendra Lake:
  - the devotees around the Lord form a "lotus" (11);
  - the Lord dozes, lying upon Advaita (13–14).
- 16 — Jagannatha returns to the temple.
- 17–45 — the daily routine:
  - rising in the last watch of the night, bathing, the red cloth;
  - darshan from the first service until the *dhupa*, with Govinda and Svarupa Damodara; tears and unblinking eyes;
  - Advaita and the Lord worship each other (30–32); Srivasa, "Narada" (33);
  - visitors received when Govinda announces them; the midday rites and the meal.
- 46–51 — the twelve yearly festivals; the festival of Makara: the Lord in the guise of a cowherd with a carrying-pole, twirling a staff (48–50).
- 52–59 — the Dola-yatra: coloured powders; Govinda, the festival Deity, is swung on the swing, and the Lord dances.
- 60–62 — every year at the Ratha-yatra He dances before the chariot of Baladeva, "the support of the world"; twenty years.
- 63 — during this time He goes to the bank of the Bhagirathi in order to go to Mathura (the transition to Sarga Nineteen).

**1.** Then, having sported abundantly there at the Chariot Festival with His own people, the Lord, full of delight, rejoiced again and again in the garden for eight days.

*Note.* Metre — *viyoginī* (odd padas ⏑⏑—⏑⏑—⏑—⏑—, even padas ⏑⏑——⏑⏑—⏑—⏑—); in the edition each printed line is a half-verse. The eight days are the days during which Jagannatha stays in the Gundicha temple; according to the Chaitanya-charitamrita (Madhya 14.104–105) He stayed there nine days, and for all nine days the Lord sported in the large garden by the Gundicha.

**2.** Here, having eagerly performed the rite of bathing in the lovely ponds as is proper, and having beheld the Sun of the Not-white Mountain, He shines here and there.

*Note.* "The Not-white Mountain" is a periphrasis for the Dark Mountain (Nilachala).

**3.** Rejoicing at the root of every tree, by every creeper, in every bower, and freely sporting on every spot adorned with sand, He shines here and there.

**4.** Listening to the playful warbling of the cuckoos, His heart tenderly disposed, and drinking with His very ears the sweet hum of the swarms of bees, He shines.

**5.** Wandering here hither and thither again and again amid all those charms of no small degree, He cast off the torment of His heart born of long separation from Vrindavana.

*Note.* For the Lord the garden by the Gundicha is Vrindavana; cf. the Chaitanya-charitamrita, Madhya 14.73–75, 96: in these days the Lord feels that Krishna has returned to Vrindavana, and with the devotees He manifests the pastimes of Vrindavana in the gardens around the Gundicha temple (Sundarachala, 14.113).

**6.** Then, at the triumphal return of the Lord of the world to the Blue Mountain after His excursion outside [the temple], He, in the same way, with throngs of His retinue, became the source of joy for all the people.

*Note.* Apparently the return procession of the chariots (*bahuda-yatra*). *Paricchada* can also be understood as "paraphernalia" ("He gladdened everyone with various paraphernalia"). The Gosvami edition (GGM) has the same text.

**7.** Then, in the course of the nine days, the Lord sported in the water of the lake called Narendra with His own people and together with the great soul Advaita.

*Note.* Cf. the Chaitanya-charitamrita, Madhya 14.76–91 — the water pastimes (in the Chaitanya-charitamrita, in a lake by the Gundicha, and in Narendra Lake, 14.102).

**8.** Approaching that lake named Narendra, He, the lotus-eyed, affectionate to His own devotees, descended [into it] with delight to allay the summer heat.

*Note.* A play on sound (*yamaka*): *sarasīṁ tāṁ sarasīruhekṣaṇaḥ*.

**9–10.** His lotus feet deep red, His form golden like molten gold, His two eyes reddened with compassion, ever bringing to an end the threefold burning [of suffering] — having thus at once entered the lake, He, His eyes languid like lotuses, celebrating the delight of sporting in the water together with all of them, shone like the moon.

*Note.* A *yugmaka* (so marked in the edition). The edition reads *सारसलालसेक्षणः* (*-lālasa-*, "longing"?), which is unclear; the Gosvami edition (GGM) reads *sārasa-sālasekṣaṇaḥ* ("with languid, lotus-like eyes"), and this reading is adopted (the metre is the same). Note the rhyme *aruṇāruṇa / karuṇāruṇa*. The threefold suffering: that caused by one's own body and mind, by other beings, and by the forces of nature.

**11.** Some stood [around Him] like clusters of petals, others like clusters of fresh filaments, and Gaurachandra Himself, in the form of the seed-pod, shone [with them] like a lotus.

*Note.* *Varāṭaka* is the seed-pod (*karṇikā*) in the centre of a lotus. The edition prints *sthitā(ḥ)*, the visarga supplied in brackets; the plural *sthitāḥ* is required.

**12.** Some sprinkled His tender lotus feet with water from their hands; others here drank the nectar of His beauty through the openings of their lotus eyes.

**13.** But He, the moon-faced Treasure-house of Mercy, creating the abundant delight of His pastimes, playfully and comfortably rested upon the body of Advaita, who lay [on the water], and shone.

*Note.* Cf. the Chaitanya-charitamrita, Madhya 14.88–90: Advaita lies on the water like Shesha, and the Lord reclines upon Him (the pastime of the Lord lying upon Shesha).

**14.** Then, having laid Lord Advaita down beneath [Him] in the water, the Treasure-house of Mercy, Himself languid, lay down upon Him and fell into a doze.

*Note.* Advaita is called *prabhu*, the usual title of Advaita Prabhu.

**15.** Having thus played abundantly right here, the Lord, full of delight, came up onto the bank, wearing His cloth, beautiful with trickling drops of water.

**16.** Then the Moon of the Blue King of Mountains in the same way made His triumphal procession inward [into the temple], and the son of Shachi, likewise beholding it, was filled with joy again and again.

*Note.* The edition has भ्रमदं (*bhramadam*); we read *pramadam* ("joy"), which gives the same metre (?). The Gosvami edition (GGM) has the same text. "The procession inward" is Jagannatha's return to the main temple.

**17.** Intent on seeing the first service of the Lord of the world, the son of Shachi, the Treasure-house of Mercy, would rise from His bed in the last watch of the night.

*Note.* From here begins the Lord's daily routine in Puri. *Avasara* is a temple term: one of the successive periods of service to Jagannatha; a *prahara* (watch) is about three hours.

**18.** Having thus risen from bed with the desire to see the All-pervading Lord, the Lord, the son of Shachi, would perform His various pure daily duties according to the rules.

**19.** Having bathed in clear, purified water, Mahaprabhu would at once put on a fine red cloth together with a waist-cord.

**20.** With the grace of an intoxicated king of elephants, making a festival of chanting His own name, His body clad in crimson cloth, He would go out most joyfully.

*Note.* "His own name" is the name of Krishna, which is His own.

**21.** Then the wise Govinda, taking the water-pot in his hand, and that renunciant named Damodara, who always remained in the Lord's company, [accompanied Him].

*Note.* The *karaka* is a sannyasi's water-pot (kamandalu). Damodara is Svarupa Damodara.

**22.** Then He, whose heart's desire shone in constantly tasting the nectar-ocean of His own name, approaching the abode of the Lord [Jagannatha] and offering obeisance, would enter [it].

*Note.* Verses 22–25 could also be taken as referring to Damodara ("the renunciant Damodara, tasting the nectar of Hari's name, entered…"). But in verse 26 the same actions are explicitly ascribed to the son of Shachi, and we take the Lord as the subject. "The abode of the Lord" is the temple of Jagannatha.

**23.** Gazing to the full at the moon of the face of the Moon on the crown of the Dark Mountain, He shines, the creeper of His body washed by the stream of tears flowing from His eyes.

*Note.* The edition has the nominative *-vadanenduḥ*; with *parilokya* the accusative *-induṁ* is needed (the metre is the same). The Gosvami edition (GGM) has the same text.

**24.** Is it not that the Moon of the Blue King of Mountains, beholding Him so full of charm and overpowered by excessive joy, ceremonially bathes Him with the waters of His own two eyes?

*Note.* A poetic fancy (*utprekṣā*): Gaura's tears are, as it were, a ceremonial bath (*abhiṣeka*) given Him by Jagannatha Himself.

**25.** Because, while one beholds Him, blinking greatly pains the heart, did the Lord, the Jewel of the Dark Mountain, at once make [Gaura's] eyes unblinking?

*Note.* Another poetic fancy: unblinking eyes are a mark of the gods.

**26.** Again and again bathing His own body with floods of tears, His body swelling to double [its size] with hairs standing on end, the son of Shachi, overcome by joy, rejoiced more and more.

**27.** Remaining there from the first service until the *dhupa*, and making obeisances and circumambulations many times, He then went to His own dwelling.

*Note.* *Dhūpa* ("incense") is the morning offering to Jagannatha, which follows the first service.

**28.** Then, coming to His own dwelling and sitting down, chanting His own names again and again, He shone like the moon, radiant and giving delight to the world.

**29.** Then, to see the Lord, the abode of compassion, who stayed there at ease within the house, [devotees] joyfully came together again and again from all sides, straight away, with their own [people].

**30.** Then Lord Advaita came here first, joyfully, reverently bearing many gifts for the worship of the Lord, overwhelmed, His hairs standing on end and His tears streaming.

*Note.* Cf. the Chaitanya-charitamrita, Madhya 15.9–11: Advaita adorns the Lord with a garland and tulasi flowers, and the Lord worships Advaita with the remaining tulasi, saying, "Whatever You are, I worship You."

**31.** Having with devotion offered most pure and fragrant water to [the Lord's] feet, He [Advaita] then anointed His forehead with an abundance of the paste of [sandalwood] born on Malaya.

**32.** Then the Lord, the Treasure-house of Mercy, [in His turn] especially honoured the master Advaita with charming flowers, and then with an abundance of pure durva grass and unbroken rice.

*Note.* Durva grass and *akṣata* (unbroken rice) are auspicious offerings. *Vililepa*, "anointed", is used here in the broad sense "honoured".

**33.** Then the moon of the race of brahmanas, he who is renowned on earth as Narada, having made a multitude of obeisances, likewise worshipped [the Lord] with the lotuses of his eyes.

*Note.* This is Srivasa Pandita, who is regarded as Narada.

**34.** Then those dear servants of the Lord's feet, tender as fresh shoots, who were [usually] kept back, approached at the [proper] time and eagerly drank His face again and again with their eyes.

*Note.* The sense of *sunivāritāḥ*, "well restrained, kept back", is unclear (?): perhaps those who [otherwise] held themselves back from the Lord. The Gosvami edition (GGM) has the same text.

**35–36.** And many others too, who had come from all sides eager to see the Lord — with the triad of fear, longing and curiosity, the movements of their hearts constantly yearning — having stood just outside for a long time with an abundance of happiness, saw in turn the very great abode of mercy, though in great timidity, after Govinda had announced them.

*Note.* A *yugmaka* (so marked in the edition). In 35 the printed *satato 'dhikaṇṭhita-* gives the fourth pada twelve syllables (⏑⏑—⏑—⏑⏑—⏑—⏑—) instead of eleven; the reading *satatotkaṇṭhita-*, given in brackets in the IAST, scans correctly (⏑⏑——⏑⏑—⏑—⏑—) and is adopted. Govinda is the Lord's door-keeper and personal servant.

**37.** Thus for two watches those renowned [devotees], with an abundant hundred feelings, saw the joyful Lord, charming like an excellent wish-fulfilling tree.

*Note.* Two watches (*praharas*) — about six hours. *Prathitāḥ* may also be taken as "unfolded, displayed": "their abundant hundred feelings unfolding". The edition has *dadṛśu* without the visarga (hence ददृशु(ः)).

**38.** Some with smiles, others likewise with a word, and others again with some [indescribable] mercy — the Lord at once made [all these] eager ones obtain the desire [for which they longed].

*Note.* The edition has कयाच नेतरान्, a wrong division of *kayācanetarān* (= *kayācana itarān*).

**39.** And whatever desires of whatever kind anyone here held in his heart — all of them He Himself, delighted, thus made fruitful.

**40.** Having seen Him, the eyes were not satisfied, nor were the eyes free from the flowing of tears; then by every person near Him tears were shed that reached down to the tips of the toes.

*Note.* The syntax is obscure (?): *nirvṛte* and *dṛṣṭī* are feminine duals ("the two eyes … satisfied"), but *rahitāśru-vāhitā* is singular and does not agree with *dṛṣṭī*; it is understood loosely, as an abstract "the state of being devoid of the flow of tears". The Gosvami edition (GGM) has the same text. Metre: the first pada has only nine syllables (*na ca nirvṛte vilokya taṁ*: ⏑⏑—⏑—⏑—⏑—), where *viyoginī* requires ten (⏑⏑—⏑⏑—⏑—⏑—): a light syllable is missing within *nirvṛte*.

**41.** Having given joy to all embodied beings by His glances, words and the like, He, His face sweet, then joyfully performed the pure midday duties.

**42.** Having bathed in pure water, having put on a kaupina and a fine outer cloth, adorned with sandal paste, the Lord shone, repeating His own names.

**43.** Having eaten pure rice, having performed the pure rinsing of the mouth and the other [rites], and having put on another garment, the Lord, pure in lustre, increased in beauty.

*Note.* The edition has *-kriyāḥ* (plural), as in the Devanagari above; as a qualifier of the Lord *-kriyaḥ* is required (so the IAST). The metre does not decide, the syllable standing at the end of the pada.

**44.** Then all of them, greedy for the honey of the Lord's lotus feet, approached again and unceasingly drank in abundance, with the cupped hands of their eyes, the nectar of His beauty.

**45.** And He, beautiful with the moon of His face sweetly smiling with the sweetness of His speech, having conversed with each of them as was fitting, as before, then made them fully joyful.

**46.** Thus, again and again drinking the nectar of His own name and adorning His daily duties, He every year eagerly plunged into an ocean of happiness at every festival.

*Note.* *Śaradi* — "in the year" (*śarad*, "autumn", also means "year"): the festivals listed below (the Dola in spring, the Ratha-yatra in summer) show that the whole year is meant.

**47.** Delighted at the twelve festivals by the festive occasion of beholding many wonders, He, as it were, took into Himself with His two eyes the Ornament on the crown of the Dark Mountain.

*Note.* The twelve *yātrās* are the annual cycle of Jagannatha's festivals. The Ornament on the crown of the Dark Mountain (*asitācala*, that is, Nilachala) is Jagannatha.

**48.** During the Makara festival the Lord, having at His pleasure assumed the appearance of a cowherd, shines, carrying ghee, milk and curd on a carrying-pole on His shoulder.

*Note.* In the Chaitanya-charitamrita (Madhya 15.17–25) the Lord, dressed as a cowherd, carries pots of milk and curd on a pole across His shoulders and whirls a staff at the Nanda-mahotsava (after Janmashtami), not at the Makara festival.

**49.** Glorious here is the One whose beautiful form is golden: at times restless from excessive happiness, again and again delighting in tossing a staff, He bestows joy like the best of cowherds.

**50.** The Lord now tosses it up, now strikes it with His foot, and now whirls it hither and thither beneath His arms, armpits, thighs, knees and lotus feet.

**51.** Thus, by His playful actions providing for people at all the festivals a very dense joy for the eyes, the Lord [Himself] attained no small bliss.

**52.** Now there is one very great and supreme festival of Hari, known as the Dola: [at it] the One whose form is golden dances in front with various playful actions indeed.

*Note.* The Dola-yatra (the swing festival, Holi) falls on the full moon of Phalguna.

**53.** The Lord shone, His form painted with those soft powders — crimson and white, and excellent [powders] of turmeric — and with the dust of [sandalwood] born on Malaya.

*Note.* Coloured powders (*abira*, *gulal*) are the custom of the Dola festival.

**54–55.** When, in a place richly encircled by clusters of areca palms bearing fruit, by banana trees bowed with fruit and by trees whose tops droop under the weight of flowers, [a place] adorned with a fine platform, the Lord upon a resplendent fine couch is swung by the host of His devotees — the moon Gauranga dances.

*Note.* A *yugmaka* (so marked in the edition). The Lord who is swung is the festival Deity, named Govinda in 56.

**56–57.** Then the two Lords, Govinda and the son of Shachi — both with forms lovely as a golden mountain, again and again eager to look at each other, in motion — [Govinda] in swinging, [the son of Shachi] in dancing — by the beauty of the splendour of Their own activities, out of unceasing rasa, with eager hearts created at the Dola festival a dense abundance of happiness for the people.

*Note.* A *yugmaka* (in the edition the mark is garbled). Govinda is the festival Deity, Dola-Govinda, who is swung on the swing. The epithet "lovely as a golden mountain", in the dual, is apparently extended to both by inertia. *Akurvatām* stands for the classical *akurutām* (imperfect, dual); the metre requires the printed form. The Gosvami edition (GGM) has the same text.

**58.** That [festival] which among the other great festivals is always renowned here as the Dola and is proclaimed equal to [the festival] of the chariot shines at the beginning of the month of Madhu.

*Note.* Madhu is Chaitra (March–April); the full moon of Phalguna falls at the beginning of Chaitra when the months are reckoned from full moon to full moon.

**59.** How then can people describe this delight of the Dola festival of the Lord of the world here — [the festival] that Mahaprabhu beholds before Him with full happiness?

*Note.* *Amum* (masculine) may refer either to the festival (*utsava*) or to the Lord of the world: "whom Mahaprabhu beholds before Him".

**60.** And then again, at the time of the Ratha-yatra, Mahaprabhu sports unceasingly in such and such ways with those who have come [from Bengal], with His sankirtana, dancing and so on.

**61.** Thus for twenty years the Lord again and again performed dances before the chariot of Baladeva, and with kirtanas scattered all this over the world.

*Note.* "Twenty years" is Karnapura's figure (so also in 63); according to the Chaitanya-charitamrita (Madhya 1.17–21), after sannyasa the Lord lived twenty-four years: six years of travels and eighteen years in Puri. Metre: the fourth pada has twelve syllables instead of eleven (*-ir idam etad vyacakiraj jagat-tale*: ⏑⏑——⏑⏑⏑—⏑—⏑—): *vyacakirat* has a superfluous light syllable. The regular imperfect of *vi-√kṝ*, *vyakirat* ("scattered"), restores the metre (⏑⏑——⏑⏑—⏑—⏑—) and the grammar.

**62.** He [Baladeva], dwelling within all beings, is glorified as the support of the world; therefore again and again He [the Lord] spread out dance and kirtana before Him.

*Note.* The verse explains the preceding one: why the Lord dances before the chariot of Baladeva in particular. "The support of the world" is an epithet of Baladeva-Sankarshana (Shesha).

**63.** Thus the son of Shachi, having sported in Sri Purushottama and having joyfully performed His play for twenty years, shone in fullness. And within this [period], out of eagerness to journey, He came to the bank of the Bhagirathi and sports [there], in order to adorn Sri Mathura [with His coming].

*Note.* Metre — *śārdūla-vikrīḍita* (———⏑⏑—⏑—⏑⏑⏑———⏑——⏑—; 19 syllables per pada). The edition has *alaṅkṛtimati* (so the Devanagari above); the metre requires a heavy syllable: *alaṅkṛtimatīṁ* (so the IAST). The verse leads into the next sarga: the journey towards Mathura by way of Bengal (cf. Chaitanya-charitamrita, Madhya 16: the Lord longs for Vrindavana and goes by way of Gauda).

**Colophon:** इति श्रीकृष्णचैतन्यचरितामृते महाकाव्ये श्रीपुरुषोत्तमविहाररथयात्रासमाप्तिरष्टादशः सर्गः । (*iti śrī-kṛṣṇa-caitanya-caritāmṛte mahākāvye śrī-puruṣottama-vihāra-ratha-yātrā-samāptir aṣṭādaśaḥ sargaḥ*) — "Thus [ends] the eighteenth sarga, 'The Pastimes in Sri Purushottama and the Conclusion of the Ratha-yatra', in the mahakavya 'Sri Krishna-Chaitanya-charitamrita'."

---

## Sarga Nineteen — verses 1–100

**Title of the sarga:** *Śrī-vṛndāvana-yātrā*, "The Journey to Sri Vrindavana" (from the colophon: *iti śrī-kṛṣṇa-caitanya-caritāmṛte mahākāvye śrī-vṛndāvana-yātrāyām ūnaviṁśaḥ sargaḥ*, "Thus [ends] the nineteenth sarga, [in the account of] the journey to Sri Vrindavana, in the great poem Sri Krishna-Chaitanya-charitamrita").
**Metre:**
- 1–99 — *anuṣṭubh* (*śloka*: 8 syllables per pada; in the even padas the fifth to seventh syllables are ⏑—⏑; in the odd padas, besides the normal ⏑——, the permitted variants (*vipulā*) are used freely). Irregularities of the printed text: in 6 the third pada (*paramānanda-purī ca*) has ⏑⏑— in the fifth to seventh places, which is neither the normal form nor a *vipulā* (a licence with a proper name); in 22 the second and third padas of the edition are each a syllable short, and in 25 the second line has 13 syllables instead of 16; in both the text is restored after the Gosvami edition (GGM); in 37 the second pada has seven syllables (*nānā nūnanū nanu*); in 49 the first pada (*avapayo 'tivimalam*) has both the second and the third syllables light; 70 has six padas; in 81 the fourth pada has nine syllables (*dṛṣṭaye tatra cakāra saḥ*). In 45 and 53 (palindromes) each printed line is a single pada.
- 100 — *mandākrāntā* (*ma-bha-na-ta-ta-ga-ga*: ————⏑⏑⏑⏑⏑——⏑——⏑——; 17 syllables per pada).

**Total verses:** 100.

**Contents of the passage:** the beginning of the journey to Vrindavana (by way of Bengal).
- 1–5 — the decision to go to Mathura by way of the bank of the Ganga:
  - 2–3 — an insertion made by the publishers from another edition (footnote);
  - Ramananda holds the Lord back; the play on "Vijaya-dashami / the tenth state" (5).
- 6–12 — the companions: Govinda, Jagadananda, Damodara Pandita, Paramananda Puri; mahaprasada for the Vaishnavas of Bengal; on the advice of Puri the sanctified cloth received from Prataparudra is to be sent to the Lord's mother (9–12).
- 13–19 — Damodara is late for the first service; the Lord, "the Ocean of love, out of love", strikes him with a little book (18–19).
- 20–34 — the farewell to Jagannatha and to Puri:
  - the Lord sends back those who follow Him, headed by Kashi Mishra (22);
  - Ramananda follows Him in a palanquin (26); the loving dispute between them (31–34).
- 35–51 — mahaprasada from Vaninatha; autumn; the Lord embraces the trees as if they were the trees of Vrindavana (43–48); Bhuvaneshvara.
- 52–80 — a village with a house built by Ramananda; Kataka (Cuttack): the temple of Gopinatha; the brahmana Svapneshvara; a garden with a bakula tree.
- 81–92 — **Prataparudra** comes with an army of four divisions (drawn up in rings, 87–88) and falls at the Lord's feet.
- 93–100 — the king orders Mangaraja and Harichandana to escort the Lord beyond the Mahanadi and to raise memorial pillars; the night's halt at Chaturdvara.

**1.** Then [the Lord], His form like molten gold, His two mighty arms like the trunk of a shining elephant, shone, [setting out] to go to Mathura.

*Note.* The metre of the sarga is *anuṣṭubh* (except where otherwise stated).

**2–3.** Wishing to set out for the bank of the Yamuna, He set His mind [first] on the bank of the Ganga: for it is on her bank that He has descended, and His love for her is very great. No sooner had Mahaprabhu returned from the south than He was already setting off for Mathura — but at this Ramananda holds Him back.

*Note.* The edition's footnote (in Sanskrit): "In this book two verses were illegible because of a printing defect; therefore, after consulting another book, they were typeset by computer." Verses 2–3 are enclosed in braces; that is, we have a later reprint with an insertion. *Bādhate* means rather "holds back"; cf. the Chaitanya-charitamrita, Madhya 16.4–8, 85–92: for years Sarvabhauma and Ramananda dissuade the Lord from going to Vrindavana, until He reproaches them.

**4.** Once, at the end of the Chaturmasya, when the Master was ready to depart, Sri Ramananda Raya said with great sorrow:

**5.** "On Vijaya-dashami the Lord's departure will take place — but I shall be in the tenth, all-conquering state before [that]."

*Note.* A play on words (*yamaka*: the first half of each line is the same), explained in the edition's Sanskrit commentary: *vijayā daśamī* is the festival of Vijaya-dashami (in autumn); in the second line *vijayā* is "she who conquers especially" (*viśeṣeṇa jayatīti*), and *daśamī daśā* is the tenth of the ten states of one in love, that is, the end of life. The commentary gives the list: "attraction of the eyes, attachment of the mind, constant thought, sleeplessness, emaciation, aversion to [other] objects, loss of shame, madness, swooning, dying — these are the states in order." The sense: "The Lord will depart on the day of Vijaya, but I shall die even before."

**6.** Govinda, Jagadananda, Sri Damodara Pandita and Paramananda Puri went together in His company.

*Note.* In the third pada (*paramānanda-purī ca*: ⏑⏑——⏑⏑—⏑) the fifth to seventh syllables are ⏑⏑—, which is neither the normal form of the odd pada of the *anuṣṭubh* (⏑——) nor one of its permitted variants (*vipulā*); a licence with a proper name.

**7.** Then the Lord ordered that mahaprasada of various kinds be taken along, to distribute it to the Vaishnavas on arriving at the bank of the Ganga.

**8.** At the Master's order they joyfully took much abundant mahaprasada — cords, sandalwood paste and so on.

*Note.* *Ḍora* — the sanctified silk cords of Jagannatha (*paṭṭa-ḍorī*).

**9.** [Wishing to give] His mother, of His own accord, a sanctified garment, Mahaprabhu adopted the excellent counsel put forward by Paramananda Puri.

*Note.* The counsel itself is given in 12.

**10.** "This excellent cloth, the sanctified [remnant] of Sriman Jagannatha, extremely rare, was given to Me by Prataparudra.

**11.** To whom shall I give it? You ought to tell Me this here, for I am now indeed in doubt."

**12.** Thus addressed, Puri Svami then said to Mahaprabhu: "This should be given to [Your] mother — that is my firm opinion."

*Note.* Puri Svami is Paramananda Puri (cf. 9).

**13.** On the day before [the departure] this Lord — full of the charm of delight in rasa, whose glance cuts away delusion, who gives joy to the heart and the eyes — said:

*Note.* A chain of repetitions at the junctions (*rasakau / rasa-kautuka*, *vibhramaḥ / vibhrama-*, *dṛṣṭiḥ / hṛd-dṛṣṭi-*). *Asakau* is a by-form of *asau* with the suffix *-ak-*, chosen here for the sound-play.

**14.** "I shall go to see Jagannatha, singing all the way, and this Damodara will certainly be [there] with Me, singing."

*Note.* In verses 6 and 24 the Lord's companion in this episode is expressly called Damodara Pandita, so presumably it is he here too, although the one who usually sings for the Lord is Svarupa Damodara. The Gosvami edition (GGM) has the same text.

**15.** Having spoken thus, at the end of the night the Lord, going along the road to the first service (*avasara*) of the All-pervading Lord [Jagannatha], shone with the raptures of His own kirtana.

**16.** As fate would have it, that Damodara had not joined [them] then, and the Lord Himself stood for a while at the Lion Gate, waiting for him.

**17.** When [the Lord] was in the state of being overcome by the all-pervading state [of separation] arising from the absence [of Damodara], the birth [of Damodara] shone forth; when the Master was in such a state, [his] glory arose on earth.

*Note.* *Dvyakṣara*, a "two-consonant" verse: the whole verse uses only the consonants *bh* and *v* (a kind of *citra-kāvya*). The translation follows the edition's Sanskrit commentary: "*bhāva* is existence; its *abhāva* is the absence of Sri Damodara; *abhi-bhāva* is the state of separation on all sides; *abhibhava* is being overcome by it; in this state *bhavaḥ*, the birth [of Damodara], shone forth, and on earth there was *vaibhavam*, honour. The sense: he in separation from whom the Lord becomes agitated — his birth is fruitful, and honour is his." The commentary adds: "From here on there follows one verse each on one, two, three and more consonants."

**18.** Then, after a slight delay, that brahmana joined [them]; the Lord, His heart absorbed [in feeling], became angry on seeing him.

*Note.* The Lord's "anger" (*kupita*) here is a play of love with His eternal associate, not a worldly emotion: the Lord is not subject to anger, and Karnapura himself names the cause in verse 19 — "out of love" (*praṇayāt*).

**19.** And He, the Ocean of love, out of love struck him many times on the back with a little book of songs and pummelled him with kicks.

*Note.* *Gītā-pustikā* is either "a little book of the Gita" or "a little book of songs". There is no such episode in the Chaitanya-charitamrita. The Gosvami edition (GGM) has the same text.

**20.** Having thus entered the temple, seen Sri Purushottama, bowed down and offered praise [to Him], the Treasure-house of Mercy resolved to set out.

**21.** Some, their hearts absorbed in longing, sang kirtana; others, their hearts absorbed in longing, danced.

*Note.* *Gomūtrikā-bandha*, "the pattern of a cow's urine trail": the verse is written out in two lines, syllable by syllable, and read in a zigzag, alternating between the upper and the lower syllable (the crosses in the edition mark the turns of the zigzag). Here the two lines differ only in their first syllable (*kī-* / *na-*), so the zigzag reading yields the same text. The explanation follows the edition's Sanskrit commentary. The edition prints *samūt* for *samut*.

**22.** All, headed by Kashi Mishra, came along behind [Him]; the Treasure-house of Mercy sent back all those who were following [Him].

*Note.* In the edition the second and third padas are each one syllable short; the Gosvami edition (GGM) reads *paścāt paścāt samāyayuḥ / samanuvrajatas tāṁs tān*, and this reading is adopted.

**23.** At the end of the night Sri Gaurasundara, stirred by the art of singing, set out [on the journey] with those who were singing kirtana again and again.

**24.** Govinda, Jagadananda, Sri Damodara Pandita and Puri Svami, the best of ascetics, went along [with Him], singing kirtana.

**25.** [The Lord], whose pastime [of going to Vraja] shone forth, who had cast aside the pastime [of living at Nilachala], restless, eager, wishing to send [everyone] away, was unsettled in His play; [and the devotees], like bees at their sport, made a series of efforts to hold back the restless One.

*Note.* *Ekākṣara*, a "one-consonant" verse: the whole verse uses only the consonant *l* (*ḍ* and *l* being treated as identical). The sense follows the edition's Sanskrit commentary: as bees do not abandon a flower swaying in the wind but try to settle on it, so Govinda, Damodara and the others did not leave the departing Shachinandana but tried to hold Him back. *Laluḥ* is the perfect of *lā*, "to take, to hold". The second *līlālīm* is taken as "a series of [efforts]" (the commentary's *ceṣṭā-ālīm*, "a series of actions") (?). In the edition the second line has 13 syllables instead of 16; the Gosvami edition (GGM) reads *līlālolo 'li-līlālīṁ līlālīṁ lolalāṁ laluḥ*, and the repeated *līlālīṁ* is restored accordingly.

**26.** After that Sri Ramananda Raya [came], having mounted a palanquin; and his people, and all the others who had assembled, came as well.

*Note.* *Te ta āyayuḥ* stands for *te te āyayuḥ* (by sandhi the final *e* before a vowel becomes *a*).

**27.** Having heard [of this], all the people there, women and men, came from all sides in great numbers, saying with longing, "Say 'Hari'!"

*Note.* *Hariṁ vada* is a Sanskrit rendering of the Bengali "Hari bol!".

**28.** Then, when the sun had risen, He whose effulgence equals that of millions of suns performed His morning duties together with those devotees of His own.

**29.** At the beginning of the journey He, the protector [even] of the protectors of the humble, could not but set out — [and He set out] giving pure joy to the servants of His feet, His face [promising] an auspicious return.

*Note.* *Muraja-bandha*: a verse whose syllables, written out in a grid, form the pattern of a *muraja* drum. The hardest part is analysed by the edition's Sanskrit commentary: *natatrātrāḥ na na ābabau*; *aṅghri-janāḥ* — "the servants of the feet" (a *karmadhāraya* with the middle member elided); *na ābabau na* — "it is not that He did not go: He went indeed". The sense: at the very start of His departure He gave the devotees joy, strengthened their hope of His return, and went quickly. The edition has *natatrātrā* without the visarga.

**30.** At dawn, seeing the mark of the blow on Damodara's back, Puri Prabhu covered it with cloth.

*Note.* Puri Prabhu is presumably Paramananda Puri (cf. 12, 24).

**31.** Having then gone some distance, Mahaprabhu stopped, engaging with Sri Ramananda Raya in a dispute of love with each other.

**32.** Left behind by the departing Lord, he, though consoled with many conciliatory words, found no consolation even for a moment.

*Note.* The edition has *tyaktā*; read *tyaktaḥ* (the metre is the same). The Devanagari also has प्रभुना (*prabhunā*) for *prabhuṇā*.

**33.** He whose gaze was fixed on the Charming One was agitated by His wandering, full of captivating grace; [yet] he, whose gaze was intimate, was evidently not bewildered by that wandering which causes delusion [even] in those who know.

*Note.* The two lines sound identical (*yamaka*) and differ only in the division into words. The subject is Ramananda (continuing from verse 32). The commentary: "Ramananda, following the Lord, did not cause the wise sorrow."

**34.** And Ramananda, a great treasure-house [of love] — how will he [live] in sorrow at not seeing this object of his love?

*Note.* A variant reading given in the edition: *kariṣyati*, "how will he act".

**35.** Then an abundance of mahaprasada of four kinds, sent by Vaninatha, at once arrived there in great quantity.

*Note.* The four kinds of food are those that are chewed, swallowed, licked and drunk. Vaninatha (Pattanayaka) is the brother of Ramananda Raya.

**36.** Seeing the splendid, unsurpassed mahaprasada that had just arrived from Nilachala, the supreme Lord rejoiced.

**37.** Oh! A certain man among many, seeing the manifold [gifts of prasada], equal to the Lord, considered them abundant, though they were quite small: neither as to their variety and quantity, nor on the ground that they fell short of the nectar of His lips, did he say, "[It is] little."

*Note.* Again an *ekākṣara*: the whole verse uses only the consonant *n* (*ṇ* being treated as equal to *n*). The sense follows the edition's Sanskrit commentary: the Lord's prasada is equal to the Lord Himself, and so even a small quantity of it was honoured by all as abundant. The commentary quotes the Brihad-bhagavatamrita: "What is offered to the Lord of the world — food, drink and the rest — is unchanging like Brahman: as Vishnu is, so is it." *Ina* — "lord, master"; *inena tulyaḥ inaḥ* — "[the prasada,] equal to the Lord". The translation is tentative (?). Metre: the second pada (*nānā nūnanū nanu*) has only seven syllables (?). The Gosvami edition (GGM) has the same text.

**The edition's word division and prose order:** *nanu nānānā nuni ā-aṇūn nānā-inenān anaṇūn anu | nānānūne na ānana-anna-ūne no nānā nut na nanu.*

**38.** Having partaken of the mahaprasada there and rested for a moment, the Treasure-house of Mercy joyfully set out again together with them.

**39.** Whenever He reached some place and stopped there, everyone came from all around, eager to see Him — that was a wonder indeed!

**40.** As soon as He stopped, the local people [living] all around were drawn, together with the wind, by the fragrances of His steadfastness and kindliness.

**41.** The wind, swaying the lotuses, desiring the female companions of the bees, restless in their play, and taking on the play proper to those who cling to their companions, moved along, well pleased (?).

*Note.* A *dvyakṣara*: the verse uses only two consonants, *l* and *n*. The commentary: "*anilaḥ* — the wind; *nalina-lālanaiḥ* — by swaying the lotuses: as a husband beckons his playful wife with his hand, so the wind, desiring the bees' companions, moved, swaying the lotuses, [its] hands." The commentary explains the verb *nalāla* as *cacāla*, "moved", by the rule that *ḍa* and *la* are identical (from the root *laḍ*); the passage is obscure (?). The conjecture *calāla* recorded in the IAST line would break the two-consonant scheme. Metre: both lines are regular (the first pada is a *na-vipulā*). The Gosvami edition (GGM) has the same text.

**42.** Going along the road together with those accomplished [companions], amid manifestations of love, He plunged into the supreme long pond of the nectar of bliss.

*Note.* A chime of sounds: *premāviṣkṛtibhiḥ kṛtibhiḥ*.

**43.** Then, seeing a splendid tree, He ran to it from afar, unrestrained, leapt up, caught hold of a bough and, hanging from it, displayed [great] beauty.

**44.** Bathed in the waters of His eyes, He embraced the tree again. Whom indeed, and by what means, did Mahaprabhu not deliver?

**45.** In the forest the peacock's cry — not alone, but [joined] by [the call of] the lāvaka bird, as if by a crow — fond of the pure showers, truly and at once giving praise, surpassed in praise [even] sweet intoxication (?).

*Note.* A *pratilomānuloma-pāda*: each pada reads the same from left to right and from right to left (a palindrome): *kā ke ne va va ne ke kā*, and so on. The printed syllables *kā kene va* are taken as *kākena iva*. The translation follows the edition's Sanskrit commentary; the sense is tentative (?). The lāvaka is a kind of quail. The Gosvami edition (GGM) has the same text.

**The commentary's word division of the second half:** *śuddha-āsāra-rasā addhā āśu nuti-rā su-surā-atinu.*

**46.** Thus, overwhelmed, He embraces [these trees as] the trees of Vrindavana; and He embraced the tree so [hard] that it seemed again and again about to crumble to powder.

**47.** When He was about to fall down onto [ground] strewn with thorns, [Paramananda] Puri and the others, in alarm, at once caught Him with their strong arms.

**48.** Then He said: "Look, look! Here is Krishnachandra all around, sporting in every tree! The world is seen to consist of Him."

*Note.* *Jagaty etan mayīkṣyate* is divided *jagatī etan-mayī īkṣyate*, "the world is seen as consisting of Him": *mayā* + *īkṣyate* would give *mayekṣyate*, so *mayī* must be the feminine of the suffix *-maya*, agreeing with *jagatī*. *Paśyāyam* = *paśya ayam* (regular sandhi).

**49.** The water (?), exceedingly clear and boundless, shone constantly, and the surface of the earth [was] free of mud; and most wonderful then was the Lord's journey.

*Note.* The label *ślokāvṛtti* ("repetition of the verse") apparently means that the verse admits a second reading with a different word division (?); how it is meant is not explained. *Avapayaḥ* is unclear (?): the neuter adjectives *ativimalam anantam* seem to agree with *payaḥ*, "water", but *ava-* remains unexplained; the first line may also be referred to the sky or to the earth (?). Clear water and dry earth mark autumn, the season after the rains (cf. 4–5). Metre: the first pada is irregular — its second and third syllables (*-va-pa-*) are both light. The Gosvami edition (GGM) has the same text.

**50.** Through the Lord's triumphal journey this autumn — rich in sap, sweetly smiling with the blossoming flowers of the kasha grass — then shone still more beautifully.

*Note.* Kasha grass (*Saccharum spontaneum*) bears white plumes in autumn; in poetics whiteness is a "smile".

**51.** Arriving at Bhubaneshvara, He saw Bhuvaneshvara [Shiva], honoured the mahaprasada and stopped right there [for the night].

*Note.* Bhubaneshvara is both the town and its deity of the same name — Shiva as Lingaraja, the "Lord of the Worlds". Cf. Chaitanya-charitamrita, Madhya 16.98–99: Ramananda follows the Lord in a palanquin, Vaninatha sends prasada, and next morning the Lord comes to Bhubaneshvara.

**52.** The next day, at the end of the night, the Lord set out with them; and meeting Haridasa ahead [of Him], Mahaprabhu entered a village.

*Note.* Which Haridasa and which village are meant is unclear; the Chaitanya-charitamrita (Madhya 16) does not mention this meeting. According to 54, Ramananda had prepared lodgings here in advance — apparently on his own land.

**53.** That [autumn] — a fresh, ever-fresh earth, full of water and of sarasa cranes, bestowing the essence on all things embodied and unembodied, filled with juices — [shone] (?).

*Note.* A *pratilomānuloma-śloka*: the whole verse, written out syllable by syllable, reads the same from the beginning and from the end. The word division is partly conjectural (?). As printed, the palindrome is imperfect: the first pada has only seven syllables (सा र सा स र सा रं), whereas the mirror image of the fourth (रं सा सा र स सा र सा) requires eight — सा र सा स र सा सा रं; and the fifth syllable of the second pada (*na* in *nūtana*) does not mirror the fourth syllable of the third (*ta*). Metre: apart from the short first pada, the verse is regular. The Gosvami edition (GGM) has the same text.

**54.** Ramananda Raya, who beforehand had by his orders had a new house and other [buildings] put up there, at once led the Lord [to it].

**55.** Seeing the clean, freshly plastered house there, the Treasure-house of Mercy stayed in it with the greatest delight, together with Paramananda Puri.

*Note.* *Lepita* — [walls and floor] plastered with clay and cow dung, a purification of a dwelling. A possible play: *paramānanda-pūriṇā* also suggests "filled with supreme bliss".

**56.** Then from Nilachala an abundance of mahaprasada — fine rice, drinks and cakes — arrived swiftly, again and again.

*Note.* The first syllables of the second half are damaged in the printed text; the reading given is a reconstruction (?). *Piṣṭaka* — rice cakes (Bengali *pitha*).

**57.** He whose command Brahma, Rudra and the others praise, placing it on their heads — can His majesty be known by people? By whom indeed [is His] majesty [known]?

**58.** Seeing it, He was supremely pleased; He honoured it with love together with them, and spent the night in conversation with Sri Ramananda Raya.

**59.** Wholly given to telling various stories together with him, the Lord, beautiful as the lord of the night, passed the night.

*Note.* A play on words: *rajanīṁ nātho rajanī-nātha-*, "the night … the Lord … the lord of the night".

**60.** The Lord and Paramananda Puri too went on ahead, while the wise Ramananda followed along behind [them].

**61.** Thus, going along the road, the Ocean of the rasa of compassion chanted His own names, His sweet lips shining.

*Note.* An *asaṁyoga*: a verse without consonant clusters. Only at the word junction *calan madhura-* do *n* and *m* meet (written in the Devanagari as the ligature न्म).

**62.** Going along thus and seeing a charming lodging by the river, He who is beautiful with sweet lips said, singing sweetly:

**63.** "You go on ahead. There, in the country of Kataka, you will see Me [again] in the temple of the Lord of the gopis."

*Note.* Kataka (Cuttack) was then the capital of Orissa; the Lord of the gopis is Gopinatha, that is, Sakshi-gopala (cf. 11.79). In the Chaitanya-charitamrita (Madhya 16.100) the Lord at Kataka visits the temple of Gopala, and the brahmana Svapneshvara invites Him for a meal.

**64.** Thus instructed, those great souls — Puri and the others — then went off, while Gauranga rested there with someone [of His companions].
