*Note.* *Kenacit* may also mean "for a while" (?).

**65.** Hearing, "The Ocean of Mercy is coming," the lord of elephants by his command had every landing-place tended by [his people's] hands.

*Note.* A *niroṣṭhya*: a verse without labial consonants (*p, ph, b, bh, m*; the anusvara is not counted). The semivowel *v* (in *śrutvā*, *gajeśvaraḥ*), being dento-labial, is evidently not reckoned as labial, nor is the vowel *u* (in *karuṇā*, *sindhuḥ*). The "lord of elephants" is the Gajapati, the title of the kings of Orissa — here Prataparudra. *Tīrtha* — a landing-place or ford for crossing a river. Cf. Chaitanya-charitamrita, Madhya 16.112–116: the king orders houses and new boats to be made ready for the Lord at the crossings, and memorial pillars to be set up.

**66.** Gopinatha, with ornaments covering all His limbs, possessing sweetness, vigour and clarity, shone like a poet's play of words.

*Note.* A double meaning: *alaṅkāra* is both "ornament" and "figure of speech", and *mādhurya*, *ojas* and *prasāda* are the three excellences (*guṇa*) of poetic diction. The edition's Sanskrit commentary explains: *mādhurya* is the delight that melts the heart; *ojas* is the brilliance that expands the heart (in the heroic, the loathsome and the furious rasas); *prasāda* is that which pervades the heart at once, as fire seizes dry wood.

**67.** Having attained a youthful longing, continually new and ever new, the charming king shone like [the month of] Chaitra accompanied by beauty.

*Note.* A double meaning: *taruṇī* is also "a young woman", and *madhura* also "vernal" (*madhu* — spring; Madhu is another name of the month of Chaitra): like spring united with beauty, the king was united with a fresh longing as with a young bride. The king is not named; probably Prataparudra is meant (cf. 65 and 81 ff.) (?).

**68.** Then these great souls, joyful, their faces beaming with contentment, entered the temple of the great Lord Gopinatha.

*Note.* A play on *prāsāda* (temple) and *prasāda* (contentment, grace).

**69.** There [the doorkeepers], canes swinging in their hands, with great joy led them — who had forgotten all other desires — into the inner chamber.

**70.** Seeing Him with love, they attained the very greatest joy. Then a certain local brahmana came and invited Paramananda Puri there for a meal.

*Note.* The verse has three lines (six padas) — perhaps a half-verse of a neighbouring verse merged with it in typesetting (?); all six padas are metrically regular. The Gosvami edition (GGM) has the same text.

**71.** Meanwhile Gaurachandra came, shining like millions of moons, resembling a blazing golden mountain — like the rising of good fortune.

*Note.* A chain of repetitions at the junctions of words: each word picks up the sound of the end of the preceding one (*-candraś candra-*, *-ujjvalaḥ jvalat-*, *-ābho lābho-*).

**72.** Having long beheld charming Gopinatha, who was like a heart's desire embodied, the Ocean of Mercy was filled with joy there.

*Note.* *Manoramam / manoratham* — a play on similar sounds.

**73.** Then this same god on earth, the brahmana named Svapneshvara, invited [the Lord] there for a meal and also brought [Him] to his house.

*Note.* *So 'yam*, "this same", presumably points to the brahmana of verse 70. In the Chaitanya-charitamrita (Madhya 16.100) the brahmana Svapneshvara invites the Lord to a meal at Kataka, and afterwards the Lord rests beneath a bakula tree (16.102).

**74.** But the others, headed by Jagadananda, devoted to bliss, Sri Ramananda Raya led to his own house.

**75.** Then he brought them at that time to a fine garden — charming, giving the pleasure of being beneficial to the household, and giving the unfailing pleasure of hill country (?).

*Note.* *Yamaka*: the two lines open with the same sequence of syllables (*āgārāhitya-sukhade*), which is divided and understood differently in each. The analysis of the edition's Sanskrit commentary: *hitaṁ karotīti hityam* ("that which does good", with the suffix *yat*); *na gacchantīti agāḥ*, "those that do not move", i.e. mountains, with the suffix *aṇ* giving *āgāḥ*; *sadārāma* is an excellent garden near a house. The translation is tentative (?). The Gosvami edition (GGM) has the same text.

**76.** Having made them glad with the delights of the garden, Ramananda, who causes people to exult with joy, then quickly went to the king.

*Note.* A play on sounds: *ārāmārāma-*, *rāmānando janānando-*.

**77.** After resting, they set about cooking there; and Puri Svami, having taken his meal, came there with the Lord.

*Note.* The edition misprints *randhona-*; read *randhana-*, "cooking" (so the text above). Puri Svami is Paramananda Puri, who was invited to a meal in verse 70.

**78.** There, in the middle of the garden, is a tall bakula tree — spreading, with dense shade, a *vakula-druma* among the families [of trees] (?).

*Note.* The second *vakula-drumaḥ* repeats the first (*yamaka*) with a different, unclear sense (?). One interpretation, noted in the IAST line, connects it with Kubera: "among the trees of its kind this bakula was rich as Kubera", i.e. like a wish-fulfilling tree. Since *va* is listed in the lexicons as a particle equal to *iva*, "like", the words can perhaps also be divided *kulānāṁ va kula-drumaḥ*, "like the head tree (*kula-druma*) of the families [of trees]" (?). The Gosvami edition (GGM) has the same text.

**79.** The line of the grove, lovely with its excellent measure [and fanned] on all sides by the breeze, then became joined with living lotuses (?).

*Note.* *Yamaka*: *paramānena* is repeated, and *rājī vanasya* is echoed in *rājīva*. The edition notes a variant: instead of *paramānena lalitā paramānena sarvataḥ* — *pavamānena lalitā pavamānena sarvataḥ*, "charming by the breeze, the breeze [blowing gently] from all sides". The sense of the verse is unclear (?). The Gosvami edition (GGM) has the same text.

**80.** Staying at the foot of the bakula tree, He shone with a beautiful smile, and with this golden form of His He conquered the jambu tree.

*Note.* The cosmic Jambu tree on Meru yields the juice from which *jāmbūnada* gold is born; the golden Gaura beneath the tree surpassed it.

**81.** Meanwhile the crest-jewel of the circle of kings, possessed of great splendour, made a royal progress there in order to see the feet of Gaurachandra.

*Note.* The king is Prataparudra. Metre: the fourth pada has nine syllables instead of eight (*dṛṣṭaye tatra cakāra saḥ*: —⏑——⏑⏑—⏑—); the text is not emended.

**82.** With Ramananda as his companion — like Smara with Spring — accompanied by forces of four divisions, he then arrived at the proper time.

*Note.* *Yamaka*: *samayāt samayāt* — first the ablative of *samaya*, "at the [proper] time", then the imperfect *sam-ayāt*, "arrived". The four divisions of an army are elephants, horses, chariots and infantry.

**83.** Descending from the back of his elephant, the most handsome [king], whose shoulders were like an elephant's, joyfully went on foot towards that garden and shone greatly.

*Note.* *Yamaka*: *gaja-skandhāt* (from the elephant's back) / *gaja-skandhāti-sundaraḥ* (most handsome, with shoulders like an elephant's).

**84.** Surrounded by huge elephants, ever flowing with ichor, [like mountains yet] not immobile, by horses and by the moving wealth of infantry, he shone more and more.

*Note.* *Yamaka*: *sadā sadānaiḥ*, *nāgair nāgair*, *patti-saṁpatti-*, *bhūyo bhūyo*. In *nāgair nāgair* the first word means "elephants"; the second is divided *na-agaiḥ*, "not immobile" (*aga*, "that which does not move", is a mountain or a tree) (?). *Dāna* is the ichor that flows from an elephant's temples in rut.

**85.** As if loudly proclaiming here and there, "There is no king equal to him, either in heaven or on earth!", the horses churned [the ground] with their hooves.

*Note.* *Uccaiḥ* may be the adverb "loudly" (with the implied proclamation) or the instrumental plural of *ucca*, "tall", agreeing with *ghoṭakaiḥ*: "the tall horses".

**86.** Taking Ramananda by the arm and stationing the host of ministers all around, the king approached, like the full moon joined with the sun.

**87.** First [came] that tract of ground, encircled by the ministers, who were like the immortals, stationed within the army; it shone.

*Note.* The edition gives the variant *antar-vana-nivāsibhiḥ* ("dwelling in the forest") and rejects it: "this reading is not good".

**88.** Outside that stood the foot-soldiers; outside them a host of horses; and outside them all the elephants: a veritable battle array was formed then.

*Note.* Cf. the three rings around the dancing Lord in 16.24.

**89.** Seeing the pair of lotus feet there, the king, his heart melting, fell to the ground again and again together with tears of joy.

*Note.* *Asandhy-akṣara*: a verse without diphthongs (*e, ai, o, au*), as the edition's subscript indicates; note also the alliteration *bhūpatir bhū-talaṁ bhūyaḥ*. Cf. the Chaitanya-charitamrita, Madhya 16.102–107: while the Lord rests beneath the bakula tree, Ramananda brings the king, who falls at His feet and offers prayers.

**90.** Having bowed down many times, the king drank with his eyes the lotus of [the Lord's] face and could not be sated. Wonderful are the doings of Gauranga!

*Note.* The edition notes the variant *na tṛptim agamad bhūpaḥ*.

**91.** Gaurachandra too, speaking to him lovingly in many ways, sprinkled his whole body with the nectar of His words, as if embracing him from head to foot.

**92.** Having received [the Lord's] command and favour, his purpose accomplished, he departed; then all the throngs of ministers quickly went to see [the Lord].

*Note.* A play on sounds: *ājñāyājñām*.

**93.** Having learnt the intention of [the Lord], who wished to go beyond the Chitrotpala — as [one would go] into the ocean for many-coloured lotuses (?) — he, the king, said to a worthy [servant]:

*Note.* *Padma-bandha* (the edition's subscript: *padma-bhedaḥ*), "the lotus pattern": a figured verse whose syllables, written out in a diagram, form the shape of a lotus (the arrangement itself is not explained (?)). The first line also contains the near-repetition *pāre-citrotpalam / akūpāre citrotpalam* (*yamaka*). The Chitrotpala is a river (a branch of the Mahanadi; cf. 94). The edition's Sanskrit commentary: *pāre-citrotpalam* is an *avyayībhāva* compound under the rule "*pāre* and *madhye* [are compounded] with a genitive"; *akūpāra* means "ocean" (according to the Amarakosha). The point of the simile is not clear (?).

**94.** "O Mangaraja, you yourself, together with Harichandana, should quickly escort Mahaprabhu beyond the Mahanadi."

*Note.* The edition has *bhavānena*; the Gosvami edition (GGM) reads *bhavān eva*, and this reading is adopted. Mangaraja and Harichandana are officers of the king; cf. the Chaitanya-charitamrita, Madhya 16.113, 126: the king entrusts Harichandana and Mangaraja with the service of the Lord, and they follow Him together with Ramananda.

**95.** By his command they — and Sri Ramananda Raya too — made ready very large boats and then brought the Lord across.

*Note.* *Cakre*, "prepared", is singular (with Ramananda), while *samānayan*, "brought", is plural.

**96.** Then the divine full moon, marked with a deer, rose, and with his rays — his hands — swept the whole road clean.

*Note.* *Kara* means both "ray" and "hand".

**97.** Then, as Gauranga was going on, a royal officer who had come at that time determined [the place] there (?) and announced the king's command to the local people:

**98.** "Hear, O most excellent ones, what His Majesty commands: a pillar is to be erected here, so that this [place] may become a holy place."

*Note.* Cf. the Chaitanya-charitamrita, Madhya 16.115: Prataparudra orders a memorial column to be set up at every place where the Lord bathes or crosses a river.

**99.** Hearing the king's command thus, they erected a pillar there and, placing [the Lord] in a boat, gladly served the Lord with joy.

*Note.* The edition has *āropyo*, a misprint for *āropya*. The play *āropya* — "having erected" (the pillar) / "having placed" (the Lord in the boat).

**100.** Thus, having crossed the river and come to Chaturdvara with all those [companions], the Lord then slept, in a night enveloped on all sides by the sweetness of the moonlight, inside the fine pavilion of Jagannatha; but the people, up to a hundred thousand [in number], could not find even a place there.

*Note.* Metre — *mandākrāntā* (————⏑⏑⏑⏑⏑——⏑——⏑——; 17 syllables per pada); the verse scans correctly. Chaturdvara is a village on the northern bank of the Mahanadi opposite Kataka (cf. the Chaitanya-charitamrita, Madhya 16.116, 122: the king has new houses built there, and on the night of the full moon the Lord crosses to Chaturdvara in a new boat).

**Colophon:** इति श्रीकृष्णचैतन्यचरितामृते महाकाव्ये श्रीवृन्दावनयात्रायामूनविंशः सर्गः ॥ (*iti śrī-kṛṣṇa-caitanya-caritāmṛte mahākāvye śrī-vṛndāvana-yātrāyām ūnaviṁśaḥ sargaḥ*) — "Thus [ends] the nineteenth sarga, 'The Journey to Sri Vrindavana', in the mahakavya 'Sri Krishna-Chaitanya-charitamrita'."

---

## Sarga Twenty — verses 1–49

**Title of the sarga:** none — the colophon of the edition gives no title for this sarga (*iti śrī-kṛṣṇa-caitanya-caritāmṛte mahākāvye śrīla-karṇapūra-kṛtau viṁśatitamaḥ sargaḥ*, "Thus [ends] the twentieth sarga in the great poem Sri Krishna-Chaitanya-charitamrita, the work of Srila Karnapura"; it goes on: "This great poem Sri Krishna-Chaitanya-charitamrita is complete. Number of verses: 1911").
**Metre:**
- 1–36 and 41 — *śālinī* (*ma-ta-ta-ga-ga*: — — — — | — ⏑ — — ⏑ — —; 11 syllables per pada, caesura after the fourth). The edition says: "up to verse 36 — *śālinī*".
- 37 and 47 — *śārdūla-vikrīḍita* (*ma-sa-ja-sa-ta-ta-ga*: ———⏑⏑—⏑—⏑⏑⏑———⏑——⏑—; 19 syllables per pada).
- 38–39 and 48 — *mandākrāntā* (*ma-bha-na-ta-ta-ga-ga*: ————⏑⏑⏑⏑⏑——⏑——⏑——; 17 syllables per pada).
- 40 — *śikhariṇī* (*ya-ma-na-sa-bha-la-ga*: ⏑—————⏑⏑⏑⏑⏑——⏑⏑⏑—; 17 syllables per pada).
- 42–45 and 49 — *vasantatilakā* (*ta-bha-ja-ja-ga-ga*: ——⏑—⏑⏑⏑—⏑⏑—⏑——; 14 syllables per pada).
- 46 — *mālinī* (*na-na-ma-ya-ya*: ⏑⏑⏑⏑⏑⏑———⏑——⏑——; 15 syllables per pada, caesura after the eighth).

**Total verses:** 49. The publisher gives the total number of verses of the poem as 1911 (in the colophon and in his note after verse 49). The printed numbering of the twenty sargas runs without gaps or repetitions and adds up to 1912 (29 + 122 + 144 + 77 + 129 + 123 + 107 + 63 + 95 + 80 + 89 + 134 + 148 + 135 + 110 + 49 + 66 + 63 + 100 + 49); the difference of one is not explained by the numbering itself and is presumably a slip in the publisher's count (his other figures in the same note are also inexact; see the note on 49). The unnumbered quotations in Sargas 6 and 7 are not included in either figure.

**Contents of the passage:** the journey through Bengal, the return, the Lord's departure; the author's conclusion.
- 1–10 — the Lord dismisses the ministers; by Prataparudra's order houses are built for Him everywhere; crowds of people; the Lord and Puri point each other out (7); Ramananda turns back from Bhadra (9).
- 11–19 — Gauda: the abode of Raghava (12); the house of Srivasa and the crowds; the secret departure by boat at night; Srivasa and the others search for Him.
- 20–25 — Shantipura: the meeting with Advaita; **Mother Shachi** (23); six days; Advaita regales the people.
- 26–30 — a place across the Ganga, west of Navadvipa (Kuliya?); crowds; the Lord hides Himself.
- 31–36 — an obscure place, "Gopinatha" (31); the way towards the Yamuna, the return to the Ganga, then to Puri (32–34); the second departure for the Yamuna (35); Ramananda gives up his life in separation (36 — at variance with the Chaitanya-charitamrita).
- 37–41 — summary: twenty years of festivals in Puri; the Lord's departure to His own abode; chronology: sannyasa in His twenty-fourth year, three years of wandering, twenty years in Puri, forty-seven years in all (40–41).
- 42–49 — the author's conclusion:
  - the source — the records of **Murari** (Gupta), which "the child saw" (42–43);
  - a blessing on the poem; the author's humility (44–45, 48);
  - the signature: the youngest son of Shivananda Sena (46);
  - the hero of the poem is *dhīrodātta* (47);
  - the date of completion: Shaka 1464 (1542 CE), the month of Ashadha, a Monday, the second tithi of the dark fortnight (49).

**1.** Hearing the warbling of the birds — "The night has gone, O Master; leave Your bed!" — He quickly rubbed with His hand His eyes, sealed by sleep, [saying to them,] "Wake up!", and then rose.

*Note.* The metre is *śālinī* (so the edition: "up to verse 36 — *śālinī*"). As printed, the second pada has only ten syllables (*ākarṇya pakṣiṇāṁ kūjitāni*: — — ⏑ — ⏑ — — ⏑ — ⏑): of the four heavy syllables of the opening only three are present, and *-ṇya* is light; something has dropped out. The printed run *pāṇināthodiyāya* is to be divided *pāṇinā atha udiyāya*, "with the hand; then [He] rose"; the division *pāṇinā thodiyāya* in the transliteration line is merely mechanical.

**2.** Seeing the sanctified food that had just arrived there, He joyfully began His daily rites; and having eaten to His heart's content, with all of them, rice, drink, cakes and the rest, He set out, pleased.

*Note.* The optative *ārabheta* is used in the sense of the past (for the metre).

**3.** There the two ministers were duly dismissed by Him. They shed abundant water from the lotuses of their eyes, and their bodies — alas! — wasted away; and such zeal [had been shown] by them! What a way the Creator has!

*Note.* The ministers are Mangaraja and Harichandana (19.94).

**4.** To land after land, on every side, came all the king's written orders: at every place a brand-new, clean dwelling was to be made ready in advance, with provisions.

**5.** Wherever this Master appeared, there, out of the excess of their joy, people numbering hundreds of thousands worshipped Him without hindrance with the lotuses of their eyes and praised Him with plaintive words.

**6.** "Today or tomorrow He will surely come here!" — such was the cry of joy that rang out very loudly ahead [of Him]; afterwards, "He is coming!"; then, "Ho, ho! He has come!" — and so again everywhere afterwards.

**7.** Some worthy people there asked: "Where is He, where is He — Krishna-Chaitanya-chandra?" Thereupon those two pointed each other out to them: [Puri showed them] the Master, that Lord, [and the Lord showed them] that Puri.

*Note.* A playful scene: each of the two points to the other. Puri is presumably Paramananda Puri, the Lord's companion on this journey (19.6, 12). There is no such scene in the Chaitanya-charitamrita (Madhya 16).

**8.** Whenever He arrived at dawn at one halting place after another, all [the people] in every land, bound by the order of the kings, built clean houses and in rapture brought provisions.

*Note.* *Rājñām* is plural, "of the kings"; perhaps it stands for *rājño*, "of the king".

**9.** Ramananda, having come as far as Bhadra and been duly dismissed by Him, turned back and, afflicted by separation, set out for [Sri] Kshetra; and Gauranga for His part went on to the north.

*Note.* Bhadra is Bhadraka. According to the Chaitanya-charitamrita (Madhya 16.151–155), Ramananda accompanies the Lord as far as Remuna, and there, taking leave, falls unconscious.

**10.** As far as Odra [extended], [the places] were provided with the king's letters; and all that way the officials in charge thus brought in abundance the sanctified remnants of the morning *dhupa* offering, fit for a king.

*Note.* Odra is Orissa.

**11.** Having reached the land of Gauda, the beautiful [Lord], impelled by the agitation of love — "The Ganga must be seen!" — shone, His inner self softened and charmed by contact with her, His limbs refreshed by those various places.

*Note.* Gauda is Bengal. The Gosvami edition (GGM) has the same text.

**12.** Having come into the abode of Sri Raghava, as the moon [comes] to the Eastern Mountain, Sri Gaura, possessed by love and delighted, rejoiced amid fragrances, garlands, flowers, incense and offerings.

*Note.* Raghava Pandita of Panihati (cf. the Chaitanya-charitamrita, Madhya 16.202–205: the Lord arrives at Panihati by boat, and Raghava Pandita leads Him with difficulty through the crowd to his house). As printed, the second pada has only ten syllables (*śrī-gauraś candravat pūrva-śailam*: — — — — ⏑ — — ⏑ — ⏑): of the five heavy syllables of the opening only four are present.

**13.** Having stayed there in Raghava's abode and spent five or six days, the Master, joyful, meanwhile lovingly sent the senior [companion] ahead to the land of Sri Navadvipa.

*Note.* "The senior" is apparently Paramananda Puri. The Gosvami edition (GGM) has the same text.

**14.** When he had gone, Gaurachandra arrived [with His companions] at the house of Srivasa, the vessel of love; and staying there, compassionate to every living being, He showed mercy everywhere.

*Note.* According to the Chaitanya-charitamrita (Madhya 16.205–206), on this visit the Lord stays at Kumarahatta with Srivasa, and then visits Shivananda Sena and Vasudeva Datta.

**15.** In two or three days here hundreds of thousands [of people], immersed again and again in an ocean of joy, came and went from all sides, and there was not [even] a gap here. Such is His power!

**16.** Some people were in the streets, at the doors of houses, in the trees; others, for the most part, on the walls — [standing] like pictures on the wall of a pleasure-house; and they never reached the end of their longing.

*Note.* *Okaḥ-dvāri* keeps the visarga before *d* against the rules of sandhi (regularly *oko-dvāri*); the metre does not require it, since *-ko-* would be heavy as well.

**17.** At night, alone and concealed, He came by boat to another place, north of that village; having gone to the house of Sri Vasudeva, He [then] set out for the house of Sri Shivananda.

*Note.* *Pāyāt* is probably a misprint for *prāyāt*, "set out"; one could also divide *gatvā 'pāyāt* (*apāyāt*, "went away"). From the context (18–19: Srivasa and the others search for Him and cannot find Him) the subject is the Lord Himself, who has secretly left the crowd. Shivananda Sena was of Kumarahatta (Kanchanapalli); Vasudeva is Vasudeva Datta: the Chaitanya-charitamrita (Madhya 16.206) names the two together. The Gosvami edition (GGM) has the same text.

**18.** Having spent one night in this house, He took His meal in that same northern quarter; and, accompanied by hundreds of thousands of the people of those parts, He boarded a boat and went to Shantipura.

**19.** Then Srivasa and the others, not seeing Him, were alarmed and searched everywhere at length; and as long as He was not found, they remained in great grief, sorely, sorely tormented.

*Note.* *Abhāvi* is an impersonal passive ("it was [so] with them"). As printed, the fourth syllable of the fourth pada is light (*gāḍhaṁ gāḍham ardya-*: — — — ⏑ — ⏑ …), where the *śālinī* requires a heavy one.

**20.** Going by boat in the midst of the Heavenly River and, His heart tremulous, singing out the song of the Names, the Master reached Advaita's village and rose out of love, extremely eager to go [to Him].

**21.** In the doorway there took place His most precious meeting with Him, amid the clamour of embraces; [yet] it was not enough to satisfy the eyes and ears of these fortunate beings.

*Note.* The printed *āsīn naiṣāṁ* is to be divided *āsīt* | *na eṣām*: *āsīt* completes the first sentence ("there was the meeting"), and *na … tṛptyai babhūva* says that what they saw and heard did not sate the eyes and ears of the fortunate onlookers — they could not have enough of it. So the printed text gives good sense, and an emendation to *āsīd eṣām* ("it became the satisfaction of the eyes and ears of these fortunate beings") is not necessary. The Gosvami edition (GGM) has the same text.

**22.** Again and again Advaita and Gaura, overwhelmed by love, pressed each other tightly in embraces. At the end of that He [Advaita] honoured Him, this [guest], thus ⟨fittingly?⟩, with worship, service and the play of words.

*Note.* *Yogam* is unclear (?); perhaps a misprint for *yogyam*, "fittingly" (*tathā yogyam*, "as was fitting"), which would scan equally. The Gosvami edition (GGM) has the same text.

**23.** Then came the goddess named Sri Shachi, truly the mother of the three worlds; seeing Him, she felt herself immersed in an ocean of joy, with delight, pain and bashfulness.

*Note.* The edition prints *trailokya-nām* (त्रैलोक्यनामेव), a slip for *trailokyānām* (or *trailokyasya*); the metre requires a heavy third syllable, that is, *trailokyānām*, as in the transliteration. The Gosvami edition (GGM) has the same text.

**24.** Right there Sri Gauranga stayed six days in succession, satisfied with the food His mother gave Him, accepting the service lovingly offered by the Acharya, and giving joy to the eyes of [all] living beings.

*Note.* The Acharya is Advaita.

**25.** In the course of those days people assembled ⟨*yāmo*?⟩ in hundreds of thousands and millions, and every day that Acharya likewise joyfully regaled them abundantly with provisions.

*Note.* *Yāmo* is unclear (?) (perhaps *yāvat* or *yāmyāḥ*, "southerners"); in the printed form it does not agree with the rest of the sentence. The Gosvami edition (GGM) has the same text.

**26.** On another day the beautiful [Lord], having come to a certain place beyond the Ganga, to the west of the land of Sri Navadvipa, fully spread joy for the eyes of all living beings by each of His limbs.

*Note.* According to the Chaitanya-charitamrita (Madhya 16.207–209), [He stayed] at the house of Vidya-Vachaspati, and then for a week at the house of Madhava Dasa, where hundreds of thousands of people flocked (by tradition, in Kuliya).

**27.** Whether mute, or lame, or blind, or old, or a child, or women — all those, whoever they were, who dwelt in the land of Sri Navadvipa, every one of them then went [to Him] out of an excess of love.

**28.** As long as Gaurangachandra stayed there, so long did all of them — hundreds of thousands and millions, women and men alike, utterly afflicted by deep longing — gather from everywhere to see Him.

**29.** From time to time, greatly troubled by the throngs of people there, He hid Himself, many times over; but the longing of all those who cried at the top of their voices grew ever more intense.

**30.** Having thus spent five or six days there in succession out of an excess of love, spreading joy for the eyes of all people, the Master set out, together with all of them, for the divine land.

*Note.* "The divine land" is unclear (?); according to verse 32 He goes westwards, towards the Yamuna. *Tais taiḥ* may also be understood as instrumental of means, as in verse 26 (*tat-tad-aṅgaiḥ … netrānandaṁ tene*): "spreading joy for the eyes of all people by those various [charms]". The Gosvami edition (GGM) has the same text.

**31.** Somewhere in that region — renowned as "Gopinatha[-shi]" (?) by the derivation "he lies at Gopinatha" — Gaurangachandra, having seen a certain [person], was overcome by love and rejoiced continually.

*Note.* An obscure verse (?). The syntax does not resolve cleanly: *kaścit* is nominative, *prasiddham* accusative or neuter. The translation takes *prasiddham* with the region and *kaścit* as the person the Lord saw. *-śīti* is probably *śī iti*: the name "Gopinatha-shi" (*śī*, "lying") explained by the derivation *gopīnāthe śete*, "he lies at Gopinatha". If *kaścit* is a misprint for *kañcit* (accusative), the sense would be "having seen a certain [one] renowned as 'Gopinatha-shi'". Perhaps a place where Gopinatha "sleeps" is meant (cf. Remuna with Kshirachora Gopinatha?); it cannot be identified from our sources. The Gosvami edition (GGM) has the same text.

**32.** Deeply longing to go to the very bank of the Kalindi, He went somewhere to the west; but, having considered something in His own heart, He turned back and came again to the bank of the Heavenly River.

*Note.* The Kalindi is the Yamuna; the Heavenly River (*svardhunī*) is the Ganga. According to the Chaitanya-charitamrita (Madhya 16.211, 265–274), the Lord, having reached Kanai Natashala, turns back, remembering Sanatana's words that one does not go to Vrindavana with a crowd.

**33.** Having again stayed to His heart's content in those various places and shown long, long compassion, He again set out for Sri Niladri. Wonderful, wonderful is His every deed!

**34.** On this or that pretext He came to the bank of the Heavenly River, where the beautiful [Lord] had wondrously descended [into the world]; having given joy to the eyes of all people, He was again [in Puri], for the delight of Him who dwells on Niladri.

*Note.* *Nīlādri-stha*, "He who dwells on Niladri", is Jagannatha.

**35.** Having stayed there for some time, Gaurachandra, full of splendour, again set out by the road (?) for the very bank of the Kalindi, leaving all of them there afflicted by separation.

*Note.* Metre: the second pada (*kañcit kālaṁ bhūyo 'dhvanaiva*) has only nine syllables (——————⏑—⏑), where *śālinī* requires eleven (—————⏑——⏑——): two syllables are missing, not one. A reading *bhūya evādhvanaiva* (*bhūyaḥ eva adhvanā eva*, cf. *bhūya eva* in verse 33) would scan correctly (?); the text is not emended. According to the Chaitanya-charitamrita (Madhya 17), the second journey to Vrindavana goes through the forest of Jharikhanda, not through Bengal; Karnapura compresses the events. The Gosvami edition (GGM) has the same text.

**36.** Ramananda, the great soul, utterly wasted away by the torment of the anguish of separation from Him, gave up his life-breaths. Surely, in separation, this is the conduct fitting for such a love as this.

*Note.* The last verse of the run in *śālinī* (26–36; verse 41 is again in *śālinī*). The statement that Ramananda gave up his life from separation diverges from the Chaitanya-charitamrita, where Ramananda remains with the Lord in Puri to the end and hears verses with Him in the Gambhira (Antya 20).

**37.** Having stayed there for some days, the beautiful Lord came again to the Dark Mountain, rejoiced, and unceasingly gladdened these people. Thus, having beheld all the festivals that took place in the course of twenty years, the Ocean of Mercy then — alas! — went to His own abode, together with some of them.

*Note.* Metre — *śārdūla-vikrīḍita* (———⏑⏑—⏑—⏑⏑⏑———⏑——⏑—; 19 syllables per pada); the verse scans correctly. The Dark Mountain (*asitādri*) is Nilachala (Puri). "Went to His own abode" — the disappearance of the Lord from the world. "With some of them" — together with those associates who departed after Him.

**38.** Having immersed the world in the boundless ocean of love, He then made it sink also in the utterly impassable fire of separation. Wonderful, wonderful! Even so, the ocean of love was always the stronger. What is this — Heaven forbid! — great power of Gaurachandra?

*Note.* Metre — *mandākrāntā* (————⏑⏑⏑⏑⏑——⏑——⏑——; 17 syllables per pada); the verse scans correctly. *Śiva śiva* — an exclamation, here of awe mingled with dismay.

**39.** Having thus gathered His own people from various lands in one place and made them fully bound to one another by close love, He — ah! — sported with all of them in Gauda and Utkala; [and] when He — alas! — had gone to His own abode, the earth sank into an ocean of the fire of separation.

*Note.* Metre — *mandākrāntā*; the verse scans correctly. Utkala is Orissa.

**40.** In the twenty-fourth [year], overpowered by His own love, which He had made manifest, He first fully accepted sannyasa, [leaving] from Navadvipa; three years He spent going hither and thither, [setting out] even from the Kshetra; and thus, beholding the festivals, He passed all of twenty years.

*Note.* Metre — *śikhariṇī* (⏑—————⏑⏑⏑⏑⏑——⏑⏑⏑—; 17 syllables per pada); the verse scans correctly. *Tri-varṣaḥ* is nominative; the sense requires "three years" as the object of *agamayat* (?). Karnapura's chronology: sannyasa at twenty-four, three years of wandering, twenty years in Puri — forty-seven years of life in all (cf. verse 41). In the Chaitanya-charitamrita (Madhya 1.15–21): twenty-four years in Navadvipa and twenty-four years after sannyasa — six years of travel and eighteen years in Puri.

**41.** Thus Sri Gauranga, sporting on earth through a succession of forty years and seven, and displaying the dance of various pastimes, then went to His own abode.

*Note.* Metre — *śālinī* (—————⏑——⏑——; 11 syllables per pada). The edition has a superfluous syllable in the fourth pada (*svaṁ svaṁ*); the Gosvami edition (GGM) reads *dhāma svaṁ*, which is adopted here and scans correctly.

**42.** Whatever charm of the pastimes was written down by a certain [person] bearing the auspicious name "Murari" — one who from childhood knew the deeds and pastimes of the Lord, who knew these things — having seen all that, this very child wrote [it].

*Note.* Metre — *vasantatilakā* (——⏑—⏑⏑⏑—⏑⏑—⏑——; 14 syllables per pada), verses 42–45. Karnapura names the records of Murari Gupta (Murari's *kaḍacā*) as the source of the poem; a comparison of the two texts is a matter for separate study. "The child" is the author himself. *Ā-śaiśavam* may also mean "from [the Lord's] childhood on". The plural instrumentals (*kaiścit … vijñaiḥ … nāmadheyaiḥ … taj-jñaiḥ*) are honorific. In the edition *keścin* is a misprint for *kaiścin*.

**43.** With palms joined upon my head, with words full of humble entreaty, I bow down again and again to him who is named Murari, of simple and tender mind — for indeed by his grace the nectar of the deeds of Chaitanya-chandra has been drunk [by me] with the eyes.

*Note.* *Asau sa* — pronouns that fill out the metre: *asau* can go with *aham* ("I, this one"), while *sa* (nominative) does not agree with the accusative *murāri-saṁjñam* (?).

**44.** May all, with joy, carry in their gaze and in their mind the most generous "Chaitanya-chandra-charitamrita", which, merely seen, unceasingly destroys the ocean of samsara, whose far shore is hard to reach and which is full of fierce predators.

*Note.* Metre: the second pada is one syllable short (*sarve dṛśā ca manasā mudā vahantu*: ——⏑—⏑⏑⏑—⏑—⏑—⏑, 13 syllables instead of 14); with *ca* after *manasā* (as the bracket in the transliteration suggests) it scans correctly (——⏑—⏑⏑⏑—⏑⏑—⏑——) (?). *Ajasram* may also be taken with *udagra-hiṁsram*: "ceaselessly [full of] fierce predators". The title of the poem is played upon. The Gosvami edition (GGM) has the same text (without *ca*).

**45.** Alas, I am not capable of praise, of bowing, or of humble entreaty, so that by these I might bring a multitude of people under my sway. But may the wise, resorting to their own compassion, do here — oh! — whatever is fitting.

**46.** Here on earth there lives a certain Sri Shivananda Sena, an embodiment of the rasa of love for the supremely merciful Gaurachandra; one younger child of his, out of utter simplicity, made this wonderful composition.

*Note.* Metre — *mālinī* (⏑⏑⏑⏑⏑⏑———⏑——⏑——; 15 syllables per pada); the verse scans correctly. The author's signature: Kavi Karnapura (Paramananda) is the youngest son of Shivananda Sena.

**47.** That [poem] in which the hero is He — *dhīrodātta*, supremely great, a treasury of virtues; in which these writings have syllables that manifest an unceasingly growing love; in which is contained the conduct of many of the very greatest minds — may this description of the deeds of Chaitanya triumph forever on earth!

*Note.* Metre — *śārdūla-vikrīḍita*; the verse scans correctly. *Dhīrodātta*, "steadfast and exalted", is the type of hero that the canon of poetics (the Sahitya-darpana) requires of a mahakavya; Karnapura shows that his poem meets the requirements of the genre.

**48.** This [poem] removes the threefold miseries; its sole seed is love alone; its very nature is nothing but the exalted glory of Sri Gauranga, imbued with love. Seeing [in it] the path of their own hearts, and not considering me, may the wise, delighted, wear this charming composition upon their necks forever!

*Note.* Metre — *mandākrāntā*; the verse scans correctly. "Wear on the neck" — as a necklace or garland.

**49.** In the Shaka year known [as] "the Vedas, the rasas, the shrutis, the moon", and indeed in the auspicious month of Shuchi, on the day bearing the name of the Nectar-rayed [moon], within the second lunar day of the dark [fortnight], the completion of this [work] took place.

*Note.* Metre — *vasantatilakā*; the verse scans correctly. A chronogram (*bhūta-saṅkhyā*): the Vedas = 4, the rasas = 6, the shrutis = 4, the moon = 1; the digits are read from right to left — Shaka 1464, i.e. 1542 CE; the month of Ashadha (June–July), a Monday, the second tithi of the dark fortnight. The publisher adds: the Lord appeared in Shaka 1407 and lived forty-seven years; the poem was written nine years after His disappearance; "it is now Shaka 1905, that is, the poem is 498 years old; it has 1911 verses". The publisher's reckoning is inexact: 1407 + 47 = 1454, and from 1454 to 1464 is ten years (according to the Chaitanya-charitamrita, the Lord's disappearance was in Shaka 1455, 1533 CE); 498 = 1905 − 1407, the years since the Lord's appearance, whereas from Shaka 1464 to 1905 is 441 years (?). On the total of 1911 (the printed numbering of the twenty sargas adds up to 1912), see **Total verses** in the header of the sarga.

**Colophon:** इति श्रीकृष्णचैतन्यचरितामृते महाकाव्ये श्रीलकर्णपूरकृतौ विंशतितमः सर्गः ॥ समाप्तमिदं श्रीकृष्णचैतन्यचरितामृतं महाकाव्यम् । श्लोकसंख्या १९११ । (*iti śrī-kṛṣṇa-caitanya-caritāmṛte mahākāvye śrīla-karṇapūra-kṛtau viṁśatitamaḥ sargaḥ || samāptam idaṁ śrī-kṛṣṇa-caitanya-caritāmṛtaṁ mahākāvyam | śloka-saṅkhyā 1911 |*) — "Thus [ends] the twentieth sarga in the mahakavya 'Sri Krishna-Chaitanya-charitamrita', the work of Srila Karnapura. Completed is this mahakavya 'Sri Krishna-Chaitanya-charitamrita'. Number of verses: 1911."
